### **Preface** 

ATP 3-96.1 provides techniques for the Security Force Assistance Brigade (SFAB) to conduct security force assistance (SFA). ATP 3-96.1 provides techniques for leaders who plan, prepare, execute, and assess SFA operations. The information in this Army techniques publication is consistently reviewed and analyzed against real-world observations of SFABs. 

The principal audience for ATP 3-96.1 is the commanders, staffs, officers, noncommissioned officers of the brigade, battalions, squadrons, and advisor teams within the SFAB. The audience includes the United States Army Training and Doctrine Command institutions and components as well as joint and Army commands that employ the SFAB in conventional operations. The publication serves as an authoritative reference for personnel developing doctrine, materiel, institutional and unit training, and standard operating procedures for the SFAB. 

To understand ATP 3-96.1, the reader must first understand: operational art, the principles of war, and the links between the operational and tactical levels of war (described in JP 1, Vol 2, JP 3-0, and ADP 3-0); strategic relationships and environments (described in FM 3-0); how offensive, defensive, and stability operations form the foundation of multidomain operations (described in detail in ADP 3-07, ADP 3-90, FM 3-0, FM 3-22, FM 3-96, ATP 3-90.1, and ATP 3-90.5); the operations process (plan, prepare, execute, and assess) and how that process relates to the Army’s military decision-making process (MDMP) and troop leading procedures (described in ADP 5-0); multinational operations, the Army’s role in large-scale combat operations and the Army’s approach to mission command and interoperability within multinational operations with mission partners (described in JP 3-16 and FM 3-16); and the concepts associated with mission command (described in ADP 6-0). Reviewing these publications assists the reader in understanding ATP 3-96.1. 

Commanders, staffs, and subordinates ensure that their decisions and actions comply with applicable United States, international, and in some cases host-nation laws and regulations. Commanders at all levels ensure that their Service members operate in accordance with the law of war and the rules of engagement. (See FM 6-27 for legal compliance.)

### **Introduction** 

SFA is a fixture in U.S. Army history which advisors have proved is vital to the strategic aims of the United States. The Army’s history is full of SFA. Examples range from training police in Bosnia, large-scale combat operations in China during World War II to training the South Korean and South Vietnamese forces during the Korean War and Vietnam War, to training Iraqi and Afghan forces during the War on Terrorism. 

The increasing complexity of the modern security environment increases the need for persistent contact and engagement with allies and partners. SFA is an economy of force method for the United States to win in or prevent conflict, reassure partners, and compete with adversaries. 

The SFAB allows the Army to set the theater effectively and shape the security environment across the range of military operations, in cooperation with joint, interagency, intergovernmental, and multinational partners’ efforts. The SFAB complements the U.S. Department of State, foreign area officers, special operations, the National Guard State Partnership Program, regionally aligned forces, security assistance team, digital liaison detachments and other joint, interagency, intergovernmental, and multinational partner activities to expand access and create options for senior military and civilian leaders. 

The SFAB conducts all three doctrinal security cooperation missions to include security force assistance (Title 10), security assistance (Title 22), and military engagement. The SFAB facilitates information flow, develops training, and prepares Soldiers, leaders, and formations to conduct effective regional engagement. The SFABs provide the combatant commander and the Army Service component command a responsive, expeditionary force with region-specific training. It has appropriate institutionalized training to support small and distributed operations and provide subject matter expertise regarding multinational military training. Through regional engagement and maintaining continuous relationships, the SFAB builds increased trust, rapport, and confidence with joint, interagency, intergovernmental, and multinational partners. The SFAB serves as an institutionalized force that coordinates military-to-military conventional training missions and collective training exercises at a variety of levels based on need. The SFAB also allows the U.S. military to increase regional understanding and preserve unified action in the theater of operations across the breadth of the competition continuum. 

ATP 3-96.1 contains seven chapters: 

Chapter 1 addresses the operational requirement, environment, and key concepts, but primarily focuses on the internal operations and organization of the SFAB. 

Chapter 2 describes SFAB mission command and its operations framework. 

Chapter 3 describes the brigade’s deployment considerations from receipt of mission through post-deployment activities. 

Chapter 4 describes the advisor team activities (both developmental and conflict integration) and advisor team employment planning considerations. 

Chapter 5 describes augmentation considerations for permissive and hostile environments. This chapter also describes the various capabilities that may be available through augmentation by warfighting function. 

Chapter 6 discusses the protection warfighting function considerations through the competition continuum and how advisor teams shape protection advising for foreign security forces partners. 

Chapter 7 describes the SFAB’s sustainment advising battalion and its responsibility of the coordination of the SFAB sustainment requirements across the competition continuum. 

Appendix A provides elements and ideas for what commanders may use for advising guidance, as well as some examples of advising guidance. 

Appendix B illustrates common terminology and terms the SFAB use to clarify organizational roles. 

**Introduction** 

Appendix C provides an advisor assessments tool which can be used to assist in the assessments process. 

Summary of changes: 

- Throughout the manual, there has been a thematic shift to focus on the SFAB in the context of large-scale combat operation. 

- Adds proponent term, advisor network. Advisor network is the system for the integrating and consolidating partner force capability and capacity improvements over time at multiple echelons. Advisor networks are essentially the connective tissue to gain shared understanding across employed advisor teams at echelon across the foreign security force. The network produces effects by and through advisor employment at scales across the depth and breadth of a partner organization over time. It also enables expertise and counsel at echelon. 

- New acronyms include battalion advising team, maneuver battalion advising team, maneuver company advisor team, maneuver advisor team, fires company advisor team, fires advisor team, engineer company advisor team, engineer advisor team, logistics company advisor team, and logistics advisor team. 

##### **Chapter 1** 

### **Organization and Capabilities** 

_SFABs are a conflict force trained and validated to assess, support, liaise, and advise partner nations at echelon, ready to enable winning the Nation’s wars._ 

Brigadier General Kevin J. Lambert, 

4th Commanding General, Security Force Assistance Command 

Chapter 1 addresses the operational requirement, environment, and key concepts, but primarily focuses on the internal operations and organization of the Security Force Assistance Brigade (SFAB). 

##### **SECTION I – ORGANIZATION OVERVIEW** 

1-1. The SFAB is a unique and purpose-built force. Through focusing on security force assistance (SFA) missions, SFABs allow Army divisions and brigade combat teams (BCTs) to focus on preparing for large-scale combat operations (LSCO). In the event of LSCO, SFABs provide comprehensive linkage, amplifying allied and partner integration into a multinational force (MNF). 

1-2. SFABs, regionally aligned to the combatant commands (CCMDs), can deploy elements during competition to develop allies and partners, can surge in support of crisis, and can conduct conflict integration activities during armed conflict in support of multinational operations. 

#### **MISSION** 

1-3. The purpose of the SFABs is to enable allies and partners in conflict. SFABs increase the capability, capacity, and interoperability of our allies and partners. Supporting CCMD theater security cooperation (SC) objectives, SFABs conduct worldwide SFA at the operating and generating levels to develop the capability and capacity of foreign security forces (FSF) and their institutions during competition. In transition from crisis to conflict, advisors can respond quickly and embed with partner force headquarters. This enables U.S. Forces to mobilize, deploy, and have access to partner forces at echelon 

#### **OPERATING ENVIRONMENT** 

1-4. Threats to U.S. and allied interests occur along a competition continuum (see figure 1-1 on page 2). The SFAB operates along the entire continuum. 

**Figure 1-1. Security Force Assistance Brigade in the strategic environment** 

##### **COMPETITION** 

1-5. The SFAB supports theater SC objectives, preserves conventional force readiness, strengthens alliances, and builds lasting partnerships. An SFAB uses the SFA developmental activities to improve the partnered force and build interoperability with U.S. Forces. The SFAB also relieves conventional BCTs of SFA missions, allowing them to modernize and build readiness in preparation for LSCO. An SFAB organizes and equips to operate in austere environments with minimal force footprints. The SFAB advisor teams provide a flexible, tailorable, and credible force providing a constant presence while engaged with allies and partners. SFABs build relationships and partner capabilities and develop trust during competition. The advisor team must be equipped to handle the unique requirements for competition. This includes expeditionary sources of power generation and power management, water purification, tailorable solutions to personal protective equipment, and any other mission- or regional-specific equipment required.

##### **CRISIS** 

1-6. Crisis is a possible transition point between competition and armed conflict. Advisor teams need to work parallel efforts with unified action partners (UAPs) to determine the necessary action required. The action may include advisor teams’ redeployment or change in authorities to continue to support partners and allies. Critical to this phase is an advisor team’s capability to communicate ground truth and requirements across multiple layers of command. SFABs during crisis can have a deterrent effect to allow a return to competition. 

##### **ARMED CONFLICT** 

1-7. SFABs assess, support, liaise, and advise (ASLA) at critical points of need, filling partner capability gaps. Based on hostile development, SFABs bring mission partners together to deter and influence the hostile environment. This builds capability, increases capacity, enhances interoperability, enables visualization, and informs decision points for the joint force commander. 

#### **REGIONAL ALIGNMENT** 

1-8. The SFAB is the Army's dedicated conventional organization for conducting SFA. The organization operates in a wide variety of threat environments, geographic locations, legal agreements, and command structures across the competition continuum. SFABs will be deployed forward continuously in theater to support SC, enabling and enhancing interoperability, enhancing situational awareness, and setting favorable conditions for future multinational operations if diplomacy and deterrence fail. 

1-9. An SFAB can rapidly deploy, conduct SFA activities and missions, and assist with theater entry operations. The SFABs are aligned regionally while remaining an Army service-retained organization. Regional alignment allows an SFAB to gain a deeper appreciation and understanding of FSF organizations, languages, and cultures (see figure 1-2 on page 4). Habitual rotations within an area of responsibility (AOR) allow an SFAB to make gains continually in developing capabilities and capacities. The benefit of access, placement, and influence within an AOR are a primary byproduct of regionally aligned advisor forces. 

1-10. While in theater, SFABs fall under the authority of their respective Army Service component commands (ASCCs). While in garrison, SFABs predominately fall under United States Army Forces Command's authority with further delegation to the Security Force Assistance Command (SFAC). SFAC further issues its own guidance and policies when it comes to SFAB training, mission readiness, and deployment readiness. While in support of joint operations, the SFAB may be tactical control (TACON) or operational control (OPCON) to the joint task force (JTF) or the land component command. Command relationships are mission dependent and tailored to allow SFAB to advise at the point of need and accomplish the higher headquarters’ desired end state for the AOR. 

1-11. During competition, the SFAB preserves conventional force readiness, and strengthens alliances. SFABs, comprised of globally network- enabled, combined arms teams, are agile and designed to operate in austere environments with minimal force footprints. SFABs are an economy of force mission; their size, agility, and network connectivity provide the joint force commander with a flexible, tailorable, and credible force that can be employed dynamically with allies and partners to shape the strategic environment. Conceptually, the SFABs gain and maintain contact to provide time and maneuver space for the main body at the strategic level. Simultaneously, commitment of the SFAB for SFA requirements, whether predictably or dynamically, relieves conventional forces of SFA missions, allowing them to build and preserve their readiness for conflict or to modernize and increase their lethality. 

1-12. Soldiers who volunteer for the SFAB must possess strong leadership, endurance, effective communication skills, be adaptable, dependable, and deployable, and exhibit teamwork, empathy, initiative, and discipline. Advisors in the SFABs possess a cultural acuity and specialized training that can unleash tremendous potential in a “whole-of-government” approach, incorporating the synergy of UAPs that is essential to winning future wars and creating lasting outcomes. 

**Figure 1-2. Security Force Assistance Brigade regional alignment** 

#### **UNIQUE TERMS AND DEFINITIONS** 

1-13. The following key doctrinal terms and definitions are used throughout this and the other chapters and appendices. They are unique to the SFAB, and their advisor missions set and warrant nuanced explanation as they will occur frequently. Refer to the publications listed in the References for additional information. 

##### **ADVISOR ACTIVITIES** 

1-14. Advisor activities increase host-nation capability through joint exercises and mil-to-mil engagements and support the partnered force's operations and modernization in conjunction with other instruments of national power. Advisor activities include the SFA developmental activities and the SFA conflict integration activities. 

##### **FORCE PACKAGES** 

1-15. Force packages are an allocation of personnel and equipment to the CCMDs to complement the U.S. Department of State, Security Cooperation Offices, Army special operations forces, regionally aligned forces, security assistance teams, State Partnership Program, liaison detachments (including theater and digital liaison detachments [DLDs]), and other UAP activities. 

##### **ADVISOR NETWORK** 

1-16. **The_advisor network_ is the system for the integrating and consolidating partner force capability and capacity improvements over time at multiple echelons.** It functions as the connective tissue to gain shared understanding across employed advisor teams at echelon across the FSF. The network also produces effects created by and through advisor employment at scales across the depth and breadth of a partner organization over time. It can take on the characteristics of either being direct (running parallel to the partner chain of command to solve problems rapidly) or indirect (leveraging previous advisor personalities and experiences to build trust).

##### **INTEROPERABILITY** 

1-17. Interoperability is the ability to act together coherently, effectively, and efficiently to achieve tactical, operational, and strategic objectives. An SFAB is designed to integrate effectively with host-nation forces for training and operational activities while providing U.S.-specific linkages back into theater and JTF mission command systems. An SFAB can effectively bridge current human dimension interoperability challenges with host-nation formations through their direct liaison officer capability. During LSCO, the advisor network is particularly effective at solving interoperability shortfalls by bridging interoperability gaps at echelon across the breadth and depth of an allied/partner formation. 

##### **COMMON OPERATIONAL PICTURE** 

1-18. A _common operational picture_ is a display of relevant information within a commander's area of interest tailored to the user's requirements and based on common data and information shared by more than one command (ADP 6-0). In the context of the SFAB, advisors must build and maintain a common operational picture (COP) containing both their own information as well as that of their partners. Information on advisors and other U.S. units operating in the operational environment (OE) is referred to as the _blue_ COP. Information pertaining to the FSF is referred to as the _green_ COP. A critical function of the SFAB is to ensure that information on the green COP is integrated into the blue COP. 

##### **ADVISOR DICHOTOMY** 

1-19. The advisor dichotomy is described as the tension between advisors executing their advisory tasks and supporting their own warfighting function tasks, either internally within the team or externally to higher headquarters. For example, when team signal advisors are actively providing signal support to their own element and their equipment, the time available to actively advise the FSF is reduced. In another example, an intelligence advisor must balance time spent communicating FSF intelligence, sensor, and analysis reporting to higher echelons, relaying higher intelligence reports to the FSF, and ensuring lateral shared understanding within the team, all while advising on information collection and maintaining operations security (OPSEC). Advisors at all levels must recognize and account for the advisor dichotomy for both them and their team, plan accordingly, and work to strike a balance between advising and operating. 

##### **SECTION II – SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE BRIGADE IN CONTEXT** 

1-20. Each SFAB has advisors organized into three maneuver advising battalions (two Infantry battalions and a Cavalry squadron), a fires advising battalion, an engineer advising battalion, a sustainment advising battalion, and a brigade headquarters and headquarters company (HHC). The SFAB's companies have a higher rank structure than conventional BCTs. This increased rank structure intends to provide the SFAB with more experienced personnel and allows SFAB personnel to effectively advise organizations at the same echelon or up to two levels higher. 

1-21. Soldiers in SFABs must understand the broader context of U.S. foreign policy and Integrated Country Strategies in the specific countries in which they operate. The SFA mission is critical to achieving U.S. national security objectives and continues to be an effective tool when developing FSF capability and capacity in support of shared U.S. and partner security interests. 

##### **Stilwell in China 1942** 

<mark>Discipline and administration rested with Chinese officers. But technique and training were American responsibilities. Hundreds of Chinese student interpreters paired off with American instructors to teach Chinese soldiers all things from how to fix flat tires to how to load a mule pack artillery. Friction developed on every level-from differing American and Chinese methods of using a rifle sling to the ultimate questions of whose name was signed to basic orders-American or Chinese.</mark> 

<mark>For the Americans, Ramgarh was Siberia, only one thin cut above jungle duty itself. Ramgarh was hot, dusty, itchy, far from anything green or pleasant. The food was bad, and the barracks cramped. The movies were old and Red Cross hamburger parlors and recreation rooms mocked rather than relieved the loneliness and exhaustion of the GIs. The task of explaining American machines to Chinese soldiers whose tradition of tool handling went no deeper than a buffalo drawn plow was exasperating and embittering; and to most Americans, unaware of its historic necessity, it seemed futile. For the Chinese soldiers Ramgarh was a wonderland. They were fed, for the first time, as much food and meat as they could stuff into their hungry bodies. They gained an average of twenty pounds each in weight. They practiced on the ranges with live shells and real bullets. Hospitals doctored them for everything from malaria to foot ulcers. And most important of all-they were paid. Payment of troops was taken out of the hands of their commanding officers, who had previously received the division payroll and cut into convenient morsels and made directly on the parade ground into the hands of the individual soldier in hard cash.</mark> 

<mark>Between 1942 and 1944, four Chinese divisions were created and equipped here of an effectiveness never before known in Chinese history.</mark> 

<mark>Joseph W. Stilwell</mark> The Stilwell Papers 

#### **SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE DOCTRINE** 

1-22. SFA doctrine exists for joint, multinational, and Army forces. The two primary doctrinal publications applicable to all SFAB Soldiers are this publication and ATP 3-07.10. This publication provides the doctrinal guidance on the SFAB’s organization and employment, and ATP 3-07.10 provides guidance for the individual advisor. 

1-23. At the operational level, FM 3-22 provides helpful tools to plan and execute SFA. This includes SFA activities, goals and functions, planning, and a discussion on other aspects of SC to include security assistance and foreign internal defense. ADP 3-0 and ADP 3-07 describe multidomain operations and how the SFAB nests within the larger Army and defense enterprise. (See figure 1-3 for a graphic depiction of these doctrine relationships.) 

**_Note._** Joint SFA and SC doctrine include JP 3-0, especially chapter 4; JP 3-07; JP 3-16; and JP 3-20. 

**Figure 1-3. Security Force Assistance Brigade doctrine** 

#### **SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE BRIGADE CAPABILITIES AND CHARACTERISTICS** 

1-24. The SFAB is a conflict force, capable of operating in an expeditionary capacity. Using the advisor network, teams leverage the body of knowledge across the network to solve complex problems. Advisor teams can advise up to two levels above echelon to tailor the team’s employment strategy to the need of the advised force. 

##### **EXPEDITIONARY** 

1-25. An SFAB is specifically designed with minimal large equipment to facilitate rapid deployment. SFAB teams are capable of task organizing for persistent, semi-persistent, and non-persistent missions in a CCMD AOR. SFABs will attempt whenever possible to use host-nation support—to include transportation support. The use of host-nation transportation assets is critical because parts and mechanics will not be available for U.S equipment—as well as minimizing the signature for enemy observers. The use of the acquisition and cross-servicing agreement (ACSA) will be critical to set sustainment conditions during competition, which can then be in place indefinitely in the case of crisis or conflict. 

##### **THE ADVISOR NETWORK (DIRECT)** 

1-26. The SFAB, when employed across echelons of FSF, can solve complex problems outside of normal channels due to its structure. By placing advisor teams at echelon throughout an FSF, the SFAB may guide the synchronization of their foreign counterpart's mission as well. For example, if a maneuver advisor team (MAT) is advising a battalion and the battalion advising team (BAT) is advising a division or corps, that MAT can work within the SFAB chain of command to reduce friction and solve problems. This concept is referred to as the advisor network. It is essentially the connective tissue to gain shared understanding across employed advisor teams at echelon across the FSF. When the advisor network is used in this way, it is called the advisor network (direct).

##### **THE ADVISOR NETWORK (INDIRECT)** 

1-27. The advisor network ( _indirect_ ) establishes a personal and a professional relationship where trust and confidence define how well the teams will be able to influence the partner FSF across multiple advisors over time. For example, if an advisor is having difficulty establishing rapport with an FSF partner, another advisor from a previous mission or at a different echelon can be leveraged to re-establish trust. The concept of advisor support coming from a previous advisor mission or different advisor echelon is known as advisor reachback. 

1-28. Another facet of the advisor network (indirect) stems from the FSF's previous experience being on the receiving end of advice or giving advice themselves. For example, some of the partner forces could have United Nations or African Union peacekeeping experience and/or other multinational military training. This experience should be accounted for during an advisor's initial meetings with their partner force. 

##### **TWO LEVELS UP ADVISING** 

1-29. Advisor teams capabilities allow them to advise up to two levels above echelon. Teams and headquarters deliberately assess the appropriate team-to-FSF echelon pairing. Teams advising a less experienced FSF generally advise at higher-than-echelon to advise at the point of greatest practicable need. The advisor teams working with an experienced, professional, and well-established FSF focus at-echelon while performing other advisor activities at higher echelon. During mission analysis, commanders and staffs must weigh the FSF experience level, the effects the SFAB is trying to achieve, resources and time available, and other relevant factors to determine the best distribution of the advisor teams. Commanders must assess risk, which includes considering whether the teams have the appropriate experience to advise their counterparts versus being able to influence the maximum number of FSF organizations. 

- 1-30. Advisor teams during competition can— 

 - Build partner force capabilities and capacities across the SFA developmental activities. 

 - Work with joint, interagency, intergovernmental, and multinational organizations to support theater SC and associated shaping activities. 

 - Provide economy of force to the supported theater or assigned JTF and Army Forces commander. 

 - Detach task-organized, mission-tailored, cross-functional advisor teams to support embassy or theater-directed SC activities as required. 

 - Develop access to host-nation support services and classes of supply. 

 - Coordinate with the U.S. Embassy to leverage ACSAs to codify a host-nation support plan. 

###### 1-31. Advisor teams during crisis can— 

- Facilitate consolidating gains and setting conditions for operational transitions because of the mission-tailorable nature of SFAB formations. 

- Assist in setting the theater when already on the ground: 

 - Provide access to host-nation logistics elements, port infrastructure, and other assets needed to support conventional force reception, staging, onward movement, and integration (RSOI). 

 - Confirm or deny the theater ASCC's, combatant commander's (CCDR's) and SC offices' understanding of the crisis (to include both friendly and enemy situation). 

###### 1-32. Advisor teams during armed conflict can— 

- Execute the SFA conflict integration activities. 

- Provide assessments of FSF partner capabilities and gaps to the joint force commander. 

- Integrate and employ joint enablers to support FSF and self-defense. 

- Task-organize to provide liaison teams in support of LSCO. 

- Advise FSF on offense, defense, and stability tasks. 

- Share information with other service and joint, interagency, intergovernmental, and multinational intelligence and collection organizations to facilitate targeting and support situational awareness. 

#### **SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE BRIGADE LIMITATIONS AND REQUIREMENTS** 

1-33. The SFAB is not a BCT. SFAB teams requires sustainment augmentation from external organizations. Advisor teams do not have the operational capacity to conduct BCT operations. 

1-34. Each brigade will have common vehicles and individual equipment; however, SFAB battalions will not have the capability-specific equipment that accompany a fully operationally capable Infantry or Armor BCT. The maneuver, engineer, and field artillery battalions will not have their own branch-specific vehicular capabilities. The sustainment advising battalion will retain essential equipment sets (such as electronic equipment tool kits), combat service support, and combat automated information systems interface equipment needed to repair vehicles, weapons, and communications systems. 

1-35. SFABs become more effective with access to higher echelon enablers, such as space and cyberspace, in sufficient capacities to execute SFA operations successfully. These enablers allow for effective monitoring of the OE and increase security of the advisor team. 

1-36. SFABs during competition are generally employed according to the following principles: 

- SFABs are normally allocated to CCMDs on an annual basis and will only deploy the required capability. 

- The remainder of the brigade remains at home station unless requested through the United States Army Forces Command. 

- Forces already deployed in theater must be prepared to transition from crisis to conflict while remaining in theater. 

- SFABs are an enabler and do not have the capability to act as the immediate response force. 

- SFABs can deploy on short notice to conduct SFA in crisis or conflict. 

- ASCCs will normally manage the utilization of SFAB advisor teams in coordination with the corresponding SFAB. 

- An SFAB normally does not have mission responsibility in more than two CCMDs simultaneously. 

- • A force package may be detached from one SFAB and attached to another in a different AOR during conflict or episodically as in the case of the 54th SFAB. 

- Advisor teams function best through the collocating of team members to maximize the multiplicative effects of the advisor network. 

- Teams may be disaggregated or distributed, when necessary, based on specific CCMD AOR and U.S. personnel constraints. 

- Advisor teams are cognizant of the minimal mission critical personnel needed to have advisor effects. 

- ASCCs are normally required to provide security, logistical and sustainment support to SFAB advisor teams while deployed in the respective CCMD AOR. 

- SFAB units will deploy and redeploy with organic communication systems. 

- SFABs may be TACON to the JTF. 

- SFABs may be OPCON to the land component commander. 

- Unless otherwise designated, by mission requirements, the highest echelon SFAB element retains OPCON of lower SFAB elements. 

1-37. The 552 trained advisors in an SFAB can be task-organized to form an advisor force package. These force packages are centered around one of the three maneuver battalions, one of the three enabling battalions, and a brigade headquarters element. When employed on an 8-month cycle, the force package will typically be one-third of the maximum available number of the advisor teams. The various advisor teams available to each SFAB include the following: 

- 1 BAT. 

- 3 maneuver battalion advising teams (MBATs). 

- 6 maneuver company advisor teams (MCATs). 

- 18 MATs. 

- 3 enabling BATs: 

 - Fires battalion advising team (FBAT). 

 - Engineer battalion advising team (EBAT). 

 - Logistics battalion advising team (LBAT). 

- 2 fires company advisor teams (FCATs). 

- 4 fires advisor teams (FATs). 

- 2 engineer company advisor teams (ECATs). 

- 4 engineer advisor teams (EATs). 

- 2 logistics company advisor teams (LCATs). 

- 4 logistics advisor teams (LATs). 

1-38. The MBAT will be sourced from either the two Infantry battalions or one Cavalry squadron (1st and 2nd Battalions as well as 3rd Squadron), while the enabling BAT will be sourced from either the fires advising battalion, engineer advising battalion, or sustainment advising battalion. These enabling battalions are commonly referred to as the 4th, 5th, and 6th Battalions. 

1-39. Advisor teams are often distributed widely across the operational area, with large physical distances between other advisor teams and their higher headquarters. Particularly in a joint operations area (JOA), higher headquarters should assign support relationships to other U.S. or allied units that are collocated with or near an advisor team. Due to the likely geographic dispersion, a single advisor team can conduct mission planning, mission command, and advisor activities, but not all simultaneously. Teams specialize in advisor activities. These can be general or direct support including functions that a typical BCT staff would provide its subordinate units, such as intelligence, fires, sustainment, movement, and other support as required. 

1-40. Because of the dispersed way in which the SFAB typically operates, command and support relationships should be codified into operation orders, so supporting organizations clearly understand their responsibilities. It is worth noting that an additional security element is not organic to the SFAB. The mission variables mission, enemy, terrain and weather, troops and support available, time available, civil considerations, and informational considerations (METT-TC-[I] will dictate if one is required to be sourced by the ASCC. 

##### **SECTION III – SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE BRIGADE ORGANIZATION** 

1-41. SFAB formations provide a cross-functional advisory capability to support theater SC activities and build partner capability and capacity. Advisor teams can operate semi-independently with host-nation FSF with augmentation or support to garner effects against threats. The SFABs' combination of diverse warfighting functions and ability to facilitate partner forces' access to cross-domains effects, provides a unique capability to the theater commander. SFABs' ability to serve across echelons builds effective FSF formations that provide the foundation for long-term host-nation stability and security. The FSF prolonged, persistent engagement capabilities keeps continuous pressure on U.S. adversaries in regions around the world and helps the United States and its partner nations achieve their goals. 

#### **SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE BRIGADE STAFF** 

1-42. A _staff section_ is a grouping of staff members by area of expertise (FM 6-0). The SFAB staff has a responsibility to manage information and resources communicated through the advisor network to inform the commander of required decisions, in addition to normal staff functions (see FM 6-0). 

##### **SUPPORT** 

1-43. The SFAB staff supports the commander and assists subordinate units, which typically involves organizing advisor teams, assigning partner relationships, coordinating advising themes and messages, managing assessments, and coordinating other relationships in support of their teams and the SFA mission. Staffs are also critical to guiding the advisor team command and support relationships early in a deployment and monitoring those relationships throughout the mission. As with most SFAB entities, more emphasis placed on advising the FSF results in less capacity to perform other tasks. Due to the size of the SFAB staff, it can conduct SFA with counterparts, in the military decision-making process (MDMP), or during command and control (C2), but likely not simultaneously.

##### **ADVISORY FUNCTIONS** 

1-44. Before the mission, staff members advise foreign counterparts in preparing staff estimates and assess courses of action for essential tasks. The staff helps write tentative plans and/or orders based on foreign counterpart commander’s planning guidance and their FSF standard operating procedure (SOP). Depending on the situation, plans may include primary, alternate, contingency, and emergency plans. 

##### **EXECUTION FUNCTIONS** 

1-45. During execution, the staff helps foreign counterparts coordinate and assess task execution. The staff assists in the dissemination of FSF plans and/or orders to senior and adjacent staff sections and supporting elements as required. The staff helps notify higher, lower, or adjacent staff sections of modified estimates and plans. The staff—led by the operations staff officer (S-3), intelligence staff officer (S-2), and their foreign counterparts—help update the commander's critical information requirements (CCIRs) with the latest information and future requirements. 

##### **COORDINATING STAFF** 

1-46. Coordinating staff officers are the commander's principal assistants who advise, plan, and coordinate actions within their area of expertise or warfighting function. Coordinating staff officers may also exercise planning and supervisory authority over designated special staff officers. Coordinating staff officers are the personnel staff officer (S-1), S-2, S-3, logistics staff officer (S-4), signal staff officer (S-6), financial management officer (S-8), and the civil affairs (CA) operations staff officer (S-9). 

###### **Personnel Staff Officer** 

1-47. The S-1 is the principal staff officer for all matters concerning human resources support (military and civilian). Specific responsibilities include manning, personnel services, personnel support, and headquarters management. The S-1 has coordinating staff responsibility for the civilian personnel officer and the equal opportunity advisor and prepares a portion of Annex F (Sustainment) to the operation order. When planning an operation, the S-1 provides accurate information regarding replacement personnel and ensures the rest of the staff includes all attachments or other assets into any plan or operation. (See FM 1-0 and ATP 1-0.1.) 

1-48. When working with foreign counterparts, the S-1 provides advice, guidance, and recommendations related to human resources. The S-1 monitors the FSF' strength, personnel accountability, disposition of casualties, unit morale, and possibly postal activities. The S-1 may provide guidance concerning the foreign pay system, leave procedures, and casualty pay procedures. 

###### **Intelligence Staff Officer** 

1-49. The S-2 is chief of the intelligence warfighting function. The S-2 is the principal staff officer responsible for providing intelligence to support current operations and plans. The S-2 gives the commander and the S-3 the initial intelligence synchronization plan, which facilitates reconnaissance and surveillance integration. The S-2 helps the S-3 develop the initial reconnaissance and surveillance plan. The S-2 is responsible for the preparation of Annex B (Intelligence) and assists the S-3 in preparing Annex L (Information Collection). The S-2 helps the staff, executive officer (XO), and commander determine which courses of action the enemy might choose. The information collection plan solves gaps in intelligence, further shaping the planned friendly course of action. The S-2 attempts to see into the minds of the enemy forces to identify enemy tactics based upon their disposition and friendly actions. (See FM 2-0 for more information.) 

1-50. The S-2 monitors FSF OPSEC to protect classified and sensitive material and operations provides OPSEC recommendations to the FSF as needed. By helping the foreign counterpart update the situation map, the S-2 helps to keep both commands up to date on the current situation. The S-2 assesses and recommends improvements to the SOPs of the command post communications framework, so the intelligence section receives situation reports. The S-2 helps the counterpart monitor the collection, evaluation, interpretation, and the dissemination of information. The S-2 assists in examining captured insurgent documents and material. The S-2 helps gather and disseminate intelligence reports from available sources to ensure the exploitation of all unit operations assets. The S-2 works with operations advisors to develop reconnaissance and surveillance plans with their counterparts.

1-51. The brigade S-2 serves as the brigade's senior intelligence officer. As the senior intelligence officer, the brigade S-2 maintains tasking authority of intelligence personnel assigned to the military intelligence (MI) company. The senior intelligence officer is responsible for task-organizing the SFAB's intelligence personnel in support of C2, advising, and augmentation according to mission requirements. 

###### **Operations Officer** 

1-52. The S-3 is responsible for coordinating movement and maneuver warfighting function activities. The S-3 is the primary staff officer for integrating and synchronizing operations for the commander. The S-3 integrates reconnaissance and surveillance during plans and operations. The S-3 synchronizes reconnaissance and surveillance throughout the operations process and develops plans and orders and determines potential branches and sequels. The S-3 coordinates and synchronizes warfighting functions in all plans and orders. The S-3 is also responsible for and prepares Annex L (Information Collection) and Annex V (Interagency Coordination). The S-3 prepares Annex A (Task Organization), Annex C (Operations), and Annex M (Assessment) for the operation order. In conjunction with the knowledge management officer, the S-3 prepares Annex R (Reports) and Annex Z (Distribution). The S-3 ensures proper dissemination of the plan. The S-3 also identifies key intersections in canalizing terrain and timed events for key transitions in phases or decision points. (See FM 6-0 for more information.) 

1-53. The S-3 helps the foreign counterpart to prepare tactical plans and orders using estimates, predictions, assessments, and information. The S-3 monitors command and communications nets, assists in preparing all plans and orders, and helps to supervise the training and preparation for operations. The S-3 assists FSF in engaging friendly, neutral, and threat networks through the operations process. (See ATP 5-0.6 for more information.) The S-3 monitors the planning process and makes recommendations consistent with the FSF' objectives and goals. 

###### **Logistics Staff Officer** 

1-54. The S-4 is the principal staff officer for sustainment planning and operations, supply, maintenance, transportation, services, field services, distribution, and operational contract support (OCS). S-4s determine supply priorities and coordinate for requisition, acquisition, and storage of supplies and equipment. The S-4 develops, monitors, and actions the unit's sustainment status reports and develops plans for intra-theater movement and the deployment of personnel and equipment. The S-4 prepares Annex F (Sustainment), Annex P (Host-Nation Support), and Annex W (Operational Contract Support) to the operation order. The S-4 works closely with the sustainment advising battalion support operations (SPO) officer to integrate the SFAB into the theater sustainment plan. The S-4 identifies requirements for external and higher echelon support requirements and, along with SPO, coordinates for those assets. (See FM 6- 0 for more information.) 

1-55. The S-4 conducts SFA activities alongside their foreign counterpart to maintain equipment readiness, monitor the support provided to the foreign unit, and recommend improvements. The S-4 helps to supervise the use of transportation assets. 

###### **Signal Staff Officer** 

1-56. The signal staff officer (S-6) is the principal staff officer for all matters concerning network operations, jointly consisting of Department of Defense (DOD) information network operations and applicable portions of the defensive cyberspace operations. The S-6 provides network transport and information services, conducts network operations to operate and defend the network, enables knowledge management, manages land warrior and combat net radio assets in the area of operations (AO), and performs spectrum management operations. The S-6 prepares Annex H (Signal) of the operation order and participates in preparing Appendix 12 (Cyberspace Electromagnetic Activities) to Annex C (Operations) with input from the S-2 and in coordination with the S-3. (See FM 6-02 and ATP 6-02.70 for more information.) 

1-57. The S-6 advises and assists the foreign counterpart for all matters concerning network operations, network transport, information services, and spectrum management operations within the SFAB's SFA mission. The S-6 monitors communications security (COMSEC) throughout planning, preparation, and execution of SFA and FSF activities. The S-6 trains personnel in the protection of sensitive communications equipment and cryptographic materials during the execution of SFA operations. The S-6 identifies communications requirements, obtains communications resources for austere locations, and ensures redundant and backup systems are available and tested.

1-58. The S-6, in coordination with the higher headquarters' signal officer, continuously assesses and refines information management coordinating, archiving, and sharing among participating interagency partners and other organizations. The S-6 assesses the communications synchronization plan between the SFAB, its foreign counterparts, and other UAPs. The S-6 uses foreign disclosure procedures and information sharing processes to disseminate information with interagency partners and other organizations. 

###### **Financial Management Officer** 

1-59. The S-8 is the principal staff officer responsible for financial management within the SFAB. The S-8 is the focal point in planning financial management support that allows the SFAB to accomplish its mission. The S-8 prepares a portion of Annex F (Sustainment). (See FM 4-80 for more information.) 

1-60. The S-8 is the principal financial management advisor to the SFAB commander. The S-8, in coordination with legal representatives, advises the commander regarding laws and financial management regulations governing obligations, expenditures, and limitations on the use of funds within SFAB's SFA mission. The S-8— 

- Coordinates financial management policies and practices with U.S. counterparts in the contracting command. 

- Identifies, certifies, and manages funds available for immediate SFA operations expenses. 

- Integrates all financial management requirements into SFA planning. 

- Analyzes total cost to develop SFA funding requirements and submit requirements to the division or higher headquarters. 

- Monitors and reports status of funding and spending plans. 

- Coordinates contracting and financial management disbursing support for field ordering officers (FOOs) and pay agents. 

- Monitors execution of the contract expenditures. 

###### **Civil Affairs Operations Staff Officer** 

1-61. The S-9 CA operations staff officer brings CA core competencies to the SFAB. The S-9 focuses on the interests, functions, capabilities, and vulnerabilities of indigenous populations, institutions, government apparatus, and UAPs that reside or operate in and around an AO. The core competencies are the following: 

- Transitional governance. 

- Civil network development and engagement. 

- Civil knowledge integration. 

- Civil military integration. 

1-62. The SFAB S-9 is not manned and equipped to perform all the traditional functions of an S-9 as described in FM 6-0 and FM 3-57. The S-9 ensures civil considerations are integrated into mission analysis by performing civil preparation of the battlefield. The S-9 considers tactical and sustainment support issues by engaging the civil component, ensuring freedom of movement, mitigating civilian interference, obtaining host-nation support, and planning for the establishment performance of stability operations tasks if required. The S-9 maintains civil military integration with interagency partners and UAPs to promote unified action. The S-9 coordinates with the country team or in-theater CA elements to assess the civil component and its impact on the advisor mission. 

1-63. The S-9 is the dedicated principal staff officer responsible for all matters concerning CA operations. The S-9 enhances the relationship between military forces and civilian authorities and personnel in the AO to ensure mission success. The roles of the SFAB S-9 include the following: 

- Evaluating civil considerations during mission analysis and integrating civil knowledge into the Army's integrating processes. 

- Recommending augmentation of additional CA operations capabilities based on the advisory mission. 

- Developing and engaging civilian networks to extend the advisor network into the civil component of the OE, including interagency and interorganizational partners. 

- Advising the commander and staff on the effect of military operations on civilians in the AO. 

- Advising the commander on options for producing desired effects in the civil component of the OE. 

- • Advising the commander on employing CA units within the command and support structure to engage and influence civil networks and the population, see chapter 2, section IV for more on employment considerations. 

- Preparing Annex K (Civil Affairs Operations) of the SFAB operation order. 

- Assisting in civil military integration between U.S. Forces, FSF, and civilian authorities supporting an operation when employed in joint, interagency, intergovernmental, and multinational environments. 

- Assisting and advising FSF in establishing a civil-military operations (CMO) capability and enabling their battleplan. 

- Supporting other requirements necessary to support the SFAB mission. 

##### **SPECIAL STAFF** 

1-64. Special staff in the SFAB include the brigade engineer; air liaison officer; aviation officer; chemical, biological, radiological, and nuclear (CBRN) officer; psychological operations officer; explosive ordnance disposal (EOD) officer; cyber electromagnetic warfare officer (EWO); fire support officer (FSO); and provost marshal. Paragraphs 1-65 through 1-85 describe special staff officers' responsibilities as well as considerations when advising mission partners. (See FM 6-0 for additional information.) 

###### **Air Liaison** 

1-65. The air liaison officer is the senior U.S. Air Force officer with each tactical air control party if assigned. The air liaison officer plans and executes close air support according to the SFAB commander's guidance and intent. The air liaison officer coordinates aerospace assets and operations, such as close air support, air interdiction, air reconnaissance, airlift, and joint suppression of enemy air defenses. (See JP 3-09.3, FM 3-52, and ATP 3-52.1 for more information.) 

###### **Aviation Officer** 

1-66. The aviation officer is the airspace control officer for the SFAB S-3 who plans and synchronizes aviation into the commander's scheme of maneuver. The aviation officer standardizes unmanned aircraft systems (UASs) employment for the SFAB and advises and plans the use of reconnaissance, attack, air assault, air movement, sustainment, and medical evacuation (MEDEVAC). As the aviation subject matter expert, the aviation officer is responsible for advising the commander and staff on the status and availability of aviation assets, their capabilities, and limitations. The aviation officer recommends and helps coordinate priorities and allocations of aviation assets, helps determine the priorities for their employment, and participates in targeting. The aviation officer helps prepare portions of Annex C (Operations) and portions of Annex D (Fires) of the operation order. (See FM 3-52 and ATP 3-52.1 for more information.) 

###### **Chemical, Biological, Radiological, and Nuclear Officer** 

1-67. The CBRN officer advises the commander and staff on operations in CBRN environments and employment of CBRN defense capabilities. A CBRN NCO is authorized in the S-3 current operations cell. CBRN staff shares information and makes recommendations to the operations and other staff sections. The CBRN staff prepares a portion of Annex E (Protection) and a portion of Annex C (Operations) of the operation order. (See ATP 3-11.36 for more information.) 

###### **Cyber Electronic Warfare Officer** 

1-68. The cyberspace EWO is the principal advisor to the commander on all issues concerning offensive and defensive cyberspace operations and electromagnetic warfare (EW). The cyberspace electromagnetic activities (CEMA) cell plans, synchronizes, and integrates CEMA functions into the concept of operations. In LSCO, the CEMA cell assesses organic and partner force cyber and EW capability gaps. Through detailed coordination with other staff members, the partner force staff, and the ASCC, the CEMA cell fills capability gaps with theater-level assets. The CEMA cell is responsible for advising the partner force on EW protection efforts and tactics, techniques, and procedures for host-nation EW equipment. The CEMA cell develops Appendix 12 (Cyberspace Electromagnetic Activities) to Annex C (Operations) to the operation order and contributes to any section that CEMA support. (See ATP 3-12.3 for additional information.)

###### **Brigade Engineer** 

1-69. The brigade engineer is the senior engineer on staff responsible for coordinating engineer support. The brigade engineer integrates specified and implied engineer tasks into the plan. Engineer officers ensure mission planning, preparation, execution, and assessment activities integrate with supporting engineer units. The brigade engineer prepares Annex G (Engineer) to the operation order and synchronizes any mobility, countermobility, survivability, and protection requirements. (See FM 6-0 for more information.) 

###### **Explosive Ordnance Disposal Officer** 

1-70. The EOD officer is responsible for activities in support of FSF EOD operations and supporting enablers (see ATP 4-32). The EOD officer leads the development of manning, equipping, training, and policies based on FSF enabler capabilities at echelon to support EOD operations. EOD officers coordinate with the Joint EOD Technical Support Center Division for technical intelligence support, research and development and foreign military sales support. The EOD officer is responsible for ensuring the transfer of FSF weapons technical intelligence to theater exploitation laboratories during LSCO. EOD officers also provide subject matter expertise to planning, preparing, executing, and advising SFA missions for EOD. EOD officers also aid in reporting and tracking explosive hazards, to include counter-improvised explosive device (C-IED) efforts across the range of military operations. The EOD officer leads efforts and coordinates with ASCC EOD staff to determine requirements and develop training programs to build partner-nation capacity. The EOD officer enables SFAB counter-explosive hazards efforts through exploitation, operations, weapons technical data and information, targeting, and force protection. The EOD officer provides expertise on explosives safety for foreign ordnance and weapons systems employed by the FSF. 

###### **Fire Support Officer** 

1-71. There is no organic FSO on the SFAB brigade staff. The brigade staff uses the fire support coordinator in the SFAB field artillery battalion when required. 

1-72. The FSO serves as the special staff officer for fires and integrates fires into the scheme of maneuver for the commander. The FSO leads fire support planning for the delivery of fires to include preparation fires, harassing fires, interdiction fires, suppressive fires, destruction fires, and deception fires. The fire support coordinator leads the brigade's targeting process, synchronizing effects across all domains with SFAB and partner security force operations in achieving the commander's objectives. The FSO leads the fire support cell (when established), prepares Annex D (Fires) of the operation order, and coordinates with the cyber EWO and the air liaison officer. The S-3 coordinates this position. (See ADP 3-19 for more information.) 

###### **Force Management Officer** 

1-73. Force management officers are trusted advisors who serve as subject matter experts on the technical processes of force management. Force management officers are experts at force development, designing operational and institutional force structure, identifying and allocating authorized resources, and force integration. 

###### **Mobility Warrant Officer** 

1-74. The mobility warrant officer plans, organizes, and supervises the preparation and execution of advisor team movements and distribution operations. Mobility warrant officers coordinate deployment and distribution actions with multinational, joint, Army, and commercial agencies through the Joint Logistics Enterprise. They prepare and validate deployment and redeployment plans, orders, and SOPs. The mobility warrant officer provides training in unit movement operations and supervises the use of joint and Army transportation systems to document Army redeployment and distribution operations. They assist commanders in planning and conducting unit movement operations by land, sea, and air.

###### **Property Book Officer** 

1-75. The property book officer is the primary advisor to the command and supported units on all property accountability and organizational-level supply matters. Property book officers develop, execute, monitor, and provide input to the budget. They coordinate funds availability and oversee the small purchase program for standard and nonstandard equipment. They manage materiel through record adjustments, issues, turn-ins, property losses, and status reports. The property book officer applies for, coordinates, and manages the unit DOD Activity Address Codes. 

1-76. The mission of the property book officer is to ensure all authorized equipment is on-hand or on requisition for all units to accomplish their mission. Typically, all property (except real property) requires continuous detailed accounting from the time of acquisition through an item's lifecycle until the property is disposed of or consumed. The property book officer maintains the property records for all accountable assets issued to supported units. The officer controls all input to and output from the automated processes supporting the property book system. The property book officer processes documents such as financial liability investigations, adjustment reports, statement of charges, cash collection vouchers, and managerial reports the command requires. 

###### **Provost Marshal** 

1-77. The provost marshal is responsible for planning, coordinating, and employing all organic, assigned, or attached military police assets. Usually, provost marshals are the senior military police officers in the command. They augment the staff, typically within the S-3. The provost marshal develops antiterrorism, and force protection, assisting the S-3 and personnel recovery officer in planning personnel recovery operations. The provost marshal prepares a portion of Annex C (Operations) and a portion of Annex E (Protection) to the operation order (see FM 3-39 for additional information). 

###### **Psychological Operations Officer** 

1-78. The psychological operations (PSYOP) officer synchronizes the military information support operations for the SFAB. The PSYOP officer's efforts enable information advantage (for more on information advantage, see ADP 3-13). They may be the primary advisor to the commander on shaping the OE to degrade enemy and adversary decision making, on information activities, and on planning, synchronizing, and coordinating influence activities with other staff, higher headquarters, and foreign counterparts. The PSYOP officer assists in adversary information analysis and exploitation, and as necessary, coordinates for division or higher-level influence support to counter or exploit adversary information activities. They are responsible for synchronizing and deconflicting information-related capabilities that are employed to support operations. The PSYOP officer coordinates and integrates information-related capabilities for information operations in the SFAB, including military information support to operations, OPSEC, and deception activities. They are the lead planner for information activities and offensive and defensive psychological effects capabilities. 

1-79. The PSYOP officer, assisted by the staff psychological operations NCO, prepares Appendices 13 (Military Information Support Operations), 14 (Military Deception), and 15 (Information Operations) to Annex C (Operations) to the operation order (see ADP 6-0 and FM 3-53 for more information). 

###### **Staff Weather Officer** 

1-80. The staff weather officer is a U.S. Air Force officer or NCO subject matter expert who coordinates operational weather support and weather service matters through the S-2 and other staff members. Staff weather officers are augmented to the SFAB as needed during LSCO. In the absence of aligned staff weather officer support, weather requests can be made at the supporting echelon. Staff weather officers lead a team of Air Force weather personnel that collect and analyze environmental intelligence to produce and disseminate mission-tailored products. These products can include an environmental running estimate, mission execution forecasts, and weather watches, warnings, and advisories. They integrate weather effects into planning and execution along with responding to requests for weather information. Weather effects information and products contribute to the SFAB commander's situational understanding, decision making, and risk management. Although aligned to the SFAB, U.S. Air Force weather personnel remain under administrative control and OPCON to the air component commander. The SFAB, through the S-2, ensures aligned U.S. Air Force weather personnel receive required Army equipment. The staff weather officer prepares Tab B (Weather) to Appendix 1 (Intelligence Estimate) to Annex B (Intelligence) and should be included in reviews of operation orders. (See FM 2-0 for additional information.) Although they are not permanently assigned, they are on the modified table of equipment and attached prior to LSCO.

###### **Surgeon** 

1-81. The surgeon coordinates health service support and operations within the command. The surgeon is the lead medical advisor and responsible for establishing medical advising efforts in support of the commander, synchronizing the medical advising efforts, and developing the partnered force capability and capacity. 

1-82. The surgeon provides and oversees medical care to Soldiers, civilians, and detainees. The brigade surgeon is responsible for the technical supervision of all medical activities in the command. As part of the special staff, the surgeon provides advice to the commander on all medical or medically related issues. The surgeon informs the commander of external medical capabilities available to the unit, the health of the command to include the foreign counterparts, plans for medical training with the FSF, and an assessment of the medical training status. (See ATP 4-02.6.) The surgeon prepares a portion of Annex E (Protection) and Annex F (Sustainment) of the operation order. The surgeon provides the S-4 with recommendations on the medical plan and subordinate unit plans for air and ground MEDEVACs, medical facility locations, and other considerations regarding health service support. (See FM 4-02 for more information.) 

1-83. The brigade surgeon plans and coordinates for the following activities: 

- Medical intelligence requirements. 

- Local population assessment. 

- Development of the FSF medical capabilities and capacity. 

- MEDEVAC by organic assets. 

- Area support MEDEVAC provided by the U.S. Army, U.S. Air Force, or U.S. Navy resources or resources from the civilian community (contract). 

- MEDEVAC using host-nation and multinational resources. 

- Medical treatment facilities afloat including hospitalization in Role 3 medical treatment facilities established by the Services, MNFs, or host nation. 

- Dental services. 

- Veterinary public and animal health services, which includes the following:  Food protection. 

 - Animal care. 

 - Zoonotic disease prevention and control. 

- Civic assistance programs within the local community. 

- Medical advise and assist missions. 

- Operational public health services. 

- Medical laboratory services. 

- Foreign humanitarian assistance (HA) programs. 

- Behavioral health and combat and operational stress control programs. 

- Army Health System assessments, estimates, and plans. 

1-84. The surgeon advises the foreign counterpart in health service support and force health protection (FHP) activities including the deployment of medical assets, establishment of tactical- and operational-level medical treatment assets, development of patient evacuation routes, procurement and distribution of medical supplies, and implementation of FHP measures to reduce disease and nonbattle injury. Medical planners work with their counterparts and the civil military advisor to train the FSF on the development and employment of health service support and FHP capabilities. 

1-85. The surgeon's staff includes a medical operations officer, an environmental science engineering officer, a medical sustainment officer, a clinical psychologist, a physical therapist officer, a nurse officer, and a senior healthcare NCO. If possible, the team should be mixed gender to ensure it can provide culturally appropriate care to both men and women. While assigned to the brigade sustainment advising battalion, these personnel retain the responsibility to perform their respective medical functions under the supervision of the surgeon. The surgeon's staff is responsible for assisting the unit surgeon with planning, coordinating, advising, and synchronizing Army Health System SPO and requirements. The section is also responsible for assessing the FSF medical requirements including medical training and support in the brigade's AO. When augmented by external medical assets, the brigade surgeon's section coordinates through the higher echelon headquarters with the medical force provider in the AO.

##### **PERSONAL STAFF** 

1-86. Personal staff officers work under the immediate control of, and have direct access to, the brigade commander. They advise the commander, provide input to orders and plans, and interface and coordinate with entities external to the SFAB headquarters. They also serve as advisors to their counterparts in the FSF. Examples of personal staff officers include the command sergeant major, the brigade judge advocate, the surgeon, the public affairs officer, and the chaplain. Personal staff responsibilities are described in paragraphs 1-87 through 1-94. 

###### **Command Sergeant Major** 

1-87. The command sergeant major is the senior NCO who advises the commander on issues related to the enlisted ranks. They provide a wealth of knowledge, experience, and advice to the commander and other Soldiers within the unit. The command sergeant major carries out policies and enforces standards for the performance, training, and conduct of enlisted Soldiers. During operations, a commander employs the command sergeant major throughout the AO to extend command influence, assess the morale of the force, and assist during critical events. 

###### **Executive Officer** 

1-88. XOs observe and provide advice to their foreign counterpart regarding organizational analysis of the foreign unit and coordinating their staff sections to ensure efficiency during the planning process. With the counterpart, XOs assist foreign staff sections as they develop estimates and plans. The XO advisor also coordinates and liaises with the FSF's higher headquarters. The XO also functions as the de facto chief-of-staff. 

###### **Chaplain and Unit Ministry Team** 

1-89. The unit ministry team consists of a chaplain and a religious affairs specialist. The chaplain serves as a personal staff officer with direct access to the commander. The unit ministry team provides religious support to all assigned or attached Service members, family members, and authorized civilians. The team provides religious, moral, and ethical advisement to the command as they impact both individuals and the organization's mission. They coordinate with higher, subordinate, and adjacent unit ministry teams and chaplain sections for area and denominational coverage requirements. The unit ministry team prepares a portion of Annex F (Sustainment) to the operation order. (See FM 3-83 and ATP 1-05.01 for additional information.) 

1-90. When appropriate, the chaplain provides advice to the foreign commander and staff on issues related to ethics, morality, and religion as it influences their forces, operations, and the local population. Understanding the impact of religion upon the operations, FSF, and the local population is pivotal to the success of the SFA objectives. Chaplains train to conduct leader engagements with key religious leaders as well as foreign military counterparts. Chaplains also provide guidance regarding religious affairs both directly and via reachback capability through supervisory channels as well as networked relationships with the CCDR and the ASCC chaplain offices. 

###### **Judge Advocate** 

1-91. The judge advocate is the senior legal advisor to the SFAB commander. The judge advocate advises the commander and staff on national security law, international agreements, military justice, administrative law, fiscal law, and other areas of the law as required and ensures the delivery of legal services to the brigade across the core legal disciplines of the Judge Advocate General's Corps.

1-92. The roles of the brigade legal team include the following: 

- Advising SFAB commanders at all echelons on legal issues or priorities that impact their commands. 

- Deploying with the brigade headquarters to support SFAB operations and exercises. 

- Serving as the appointed ethics counselor for the brigade and managing the brigade's ethics and Office of Government Ethics program where applicable, using OGE Form 450 ( _Confidential Financial Disclosure Report)_ . 

- Overseeing all brigade investigations to include the following: 

 - AR 15-6 investigations. 

 - Financial liability investigations of property loss. 

 - Line of duty investigations. 

 - Safety investigations. 

- Coordinating for outside legal review of investigations where necessary. 

- Serving as the brigade representative to military and civilian law enforcement agencies and prosecutorial offices. 

- Drafting, maintaining, and executing military justice actions to include the following:  Article 15 punishments. 

 - Reprimands. 

 - Separations/eliminations. 

 - Courts-martial where the chain of command possesses both convening and referral authority. 

- Coordinating with local Special Trial Counsel offices on any investigations or adverse actions that involve one or more covered offenses. 

- Participating in all brigade-level operational planning events whether for real-world operations or exercises. 

- Reviewing all concepts of operation originating from their respective SFAB for legal sufficiency prior to submission to higher headquarters for review/approval. 

- Advising SFAB commanders at all echelons on lethal and nonlethal fires effects before employment of said effects. 

- Executing annual training for all SFAB personnel on the law of armed conflict and on government ethics. 

- Assuring, with the assistance of SFAB leaders and in coordination with their servicing ASCC, that all SFAB personnel employing as part of a force package, deploying to support exercises or operations, or participating in other overseas training or advising missions with FSF partners, receive a pre-employment/predeployment brief consisting of the following: 

 - Overview of the law of armed conflict. 

 - Any applicable rules of engagement. 

- Operational authorities relevant to the upcoming mission: 

 - Applicable Status of Forces Agreements or other status protections. 

 - Local laws that may impact the mission. 

- Providing legal reviews of any contracting actions if not expected to occur at supporting contracting or field support brigades. 

- Maintaining a repository of international and domestic legal resources for the brigade. 

- Serving as the brigade representative to legal entities affiliated with joint, intergovernmental, interagency, and multinational partners. 

- Preparing portions of Annex C (Operations) and Annex F (Sustainment) to the operation order. (See AR 27-1 and FM 3-84 for more information.) 

1-93. The legal team consists of one judge advocate and one paralegal NCO. The legal team can perform advising tasks for FSF legal personnel at the direction of the SFAB commander. As available and approved by the SFAB command, the legal team may also participate in predeployment site surveys (PDSSs), American Embassy engagements, and other in-theater legal interactions which may support the ability of the SFAB to accomplish its mission. The legal team may, on an occasional basis, also perform nonlegal advising tasks for nonlegal FSF personnel consistent with receiving proper training, concurrence of legal leaders as expressed under AR 27-1, and approval by the SFAB commander. Such duties, however, will not supersede the legal team's legal support responsibilities to the command. In service of these legal and nonlegal advising tasks, the legal team will also coordinate with their immediate ASCC legal offices to apprise them of forecasted advisement missions and, as necessary, other military legal organizations with equities impacted by the legal team's in-country advising missions. While the legal team may discuss matters of law and policy with foreign commanders or personnel, they will not provide legal advice to any foreign commanders or personnel. 

###### **Public Affairs Officer** 

1-94. The public affairs officer develops strategies, leads, and supervises the conduct of public information, community engagements, and command information. The public affairs officer's principal role is providing advice and counsel to the commander and the staff on how affected external and internal publics will accept and understand the unit's operations. The public affairs officer understands and coordinates the flow of information to Soldiers, the Army community, and the public and prepares Annex J (Public Affairs) to the operation order. The public affairs officer coordinates closely with the CCMD, country team, or theater command public affairs office, and the SFAB information operations officer to publicize SFA activities as appropriate to achieve specific effects within the information domain. (See FM 3-61 for more information.) 

#### **SUBORDINATE UNITS** 

1-95. An SFAB can deploy 18 multifunctional and 18 functional advising teams. Each team is a bespoke and tailored unit, capable of performing the conflict integration and developmental advisor tasks alongside mission partners to advise and coordinate mission success in austere environments within the entire gamut of the strategic environment. 

##### **SUBORDINATE UNIT ROLES** 

1-96. The two most important roles of the brigade, battalion, and squadron headquarters are to advise FSF and coordinate the SFA mission of their subordinate advisor teams. This includes task-organizing advisor teams, assigning partner relationships, coordinating advising themes and messages, managing assessments, and coordinating other relationships in support of their teams and the SFA mission. By placing advisor teams at echelon throughout the FSF, the SFAB may guide the synchronization of their foreign counterpart's mission as well. 

1-97. The SFAB brigade and battalions manage, coordinate, and organize advisor teams, in addition to supervising the SFA mission of their subordinate organizations. They have the capacity to conduct SFA operations alongside their FSF counterparts while managing the larger SFA mission. However, these organizations do not have the capacity or capability to conduct independent operations outside of their assigned SFA mission. Therefore, the SFAB should generally not be assigned an AO. The exception is when an FSF formation with embedded SFAB advisors is assigned an AO. For example, this would occur in the context of a multinational LSCO where the SFAB was embedded with a partner nation force conducting LSCO adjacent to a U.S. Force, and both the U.S. Force and partner force (with embedded SFAB elements) have distinct separate boundaries. Graphically, the organizational boundary could show the FSF with a note that the SFAB has the same boundaries. Ultimately, this depends on the C2 arrangement within the MNF. 

##### **SUBORDINATE UNIT STRUCTURE** 

1-98. The brigade commander determines the task organization for employment to execute operations across echelons, time, and terrain, depending on the CCDR's SFA requirements. These teams are designed to advise and operate at the tactical level—company, battalion, brigade, division, or corps—as directed by the CCDR. The six battalions' cross functional design within an SFAB facilitates the brigade commander's ability to task organize and tailor trained advisor teams to meet mission requirements. Each Infantry battalion and Cavalry squadron have an HHC or a headquarters and headquarters troop, and two maneuver companies or troops. The fires advising battalion has a headquarters and headquarters battery and two fires advisor batteries. The engineer advising battalion has one HHC, two engineer, one signal, and a MI company. The sustainment advising battalion has an HHC that has limited distribution, maintenance, and medical capabilities (See figure 1-4 on page 22.) 

**Figure 1-4. Organization** 

###### **Brigade Headquarters and Headquarters Company** 

1-99. The brigade HHC can task-organize to provide a BAT to meet its advising requirement at the tactical level at a variety of FSF echelons and types. This BAT is fully capable of advising in either the FSF generating or operational force based on CCDR or chief of mission (COM) guidance. The SFAB HHC coordinates the SFA activities of subordinate battalions and manages functional companies and attachments. The HHC prepares to develop the capability and capacity of its foreign counterparts—generally at division level—to plan, coordinate, and synchronize operations. The brigade staff provides limited capabilities in support of various missions and tasks. The staff also coordinates with echelons above brigade for additional assets and capabilities as required. 

###### **Military Intelligence Company** 

1-100.
