The MI company works closely with the brigade S-2 and provides additional limited capabilities including counterintelligence (CI), signals intelligence, geospatial intelligence, human intelligence, allsource intelligence, open-source research, and division or corps aligned U.S. Air Force reachback weather support. The MI company has no dedicated advisor teams, however, as necessary, Soldiers in this company may be task-organized to augment advisor teams and the brigade intelligence section to provide technical expertise. Task organization decisions must balance the tensions inherent in the advisor dichotomy.

1-101. The MI company contains the brigade intelligence support element. The brigade intelligence support element provides the SFAB S-2 with limited processing, exploitation, and dissemination, all-source analysis and production, and intelligence reach and dissemination capabilities. The MI company has limited personnel, equipment, and times to analyze and process single-source intelligence. The brigade intelligence support element also assists the brigade S-2 with the development and evolution of the CCIRs. 

###### **Signal Company** 

1-102. The signal company works closely with the brigade S-6 to provide the SFAB with voice and data transmission expertise for its tactical radio network and worldwide digital voice and data communications using organic and external assets. These capabilities allow SFAB commanders to conduct C2 of their SFA missions. The signal company has no dedicated advisor teams. When necessary, and accounting for the risk inherent in the advisor dichotomy, commanders may task-organize Soldiers of this company with advisor teams to provide technical expertise to the team or the FSF. 

1-103. Through small communications support teams, the signal company provides beyond line-of-sight voice and data transmission capability to SFAB advisor teams. The signal company has limited organic radios, but the support teams can employ the SFAB's multiband, multimission radios that allow broadband voice and data transmission. The support teams also maintain and employ digital voice and data systems to provide flexibility in a mission partner environment. 

###### **Medical Support Section** 

1-104. SFAB commanders may task-organize advisors in the medical support section (MSS) to other advisor teams across the brigade to provide specialized medical advising capability. When appropriate, the MSS may also establish a medical advisor team. The medical advisors develop foreign medical treatment units or facilities that provide Role 1 through Role 3 care. Medical advisors retain the responsibility for providing basic medical care to advisors within their formation. 

###### **Distribution Section** 

1-105. The distribution section is a subordinate unit of the sustainment advising battalion and contains sustainer specialties which are not assigned to the battalion's other advisor teams. Members of the distribution section task organized episodically to the advisor teams in order provide specific sustainment expertise. When a force package requires a deeper understanding of liquid logistics, ammunition handling and supply management, or like specialty, the distribution section personnel are aligned to the need as dictated by mission requirements. 

###### **Maintenance Section** 

1-106. The maintenance section of the sustainment advising battalion can advise on most materiel maintenance needs including but not limited to wheeled vehicle maintenance, weapons maintenance, generator maintenance, and supply processing capabilities. While the maintenance section seldom deploys as a standalone team, the maintenance section members are habitually aligned to force packages to provide tailored maintenance and materiel ordering expertise to the FSF or partner forces. 

###### **The Advising Battalions** 

1-107. The SFAB advising battalions coordinate the SFA activities of subordinate companies or troops, conduct advisor activities alongside their assigned foreign counterparts, and task staffs to develop capacity and capability of counterparts. The advising battalion must be prepared to execute tasks with FSF at the same, one-up, and two-up level(s) across the range of military operations across the competition continuum. In addition to their advisor duties, advising battalion staffs plan, coordinate, and synchronize operations while managing the COP of the SFA operation. 

###### **_Maneuver Advising Battalions and Squadron_** 

1-108. The SFAB Infantry battalions and the SFAB Cavalry squadron form the SFAB's MBATs. MBATs coordinate the SFA activities of subordinate companies or troops while their designated battalion or squadron advisor teams conduct advisor activities alongside their assigned foreign counterparts. The MBAT must be prepared to execute tasks with FSF across the range of military operations including military engagement, SC, deterrence, limited contingency, crisis response, and LSCO. The MBATs' staffs develop the capability and capacity of their counterparts to plan, coordinate, and synchronize operations in addition to managing the COP of the SFA operation. MBATs should be prepared to execute advising at both the one-up (FSF brigade) and two-up (FSF division) levels. 

###### **_Fires Advising Battalion_** 

1-109. The SFAB fires advising battalion forms the SFAB's FBAT. The FBAT develops the fires capability and capacity of FSF to conduct targeting, deliver fires, and integrate fires at multiple echelons within the OE. Advisor teams advise, train, organize, rebuild, build, and assess FSF within the fires warfighting function. Advisor teams are prepared to support FSF with indirect fires through coordination of joint fire assets, if necessary. The fires battalion headquarters forms a functional advisor team that has subject matter expertise of the fires warfighting function across echelons. The fires battalion is not equipped with organic cannon or rocket artillery systems to conduct the fires function for the FSF. 

###### **_Engineer Advising Battalion_** 

1-110. The SFAB engineer advising battalion forms the SFAB's EBAT. The EBAT develops the engineering capability and capacity of FSF to SPO by overcoming and enhancing the effects of terrain in the OE through the engineer disciplines of combat, and general and geospatial engineering. The EBAT and its corresponding ECATs and EATs train the FSF on construction tasks and construction operations as necessary. The engineer battalion also has a small MI company and a signal company that, in addition to their advising role, provide limited technical support to the brigade. These companies work with and provide support to the brigade staff. The engineer battalion has limited capability and capacity to facilitate friendly mobility without significant augmentation of personnel and equipment. 

###### **_Sustainment Advising Battalion_** 

1-111. The sustainment advising battalion forms the SFAB's LBAT. The LBAT provides advisor teams to develop the logistics capability and capacity of the FSF. In a JOA, the SPO section may coordinate sustainment support between the advisor team and the theater support structure. The sustainment advising battalion consists of a headquarters support company, two LCATs, with four LATs, and the MSS. The MSS provides a medical advising capability for Role 1 through Role 3 medical facilities and assets. The sustainment advising battalion and subordinate advisor teams can advise FSF on sustainment operations from brigade through theater level. The LBAT can be custom-built from the members of the headquarters support company based on the recommendations of the battalion commander and accounting for the advisor dichotomy. The LBAT can provide C2 of advising teams when needed. 

##### **<mark>SECTION IV – ADVISOR TEAM: ADVISING ECHELON AND TASK ORGANIZATION</mark>** 

1-112. Advisor teams provide the knowledge and experience to develop FSF across the warfighting functions for any OE. Composed of technically and tactically competent Soldiers, these advisor teams must be creative, flexible, resilient, and prepared to conduct decentralized operations in austere environments located far away from U.S. support bases. As distinct from the organizational diagram depicted in figure 1-4 (on page 22), figure 1-5 depicts the available advisor teams, by type, at echelon. 

**Figure 1-5. Advisor teams by echelon** 

#### **TASK ORGANIZATION** 

1-113. Individual advisor teams are organized with different responsibilities based off warfighting function experience and intended level of FSF partnership. While these teams are ostensibly separated based on maneuver, fires, engineer, and sustainment, they can and should be regrouped into cross-functional teams as the mission dictates. An effective team will be able to utilize the skill set and training of each of their advisor team members to achieve operational success. Figure 1-6 on page 26 depicts examples of advisor team nomenclature and graphical depiction at a few possible echelons after they have been apportioned to a partner force. 

**Figure 1-6. Advising at echelon** 

1-114. Task-organized advisor teams include all the necessary enablers to accomplish their assigned mission. This includes receiving any required augmentation from outside the SFAB, security elements being the most common. Other common members include sustainment, signal, engineer, fires, and other technical experts in their specific fields (see figure 1-7). Chapter 5 describes augmentation considerations in more detail. 

**Figure 1-7. Advising team composition** 

#### **BRIGADE AND BATTALION ADVISING TEAMS** 

1-115. The BAT concentrates its advising effort on advising brigade and above echelons. The BAT is the only advisor team formation capable of providing robust advising support to levels above the brigade and contains advisors with unique skills and proficiencies due to the brigade-level modified table of organization and equipment positions such as judge advocate general, geographic information system, and provost marshal personnel. When included in a force package, the BAT also supervises, and task organizes its subordinate teams at the company and team level to disperse in accordance with the CCMD advisor guidance. 

1-116. The BAT and MBATs have numerous trained advisors that provide the core of the advisor teams. The fires, engineer, and sustainment battalions have fewer personnel and are generally task-organized to provide technical expertise to other advisor teams. Using U.S. Force structure as a guide, specialty MBATs operate at the battalion, brigade, or division level, while the BAT can advise up to the corps or even ministry of defense (MOD) level if no MOD-specific advisors from the CCMD are available. 

1-117. Mission analysis determines the structure of the brigade and battalion advisor teams. After assessing the SFA mission and FSF requirements, the SFAB organizes these higher echelon brigade and BATs. These task-organized teams may include security or other external augmentation required for the specific mission. 

1-118. The BAT and MBATs are charged with managing the flow of information requirements utilized through the advisor network and advisor reachback capabilities that exist within the SFAB. Their ability to track and manage these information requirements directly informs the commander's ability to achieve mission success within SFA objectives. 

1-119. The FBAT provides technical expertise and advisor activities to fires elements of FSF at the battalion and above levels. The FBAT can provide specialized advising capabilities to FSF fires units, personnel, and integrators. 

1-120. The EBAT provides technical expertise and advisor activities to engineer and protection elements of the FSF at the battalion and above levels. The engineer advising battalion is also uniquely capable of providing robust signal and military intelligence advising augmentation to other teams through their military intelligence and signal companies. 

1-121. The LBAT is the greatest source of sustainment advising capability and contains subordinate advising teams, the MSS, and sustainment functional sections manned for maintenance and distribution advising. The MSS, distribution, and maintenance sections also augment other advising teams as dictated by the mission. The LBAT is assigned a robust package of sustainment specialties but possesses insufficient manning and materiel to serve as a brigade support battalion. 

#### **COMPANY ADVISOR TEAMS** 

1-122. Company and troop advisor teams (MCATs, FCATs, ECATs, and LCATs) perform C2 and advisor activities in addition to management, coordination, and supervision of SFA mission of their subordinate teams. Company advisor teams are led by a company commander, or company team leader and are usually responsible for the employment and C2 of two advisor teams plus the company advisor team itself. Paragraphs 1-123 through 1-127 detail the employment of company advisor teams within each specialty. 

##### **MANEUVER COMPANY ADVISOR TEAM** 

1-123. The companies and troops of the Infantry battalions and Cavalry squadron make up the core of the MCATs. MCATs manage, coordinate, and supervise the SFA mission of their subordinate advisor teams while developing their assigned FSF. Outside of SFA activities, in conjunction with FSF, these teams do not have the capacity or capability to conduct independent operations. MCATs may perform duties typical of a conventional command post, however, this will be at the expense of their advising capability. (See chapter 4, section I, for more information on organizational reporting structures.) MCATs must be prepared to execute tasks with FSF across the range of military operations including SC, limited contingency operations, crisis response, and LSCO. MCATs should be prepared to execute advising at both the one-up (FSF battalion) and two-up (FSF brigade) levels. 

1-124. Advisor teams are the primary SFAB organizations that influence FSF through the execution of SFA activities. The SFAB contains maneuver, fires, engineer, and sustainment advisor teams along with trained medical advisors in the MSS. Advisor teams conduct activities alongside their foreign counterparts in support of their assigned SFA activities. Advisor teams operate at echelon or up to two levels higher. At each echelon, advisor teams generally organize into an operations and support section to facilitate C2 while executing operations (see figure 1-5 on page 25). 

##### **FIRES COMPANY ADVISOR TEAM** 

1-125. The FCATs can advise at the company, battalion, and brigade levels. While the FCAT is not assigned indirect fire assets, they provide substantial fires functional knowledge and experiential expertise. FCAT personnel cannot provide a full fires cell capability to the FSF but can enhance preexisting fires infrastructure within FSF capabilities as well as conduct gap-analyses to directly foster interoperability across echelons and multinational targeting. 

##### **ENGINEER COMPANY ADVISOR TEAM** 

1-126. ECATs can advise on engineer functions at the company through brigade levels and are versed in protection, horizontal and vertical construction, and mobility-countermobility-survivability operational tasks. ECATs are routinely included in force packages to develop FSF and partners on engineering actions which involve cross-functional mission requirements at scale. 

##### **LOGISTICS COMPANY ADVISOR TEAM** 

1-127. The LCAT can advise on logistics functions at the company through brigade echelon, and are versed in maintenance, transportation, supply, field services, and distribution operational tasks. The LCATs of the sustainment advising battalion are the smallest footprint advisor team by modified table of equipment, however, they are habitually augmented with sustainment advisors organic to the LBAT in a modular fashion to advise at the point of need. Versed in the multifaceted functions of sustainment, LCAT employment ranges from tactical logistics operations advising to wide-scale sustainment operations. LCAT advisor team members must be versed in staff operations processes. 

#### **ADVISOR TEAMS** 

1-128. Advisor teams comprise the majority of SFAB personnel and are uniquely equipped among conventional Army formations to perform the advisor role. Company advisor team commanders leverage the unique personalities, interpersonal and military skills, and experiences of the advisor teams to advise at the point of need. Advisor teams are led by a team leader who advises his commander on gainful employment of his team, considering operational and mission variables. The assistant team leader, who is the most senior NCO, assists the team leader and shares the responsibility of leading the team in the team leader’s absence or as determined by necessity. 

##### **MANEUVER ADVISOR TEAMS** 

1-129. The MATs—located in companies and troops of the Infantry battalions and Cavalry squadrons—have a greater variety of advisors already assigned to their teams relative to other battalions. These teams are a four-advisor, cross-functional team with three primary advisors and seven enabling advisors. These teams span all warfighting functions, to include advisors from sustainment, signal, fires, medical, maintenance, and other fields, providing these advisor teams with organic flexibility and depth. The fires, engineer, and sustainment advisor teams are specialized in their requisite skills and do not have the same breadth of specialties as the MATs. SFABs generally task-organize their advisor teams with the MATs to provide their required technical expertise for the specific mission. MATs must be versed in staff operations and these MATs conduct operations at the company, battalion, or brigade level. 

1-130. MATs are the base element in the SFAB and are unique among conventional Army formations. Commanders and team leaders assign personnel to the advisor team based on operational and mission variables. Once higher headquarters assigns personnel to the team, the leader organizes the element to facilitate C2 while executing operations. Figure 1-8 on page 30 depicts an example of a task-organized MAT. 

**Figure 1-8. Maneuver advisor team, example** 

##### **FIRES ADVISOR TEAMS** 

1-131. FATs provide technical and tactical fires and targeting expertise to their FSF counterparts. The FATs do not have the same depth of organic advisors as MATs. SFABs should generally task organize their FATs with MATs to provide their required technical expertise for the specific mission. For example, FATs augment BATs aligned against division to provide more robust fires cells. When operational and mission variables allow, they may operate independently as well. FATs must be versed in staff operations. These advisor teams have the capability to conduct operations at the company, battalion, or brigade level. 

##### **ENGINEER ADVISOR TEAMS** 

1-132. EATs provide technical and tactical expertise in mobility, countermobility, survivability, and general engineering to their FSF counterparts. EATs have familiarity with commercial and military geospatial tools and have an additional reachback capability to the United States Army Corps of Engineers Reachback Operations Center for additional technical engineering expertise. 

1-133. The EATs do not have the same depth of organic advisors as MATs. SFABs should generally task organize their EATs with MATs to provide their required technical expertise for the specific mission. When operational and mission variables allow, they may operate independently as well. EATs must be versed in staff operations or employ a battle staff graduate. These advisor teams conduct operations at the company, battalion, or brigade level. 

##### **LOGISTICS ADVISOR TEAMS** 

1-134. LATs develop FSF sustainment processes, procedures, and systems. LATs advise FSF at the platoon through battalion echelons (or equivalent) on full spectrum of logistic activities defined in FM 4-0. The substantial difference between a typical advisor team and a LAT is the emphasis given to the sustainment advising capability. These advisor teams must be versed in staff operations. 

##### **SECTION V – ADVISOR TEAM MEMBER DUTIES AND RESPONSIBILITIES** 

Your MOS gets you in the door, but your job is to be an advisor. 

<mark>Major General Donn H. Hill 3rd Commanding General</mark> Security Force Assistance Command 

1-135. SFAB operations require leaders, staffs, and advisors to operate in unfamiliar situations influenced by a substantial number of variables. Therefore, it is not possible to generate a list of all-inclusive duties and responsibilities for all positions and situations. This section provides a basic framework for typical duties and responsibilities for advisors and staff. Units should further refine these responsibilities through direction, SOPs, and task organization. Refer to appendix A for a list of common types of teams used by the SFAB and the number classification system to identify each SFAB team member. 

1-136. Advisor teams conduct advising activities through continuous assessments. It is the team's responsibility to update their assessments on the FSF repeatedly and routinely. 

1-137. Advisor functions are divided into two areas, internal and external, as described in the following: 

- Internal functions directly support the advisor teams and include planning, operations, administrative, maintenance, and supply. 

- External functions pertain to the advisor team's mission as it relates to the FSF and may be narrow in scope or apply across warfighting areas: 

 - External functions illustrate a method of dividing responsibilities among advisors. 

 - External functions are not all-inclusive or appropriate for all situations. 

**_Note._** While the normal duties and responsibilities of advisors are discussed further, advisors may have to fill more than one position at a time. All advisors should cross train and understand other positions on their respective teams and always seek to advise to the need. 

##### **Korean Military Advisory Group – The Advisor Mentality** 

<mark>In the execution of his advisory mission, the advisor begins with considerable prestige which he must continually endeavor to maintain. With finesse and intelligence many advisors have made recommendations to their counterparts so logically and effectively that United States military doctrine stands out clearly in the issuance and execution of orders by Korean counterparts.</mark> 

Advisors Handbook, 1951 

#### **COMPANY ADVISOR TEAM MEMBERS** 

1-138. The following duty positions exist in the company level advising teams and are only present in with the MCAT, FCAT, ECAT, and/or LCAT, respectively. 

##### **COMMANDER (TEAM LEADER)** 

1-139. The company, troop, or battery commander also functions as the team leader for the company, troop, or battery advisor team. The team leader is directly involved with advising the FSF commander and senior staff. The team leader is responsible for leading the team through the execution of battle drills. 

The team leader performs the following internal functions: 

 - Leading all team operations, planning, and actions. 

 - Taking responsibility for the deployment/redeployment readiness of the team. 

 - Taking responsibility for the collective training of the team. 

 - Assessing the FSF capabilities. 

 - Approving all outgoing reports. 

 - Providing input for, and submitting, the FSF training and readiness reports. 

 - Ensuring the establishment of requisition and tracking systems. 

 - Conducting operations in a safe manner through the integration of risk management (see ATP 5-19). 

 - • Accounting for team equipment. 

 - Educating self and team on the culture, region or country, history, and key figures in the OE. 

- 1-140. The team leader performs the following external functions: 

 - Advising the counterpart commander within their functional specialty. 

 - Liaising with higher, adjacent, and subordinate units. 

 - Conducting cross-cultural negotiations with foreign counterparts. 

 - Supporting the FSF with capabilities. 

1-141. The team leader will generally organize the team to include at least two subordinate leaders, usually a senior advisor in charge of an operations and support section. This method facilitates control and provides efficiency when working with the FSF. 

##### **FIRST SERGEANT (ASSISTANT TEAM LEADER)** 

1-142. The company, troop, or battery first sergeant also functions as the assistant team leader on the company, troop, or battery advising team. The assistant team leader is the senior NCO on the advisor team. The assistant team leader is responsible for monitoring daily operations of the advisor team. Assistant team leaders fill the team leader role in the latter's absence. The assistant team leader performs the following internal functions: 

 - Accounting for all personnel assigned to the team both administratively and for training. 

 - Developing the foundational training cycle in accordance with SFAC's annual training guidance. 

 - Assisting the team leader in the collective training cycle in accordance with SFAC's annual training guidance. 

 - Assisting the team leader with team deployment/redeployment readiness requirements. 

 - Leading the team in the absence of the team leader. 

 - Planning training to ensure the team's combat efficiency, discipline, administration, and welfare. 

 - Reviewing the team's operations and training calendars. 

 - Coordinating staff actions before they reach the team leader. 

 - Directing the team's budget. 

 - Managing and directing interpreters. 

 - Writing outgoing reports and messages. 

- 1-143. The assistant team leader performs the following external functions: 

 - Representing the team in the absence of the team leader. 

 - Advising FSF leaders. 

 - Assisting the senior NCO counterpart with enforcing the commander's intent, maintaining discipline, and conducting precombat checks. 

- Providing input for the FSF assessments. 

- Reviewing training prior to execution. 

- Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

##### **TRAINING AND ADVISE NONCOMMISSIONED OFFICER** 

1-144. The training and advise NCO at the MCAT level performs duties and functions like the training and advise NCO at the MAT level. (See paragraphs 1-168 through 1-169 for more information.) 

##### **INTELLIGENCE ADVISOR** 

1-145. The intelligence advisor at the MCAT level performs duties and functions like the intelligence advisor at the MAT level. (See paragraphs 1-170 through 1-172 for more information.) 

##### **FIRE SUPPORT NONCOMMISSIONED OFFICER** 

1-146. The fire support NCO at the MCAT level performs duties and functions like the fires advisor at the MAT level. (See paragraphs 1-173 through 1-174 for more information.) 

##### **SIGNAL ADVISOR** 

1-147. The signal advisor at the MCAT level performs duties and functions like the signal advisor at the MAT level. (See paragraphs 1-175 through 1-176 for more information.) 

##### **LOGISTICS ADVISOR** 

1-148. The logistics advisor at the MCAT level performs duties and functions like the logistics advisor at the MAT level. (See paragraphs 1-177 through 1-178 for more information.) 

1-149. The LCAT also has two logistics advisors who advise on the functions of their military occupational specialty (MOS) as per the duties and responsibilities of the sustainment advising battalion distribution section. 

##### **MECHANIC ADVISOR (MAINTENANCE ADVISOR)** 

1-150. The mechanic advisor, or maintenance advisor, at the MCAT level performs duties and functions like the maintenance advisor at the MAT level. (See paragraphs <u>1-179 through 1-180</u> for more information.) 

1-151. The LCAT also has a maintenance advisor who advises on the functions of the MOS as per the duties and responsibilities of the sustainment advising battalion maintenance section. 

##### **EXPLOSIVE HAZARD ADVISOR** 

1-152. The explosive hazard advisor at the MCAT level performs duties and functions like the explosive hazard advisor at the MAT level. (See paragraphs 1-181 through 1-183 for more information.) 

##### **HEALTH CARE SERGEANT (MEDICAL ADVISOR)** 

1-153. The health care sergeant, or medical advisor, at the company advisor team level performs duties and functions like the medical advisor at the advisor team level. (See paragraphs 1-184 through 1-185 for more information.) 

##### **FIRES SUPPORT ADVISOR** 

1-154. The FCAT fires support advisor has duties and responsibilities similar, but not limited to, the duties and responsibilities of the MAT fire support NCO and the FAT senior fire control NCO, but at the company/battery and higher levels. (See paragraphs <u>1-173 through 1-174</u> as well as paragraphs <u>1-194 through 1-195</u> for more information.) 

##### **TEAM ADVISOR (ENGINEER SUPPORT ADVISOR)** 

1-155. The ECAT team advisor, or engineer support advisor, has duties and responsibilities similar, but not limited to, the duties and responsibilities of the brigade engineer and the EAT advisor, but at the company level. (See paragraph <u>1-69</u> as well as paragraphs 1-202 through 1-203 for more information.) 

##### **SUPPLY ADVISOR** 

1-156. The LCATs have a supply advisor. The supply advisor is versed in support and supply operations like the LAT supply advisor but at the company level. (See paragraphs <u>1-215 through 1-216</u> for more information.) 

##### **LOGISTICS (PETROLEUM SUPPLY) ADVISOR** 

1-157. The LCATs have a logistics (petroleum supply) advisor. Much like the supply advisor (paragraph 1-154), the logistics (petroleum supply) advisor is versed in general supply discipline and operations. However, this advisor also has technical training in the supply chain, evaluation and testing, dispensing, and ecological preservation measures regarding fuel logistics operations. 

##### **LOGISTICS WARRANT OFFICER ADVISOR** 

1-158. The LCAT has the logistics warrant officer advisor serve as the LCAT’s and LBAT’s technical expert. They serve as the senior technical advisor to the advisor team leader and the FSF, specifically regarding logistics systems and processes. They are responsible for the integration of systems and processes across the tactical and operational domain and facilitate support relationships with higher echelon sustainment organizations. The logistics warrant officer advisor provides the following internal functions: 

- Assisting the LAT's leaders or commanders in developing the advising strategy and objectives for the FSF. 

- Synchronizing sustainment support between sustainment advisors in supported SFAB organizations and the higher echelon of sustainment. 

- Serving as the XO for LCAT by managing systems and processes that support the organization. 

- Developing and providing logistical analysis and running estimates to support the logistics advising company. 

- Tracking and analyzing sustainment trends and issues for the sustainment advising company and other supported organizations. 

1-159. The logistics warrant officer develops and provides logistical analysis and running estimates to support the logistics advising company and performs the following external functions: 

- Serving as the senior technical advisor for sustainment systems and processes for the FSF. 

- Providing input and guidance on maintaining readiness to foreign counterparts. 

- Adhering to the principles of cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

- Supporting the FSF with capabilities as appropriate. 

#### **ADVISOR TEAM MEMBERS** 

1-160. The following details the cross-functional advisor teams which contain the bulk of the advising capabilities and personnel within the SFAB. 

##### **MANEUVER ADVISOR TEAMS** 

1-161. MATs provide the bulk of the SFAB's tactical operations advising capability and capacity. These flexible, scalable, and well-rounded advisor teams provide the expertise to advise and assist the FSF across the warfighting functions. These teams often receive additional augmentation, to include security, EATs, FATs, or other external enablers. They often receive direct or general support (GS) from other organizations outside the SFAB to include contracting, sustainment, and even external staff support for processing intelligence, requesting fires, and submitting movement requests. Section IV describes the command and support relationships in more detail. 

1-162. A MAT is task-organized to meet specific requirements identified through initial or ongoing assessments of the FSF. The team leader and the task organization will determine the specific duties and responsibilities of each member. The following personnel in paragraphs 1-163 through 1-185 form the typical MAT. 

###### **Training and Advise Team Leader (Team Leader)** 

1-163. The training or advise team leader, or team leader, is directly involved with advising the FSF commander and senior staff. The team leader leads the team through the execution of battle drills. The team leader performs the following internal functions: 

- Leading all team operations, planning, and actions. 

- Maintaining responsibility for the deployment/redeployment readiness of the team. 

- Maintaining responsibility for the collective training of the team. 

- Assessing the FSF capabilities. 

- Approving all outgoing reports. 

- Providing input for and submitting the FSF training and readiness reports. 

- Ensuring the establishment of requisition and tracking systems. 

- Conducting operations in a safe manner through the integration of risk management (see ATP 5-19). 

- • Accounting for team equipment. 

- Accounting for the _advisor dichotomy_ in planning and striking an operational balance. 

- Educating self and team on the culture, region or country, history, and key figures in the OE. 

- Planning team force protection considerations. 

- Monitoring family readiness and maintaining a team social roster. 

1-164. The team leader performs the following external functions: 

- Advising the counterpart commander. 

- Advising the counterparts on operations to include— 

 - Current and future operations planning. 

 - Battle tracking. 

 - Reporting. 

- Liaising with higher, adjacent, and subordinate units. 

- Conducting cross-cultural negotiations with the foreign counterparts. 

- Supporting the FSF with capabilities. 

- Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

- Populating the green COP ensuring FSF positions are understood at higher friendly echelons. 

1-165. Generally, the team leader will organize the team to include at least two subordinate leaders, usually a senior advisor in charge of an operations and support section. This method facilitates control and provides efficiency when working with the FSF. 

###### **Training and Advise Noncommissioned Officer in Charge (Assistant Team Leader)** 

1-166. The training and advise noncommissioned officer in charge (NCOIC), also called the assistant team leader, is the senior NCO on the advisor team. The assistant team leader is responsible for monitoring daily operations of the advisor team. Assistant team leaders fill the team leader role in the latter's absence. The assistant team leader performs the following internal functions: 

- Accounting for all personnel assigned to the team both administratively and for training. 

- Developing the foundational training cycle. 

- Assisting the team leader in collective training cycle. 

- Assisting the team leader with deployment/redeployment readiness requirements of the team. 

- • Leading the team in the absence of the team leader. 

- Planning training to ensure the team's combat efficiency, discipline, administration, and welfare. 

- • Reviewing the team's operations and training calendars. 

- Coordinating staff actions before they reach the team leader. 

- Directing the team's budget. 

 - Managing and directing interpreters. 

 - Writing outgoing reports and messages. 

 - Supporting planning of team force protection. 

 - In most cases, serving as the advisor team pay agent according to CCMD guidance and procedures. 

 - Ensuring team members are administratively ready to deploy with the following:  Wills. 

 - Powers of attorney. 

 - Insurance. 

 - Record of emergency data. 

 - Emergency notifications. 

 - Theater entry requirements. 

 - Preparing personnel accountability and morning reports. 

 - Maintaining a team administrative records book. 

 - Managing promotions, reenlistments, pay issues, mail, and awards. 

- 1-167. The assistant team leader performs the following external functions: 

 - Representing the team in the absence of the team leader. 

 - Advising FSF leaders. 

 - Advising the FSF staff in base defense, traffic control posts, detainee operations, and other relevant operations. 

 - Assisting the senior NCO counterpart with— 

 - Enforcing the commander's intent. 

 - Maintaining discipline. 

 - Conducting precombat checks. 

 - Providing input for FSF assessments. 

 - Reviewing training prior to execution. 

 - Advising the foreign counterparts on human resource operations including military pay and personnel accountability. 

 - Assisting the FSF in developing personnel procedures. 

 - Training counterpart administrative personnel. 

 - Assessing the FSF’ personnel systems and operations. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

 - If team leader is unavailable, populating the green COP to ensure FSF positions are understood at higher friendly echelons. 

**_Note._** While the modified table of equipment breaks out certain positions as belonging to the “operations section” or “support section,” advisor team size requires constant overlap between the sections and a collaborative effort from everyone on the team in pursuit of the team's advisor mission. 

###### **Training and Advise Noncommissioned Officer** 

1-168. The training and advise NCO is responsible for advising the FSF on the support functions that generally include medical, maintenance, sustainment, and signal. This position controls the support section while executing battle drills. The training and advise NCO performs the following internal functions: 

- Leading and controlling the support section. 

- Assisting the team leader with developing a training and support plan to include the following: 

 - A training schedule, consolidating the information into the team synchronization matrix. 

 - Inputting training into the Digital Training Management System. 

- Supporting the assistant team leader with deployment/redeployment readiness requirements of the team. 

- Maintaining a weekly training and operations calendar. 

- Supporting the assistant team leader with writing and reviewing required reports. 

 - Ensuring all team members can train and advise FSF. 

 - Managing the team's driver training program. 

 - Coordinating for team religious support. 

 - Ensuring team equipment is serviceable. 

 - Ensuring uniform standards are met. 

- 1-169. The senior support advisor performs the following external functions: 

 - Advising and supporting the FSF support plans. 

 - Advising foreign counterparts on the basic tenets of troop welfare. 

 - Consolidating and coordinating the FSF's external religious support requests. 

 - Providing input on the counterpart training plans and execution. 

 - Adhering to the principles of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

###### **Intelligence Advisor** 

1-170. The intelligence advisor's responsibilities vary throughout the competition continuum and are SC authority dependent. Intelligence tasks include, but are not limited to the following: 

- Preparing team intelligence reports. 

- Advising on intelligence preparation of the operational environment (IPOE). 

- Advising on building an information collection plan. 

- Requesting external resources. 

1-171. The intelligence advisor assists the FSF with the same tasks. Intelligence advisors generally serve as an operations team member and report to the senior advisor of the operations section. The intelligence advisor performs internal functions that include the following: 

 - Advising the team leader on intelligence. 

 - Monitoring routine situational updates (weather, road conditions, civil considerations, and recent activities). 

 - Providing reachback capability to leverage multidiscipline, multiechelon intelligence enablers in support of advisor team operations using existing intelligence programs of record. 

 - Maintaining the cultural calendar and advising the team of key dates and significant activities. 

 - Training and advising the team in the use of biometric and forensic equipment used in tactical site exploitation. 

 - Advising on the IPOE to SPO. 

 - Advising on the preparation of the information collection plan. 

 - Assisting the team with collecting information for required reports during missions. 

 - Coordinating through higher headquarters for CI support for vulnerability assessments regarding insider threat, international terrorism, and force protection assessments. 

 - Providing input for intelligence training evaluations. 

 - Assisting with monitoring the accountability for, and welfare of, interpreters. 

- 1-172. The intelligence advisor performs the following external functions: • Advising the foreign counterpart intelligence section. 

 - Advising the counterparts on using intelligence assets. 

 - Advising the FSF on processing tactical information into predictive analysis. 

 - Supporting intelligence for the counterpart's combat operations. 

 - Supporting intelligence briefings to the FSF commander. 

 - Integrating intelligence reporting with joint, interagency, intergovernmental, and multinational partners' intelligence enterprises, where allowed. 

 - Advocating for sharing of intelligence and information with joint, interagency, intergovernmental, and multinational partners by pursuing Advanced Affirmative Disclosure Determinations (also called A2D2s) and emphasizing writing for release (see ATP 3-16.02). 

 - Receiving and distributing the green COP and green intelligence picture and assessments. 

- Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

- Supervising and enabling team participation in Foreign Military Intelligence Collection Activities (also called FORMICA), when possible. 

###### **Fires Advisor** 

1-173. The team fires advisor is responsible for developing a fires plan through integrating and synchronizing Army, joint, and multinational fires, and managing clearance of fires to enable and enhance FSF targeting objectives. The fires advisor assists the FSF with many of the same functions. Fire advisors usually serve in the operations section and report to the operation section's senior advisor. The fires advisor performs the following internal functions: 

- Assisting the team leader with fires planning and coordinating with higher headquarters, verifying and requesting the availability of fire support assets. 

- Requesting Army indirect fires and joint fires assets through the advisor network, including but not limited to the following: 

 - The lethal effects of fixed- and rotary-wing aircraft. 

 - Naval surface fire support. 

 - Artillery. 

 - Rockets and missiles. 

 - Nonlethal effects of some information operations. 

 - Cyber operations. 

 - Electromagnetic activities. 

 - Space control operations. 

 - Other nonlethal capabilities. 

- Providing team training on fire support procedures, assets, and capabilities. 

- Assisting the team leader with tracking all partnered force elements in the AO. 

- Providing input for training evaluations. 

1-174. The fires advisor performs the following external functions: 

- Assisting with planning, coordinating, and employing fire support for the counterparts. 

- Training and assisting the FSF with improving their fire support processes. 

- Supporting counterparts with U.S. fires within the scope of the mission and assets available. 

- Assisting foreign forces in the clearance of U.S. or joint fires prior to execution. 

- Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

###### **Signal Advisor** 

1-175. The signal advisor is responsible for establishing and maintaining communication networks for the team while advising foreign counterparts on building a communications network, developing communication contingency plans, and conducting communications maintenance. Signal advisors generally serve as a support team member and report to the senior advisor for the support section. The signal advisor performs the following internal functions: 

- Providing input for training evaluations. 

- Advising on radios and communications capabilities. 

- Providing communications maintenance support for the advisor team's vehicles and communications equipment status utilizing the advisor network. 

- Verifying faults, requesting Class IX (repair parts and components for equipment maintenance) parts, and maintaining maintenance records of communications equipment. 

- Ensuring communication readiness for deployment in specific AORs. 

- Managing teams signal minimization and emissions control appropriate for AOR. 

- Managing COMSEC. 

- 1-176. The signal advisor performs the following external functions: 

 - Advising the FSF on communications planning, training, management, and COMSEC. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

 - 

- Supporting counterparts with capabilities as operational and mission variables allow. 

###### **Logistics Advisor** 

1-177. Logistics advisors are responsible for managing the team's sustainment and advising the FSF on issues that include property accountability, managing funds, and processing external support requests. They generally serve as a support team member and report to the senior advisor for the support section. 

The logistics advisor performs the following internal functions: 

 - Advising the team leader on sustainment. 

 - Collecting, processing, and coordinating for team sustainment support through the next higher headquarters. 

 - Maintaining equipment accountability and conducting periodic inventories of team equipment. 

 - Supporting the assistant team leader with managing team funds. 

 - Assisting with tracking and monitoring contracts and deliverables. 

 - Coordinating with the supporting contracting organization on service delivery. 

 - Serving as the team armorer. 

 - Providing input for training evaluations. 

 - Maintaining and updating the green logistics common operational picture (LOGCOP). 

- 1-178. The logistics advisor performs the following external functions: 

 - Advising the FSF on sustainment, specifically supply and transportation. 

 - Assisting counterparts with establishing and maintaining a system to track their deceased Soldiers. 

 - Advising the FSF on how to track and monitor contract deliveries. 

 - Adhering to the principles, tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

 - Supporting the counterparts with capabilities as operational and mission variables allow. 

 - Updating daily logistics statistics utilizing the advisor network. 

 - Informing the joint force on FSF combat power status. 

 - Communicating details on the green LOGCOP as required. 

###### **Mechanic Advisor (Maintenance Advisor)** 

1-179. The mechanic advisor, or maintenance advisor, is responsible for verifying faults, ordering parts, and conducting repairs on the team's vehicles. Maintenance advisors assist the FSF with developing a maintenance SOP, establishing service schedules, ordering parts, and training their maintenance personnel. They serve as a support team member and report to the senior advisor for the support section. The maintenance advisor performs the following internal functions: 

 - Providing input for training evaluations. 

 - Providing limited field-level maintenance support and services for the advisor team vehicles and equipment, other than medical and communications equipment. 

 - Verifying faults, requesting Class IX (repair) parts, and maintaining maintenance records other than medical and communications equipment utilizing the advisor network. 

 - Collecting, processing, and coordinating for team maintenance requests through the advisor network. 

- 1-180. The maintenance advisor performs the following external functions: 

 - Assisting FSF with developing a maintenance program to include routine maintenance and service schedules, mechanic training, troubleshooting, and ordering parts. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

 - Supporting counterparts with capabilities as operational and mission variables allow. 

###### **Explosive Hazard Advisor** 

1-181. Explosive hazard advisors are EOD NCOs and EOD officers (MOS 89D/E). These advisors are responsible for providing explosive hazard technical and tactical advice to the advisor team leader. They serve as an operations team member and report to the operations officer and operations senior enlisted advisor. The explosive hazard advisors perform these internal functions: 

 - Providing input for training. 

 - Providing explosive hazard technical and tactical advice. 

 - Managing the team's CBRN equipment. 

- 1-182. Explosive hazard advisors perform the following external functions: 

 - Provide explosive hazard technical and tactical advice to the FSF, increasing their ability to identify, render safe, and exploit explosive ordnance and other explosive hazards. 

 - •Assess FSF EOD vulnerabilities. 

 - Coordinate FSF external EOD requests for support with U.S. or mission partner EOD. 

 - Identify weapons of mass destruction (WMD) for FSF in support of the CCDR's countering-WMD mission. 

 - Adhere to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

1-183. SFAB EOD advisors have limitations and restrictions. EOD advisors are limited in that they may not render safe explosive hazards; they lack the equipment and publications required to render safe explosive ordinance. EOD advisors are restricted in that they can provide immediate action to mitigate an explosive hazard but only to prevent the loss of life when no other EOD capability can reasonably respond. 

###### **Health Care Sergeant (Medical Advisor)** 

1-184. The health care sergeants (also called medical advisors) are responsible for providing medical support to the advisor team and advising the FSF on triage procedures, first responder medical training, field sanitation, medical logistics, medical planning, and FHP. They serve as a support team member and generally report to the senior advisor for the support section. The medical advisor performs the following internal functions: 

 - Providing input for training evaluations. 

 - Assisting the advisor team leader with medical planning. 

 - Maintaining principal responsibility to procedures, principles, and fundamentals of the following:  Point of injury care. 

 - Self-aid. 

 - Buddy-aid. 

 - Combat lifesaver. 

 - Tactical combat casualty care. 

 - Training casualty evacuation (CASEVAC) and MEDEVAC concepts from point of injury through continuum of care. 

 - Assessing, conceiving, and implementing mass casualty concepts. 

 - Gathering and disseminating information on known health threats within the AO. 

 - Advising on medical capabilities of nearby assets. 

 - Stabilizing patients and prepares them for MEDEVAC. 

 - Training and assisting the team in managing field sanitation. 

 - Maintaining Class VIII (medical) supply utilizing the advisor network. 

 - Verifying faults. 

 - Requesting Class VIII parts. 

 - Maintaining maintenance records of team medical equipment. 

- 1-185. The medical advisor performs the following external functions: 

 - Training the FSF on the following: 

 - Rapid trauma assessment and trauma medicine. 

 - Point of injury care. 

 - CASEVAC. 

 - MEDEVAC. 

 - Mass casualty operations. 

 - Concepts of far-forward medical care. 

- Maintaining a medical COP with the foreign partner that supports medical advising efforts. 

- Stabilizing patients and preparing them for MEDEVAC. 

- Advising counterparts on maintaining Class VIII supplies and equipment. 

- Training and assisting the FSF on field sanitation, field hygiene, and personnel protective measures. 

- Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of the following: 

 - Effective cross-cultural communication. 

 - Problem solving. 

 - Conflict resolution. 

- Supporting the FSF with capabilities as operational and mission variables allow. 

##### **FIRES ADVISOR TEAM MEMBERS** 

1-186. FATs enhance the SFAB's capability and capacity to advise FSF on targeting and fires proficiencies. The internal and external responsibilities of FAT positions in paragraphs 1-185 through 1-193. 

###### **Platoon Leader (Team Leader)** 

1-187. The platoon leader, or team leader, is directly involved with advising the FSF commander and senior staff. The team leader is prepared to assume the role of FCAT team leader in the absence or loss of the commander, according to the succession of command. The team leader is responsible for leading the team through the execution of battle drills. The team leader performs the following internal functions: 

- Leading all team operations, planning, and actions. 

- Having responsibility for the deployment/redeployment readiness of the team. 

- Having responsibility for the collective training of the team. 

- Assessing the FSF capabilities. 

- Approving all outgoing reports. 

- Providing input for and submits the FSF training and readiness reports. 

- Ensuring the establishment of requisition and tracking systems. 

- Conducting operations in a safe manner through the integration of risk management (see ATP 5-19). 

- • Accounting for team equipment. 

- Educating self and team on the culture, region or country, history, and key figures in the OE. 

1-188. The team leader performs the following external functions: 

- Advising the counterpart commander. 

- Liaising with higher, adjacent, and subordinate units. 

- Conducting cross-cultural negotiations with foreign counterparts. 

- Supporting the FSF with capabilities. 

- Informing the joint air-ground integration cell or SFAB effects integration cell on FSF partner fires capability. 

###### **Section Chief (Assistant Team Leader)** 

1-189. The section chief, or assistant team leader, is the senior NCO on the advisor team. The assistant team leader is responsible for monitoring daily operations of the advisor team. Assistant team leaders fill the team leaders' role in their absence. The assistant team leader performs these internal functions: 

- Accounting for all personnel assigned to the team both administratively and for training. 

- Overseeing the developing of the foundational training cycle in accordance with SFAC's annual training guidance. 

- Assisting the team leader in collective training cycle in accordance with SFAC's annual training guidance. 

- Assisting the team leader with deployment/redeployment readiness requirements of the team. 

- Leading the team in the absence of the team leader. 

- Planning training to ensure the team's combat efficiency, discipline, administration, and welfare. 

 - Reviewing the team's operations and training calendars. 

 - Coordinating staff actions before they reach the team leader. 

 - Directing the team's budget. 

 - Managing and directing interpreters. 

 - Writing outgoing reports and messages. 

- 1-190. The assistant team leader performs the following external functions: 

 - Representing the team in the absence of the team leader. 

 - Advising FSF leaders. 

 - Assisting the senior NCO counterpart with— 

 - Enforcing the commander's intent. 

 - Maintaining discipline. 

 - Conducting precombat checks. 

 - Providing input for the FSF assessments. 

 - Reviewing training prior to execution. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

###### **Gunnery Sergeant Advisor** 

1-191. The gunnery sergeant advisor is responsible for howitzer operation considerations within the FAT. The gunnery sergeant advisor assists the FSF with firing unit advanced party operations. 

###### **_Howitzer Operations and Other Internal Functions_** 

- 1-192. The gunnery sergeant advisor performs these internal functions: 

 - Assisting the team leader with mission planning. 

 - Forecasting and requesting necessary classes of supply. 

 - Managing the team's maintenance program. 

 - Providing team training on MOS-specific tasks to include— 

 - Laying platoons and batteries. 

 - Defense. 

 - Degraded operations. 

 - Providing input for training evaluations and programs of instruction for towed, self-propelled, and rocket weapon systems. 

###### **_External Functions_** 

- 1-193. The gunnery sergeant advisor performs the following external functions: 

 - Assisting with planning, coordinating, and employing fire support for counterparts. 

 - Training and assisting the FSF with improving their fire support processes. 

 - Supporting counterparts with U.S. fires within the scope of the mission and assets available. 

 - Assisting the foreign forces in the clearance of U.S. or joint fires prior to execution. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

###### **Senior Fire Control Noncommissioned Officer (Advisor)** 

1-194. The senior fire control advisor is responsible for all aspects of fire direction for the FAT. The senior fire control advisor assists the FSF with fire direction operations. The senior fire control advisor performs these internal functions: 

- Assisting the team leader with the advising scheme. 

- Providing team training on the fundamentals of fire direction to include manual and automated processes. 

- Assisting the team leader with training management. 

- Providing input for training evaluations. 

- 1-195. The senior fire control advisor performs the following external functions: 

 - Assisting with planning, coordinating, and employing fire support for the counterparts. 

 - Training and assisting the FSF with improving their fire control processes. 

 - Supporting counterparts with U.S. fires within the scope of the mission and assets available. 

 - Assisting the foreign forces in the clearance of U.S. or joint fires prior to execution. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

##### **ENGINEER ADVISOR TEAM MEMBERS** 

1-196. EATs enhance the SFAB's engineer advising capability and capacity with the FSF. The internal and external responsibilities of these positions are described in paragraphs 1-195 through 1-205. 

###### **Team Leader** 

1-197. The team leader is directly involved with advising the FSF commander and senior staff. The team leader is responsible for leading the team through the execution of battle drills. The team leader performs these internal functions: 

 - Leading all team operations, planning, and actions. 

 - Having responsibility for the deployment and redeployment readiness of the team. 

 - Having responsibility for the collective training of the team. 

 - Assessing the FSF capabilities. 

 - Approving all outgoing reports. 

 - Providing input for, and submits, the FSF training and readiness reports. 

 - Ensuring the establishment of requisition and tracking systems. 

 - Conducting operations in a safe manner through the integration of risk management (see ATP 5-19). 

 - • Accounting for team equipment. 

 - Educating self and team on the culture, region or country, history, and key figures in the OE. 

- 1-198. The team leader performs the following external functions: 

 - Advising the counterpart commander. 

 - Liaising with higher, adjacent, and subordinate units. 

 - Conducting cross-cultural negotiations with foreign counterparts. 

 - Supporting the FSF with capabilities. 

1-199. Generally, the team leader will organize the team to facilitate control and provide efficiency when working with the FSF. 

###### **Team Noncommissioned Officer in Charge (Assistant Team Leader)** 

1-200. The train and advise NCOIC, also called the assistant team leader, is the senior NCO on the advisor team. The assistant team leader is responsible for monitoring daily operations of the advisor team. Assistant team leaders fill the team leader role in the latter's absence. The assistant team leader performs the following internal functions: 

- Accounting for all personnel assigned to the team both administratively and for training. 

- Developing the foundational training cycle in accordance with SFAC's annual training guidance. 

- Assisting the team leader in collective training cycle in accordance with SFAC's annual training guidance. 

- Assisting the team leader with deployment/redeployment readiness requirements of the team. 

- Leading the team in the absence of the team leader. 

- Planning training to ensure the team's combat efficiency, discipline, administration, and welfare. 

- Reviewing the team's operations and training calendars. 

- Coordinating staff actions before they reach the team leader. 

- Directing the team's budget. 

- Managing and directing interpreters. 

- Writing outgoing reports and messages. 

- 1-201. The assistant team leader performs the following external functions: 

 - Representing the team in the absence of the team leader. 

 - Advising FSF leaders. 

 - Assisting the senior NCO counterpart with enforcing the commander's intent, maintaining discipline, and conducting precombat checks. 

 - Providing input for the FSF assessments. 

 - Reviewing training prior to execution. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

###### **Team Advisor (Combat Engineer Advisor)** 

1-202. The team advisor (more accurately called the combat engineer advisor) is responsible for providing mobility, countermobility, and survivability technical and tactical advice to the team leader. Combat engineer advisors provide the same advice to their foreign counterpart, which includes training the foreign engineer personnel when appropriate. The combat engineer advisor performs the following internal functions: 

 - Providing input for training evaluations with limitations/restrictions. 

 - Providing mobility, countermobility, and survivability technical and tactical advice upon completion of foreign disclosure review. 

 - Providing survivability support within their explosive ordnance clearance agent (EOCA) capability to mitigate blast and fragmentation danger areas of certain identified explosive ordnance. 

- 1-203. Combat engineer advisors perform the following external functions: 

 - Providing mobility, countermobility, and survivability technical and tactical advice to the FSF. 

 - Assisting coordination for external EOD assets when the explosive hazard advisor is not available. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

 - Supporting the FSF with capabilities as operational and mission variables allow. 

**_Note._** Engineer advisors are not organically filled positions on MATs. However, they are often used in place of an explosive hazard advisor. 

###### **Construction Engineer Advisor** 

1-204. The construction engineer advisor is responsible for advising FSF on vertical and horizontal construction projects management and construction-related training. The construction engineer advisor assists the FSF with revising, planning, resourcing, and executing construction projects. The construction engineer advisor also provides basic light and heavy equipment training. The construction engineer advisor performs the following internal functions: 

 - Assisting the team leader with advising FSF on their construction projects. 

 - Requesting status of equipment and materials for the foreign partner's construction projects. 

 - Providing team training on technical construction equipment and processes. 

 - Assisting the team leader to understand foreign partner construction requirements, and any climate or terrain-specific construction methods. 

 - Providing input for training evaluations. 

- 1-205. The construction engineer advisor performs the following external functions: 

 - Assisting with training, planning, resourcing, and execution of construction requirements. 

 - Training and assisting the FSF with light and heavy construction equipment. 

 - Supporting counterparts with planning and preparation of construction projects. 

 - Assisting the foreign forces in the execution of construction projects and training. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

###### **Technical Engineer Noncommissioned Officer (Advisor)** 

1-206. The technical engineer advisor is responsible for advising FSF on construction site development in areas such as technical investigation, surveys, drafts, and construction plans and specifications. They also advise FSF on conducting land surveys, map making, and preparing detailed plans for construction projects. The technical engineer advisor assists the FSF with project management. The technical engineer advisor performs the following internal functions: 

 - Requesting geodetic and construction surveys and results. 

 - Providing team training for survey and design work as well as construction site management. 

 - Assisting the team leader with technical designs. 

 - Providing input for training evaluations. 

- 1-207. The technical engineer advisor performs the following external functions: 

 - Assisting with training, planning, designing, resourcing, and executing construction projects. 

 - • Training and assisting the FSF with survey and design tools. 

 - Supporting counterparts with construction site development. 

 - Assisting the foreign forces in conducting land surveys, mapmaking, and preparing detailed plans for construction projects. 

 - Utilizing the United States Army Corps of Engineers Reachback Operations Center and other reachback capabilities for supplemental technical expertise. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

##### **LOGISTICS ADVISOR TEAM MEMBERS** 

1-208. The LAT enhances the SFAB's sustainment advising capability and capacity with the FSF. While several team members have the same or similar functions as described above, there are a few positions that are unique to LATs. The internal and external responsibilities of those positions are described in paragraphs 1-209 through 1-218. 

###### **Logistics Advisor Team Leader (Team Leader)** 

1-209. The LAT leader, or team leader, is directly involved with advising the FSF commander and senior staff. The team leader is responsible for leading the team through the execution of battle drills. The team leader performs the following internal functions: 

 - Leading all team operations, planning, and actions. 

 - Having responsibility for the deployment/redeployment readiness of the team. 

 - Having responsibility for the collective training of the team. 

 - Assessing the FSF capabilities. 

 - Approving all outgoing reports. 

 - Providing input for and submits the FSF training and readiness reports. 

 - Ensuring the establishment of requisition and tracking systems. 

 - Understanding, updating, and communicating the green LOGCOP. 

 - Conducting operations in a safe manner through the integration of risk management (see ATP 5-19). 

 - • Accounting for team equipment. 

 - Educating self and team on the culture, region or country, history, and key figures in the OE. 

- 1-210. The team leader performs the following external functions: 

 - Advising the counterpart commander. 

 - Liaising with higher, adjacent, and subordinate units. 

 - Conducting cross-cultural negotiations with the foreign counterparts. 

 - Supporting the FSF with capabilities. 

###### **Logistics Advisor Noncommissioned Officer in Charge (Assistant Team Leader)** 

1-211. The logistics advisor NCOIC, or assistant team leader, is the senior NCO on the advisor team. The assistant team leader is responsible for monitoring daily operations of the advisor team. Assistant team leaders assist fill the team leader role in the latter's absence. The assistant team leader performs the following internal functions:

 - Accounting for all personnel assigned to the team both administrative and for training. 

 - Overseeing the developing of the foundational training cycle in accordance with SFAC's annual training guidance. 

 - Assisting the team leader in the collective training cycle in accordance with SFAC's annual training guidance. 

 - Assisting the team leader with deployment/redeployment readiness requirements of the team. 

 - Leading the team in the absence of the team leader. 

 - Planning training to ensure the team's combat efficiency, discipline, administration, and welfare. 

 - • Reviewing the team's operations and training calendars. 

 - Coordinating staff actions before they reach the team leader. 

 - Directing the team's budget. 

 - Managing and directing interpreters. 

 - Writing outgoing reports and messages. 

 - Collaborating with team leader to maintain the green LOGCOP. 

- 1-212. The assistant team leader performs the following external functions: 

 - Representing the team in the absence of the team leader. 

 - Advising FSF leaders. 

 - Assisting the senior NCO counterpart with enforcing the commander's intent, maintaining discipline, and conducting precombat checks. 

 - Providing input for the FSF assessments. 

 - Reviewing training prior to execution. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

###### **Maintenance Advisor** 

1-213. The mechanic advisor, or maintenance advisor, is responsible for verifying faults, ordering parts, and conducting repairs on the team's materiel. Maintenance advisors assist the FSF with developing a maintenance SOP, establishing service schedules, ordering parts, and training their maintenance personnel. They serve as a support team member and report to the senior advisor for the support section. The maintenance advisor performs the following internal functions: 

 - Providing input for training evaluations. 

 - Providing limited field-level maintenance support and services for the advisor team vehicles and equipment, other than medical and communications equipment. 

 - Verifying faults, requesting Class IX (repair) parts, and maintaining maintenance records other than medical and communications equipment utilizing the advisor network. 

 - Collecting, processing, and coordinating for team maintenance requests through the advisor network. 

- 1-214. The maintenance advisor performs the following external functions: 

 - Assisting FSF with developing a maintenance program to include routine maintenance and service schedules, mechanic training, troubleshooting, and ordering parts. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

 - Supporting counterparts with capabilities as operational and mission variables allow. 

###### **Supply Advisor** 

> 1-215. Supply advisors are responsible for managing the team's sustainment and advising the FSF on issues that include property accountability, managing funds, and processing external support requests. They generally serve as a support team member and report to the senior advisor for the support section. The logistics advisor performs the following internal functions: 

- Advising the team leader on sustainment. 

- Collecting, processing, and coordinating for team sustainment support through the next higher headquarters. 

- Maintaining equipment accountability and conducting periodic inventories of team equipment. 

- Supporting the assistant team leader with managing team funds. 

- Assisting with tracking and monitoring contracts and deliverables. 

- Coordinating with the supporting contracting organization on service delivery. 

- Serving as the team armorer. 

- Providing input for training evaluations. 

- Maintaining and updating the green LOGCOP. 

1-216. The supply advisor performs the following external functions: 

- Advising the FSF on sustainment, specifically supply and transportation. 

- Assisting counterparts with establishing and maintaining a system to track their deceased Soldiers. 

- Advising the FSF on how to track and monitor contract deliveries. 

- Adhering to the principles, tactics, techniques, and procedures of effective cross-cultural communication, problem solving, and conflict resolution. 

- Supporting the counterparts with capabilities as operational and mission variables allow. 

- Updating daily logistics statistics utilizing the advisor network. 

- Informing the joint force on FSF combat power status. 

- Communicating details on the green LOGCOP as required. 

###### **Health Care Sergeant** 

1-217. The health care sergeants (also called medical advisors) are responsible for providing medical support to the advisor team and advising the FSF on triage procedures, first responder medical training, field sanitation, medical logistics, medical planning, and FHP. They serve as a support team member and generally report to the senior advisor for the support section. The medical advisor performs the following internal functions: 

- Providing input for training evaluations. 

- Assisting the advisor team leader with medical planning. 

- Maintaining principal responsibility to procedures, principles, and fundamentals of the following:  Point of injury care. 

 - Self-aid. 

 - Buddy-aid. 

 - Combat lifesaver. 

 - Tactical combat casualty care. 

- Training CASEVAC and MEDEVAC concepts from point of injury through continuum of care. 

- Assessing, conceives, and implements mass casualty concepts. 

- Gathering and disseminating information on known health threats within the AO. 

- Advising on medical capabilities of nearby assets. 

- Stabilizing patients and prepares them for MEDEVAC. 

- Training and assisting the team in managing field sanitation. 

- Maintaining Class VIII (medical) supply utilizing the advisor network. 

- Verifying faults. 

- Requesting Class VIII parts. 

- Maintaining maintenance records of team medical equipment. 

- 1-218. The medical advisor performs the following external functions: 

 - Training the FSF on the following: 

 - Rapid trauma assessment and trauma medicine. 

 - Point of injury care. 

 - CASEVAC. 

 - MEDEVAC. 

 - Mass casualty operations. 

 - Concepts of far-forward medical care. 

 - Maintaining a medical COP with the foreign partner that supports medical advising efforts. 

 - Stabilizing patients and preparing them for MEDEVAC. 

 - Advising counterparts on maintaining Class VIII supplies and equipment. 

 - Training and assisting the FSF on field sanitation, field hygiene, and personnel protective measures. 

 - Adhering to the principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures of— 

 - Effective cross-cultural communication. 

 - Problem solving. 

 - Conflict resolution. 

 - Supporting the FSF with capabilities as operational and mission variables allow. 

##### **Chapter 2** 

### **Security Force Assistance Brigade Operations** 

_The character of war has probably changed more in the last couple of years, and it’s a more complex environment, than certainly I’ve seen for 35 years._ 

General Randy George, 41st Chief of Staff of the Army 

Chapter 2 describes the SFAB role in multidomain operations across the competition continuum. 

##### **<mark>SECTION I – MULTIDOMAIN OPERATIONS AND SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE</mark>** 

2-1. This section introduces the framework of multidomain operations as established by FM 3-0. It also describes how SFABs and their advisor teams conduct operations as part of a multinational and multidomain force across the competition continuum. 

#### **MULTIDOMAIN OPERATIONS** 

2-2. Multidomain operations fracture the coherence of threat operational approaches by destroying, dislocating, isolating, and disintegrating their interdependent systems and formations, and exploiting the opportunities these disruptions provide to defeat the enemy in detail (see FM 3-0). SFABs support the joint force in four strategic roles: shaping operational environments, countering aggression on land during crisis, prevailing during LSCO, and consolidating gains. The Army fulfills its mission and clarifies the overall goals in multidomain operations with allies and partners (see FM 3-22). Figure 2-1 describes how SFABs conduct their activities of ASLA during multidomain operations across the competition continuum. 

**Figure 2-1. Security Force Assistance Brigade in the competition continuum** 

#### **SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE** 

2-3. _Security force assistance_ is the Department of Defense activities that support the development of the capability and capacity of foreign security forces and their supporting institutions (JP 3-20). FSF include both military and nondefense ministry forces and their supporting institutions. 

##### **SECURITY FORCE INSTITUTIONAL FUNCTIONS** 

2-4. Organizationally, a security force institution performs three functions: executive, generating, and operating (see FM 3-22). This construct enables SFA planners to identify the gaps in a partner nation’s capability and capacity within the executive (ministerial) function, the force generation function, or within the partner nation’s security forces (operating) function.

##### **SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE WITHIN THE EXECUTIVE FUNCTION** 

2-5. The executive function includes strategic direction that provides oversight, policy, and resourcing for FSF generating and operating functions. The executive function occurs at the MOD level generally and encompasses administrative efforts and processes and disseminates resources to direct and support the FSF.
