Executive functions also include formulating policy, advising political leaders, strategic planning and budgeting, assessing readiness, reviewing and analyzing current and future capability and capacity gaps, coordinating with other departments or agencies, and supervising intelligence activities. 

##### **SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE WITHIN THE GENERATING FUNCTION** 

2-6. The generating function occurs across the air, land, and maritime components to develop and sustain the capabilities of the operating force. SFA efforts assist the host nation by identifying, resourcing, and resolving capability gaps in doctrine, organization, training, materiel, leadership and education, personnel, facilities, and policy (DOTMLPF-P). 

##### **SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE WITHIN THE OPERATING FUNCTION** 

2-7. The FSF employ the dynamics of combat power during operations. _Combat power_ is the total means of destructive and disruptive force that a military unit/formation can apply against an enemy at a given time (JP 3-0). SFA enhances the skills of FSF to use this combat power. The dynamics of combat power are leadership, firepower, information, mobility, and survivability. All warfighting functions contribute to generating and applying combat power. The green COP is a tool used during operations to communicate FSF combat power disposition to relevant headquarters and mission partner elements. A _warfighting function_ is a group of tasks and systems united by a common purpose that commanders use to accomplish missions and training objectives (ADP 3-0). 

##### **<mark>SECTION II – SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE BRIGADE IN THE COMPETITION CONTINUUM</mark>** 

2-8. The combined force is never solely in one section of the competition continuum. Campaigns iterate through mixtures of competition, crisis, and armed conflict calculated to achieve the desired strategic objectives. (See figure 2-2.) Adversaries will contest the combined force in all domains of operations to include land, air, sea, space, and cyberspace. Adversaries will also contest advising. 

**Figure 2-2. Security Force Assistance Brigade during competition** 

#### **COMPETITION** 

2-9. During competition, Army forces deter malign adversary action, set conditions for armed conflict on favorable terms when deterrence fails, and shape an OE with allies and partners in ways that support U.S. strategic interests and policy aims. Persistent competitive advantage requires shifting competition into areas where the United States has enduring relative advantages over its adversaries, such that U.S. actions deter adversarial action, keep them on the defensive, or force them to take actions that are relatively costly or counterproductive in pursuit of their strategic goals. 

2-10. Strategic guidance calls for a renewal and strengthening of partner relationships to ensure national and global security. SFA activities provide the “ways” to accomplish this end state by nurturing current alliances, building new partnerships designed to increase partner capability and capacity, and enabling interoperability in joint, multidomain, and multinational operations. SFA activities enable the Army to protect and advance U.S. national interests, deter aggression, counter adversarial competitive strategies, and set the conditions for armed conflict if deterrence and competition fail. 

##### **REGIONAL ALIGNMENT** 

2-11. SFABs in competition are regionally aligned to a CCMD. The SFAB staff works in close cooperation with the CCMD and the theater ASCC to develop an employment plan to fit the theater campaign plan. 

2-12. SFABs maintain a persistent presence in their assigned theater through force package rotations. Typically, a force package consists of a task force combining a maneuver advisor battalion headquarters with an enabling advisor battalion headquarters. The composition of the teams in the force package are determined by the SFAB staff working in conjunction with the ASCC staff. 

2-13. Typically, force packages will rotate on a 24-month cycle. Task forces spend 8 months in an individual training cycle, followed by 8 months of collective training and mission-specific preparation, and 8 months employed in theater. 

##### **MISSION COMMAND** 

2-14. Multiple stakeholders contribute to the mission intent of an advisor team. The weight of each stakeholder changes as the situation develops from competition to crisis and possibly to conflict. Stakeholders include ASCC, country offices of defense co-operations, regionally aligned forces, the SFAB itself, and partner FSF. In competition, an advisor team derives its commander’s intent largely from the offices of defense co-operations for the country it is assigned to in partnership with the SFAB command. As the situation develops into crisis and conflict, the SFAB commander or ASCC commander may assign command relationships between advisor teams and other U.S. Forces or allied forces. Leaders for any specific mission must balance the intent of the various stakeholders when making decisions. 

##### **GAINING AND PROVIDING IMPROVED SITUATIONAL AWARENESS AND UNDERSTANDING OF THE OPERATING ENVIRONMENT** 

2-15. SFAB teams expand situational awareness of the Army in their AOR through their presence and access to key land, areas, and populations. SFAB team's presence assures mission partners, enables interoperability and agility of the MNF, and enhances cooperation throughout their AOR. Establishing effective liaison with multinational partners through embedded teams, collaborative systems, and leader contact is critical to establishing a COP and maintaining situational understanding (see FM 3-0). SFABs maintain the green COP to provide a vital and accurate picture of friendly forces in their AOR. 

2-16. By consistently conducting and writing assessments of their partnered force, SFABs enable all echelons of Army leaders in a theater to understand the capabilities of FSF at every level. This is vital to inform the CCMD campaign plan. 

##### **BUILD PARTNER CAPABILITY AND CAPACITY** 

2-17. SFA consists of those SC activities tied directly to the capability and capacity of a partner FSF. Army SFA activities develop the capability and capacity of partner nation security forces to defend against internal and external security threats, contribute to and enable multinational and multidomain operations and interoperability, and assist other countries to provide for their own security. Capability is the ability to complete a task or execute a course of action under specified conditions and levels of performance (outputs), while capacity is the measurable aspect of capability and describes the scale and duration to which a task can be performed (outcomes). See FM 3-22 for more information on outputs and outcomes.

2-18. Developing partner capability is not an end state but rather an interim step toward building partner capacity. Developing partner FSF capabilities can affect a region’s balance of power, and building a partner FSF capacity to permanently maintain that power may or may not be a desired strategic end state. For example, a partner FSF trained to defeat an insurgency, once defeated, may turn on a bordering neighbor or launch a military coup to overthrow the existing government. Therefore, depending on U.S. strategic objectives, SFA activities may be planned and executed to provide a temporary or limited enhancement of partner FSF capability in the near term, a more involved institutional program that builds enduring capacity over the long term, or a hybrid combination of both. In any scenario, SFA planners and practitioners should have a clear and thorough understanding of strategic guidance and intent, partner nation requirements, and U.S. legal authorities for developmental activities. (see FM 3-22). Advisor teams deploy under various SC authorities (see figure 2-3 for examples of Title 10 SC authorities). 

**Figure 2-3. Security cooperation authorities** 

2-19. Activities that SFABs conduct to build partner capability and capacity in competition include the following: 

- Training and developing FSF leaders. 

- Assisting FSF to develop doctrine and training methods. 

- Developing integrating processes in partner staffs. 

- Supporting the FSF in developing tactics, techniques, and procedures. 

##### **SFA in South America – Bigger is not always better** 

<mark>The US counterinsurgency effort, to be effective, must have security, advisory, and training assistance experts who can assess the situation, advise the host country forces on the proper counterinsurgency techniques and training, and equip those forces to do the job.</mark> 

<mark>In the 1960s, with our help, most guerilla movements in the region were effectively neutralized by Latin-American armies….In 1967, for example, a 15-man Special Forces mobile training team trained the Bolivian Rangers that made short work of Ernesto</mark> “ <mark>Che</mark> ” <mark>Guevara</mark> ’ <mark>s adventure.</mark> 

<mark>Colonel John D. Waghelstein</mark> Military Review 

##### **ACHIEVING INTEROPERABILITY** 

2-20. The success of allied forces relies on their ability to work together effectively and efficiently, mitigating mission command complexities and shortfalls. Interoperability priorities include Army, Joint, interorganizational, and multinational cooperation. Allied joint operations must be planned and executed to optimize the unique strengths of each nation's forces. The integration and collaboration of participating countries' forces at the operational level are crucial for achieving consistency across stakeholder efforts within the theater and country objectives of the CCMD and the COM’s integrated country strategies (see AR 34-1). (See AJP-01 [Edition F, Version 1]; Center for Army Lessons Learned interoperability reference guides No. 16-18, and No. 20-12 for more information.) 

2-21. Army forces conduct operations in support of joint campaigns which (for the most part) occur as a part of a larger coalition operation. They must employ and leverage the capability and capacity of mission partners in any future conflict and across the competition continuum which requires an interest in interoperability. However, the mission partners' objectives will influence the desired level of collaboration and collective interoperability for specific multinational operations. (See FM 3-16 for more on multinational operations.) 

###### **Exchange of Information** 

2-22. The use and exchange of technology, information, tactics, techniques and procedures, and the employment of support and liaison elements during crisis and conflict can enhance interoperability to enable the integration of lethal and nonlethal effects between allies and partners. The Army recognizes four levels of standardized interoperability with UAPs as depicted in Table 2-1 on page 54, and the levels are described in more detail in AR 34-1 and DA Pam 11-31. 

**Table 2 1. Levels of interoperability** 

|**_Level_**|**_Description_**|
|---|---|
|0-Not interoperable|UAPs have no demonstrated interoperability.<br>Command and control interface with the Army is only<br>at the next higher echelon. UAP formations must<br>operate independently from U.S. Army formations<br>and operations.|
|1-Deconflicted|U.S. Army and UAPs can co-exist but do not interact.<br>Requires alignment of capabilities and procedures to<br>establish operational norms, enabling UAPS and the<br>U.S. Army to work together.|
|2-Compatible|U.S. Army and UAPs can interact with each other in<br>the same geographic area in pursuit of a common<br>goal. U.S. Army and UAPs have similar or<br>complementary processes and procedures, and they<br>can operate effectivelywith each other.|
|3-Integrated<br>Legend: UAP—unified action partner;|U.S. Army and UAPs can integrate upon arrival in<br>theater. Interoperability is network-enabled to provide<br>the full range of military operations capability. UAPs<br>can routinely establish networks and operate<br>effectivelywith or aspart of U.S. Armyformations.<br>U.S.–United States|

###### **Three Dimensions of Interoperability** 

2-23. SFABs recognize three dimensions of interoperability: human, procedural, and technical (see FM 3-16). SFAB teams enable interoperability across each. 

###### **_Human_** 

2-24. The human dimension relates to the mutual understanding of culture, values, language and perspectives. SFAB teams can enable gaining understanding of shared experiences, education, and training as partners. Such a force is better attuned to operate within a dynamic technological and information environment. 

###### **_Procedural_** 

2-25. The procedural dimension relates to the simplicity and standardization of concepts, doctrine, organizations, policies and procedures. Simplicity and standardization increase the ability to C2 and harness the full resources of the organization. To achieve interoperability, forces gain commonality through application of common doctrine and procedures (SOPs). Minimally wherever possible, C2 data, and information and knowledge management procedures must be interchangeable. 

###### **_Technical_** 

2-26. The technical dimension relates to the interoperability of hardware, software and systems. Technical interoperability enhances the effectiveness of the force elements and sets conditions for interchangeability requirements. Interoperability requires forces that are technically capable of aggregating and applying power with partners. Minimally, technical capability must exist to develop communications architecture to support primary, alternate, contingency and emergency plans interchangeable with U.S. systems. If a partner does not have equipment to be interchangeable with the United States, SFAB teams are equipped to transition information from partner systems to U.S. systems. 

##### **TRANSITIONING TO CRISIS** 

2-27. Current host-nation and partner FSF capability and capacity assessment data are integral to the planning process, especially if there is a transition to crisis. These assessments may include quantitative and qualitative performance measures and estimates based on assessing, monitoring, and evaluating best practices. Key partner FSF entity variables may include the force’s purpose, structure, their will to fight, strengths and weaknesses by warfighting function and domain. This information builds the green COP.

2-28. During competition, SFAB force packages typically operate on specific Title 10 authorities section codified in the CCMD’s theater SC mission (for example, section 311, section 321, section 332). Should competition transition into crisis and a change of mission be warranted, an authorities change will occur through the CCMD’s theater SC office. This change will likely appear in the form of a specific execution order communicated by the Secretary of Defense (SecDef) through the appropriate CCMD. 

2-29. Typically, crisis response and limited contingency operations are focused on scope and scale and conducted to achieve a specific strategic or operational level objective in an operational area (see FM 3-0). In figure 2-4 on page 56, during a crisis, SFAB units increase the ratio of support and liaison activities, making them proportionately even with the amount of advising they do. In these limited contingency operations, SFAB units may accompany a partner force in operations. 

##### **Advisors in Korea—Counter-Guerrilla Operations** 

<mark>From KMAG’s [Korean Military Advisory Group] perspective in mid-1949, the primary reason for ROKA’s [Republic of Korea Army] failure to train was the need to counter increased (both in quality and quantity) guerrilla activity in the latter half of 1949. Unfortunately, the pressing issue was how to tame the guerrilla problem before sustained operations wore the army down to impotence. For most ROK [Republic of Korea] units, antiguerrilla and counter-infiltration operations had displaced formal training. This condition became so normal that KMAG attempted to make a virtue out of necessity. Based on the ROKA’s combat performance to date, Roberts (Brigadier. General. William L. Roberts, Chief of KMAG from August 1948-June 1950) realized that the crisis moment had arrived. He pushed additional resources (mainly advisors) down to the battalion level with an increased focus on specific assistance to counterguerrilla operations. Under Roberts’s advice, the ROKA established specialized task forces to deal with guerrillas in the Chiri-san and Taebaek-san regions; a similar tasks force was organized on the Ongjin peninsula with a specially designated KMAG detachment assigned to it. In this way, Roberts hoped to keep his finger on the pulse of the ROKA by getting a continuous stream of filed reports of ongoing combat operations and any training opportunities that arose. Perhaps even more significant for the long-term health of the ROKA, these initiatives, for the first time, put the advisory effort on a clearly directed focus to observe, advise, and report. The advisors were also largely successful in helping the ROKA to subdue guerrillas and hold its own against North Korean security forces that became increasingly aggressive along the 38th parallel. If Roberts perhaps went overboard with his public statements about the ROKA’s progress, at least he had reason to be proud of both KMAG and the ROKA. By March 1950, they had suppressed the guerrilla movement and defeated North Korean infiltrations and attacks along the 38th parallel boundary.</mark> 

<mark>Bryan R. Gibby</mark> 

The Will to Win: American military advisors in Korea, 1946-1953 **Figure 2-4. Security Force Assistance Brigade during crisis**

#### **CRISIS** 

2-30. A _crisis_ is an emerging incident or situation involving a possible threat to the United States, its citizens, military forces, or vital interests that develops rapidly and creates a condition of such diplomatic, economic, or military importance that commitment of military forces and resources is contemplated to achieve national and/or strategic objectives. (JP 3-0). A crisis can be long in duration, but it can also reflect a nearsimultaneous and rapid transition to armed conflict if deterrence fails (see FM 3-22). 

##### **ACTIONS DURING CRISIS** 

2-31. The strategies used for SFA while in crisis will vary depending on the circumstances, but successful action will have distinct traits. The first step is to have a thorough understanding of how relevant actors will view or respond to an SFA action. Second, the SFAB and partner FSF should carry out a wide range of tasks, such as establishing access to critical areas, forward positioning of forces, establishing an appropriate and timely presence, organizing exercises, sharing intelligence, continuous operational preparation of the environment, and conducting information operations to counter and undermine the adversarial narrative. Third, the SFAB and the partner FSF should ensure that these tasks can be carried out in a creative and flexible manner and are adaptable to changes in the strategic environment. Finally, the SFAB should conduct continuous assessment and evaluation of adversarial capabilities and intent. 

2-32. The collective military capability and capacity generated during competition sets the conditions and provides the leverage to win in crisis or resolve the situation with conditions favorable to the United States and its allies and partners. In crisis, SFA enables the joint and MNF to present credible and integrated deterrent options that demonstrate the ability to impose costs on malign action. During crisis, SFA personnel may provide unique capabilities to advance partnerships, influence adversarial behavior, and support partner FSF and defense ministry crisis response operations (see FM 3-22). 

2-33. It is possible that at the emergence of a crisis, SFAB elements may already be on ground in the AOR and thus, represent an inside force for the CCMD and ASCC to assist in setting the theater. For instance, the forward deployed SFAB force packages could already be inside an adversary’s antiaccess area denial which can both mitigate the risk to the mission presented by the adversary’s antiaccess area denial assets, but also increase the risk to the force in terms of the advisors themselves. 

2-34. The following are additional critical questions and concepts for SFABs to anticipate when possibly transitioning to crisis: 

- What is the plan and status of weapons and equipment? 

 - Are weapons/ammunition/communications equipment pre-positioned? 

 - What are the considerations for doing so? 

 - What if the antiaccess area denial approach prevents further equipment inflow? 

- If the antiaccess area denial approach cuts off the force, are sufficient ACSAs in place to draw supplies/equipment from the host nation? 

- How does the SFAB mitigate risk to the advisor force while in transition? 

- What needs to be done during competition to set conditions for success if there is a transition? 

- What role does the embassy see for the SFAB? 

 - Will the SFABs be considered noncombatants being evacuated? 

 - Do they use their organic communications to assist in manning collection points? 

 - Do they coordinate with FSF to ensure outer security of collections point? 

 - What should be done during competition to facilitate this? 

 - Do they know the noncombatant evacuation coordinator at the embassy? 

- What advantage can the SFABs provide in intelligence gathering and dissemination? 

 - What is the role for the SFAB in providing ground truth to the CCMD and intelligence community during a crisis? 

 - What is the role for the SFAB to disseminate information from the CCMD and intelligence community to the host nation? 

- What is the SFAB’s role if the crisis is a natural disaster? 

##### **TRANSITIONING TO CONFLICT** 

2-35. During crisis response, the SFAB may or may not be involved in FSF tactical operations. It is critical that if the U.S. Army’s response transitions from crisis to conflict, SFAB task forces manage that transition by providing U.S. commanders with timely and accurate information of on-the-ground actions and assessments of FSF units they may operate alongside. A suitable adjustment of legal authorities will be required. 

2-36. SFAB personnel deployed under SC authorities cannot conduct combat operations unless authorized to do so by Congress or the President of the United States. This authorization is the mechanism by which the legal authorities are changed. Regardless of the legal authorities’ issue, Soldiers in any and every capacity always retain the right to self-defense. 

2-37. As in the transition to crisis, the transition to conflict will likely take the form of a specific execution order communicated by SecDef through the appropriate CCMD. This execution order will operationalize the theater SC section of a theater campaign plan. 

2-38. In most cases, the CCMD will form a JTF as authorized by SecDef to address the crisis. The SFAB may be TACON to the JTF for the purpose of integrating the FSF into the wider command structure thru the liaising or supporting of FSF. They may also be OPCON to the coalition forces land component commander (CFLCC) to execute coordination and synchronization of the FSF for direct action. 

2-39. In all phases, but especially in a transition to conflict, advisors must ensure that information pertaining to the green COP is communicated to, and understood by, the JTF, CFLCC, or whatever command authority is providing direction. SFABs improve the shared understanding and enhance the visualization of these command authorities by pushing up the green COP, rather than requiring the command to pull it. 

2-40. During LSCO, SFAB teams may engage in all the activities previously listed during competition and crisis. As figure 2-5 on page 58 shows, however, the balance of SFAB activities becomes more heavily involved in liaise and support functions. (See chapter 4 for more discussion of how SFAB units conduct liaise and support during LSCO.) 

#### **ARMED CONFLICT** 

2-41. Armed conflict occurs when a state or non-state actor uses lethal force as the primary means to satisfy its interests. The emergence of armed conflict can be an extensive and comprehensive effort in terms of scale, scope, and totality. In conflict, success depends on the effectiveness of cooperative and competitive activities (see JP 3-0). LSCO are at the far right of the competition continuum and associated with outright war. Battlefields in LSCO have historically been more chaotic, intense, and highly destructive than those the Army experienced in the 2000s and 2010s. 

**Figure 2-5. Security Force Assistance Brigade during armed conflict** 

##### **LARGE-SCALE COMBAT OPERATIONS** 

2-42. SFABs in LSCO may support and liaise with the partner FSF to converge joint and multinational effects to achieve operational objectives. SFAB teams provide unit commanders with more options to exploit both strategic and operational indirect approaches in a globally integrated operation against a peer adversary. SFABs can also enable and leverage partner FSF capability and capacity to strike in-depth with lethal and nonlethal cross-domain effects to create overmatch (see FM 3-22). 

2-43. SFAB teams may collocate or embed with the partner FSF to support and enable synchronization of activities, fill key capability gaps, and help to ensure unity of purpose and mutual support during combat operations. 

##### **The Security Force Assistance Brigade in Large-Scale Combat<mark>Operations: Liaison During Forward Passage of Lines</mark>** 

<mark>The 4th SFAB, consisting of a brigade headquarters, two Infantry maneuver, one Cavalry maneuver, a field artillery advising battalion, an engineer advising battalion, and a sustainment advising battalion are conducting an advising mission with partner forces in their geographic CCMD’s AOR (see organizational chart in figure 2.6). At the present time, and given increasing regional tensions, one advisor task force is physically present in the AOR. The advisor task force on the ground consists of the brigade advisor team, one MBAT, one enabler BAT, and 12 subordinate advisor teams.</mark> 

<!-- Start of picture text -->
subordinate advisor teams.<br>Figure 2-6. Passage of lines organizational chart<br>The partner force, the 1st Atropian Armored Division, is in established defensive positions within<br>Atropia based on provocations from their Donovian neighbor. During the advising mission, a<br>crisis occurs resulting in a decision to forward deploy U.S. Army conventional forces to Atropia.<br>The 4th Infantry Division subsequently deploys under a MNF with the 3rd Armored Brigade<br>Combat Team (ABCT), 4th Infantry Division (3/4 ABCT) pushed forward into the AO in support<br>of the defense of Atropia. Donovian forces cross the border into Atropia, resulting in continuous<br>contact with the 1st Atropian Armored Division’s defensive line. The defensive line holds with<br>considerable losses on both sides.<br>The multinational force land component commander (MNFLCC), in the attempt to seize the<br>moment of relative advantage makes the decision to conduct a forward passage of lines with 3/4<br>ABCT through the 1st Atropian Armored Division’s defensive positions. To do this, the MNFLCC<br>directs the already present elements of the 4th SFAB to act as liaisons to facilitate the passage<br>of lines.<br><!-- End of picture text -->

##### **The Security Force Assistance Brigade in Large-Scale Combat<mark>Operations: Liaison During Forward Passage of Lines (continued)</mark>** 

<mark>Upon receiving the mission, the brigade advisor team and MBAT identify internal advisor team members with the relevant experience, skill sets, and doctrinal knowledge to execute the mission. After ensuring the MNFLCC’s guidance is understood, advisor teams then re-task organize as identified by the brigade advisor team and MBAT to accomplish the mission. Utilizing advising guidance of “accompany and enable” allows for clear and precise execution of the forward passage of lines. Advisors then provide information and recommendations on the specific FSF partner units within the 1st Atropian Armored Division to execute the stationary unit responsibilities as well as building points of contact within 3/4 ABCT. (While the primary responsibility for planning and execution remains between the Atropians and 3/4 ABCT, the SFAB experience and knowledge of the FSF acts as the glue to bind together conventional U.S. units with an unfamiliar partner.</mark> 

<mark>With the exact units identified for the operation, the advisor teams will commence building shared understanding of which technique will be utilized between the stationary and passing units. Advisors will ensure that the stationary FSF units understand the assembly area location of 3/4 ABCT. Once established, the FSF will need to understand where the passage point will be executed, route management, contact points, the estimated transit time, and the near and far recognition signals. Advisors will also assess and relay the combat support and combat service support assets available from the FSF which could support the passing unit in an emergency. For the passing units, advisors will relay information that pertains to partner force defensive arrayment. They will then share their partner’s internal capabilities and assessments of the enemy situation in the close fight. Advisors will also ensure 3/4 ABCT understands the FSFs terrain observations, ensure shared understanding of the location for the battle handover line, ensure all parties clearly understand the responsibilities once battle handover has occurred, inform on obstacle or contaminated areas, confirm the routes through obstacles, the lane marking systems, and the accuracy of 3/4 ABCT’s overlays. They will also propose alternate routes and articulate stationary unit traffic control procedures.</mark> 

<mark>At the completion of the planning stage, the advisor teams continue with their liaise task and accompany and enable advising approach through the execution of the passage of lines. Advisors embed at the points of greatest friction to ensure redundancy in the FSF partner manning of passage lanes and contact points, confirm the lane is open, confirm the number of vehicles by type, ensure the FSF displays recognition signal, and assist as needed. Advisors moving with 3/4 ABCT provide redundancy with the two units making contact and display of the recognition symbols, confirming arrival at correct passage lanes, reporting progress through passage lanes, and confirming for FSF once the 3/4 ABCT passage is complete. Of particular concern will be to ensure that the FSF provide covering fires to the greatest extent possible to support the 3/4 ABCT passage while ensuring the blue (U.S.) and green (FSF) COPs are understood by all to reduce the risk of fratricide.</mark> 

<mark>With the completion of the passage of lines, advisor teams confirm that all restrictions placed on 3/4 ABCT while passing through the FSF are lifted and ensure the FSF understand this change. The advisor teams then stand down from the more robust liaising task as well as the accompany and support posture required by this specific mission and return to the advising posture they</mark> were in before the mission <mark>.</mark> 

<mark>(See FM 3-98 and ATP 3-20.98, chapter 3, paragraph 3-133 for more on passage of lines</mark> operations.) 

##### **BUILD AND MAINTAIN SITUATIONAL AWARENESS** 

2-44. Success in operations demands timely and effective decisions based on applying judgment to available information and knowledge. As such, commanders, staffs, and advisor teams seek to build and maintain situational understanding (in the form of the blue and green COP) throughout the operations process. _Situational understandin_ g is the product of applying analysis and judgment to relevant information to determine the relationships among the operational and mission variables (ADP 6-0). Commanders, staffs, and advisors continually strive to maintain their situational understanding and work through periods of reduced understanding as a situation evolves. Effective commanders accept that uncertainty can never be eliminated and train their staffs and advisors to function in uncertain environments. 

2-45. As commanders, staffs, and advisors build their situational understanding, they share their understanding across the force and with UAPs. Creating shared understanding is a principle of mission command and requires communication and information sharing from higher to lower and lower to higher. Higher headquarters ensures subordinates understand the larger situation to include the operation’s end state, purpose, and objectives. Staffs and advisor teams from lower echelons share their understanding of their situation and provide feedback to the higher headquarters on the operation’s progress. Communication and information sharing with adjacent units and UAPs is also multidirectional. Several tools assist leaders in building situational understanding and creating a shared understanding across the force to include the following: 

- Operational and mission variables. 

- DOTMLPF-P analysis. 

- Running estimates. 

- Collaboration. 

###### **Mission Variables and Operational Variables** 

2-46. Mission and operational variables are categories of relevant information commanders and staffs use to help build their situational understanding. For an advisor team, the mission variables (see table 2-2 on page 61 and page 62) and operational variables (see table 2-3 on page 63) are used to understand the country or region in which it is assigned. 

2-47. Commanders, staffs, and advisor teams use METT-TC (I) to help understand the mission environment. _Mission variables_ are categories of specific information needed to conduct operations (ADP 1-01). 

2-48. Commanders, staffs, and advisor teams use the eight interrelated operational variables—political, military, economic, social, information, infrastructure, physical environment, and time (PMESII-PT)—to help understand an OE. Operational variables are those aspects of an OE, both military and nonmilitary, that may differ from one operational area to another and affect operations. (See table 2-3 on page 63). 

**Table 2-2. The mission variables** 

|**_METT-TC(I) Variable_**|**_Description_**|
|---|---|
|Mission|Commanders and staffs view all the mission variables in<br>terms of their impact on mission accomplishment. The<br>mission is the task, together with the purpose, that clearly<br>indicates the action to be taken and the reason. It is always<br>the first variable commanders consider during decision<br>making. A mission statement contains the “who, what,<br>when,where,and why” of the operation.|
|Enemy|The second variable to consider is the enemy’s dispositions<br>(including organization, strength, location, and tactical<br>mobility), doctrine, equipment, capabilities, vulnerabilities,<br>andprobable courses of action.|
|Terrain and weather|Terrain and weather analysis are inseparable and directly<br>influence each other’s impact on military operations. Terrain<br>includes natural features (such as rivers and mountains)<br>and man-made features (such as cities, airfields, and<br>bridges). Commanders analyze terrain using the five military<br>aspects of terrain that include: observation and fields of fire,<br>avenues of approach, key terrain, obstacles and cover and<br>concealment (OAKOC). The military aspects of weather<br>include visibility, wind, precipitation, cloud cover,<br>temperature,and humidity.| **Table 2-2. The mission variables (continued)**

|**_METT-TC(I) Variable_**|**_Description_**|
|---|---|
|Troops and support available|This variable includes the number, type, capabilities, and<br>condition of available friendly troops and support. These include<br>supplies, services, and support available from joint partners,<br>host-nation partners, and unified action partners. It also includes<br>support from civilians and contractors employed by military<br>organizations, such as the Defense Logistics Agency and the<br>ArmyMateriel Command.|
|Time available|Commanders assess the time available for planning, preparing,<br>and executing tasks and operations. This includes the time<br>required to assemble, deploy, and maneuver units in relationship<br>to the enemyand conditions.|
|Civil considerations|Civil considerations are the influence of man-made<br>infrastructure, civilian institutions, and attitudes and activities of<br>the civilian leaders, populations, and organizations within an<br>area of operations on the conduct of military operations. Civil<br>considerations comprise six characteristics including areas,<br>structures, capabilities, organizations, people, and events<br>(ASCOPE).|
|Informational considerations|Informational considerations reflect that all operations and<br>missions are conducted in the context of the information<br>environment. Commanders assess how their operations will be<br>perceived or could be perceived and how that perception affects<br>the information narrative.|

2-49. Upon receipt of a mission, commanders and staffs filter information categorized by the operational variables into relevant information with respect to the mission. They use the mission variables, in combination with the operational variables, to refine their situational understanding and to visualize, describe, and direct operations. Commanders, staffs, and advisors view all the operational and mission variables in terms of their impact on mission accomplishment. 

2-50. Culture, as part of the social operational variable, is particularly important to the mission of advisors. Commanders and advisors consider how culture (their own, their counterparts,’ and the local population) affects operations when building situational understanding and conducting operations. Cultural understanding is paramount to the SFAB’s success. Culture is composed of shared beliefs, values, norms, customs, behaviors, and artifacts members of a society use to cope with the world and each other. Advisor teams must consider how culture, and in particular gender dynamics, influences their actions and the actions of their counterparts and the local population. Making decisions through a strictly U.S. Army viewpoint may inhibit mission success. Understanding the culture of other UAPs is also crucial to building mutual trust and shared understanding (see table 2-3 on page 63). 

**Table 2-3. The operational variables** 

|**_PMESII-PT Categories_**|**_Sample Considerations_**|
|---|---|
|Political|How do the political leaders influence the military? How do the area’s<br>leaders control the population? How widespread is government support<br>among the people? Was the government democratically elected? What<br>are the current domesticpolitical issues?|
|Military|What is the current status of military facilities and equipment to train with<br>the FSF? What foreign weapon systems do they employ? What is the<br>frequency, proximity, and intensity of the OE in which I am partnering<br>with the FSF?|

Will partner forces or host-nation stakeholders feel rushed? Are<br>there anykeydates or events within the AO?<br>ations; ASLA—assess, support, liaise, and advise; FSF—foreign security<br>vironment;OTEBA—organize,train,equip,build,and advise|

###### **The Army Capabilities Based Assessment Framework** 

2-51. DOTMLPF-P is a framework for analysis used by the U.S. Army for assessing itself. It can also be used to assess FSF partners. Advisors can use this framework to create shared understanding of how the FSF operates. This analysis includes DOTMLPF-P governing the partner unit. Understanding these elements guide how advisors may exert influence in their partner (see table 2-4 on page 64). 

**Table 2-4. The Army capabilities-based assessment framework** 

|**_Category_**|**_Defined_**|
|---|---|
|Doctrine|The way U.S. Forces and partners fight (for example,<br>emphasizing maneuver warfare, combined air-ground<br>campaigns).|
|Organization|How U.S. Forces and partners organize to fight, such<br>as divisions,air wings,Marine air-ground task forces.|
|Training|How U.S. Forces and partners prepare to fight<br>tactically (basic training to advanced individual<br>training,unit training, joint exercises).|
|Materiel|All the items necessary to equip U.S. Forces or allies<br>and partners that_does_or_does not_require a new<br>development effort (for example, weapons, spares,<br>test sets that are “off the shelf” both commercially<br>and within thegovernment).|
|Leadership and Education|How U.S. Forces’ leaders and FSF counterparts are<br>prepared to lead the fight (squad leader to four-star<br>general/admiral—professional development).|
|Personnel|Availability of qualified personnel for peacetime,<br>wartime,and various contingencyoperations.|
|Facilities|Real property, installations, and industrial facilities<br>(such as government-owned ammunition production<br>facilities).|
|Policy<br>Legend: DOD—Department of Defense;FSF—fore|DOD, interagency, or international policy that impacts<br>the other seven non-materiel elements.<br>ign securityforces;U.S.—United States|

###### **Running Estimate** 

2-52. A _running estimate_ is the continuous assessment of the current situation used to determine if the current operation is proceeding according to the commander’s intent and if planned future operations are supportable (ADP 5-0). Running estimates assist commanders and staffs with understanding situations, assessing progress, and making decisions throughout an operation. Effective plans and successful execution hinge on current and accurate running estimates. 

2-53. Each staff section and advisor should maintain a running estimate within their specified area of expertise and advisor role. When building and maintaining a running estimate, staff sections and advisors monitor current operations and continuously consider the following in context of the operations: 

- Facts. 

- Assumptions. 

- Friendly status including location, activity, and combat power of subordinate units and FSF. 

- Enemy status including composition, disposition, and strength. 

- Civil considerations. 

- Conclusions and recommendations. 

2-54. Running estimates cover essential facts and assumptions, including a summary of the current situation. Running estimates always include recommendations for anticipated decisions. During planning, commanders and advisor team leaders use these recommendations to select valid (feasible, acceptable, suitable, distinguishable, and complete) courses of action for further analysis. During preparation and execution, leaders use recommendations from running estimates to inform their decision making. 

2-55. Advisors use running estimates primarily as a tool to understand themselves (blue COP) and their partners (green COP). Advisors maintain running estimates for their warfighting function to know what assets are available to their current mission among all organizations with whom they interact. 

###### **Collaboration** 

2-56. Commanders, staffs, and advisors actively build and maintain shared understanding within the force and with UAPs by continually collaborating throughout the operations process. Collaboration is more than coordination. It is multiple people and organizations working together towards a common goal by sharing knowledge and building consensus. It requires dialogue that involves a candid exchange of ideas or opinions among participants and encourages frank discussions in areas of disagreement. Throughout the operations process, commanders, advisors, staffs, and UAPs collaborate, share and question information, perceptions, and ideas to understand situations and make decisions. 

2-57. Throughout the planning process, commanders, staffs, advisor teams, FSF counterparts, and other UAPs actively collaborate to understand the situation and make decisions. When possible, they conduct collaborative planning during mission analysis, course of action development, and wargaming to resolve conflicts before issuing the operation order. Through collaborative participation, they improve situational understanding and develop common priorities and objectives upon which to build future efforts and mutual trust. 

#### **INTEGRATING PROCESS** 

2-58. Throughout the operations process, commanders, staffs, and advisor teams integrate the warfighting functions to synchronize the force according to the commander’s intent and concept of operations. Commanders, staffs, and advisors use several integrating processes and continuing activities to do this. The integrating processes are the following: 

- IPOE. 

- Information collection. 

- Targeting. 

- Risk management. 

- Knowledge management. 

##### **INTELLIGENCE PREPARATION OF THE OPERATIONAL ENVIRONMENT** 

2-59. _Intelligence preparation of the operational environment_ is the systematic process of analyzing the mission variables of enemy, terrain, weather, and civil considerations in an area of interest to determine their effect on operations (FM 2-0). Led by the intelligence officer or advisor, the entire staff or team participates in IPOE to develop and sustain an understanding of the enemy, terrain and weather, and civil considerations. IPOE helps identify options available to friendly and threat forces. 

2-60. IPOE supports all activities of the SFAB operations process. IPOE identifies gaps in current intelligence. Due to their small size relative to other conventional organizations, SFAB intelligence sections may have difficulty processing large amounts of information into intelligence and may require reachback capabilities. IPOE products help commanders, staffs, and advisor teams understand the threat, physical environment, and civil considerations throughout the operations process. (See chapter 5 for more information regarding augmentation.) 

2-61. SFAB staffs and advisor teams conduct IPOE, internally and with their foreign counterparts. Bringing the FSF into the IPOE process can provide both training to their commanders and staffs as well as building trust and shared understanding. Staff and advisors must adhere to the appropriate procedures and SOPs regarding the classification of material shared with foreign counterparts. If necessary, alter the specific inputs and outputs associated with the IPOE based on information-sharing constraints. 

##### **INFORMATION COLLECTION** 

2-62. Information _collection_ is an activity that synchronizes and integrates the planning and employment of sensors and assets as well as the processing, exploitation, and dissemination systems in direct support of current and future operations (FM 3-55). It integrates the functions of the intelligence and operations staffs that focus on answering CCIRs. Information collection includes acquiring information and providing it to processing elements. 

It has three steps that include the following: 

- Collection management. 

- Task and direct collection. 

- Execute collection. 

2-63. The SFAB conducts information collection activities in conjunction with their counterpart FSF. Information collection includes information relating to the FSF themselves, to include staffing, equipping, and disposition. This information provides inputs to SFA mission analysis and assessments. Advisors must also maintain an awareness of the FSF operational norms to identify environmental changes that may portend increased risk to mission and force. The intelligence officer develops indicators, which assist teams in identifying and reporting potential internal FSF threats. 

2-64. Information collection helps the commander and advisors understand and visualize the operation by identifying gaps in information and aligning reconnaissance, surveillance, security, and intelligence assets to collect information on those gaps. The “decide” and “detect” steps in paragraphs 2-65 through 2-67 on targeting tie heavily to information collection. (See FM 3-55 for a detailed discussion of information collection to include the relationship between the duties of intelligence and operations staffs.) 

##### **TARGETING** 

2-65. _Targeting_ is the process of selecting and prioritizing targets and matching the appropriate response to them, considering operational requirements and capabilities (JP 3-0). Targeting seeks to create specific desired effects through lethal and nonlethal actions. The emphasis of targeting is on identifying enemy resources (targets) that if destroyed or degraded will contribute to the success of the friendly mission. Targeting begins in planning and continues throughout the operations process. The steps of the Army’s targeting process are— 

- Decide. 

- Detect. 

- Deliver. 

- Assess. 

2-66. The SFAB does not have organic fires capability and must coordinate with higher headquarters for fires support. Brigade and battalion fires cells require augmentation if expected to operate a fires cell in the same manner as a typical BCT. Advisor teams must be prepared to assist their foreign counterparts with employing and deconflicting artillery or other joint fires. 

2-67. An important part of targeting is identifying possibilities for fratricide and collateral damage. In addition to their own internal control measures, SFAB commanders and advisors assist the FSF in establishing the control measures to minimize these risks. Control measures (such as fire support coordination lines, no-strike lists, and airspace coordinating measures) are included in operation orders. Control measures should be coordinated with the foreign counterparts and understood thoroughly at all levels to prevent fratricide, reduce collateral damage, and increase the efficiency of the targeting process. 

##### **RISK MANAGEMENT** 

2-68. Risk—the exposure of someone or something valued to danger, harm, or loss—is inherent in all operations. Because risk is part of all military operations, it cannot be avoided. Identifying, mitigating, and accepting risk is a function of command and a key consideration during planning and execution. 

2-69. _Risk management_ is the process to identify, assess, and mitigate risks and make decisions that balance risk cost with mission benefits (JP 3-0). Commanders, staffs, and teams use risk management throughout the operations process to identify and mitigate risks associated with hazards (to include ethical risk and moral hazards) that have the potential to cause friendly and civilian casualties, damage or destroy equipment, or otherwise impact mission effectiveness. Like targeting, risk management begins in planning and continues through preparation and execution. 

Risk management consists of the following steps: 

- Identifying hazards. 

- Assessing hazards. 

- Developing controls and making risk decisions. 

- Implementing controls. 

- Supervising and evaluating. 

2-70. All staff elements and advisors incorporate risk management into their running estimates and provide recommendations to mitigate risk within their areas of expertise. The operations officer or advisor team leader coordinates risk management throughout the operations process. (See ATP 5-19 for a detailed discussion of the risk management process.) 

##### **KNOWLEDGE MANAGEMENT** 

2-71. _Knowledge management_ is the process of enabling knowledge flow to enhance shared understanding, learning, and decision making (ADP 6-0). It facilitates the transfer of knowledge among commanders, staffs, and advisor teams to build and maintain situational understanding. Knowledge management helps get the right information to the right person at the right time to facilitate decision making. Knowledge management uses a five-step process to create shared understanding that includes: 

- Assess. 

- Design. 

- Develop. 

- Pilot. 

- Implement. 

2-72. Knowledge management is particularly important to an SFAB’s success. Without the ability to aggregate assessments and reports from advisor teams working with FSF, the SFAB commanders and staffs will be unable to manage the larger SFA mission. Therefore, commanders, staffs, and advisor team leaders must emphasize knowledge management plans and SOPs prior to employment. These plans should include a mechanism for archiving assessments and information through higher headquarters for future advisor teams and other organizations that may find themselves in the same operational area (see ATP 6-01.1 for more information on knowledge management). 

2-73. Proper execution of knowledge management can enable to track partner force capability and capacity improvements over time. This is useful while the OE remains in competition but can also prove vital to ensuring shared understanding of the green COP during an inflection point or transition to crisis or armed conflict. 

#### **BATTLE RHYTHM** 

2-74. _Battle rhythm_ is a deliberate daily cycle of command, staff, and unit activities intended to synchronize current and future operations (FM 6-0). Commanders and staffs must integrate and synchronize numerous activities, meetings, and reports within their headquarters, with their higher headquarters, and with subordinate units as part of the ongoing operations. Advisors integrate and synchronize their activities with the foreign counterparts. They do this by first understanding the FSF’s battle rhythm and then building their battle rhythms to be compatible and complementary with their counterpart’s schedule. 

2-75. The SFAB battle rhythm sequences the actions and events within a headquarters by sharing information in support of the decision-making process. With significantly less personnel than a typical BCT, the SFAB must carefully plan its battle rhythm, otherwise, information and events will quickly overwhelm commanders, staffs, and advisor teams. An effective battle rhythm— 

- Facilitates interaction among the commander, staff, and advisor teams. 

- Supports building and maintaining shared understanding throughout the headquarters. 

- Establishes a routine for staff and advisor interaction and coordination. 

2-76. There is no standard battle rhythm for every situation. Different echelons, types of units, and types of operations require commanders and staffs to develop a battle rhythm based on the situation. During LSCO, where lethality and time constraints require rapid planning and decision cycles, the unit’s battle rhythm focuses on defeating the enemy. Daily battle rhythm events may consist of a morning or evening current operations update brief, a targeting meeting, and a combined plans and future operations update brief. In operations dominated by stability tasks, where headquarters are often static, the battle rhythm may be more deliberate with daily, weekly, and monthly working groups and boards. While the battle rhythm establishes a routine for a headquarters, the unit’s battle rhythm is not fixed. Commanders modify the battle rhythm as the situation evolves (see ATP 6-0.5 for a detailed discussion of battle rhythm to include examples of common meetings, working groups, and boards).

2-77. Commanders and staffs plan and develop tracking and reporting tools to anticipate and monitor changes in the environment. Small changes in operational or mission variables in a partner or host nation could have significant impacts. These may include SFAB security and protection requirements and may affect advisor interactions with their counterpart. Advisors maintain situational awareness of the routine activities of FSF counterparts to identify potential leading indicators of a significant event. Base these indicators on FSF accountability, unplanned changes in their protection measures, and other potential indicators. SFAB staffs and advisors must anticipate these events and have contingency plans in place. 

2-78. The SFAB plans reporting requirements to account for their reduced personnel involved with receiving and processing reports and to account for the unpredictable nature inherent in working with FSF. Challenges, such as scheduling conflicts, personnel availability, the advisor dichotomy, language difficulties, changing protection requirements, and others, add complexity to these reports. Reporting requirements must assist in quickly and efficiently gathering reports, parsing information, and providing the relevant portions to decision makers. 

2-79. Commanders, staffs, and advisors identify critical and routine information requirements and prioritize reporting procedures accordingly. Reports, such as accountability, maintenance status, and the counterpart’s training status, should be routine and simplified. Commanders, staff, and advisors should develop reporting criteria that quickly highlights deficiencies and update CCIR regularly in accordance with changes in the OE. Important information needs to be apparent, easily identified, and capable of transmitting through multiple mediums at low cost and low stress on the communication system. 

2-80. Build redundancy into reporting systems and procedures within the SFAB. Network, personnel, connectivity, and even electrical power availability limitations may hinder collecting required reports from a single network or system. 

2-81. Commanders adjust the unit’s battle rhythm as operations progress. For example, early in the operation a commander may require a daily update briefing. As the situation changes, the commander may require an update briefing only weekly. Some factors that influence a unit’s battle rhythm include the staff’s proficiency, higher headquarters’ battle rhythm, reporting requirements, the foreign counterpart’s battle rhythm, operating tempo, the current mission, and planning requirements. 

2-82. Commanders also adjust the battle rhythm and staff processes based on advising requirements of subordinate echelons. Due to the inverse relationship of C2 capacity and advising responsibilities, brigade commanders may “flatten” the organization and centralize more staff functions at the brigade level if subordinate battalion headquarters have significant advisor responsibilities. 

2-83. Meetings typically consume a large amount of a unit’s battle rhythm. Meetings are gatherings to present and exchange information, solve problems, coordinate action, and make decisions. SFAB meetings should have an agenda and focus on the meeting’s task and purpose. Dispersed locations, lack of personnel, connectivity challenges, and language barriers preclude long detailed, and complicated meetings. This is especially true for those involving the FSF counterparts. 

**_Note._** As in all operations, rehearsals are vital. This is even more true for SFABs executing advising operations. In all the battle rhythm meetings and events, it is important to rehearse with the FSF, to include an interpreter, to anticipate problems, cultural objections, and identify new terms that may need translation or clarification. 

##### **SECTION III – OPERATIONAL TENETS AND IMPERATIVES** 

2-84. The tenets and imperatives of operations guide the conduct of operations and provide options for leaders to apply and preserve combat power against challenges posed by peer threats (see FM 3-0). The SFAB is uniquely positioned to enable achievement of the operational tenets through both their own contributions to the joint force and the enhancement of partner force contributions. 

#### **OPERATIONAL TENETS** 

2-85. The SFAB is uniquely positioned to enable achievement of the operational tenets through both their own contributions to the joint force and the enhancement of partner force contributions. 

2-86. _Agility_ is the ability to move forces and adjust their dispositions and activities more rapidly than the enemy (FM 3-0). SFABs themselves are agile due to their small size and can deploy quickly to establish or increase presence in a prescribed area. SFABs also enable the agility of FSF, U.S. BCTs, and the joint force by increasing the rate of integration and shared understanding by employing the advisor network to relieve the tensions of “first contact” and enable the follow-on missions of their advised and adjacent units. Advisors provide commanders the ability to drive the operations process and improve visualization of the OE by building the green COP and leveraging their preestablished relationships with the FSF. 

2-87. _Convergence_ is an outcome created by the concerted employment of capabilities from multiple domains and echelons against combinations of decisive points in any domain to create effects against a system, formation, decision maker, or in a specific geographic area (FM 3-0). Achieving convergence in an operation is derived from how well leaders know themselves and the enemy, determine desired effects or opportunities, integrate the joint force, synchronize capabilities, assess interdependent effects, and assume risk. SFABs provide a calibrating effect for commanders, advising them on the capabilities and limitations of the FSF, increasing the effectiveness of FSF contributions, and reducing counterproductive imbalances during operations. 

2-88. _Endurance_ is the ability to persevere over time throughout the depth of an operational environment (FM 3-0). The SFAB contributes to the creation of endurance through setting the theatre across warfighting functions, improving interoperability with allies and other UAPs, and enabling access to host-nation assets to support the joint force. 

2-89. _Depth_ is the extension of operations in time, space, or purpose to achieve definitive results (ADP 3-0). During competition, the SFAB achieves depth by operating in all CCMD AORs, enhancing U.S. posture, and contesting adversary efforts worldwide. In the event of crisis or conflict, SFAB advisor teams (possibly already in theater) hold the unique position of operating in the deep fight, within an adversary antiaccess area denial bubble, to advise a partner force. 

#### **OPERATIONAL IMPERATIVES** 

2-90. The SFAB complies with the imperatives by operating as a purpose-built force to perform the advisor mission throughout the competition continuum. In leveraging the advisor network to synergize U.S. Force integration and partnering with FSF allies, it allows U.S. BCTs to focus on training and deployment to fight and win the Nation’s wars. SFABs are subject matter experts of their AORs and not only abide by the imperatives themselves but also allow adjacent U.S. Forces to gain higher fidelity with them as well. SFABs create shared understanding at the tactical, operational, and strategic level within the OE. 

2-91. SFABs achieve the imperatives outlined in FM 3-0 in the following ways: 

- See yourself, see the enemy, understand the OE: 

 - Self—advisor teams build the green COP to enable CCMD visualization of entire MNF. 

 - Enemy—advisor team partnership gives them unique access into FSF insight on the enemy as well as allow U.S. Forces access to FSF sensors. 

 - OE—because of regional alignment, advisors can study their AOR deeply. 

- Accounting for being under constant observation and all forms of enemy contact: 

 - Imbedded presence with an FSF partner signals U.S. commitment to the theater and the mission. 

 - Small teams mean small target signature. 

 - Enables an insider look at the FSF and host-nation perspectives of the OE. 

- Creating and exploiting relative physical, information, and human advantages in pursuit of decision dominance: 

 - Integrating with the partner force increases information and human advantages by promoting the cohesion of the MNF. 

 - Assessing FSF capability and capacity enabling a more thorough visualization and understanding which promotes decision making. 

- Making initial contact with the smallest element possible: 

 - Enabling the joint force to make adversary during competition. 

 - Employing small subcomponents of a force package to minimize risk to force. 

- Imposing multiple dilemmas on the enemy: 

 - The enemy must contend with partner FSFs enhanced by advisor efforts to build partner capabilities and capacities. 

 - Enabling better FSF integration into multinational operations which enhances the combat power of the joint force. 

- Anticipating, planning, and executing transitions: 

 - Advisor teams facilitating transitions by unburdening conventional forces from SFA activities. 

 - Lending inherent expertise and influence on lowering operational load and friction of transitions with partner force. 

- Designating, weighing, and sustaining the main effort: 

 - Acting as an economy of effort force against SFA activities, freeing other resources. 

 - Relying on myriad sustainment sources to lighten operational load on CCMD and raise likelihood of continuous sustainment support. 

- Consolidating gains continuously: 

 - Enabling immediate consolidation of gains through SFA expertise. 

 - Enhancing FSF capabilities and capacities promoting a long run consolidation of gains. 

- Understanding and managing the effects of operations on units and Soldiers: 

 - Comprising advisor teams with skilled and experienced leaders. 

 - Supplementing dedicated regional advisor teams with advisor teams from other regions (to include teams from 54th SFAB) to alleviate advisor workload. 

##### **SECTION IV – HIGHER HEADQUARTERS EMPLOYMENT CONSIDERATIONS** 

2-92. Higher headquarters employ the SFAB as a capability to conduct SFA activities and missions alongside FSF. This section describes considerations for SFAB operations in and outside of a JOA, various higher headquarters command structures, and describes what command and support relationships should look like. 

#### **JOINT OPERATIONS AREA** 

2-93. SFABs have the capability to operate both inside and outside of an established JOA. Each scenario presents different legal and operational considerations. SFABs may be integral to an operational area transition. 

2-94. When operating inside of an established JOA, DOD is usually the lead agency with the U.S. Department of State as the supporting agency. In this scenario, SFAB commanders must operate within DOD-nested concept for SFA. Under most circumstances when operating in an established JOA, DOD gives OPCON of the SFAB to the joint force land component commander (JFLCC) or CFLCC for employment. The JFLCC may retain the SFAB for direct employment. When conducting SC activities under the threshold of armed conflict, the SFAB will, in most circumstances, be assigned to the theater army. Operations inside of an established JOA give the joint force commander significant authorities and responsibilities. The joint force commander assigns subordinate organizations to conduct military operations within those areas of operations. The joint force commander assigns tactical or operational level SFA activities to the SFAB. 

2-95. The SFAB requires significant staff augmentation, specifically in current operations, to exercise C2 of forces outside of its SFA mission. Due to its lack of capacity, higher headquarters should not assign an AO to an SFAB without significant augmentation. If given responsibilities outside of its primary SFA mission, the SFAB will require augmentation, which can come in the form of other military forces or, in some cases, the FSF, to conduct the following: 

- Terrain management. 

- Information collection. 

- CMO. 

- Air and ground movement control. 

- Clearance of fires. 

- Security. 

- Personnel recovery. 

- Medical support. 

- Other functions based on the environment. 

2-96. The SFAB, operating inside an established JOA, needs an established sustainment network for support. The SFAB has no capability to sustain itself while deployed. Inside the JOA, the SFAB formation depends on the supporting command’s sustainment network for administration control and life support functions. Chapter 3 and 7 describe these planning considerations. 

2-97. In many cases, the SFAB requires additional support for other warfighting functions as well. These often include security, intelligence, fires, and protection. Higher headquarters should clearly define these support relationships for all advisor teams within the SFAB. 

#### **HIGHER HEADQUARTERS** 

2-98. The various theater command structures and environments worldwide have significant impacts on the SFAB planning and operations processes. Specifically, the differences between operating within a designated JOA and working outside of a JOA are significant. 

##### **THEATER COMMAND STRUCTURE** 

2-99. Political considerations heavily influence the ultimate shape of the C2 structure in which U.S. Forces conduct SFA. However, participating nations should strive to achieve unity of command for the operation to the maximum extent possible. All participants should understand missions, tasks, responsibilities, and authorities. While well-defined in U.S. doctrine, command relationships are not necessarily part of the doctrinal lexicon of nations with which the United States may operate in coalition or partnered operations. 

2-100. The SFAB may be a supported command—from a theater perspective—while at the same time acting as a supporting command for the FSF. For example, an SFAB may support a joint interagency task force conducting international police training. Another example is a joint command supporting an SFAB with joint fires, intelligence, surveillance, CMO, and reconnaissance; or other capabilities while the SFAB supports the FSF in training or operations. The basic structures for multinational operations fall into one of three types: lead nation, integrated, or parallel command. Alliance and coalition commands use these structures (see JP 3-16). 

2-101. A lead-nation command structure exists when all member nations place their forces under one nation’s control. The lead-nation command has a dominant lead-nation command and staff arrangement with subordinate elements retaining strict national integrity. The command may have an integrated staff and multinational subordinate forces. Integrating the staff allows the commander to draw upon the expertise of multinational or coalition partners in areas where the lead nation may have less experience. 

2-102. An integrated command structure provides unity of effort in a multinational setting. An integrated, combined command has several distinguishing features. The staff consists of representatives from all member nations underneath a designated single commander. To maximize effectiveness, integrate subordinate commands and staffs to the lowest echelon possible. 

2-103. A parallel command structure does not designate a single force commander. The coalition leaders develop a means for coordination among the participants to attain unity of effort by using coordination centers. A parallel command structure is the least effective when conducting SFA due to the lack of a single commander.

##### **ALLIANCE COMMAND STRUCTURE** 

2-104. Alliance commands address national political objectives within MNF objectives at the alliance treaty level. Alliance command relationships often reflect either an integrated command structure or a lead-nation command structure. In alliance operations, such as SFA, these structures should be used to the maximum practical extent. Alliance command and force structures often mirror the degree of allied member participation. Senior military officers from member nations often lead subordinate commands. Effective operations within an alliance require senior political and military authorities to agree on the type of command relationships that govern the operations of the forces. Potential political sensitivities associated with SFA can influence command relationships and operating procedures. 

##### **COALITION COMMAND STRUCTURE** 

2-105. Coalitions often form in response to unforeseen crises that occur outside the area of an established alliance, which is often the case for SFA. Coalition command relationships routinely evolve as the coalition develops. For SFA, all participating coalition members should augment the partner nation’s headquarters staff with liaisons. These representatives might include designated deputies or assistant commanders, planners, and logisticians. Such staffing provides the coalition commander with representative leaders, accessible expertise on the capabilities of the coalition members, and facilitates the planning process. Integrating multinational command elements into the coalition planning process should occur as early as practical. 

##### **ARMY OR JOINT FORCE COMMAND STRUCTURE** 

2-106. The corps and division are the U.S. Army’s unit of action in LSCO with either headquarters potentially acting as the JFLCC during operations. Whether it is an Army or a joint headquarters, the SFAB may be the assigned to augment this headquarters while deployed in a JOA. SFAB advisor teams allow such headquarters to integrate an FSF partner force more completely into operations and advise at the point of need. The SFAB advisor team may be assigned to liaise between the FSF and the corps and division staffs to link the efforts of the U.S. Forces and the FSF and allow allied and partner forces to be incorporated into achieving convergence. 

##### **OPERATING OUTSIDE A DESIGNATED JOINT OPERATIONS AREA** 

2-107. When operating outside of a JOA, the SFAB operates at the invitation of the partner nation. The COM (ambassador) facilitates the invitation through the CCDR under the auspices of SC. The partner nation, the ambassador, and the status-of-forces agreement establish the laws and rules under which military personnel conducting SFA operations outside of a JOA operate. Unless otherwise specified, the partner nation is responsible for the security of the personnel assigned to the SFAB formations; however, SFAB personnel always have the right of self-defense. 

2-108. The SFAB formation will not have a well-established sustainment network. The SFAB formation depends on the ASCC and the partner nation for many administrative control and life support functions (field feeding, all classes of resupply, and field-level and sustainment-level maintenance of all equipment), as well as some religious, legal, FHP and health service support, finance, postal, personnel, and administrative services. In some cases, the American embassy country team contract support or the contracting support brigade provide sustainment via contracting support in the supporting ASCC’s AO. To facilitate SFA operations, SFAB headquarters elements may be collocated with American embassies, the ASCC, a theater special operations command, or as directed by the CCDR based on operational and mission variables. 

##### **COUNTRY TEAM** 

2-109. The country team plays a vital role with respect to SFA. Figure 2-7 depicts the typical country team structure. The _country team_ is the senior, in-country United States coordinating and supervising body, headed by the chief of the United States diplomatic mission, and composed of the senior member of each represented United States department or agency, as desired by the chief of the United States diplomatic mission (JP 3-07.4). The COM is the highest U.S. civil authority in a foreign country. As the senior U.S. Government official permanently assigned in the partner nation, the COM is responsible to the President for policy oversight of all U.S. Government programs.

**Figure 2-7. Country team structure** 

2-110. The senior defense official is the principal DOD official in an American Embassy as designated by SecDef. This individual serves the following roles: 

- Acting as the diplomatic mission’s principal military advisor on defense and national security issues. 

- Filling the role of the senior diplomatically accredited DOD military officer assigned to a diplomatic mission. 

- Serving as the single point of contact for all DOD matters involving the embassy or DOD elements assigned to or working from the embassy. 

2-111. The senior defense official is the chief of both the SC organization and the defense attaché (DATT) office in the embassy. The country team works with the partner nation to develop measures to promote security by developing a yearly theater security cooperation plan (TSCP). 

2-112. The TSCP links the CCDR’s regional strategy to military operations. The Foreign Service Act assigns the mission chief to a foreign country with the responsibility of directing, coordinating, and supervising all government executive branch employees in that country, except for U.S. Service members and employees under the command of a U.S. area military commander (see FM 3-22 and JP 3-07).

#### **COMMAND AND SUPPORT RELATIONSHIPS** 

2-113. Properly established command and support relationships are critical to the success of the SFAB and its SFA mission. The SFAB’s higher headquarters should consider specific SFAB traits prior to establishing their command relationships. The SFAB— 

- Is optimized to operate at the advisor-team level because breaking teams apart degrades their ability to conduct advisor team activities. 

- Can task-organize advisor teams from across the brigade against unique FSF requirements by location. 

- Is designed to manage, coordinate, and adjust the SFA activities and missions at the tactical and operational level in support of strategic goals. 

- Has limited capacity to C2 in an AO without significant augmentation. 

- Cannot conduct independent combat operations against an enemy force. 

- Has limited ability to sustain themselves. 

2-114. The SFAB works best when its higher headquarters, in coordination with the partnered nation or security force, gives it objectives for developing FSF. Usually, this is done through a TACON relationship between senior SFAB commanders and their next higher headquarters. This allows the SFAB commanders and staffs to utilize their advisors and resources against those objectives in a manner best suited to accomplish the SFA mission. 

##### **OPERATIONAL CONTROL** 

2-115. _Operational control_ is the authority to perform those functions of command over subordinate forces involving organizing and employing commands and forces, assigning tasks, designating objectives, and giving authoritative direction necessary to accomplish the mission (JP 1, Vol 2). 

2-116. OPCON normally includes authority over all aspects of operations and joint training necessary to accomplish missions. It does not include directive authority for sustainment or matters of administration, discipline, internal organization, or unit training. OPCON does include the authority to delineate functional responsibilities and operational areas of subordinate joint force commanders (see FM 6-0). 

2-117. In almost all circumstances, it is preferred for senior SFAB commanders to maintain OPCON of their own forces. Building effective advisor teams requires that SFAB commanders and staffs task-organize teams from across the brigade as necessary to achieve the objective that the ground force commander has requested. FSF advising requirements vary by each location and mission. This requires a flexible organization, much of which is lost when subordinate units are under the OPCON of other commands. 

##### **TACTICAL CONTROL** 

2-118. TACON is inherent in OPCON. Commanders at any echelon at or below the level of CCMD may delegate and exercise TACON. TACON provides sufficient authority for controlling and directing the application of force or tactical use of combat support assets within the assigned mission or task.
