TACON does not provide organizational authority or authoritative direction for administrative and logistic support; the commander of the parent unit continues to exercise these authorities unless otherwise specified in the establishing directive (see FM 6-0). 

2-119. In most cases, the SFAB’s higher headquarters should direct its operations through a TACON relationship. This allows the higher headquarters to direct the required effects while enabling the SFAB commanders and staffs to task-organize and apply their advisor teams to meet the SFA mission. Used in this manner, the SFAB provides an advising capability to its higher headquarters. The SFAB forces will be able to focus on completing the SFA mission with FSF instead of other external requirements potentially distracting them from their primary mission. Figure 2-8 illustrates how these command relationships may look when the SFAB is providing an advising capability to a theater. 

**Figure 2-8. Command relationships** 

##### **SUPPORT** 

2-120. Support is a command authority in joint doctrine. A superior commander establishes supported and supporting relationships between subordinate commanders when one organization should aid, protect, complement, or sustain another force. Designating supporting relationships is important. It conveys priorities to commanders and staffs planning or executing operations. Many of the typical staff supporting functions do not exist in sufficient capacity to accomplish the same tasks as would a typical BCT, so ensuring the correct point of contact is identified within the SFAB element receiving support is vital. Designating a support relationship does not provide authority to organize and employ commands and forces, nor does it include authoritative direction for administrative and logistic support (see FM 6-0). 

2-121. Advisor teams are likely to receive support from other organizations. This support can include items like security, intelligence, fires, protection, and sustainment. Every advisor team must understand from where they could theoretically receive this support. In a JOA, this will generally come from a support relationship with sustainment brigades and BCTs. In a non-JOA environment, coordination through the ASCC, embassy country team, contracting support brigade, or other methods provide the required support. During LSCO, advisor teams likely both provide and receive direct support from their FSF counterparts.

2-122. _Direct support_ is a mission requiring a force to support another specific force and authorizing it to answer directly to the supported force’s request for assistance (JP 3-09.3). Of note, joint doctrine considers direct support as a mission rather than a support relationship. A unit assigned a direct support relationship retains its command relationship with its parent unit but is positioned by and has priorities of support established by the supported unit. Advisor teams could theoretically be on either end of a direct support relationship. In LSCO, an SFAB could surge to support a U.S. or partner force directly with advising expertise for a specific mission. Alternatively, an SFAB could receive direct support from a logistics or transportation unit to help move it around the AOR to advise at point of need. 

2-123. _Reinforcing_ is a support relationship requiring a force to support another supporting unit (FM 3-0). Only like units can be given a reinforcing mission. A unit assigned a reinforcing support relationship retains its command relationship with its parent unit but is positioned by the reinforced unit. A unit that is reinforcing has priorities of support established by the reinforced unit first, and then by the parent unit. This type of relationship could occur when multiple units support a single SFAB’s activities, or when another SFAB (like 54th SFAB) is tasked to support another SFAB’s typical AOR. 

2-124. _General support-reinforcing_ is a support relationship assigned to a unit to support the force as a whole and to reinforce another similar-type unit (FM 3-0). A unit assigned a GS-reinforcing relationship is positioned and has priorities established by its parent unit and secondly by the reinforced unit. 

2-125. _General support_ is support given to the supported force as a whole and not to any particular subdivision thereof (JP 3-09.3). Units assigned a GS relationship are positioned and have priorities established by their parent unit. For example, while advising an FSF, a combined JTF commander may place the SFAB in a GS role over all partner forces in an AOR and allow the SFAB leaders the ability to allocate their teams accordingly. Alternatively, a partner force being advised might be placed in GS of a U.S. BCT in the same scenario. In that case, the U.S. BCT commander would not direct the actions of the advisor teams or the partner force. 

2-126. While the application of the different types of support relationships may or may not be relevant to the advisor teams themselves, they are especially worth understanding should a partner force be given a similar type of task or relationship. 

##### **Chapter 3** 

### **Predeployment, Deployment, and Redeployment** 

_Given the nature of the threats we face today and the challenges we are likely to face in the future, I cannot imagine a scenario in which the United States would not be standing alongside allies and partners across the globe._ 

General Joseph Dunford, 19th Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff 

Chapter 3 describes the brigade’s deployment considerations from receipt of mission through post-deployment activities. See TC 3-22 for more information. 

##### **SECTION I – PREDEPLOYMENT** 

3-1. Planning for deployment is one of the most crucial phases of an operation. Personnel shortages, time and equipment limitations, language differences, cultural differences, dispersed operating locations, and fragile lines of communication challenge units as they begin preparations to deploy. Within the SFAB, deployment planning must be a multiechelon and collaborative effort among commanders, staffs, and advising team leaders. SFA missions occur across the range of military operations and the competition continuum. Figure 3-1 on page 78 shows the recurring steps in the SFAB’s mission cycle. 

**Figure 3-1. Mission cycle** 

3-2. A typical mission cycle begins when the CCDR issues guidance to the ASCC, which then submits a request for forces to the Joint Chiefs of Staff. The joint staff, through the Department of the Army, issues the deployment order to United States Army Forces Command/SFAC, and ultimately to the designated SFAB. The order includes the plan for the SFAB’s RSOI and includes details on base support, communications, and sustainment. SFAB parallel planning during the request for forces process is critical to maximizing the predeployment planning and coordination time available, as many planning tasks can be accomplished prior to request for forces approval and force allocation. When possible, the SFAB conducts PDSSs and subsequent coordination for clearance to the theater to facilitate mission planning and sustainment for the duration of the mission. 

3-3. SFAB subordinate organizations and elements begin detailed preparations immediately upon receiving their mission. Using known requirements, the unit begins task-organizing and predeployment training immediately. The unit tailors the training to mission specifics gathered during PDSS and or reconnaissance. The unit obtains supplies, equipment, and training materials to conduct individual, leader, and collective training to prepare for deployment. Units may request or be granted direct liaison authorized with UAPs as required. The SFAB’s higher headquarters approves direct liaison authority between the supported ASCC and the projected SFAB as early as possible to facilitate parallel planning ahead of force allocation. 

#### **INITIAL COORDINATING AND PLANNING EVENTS** 

3-4. Through mission analysis, SFAB leaders and staffs determine if the unit is sufficiently organized and equipped to meet the mission parameters stated in the order. SFAB staffs refer to the order for the necessary information to begin coordination for entering the country with their required equipment. Leaders and staffs generate information requests for information not contained in the order.

##### **MULTIECHELON PLANNING** 

3-5. The SFAB identifies the initial command and support relationships for the deploying element to determine their command and support relationships while in transit and in theater. The CCDR provides the ASCC with exercises, training terms, and funding lines available for the deployment and the conduct of the mission. The information provides operational requirements, funding, and authorities linked to the assigned exercise or training missions. 

3-6. The SFAB reviews the TSCP and the relevant regional or country plans to conduct detailed planning. SFAB commanders and staff define success by focusing on long-term and sustainable goals in support of the TSCP. With long-term goals in mind, the SFAB develops measures of performance (MOPs) and measures of effectiveness (MOEs) for each potential course of action. This is part of the integrating process of operational assessment covered in chapter 4. The theater and country plans may contain MOPs and MOEs, but the SFAB must do more than merely refine the higher headquarters’ plans. SFABs must conduct their own analysis and nest their plan with higher guidance ensuring both are conducive to lower-level execution. 

##### **FUNDING AUTHORITIES** 

3-7. The CCMD identifies funding under Title 10 or Title 22 of the U.S. Code and coordinates with the American Embassy and U.S. Department of State to facilitate SFAB deployment and mission support as required by the mission variables of METT-TC (I). The SFAB S-8 coordinates with the ASCC assistant chief of staff of financial management to identify lines of accounting for deployment-related expenses. DOD determines if the SFAB executes SFA under Title 10 or Title 22 authorities, which has implications for authorized SFA activities. If under Title 10 authority, the ASCCs (or a designated JTF) receive funds through the established base budget, overseas contingency operations funding, or funds otherwise appropriated by Congress. If required, the CCMD, in conjunction with the country team, negotiates an ACSA with the host nation to ensure the host nation receives payment for logistical support services. Other bilateral agreements may also exist, ensuring the availability of appropriated host-nation resources. These agreements may include payment to the United States through foreign military sales. 

##### **PRE-MISSION PLANNING** 

3-8. In preparation for deployment, the SFAB identifies and reviews existing campaign plans and country plans from the CCMD, the ASCC, and the COM, as well as other documents critical to U.S. interests and strategic goals in the region. The SFAB cross-references and compares these plans to operational and environmental assessments to identify potential gaps in FSF capability or capacity. This early information is included in the SFAB’s mission analysis. (See figure 3-2 on page 80 for a graphic depiction of how these plans influence each other.) (See FM 3-22 for more information.) 

**Figure 3-2. Relationship between country plan, theater campaign plan, and integrated country strategy** 

##### **PRE-MISSION ACADEMICS** 

3-9. Staffs develop and distribute study materials and briefings on the operational area to leaders and Soldiers. These products emphasize host-nation culture and language and provide cultural tips for developing good rapport with foreign personnel. Advisors reach out to the country team and other regional experts to gain a better understanding of the history, culture, motivations, and politics of the host nation. 

3-10. This information provides the inputs into early environmental and institutional assessments of the FSF. These initial assessments help the SFAB to identify FSF capability or capacity gaps and cross-reference these potential gaps to existing campaign plans and their tentative mission. 

3-11. Since battalions and below can be assigned missions widely dispersed across a geographical CCMD, each advisor team must be prepared to leverage the expertise of its members to conduct its own academics for its mission, rather than relying on staffs at higher echelons. The persistent presence that each SFAB has in its assigned AO provides materials for teams to build their pre-mission academic programs. Teams must leverage assessments from previous teams that have conducted missions in their region, focusing on operational variables and DOTMLPF-P analyses. 

##### **HOST-NATION APPROVAL AND COUNTRY CLEARANCES** 

3-12. The SFAB can expect delays during the visa application process. Some countries may require a letter of introduction through the supported embassy’s SC organization. An approved visa is often required for country clearance and can delay the Aircraft and Personnel Automated Clearance System approval process. The SFAB S-1 or S-2 is usually responsible for planning and monitoring the visa process. However, due to the small staff, subordinate leaders and advisor team leaders must track this information as well. (The U.S. Passports and International Travel website provides additional information and specific requirements for each country.) The SFABs are likely to require formalized support from their installation passport office to process a high density of passport requirements that are uncommon for conventional forces. Personnel assigned to SFABs must receive a special authorization to request official passports prior to receipt of mission.

3-13. The SFAB submits proposed PDSS dates to the ASCC or country team. The proposed dates include time spent going to and from the CCMD headquarters for briefings and guidance, as well as travel to the host nation. Staff planners must consider encountering delays when transiting through foreign countries. Planners must anticipate passport and visa requirements in those countries as well. Lastly, planners must consider using commercial aircraft or U.S. military aircraft. (See the DOD Electronic Foreign Clearance Guide website for a list of country clearance requirements and the Aircraft and Personnel Automated Clearance System website for country clearance issuances.) 

##### **PRE-MISSION LEGAL BRIEFS** 

3-14. Prior to employment, the brigade Judge Advocate of the deploying SFAB provides a brief to ensure understanding of the rules of engagement, standing rules of force, or status-of-forces agreements of the countries where they are deploying to. Any pre-mission questions about local area laws and customs can be answered through the brigade judge advocate. 

#### **PREDEPLOYMENT SITE SURVEY** 

3-15. The PDSS aims to present information accurately based on the existing mission variables. The PDSS also provides detailed information on the C2, support, protection, and sustainment relationships for the follow-on unit’s mission. The site survey coordinates the in-country reception of the SFA forces. The coordination requires all units, outgoing and incoming, to apply a comprehensive approach at all levels of command. 

3-16. The SFAB conducts a PDSS 180 to 270 days prior to deployment. This gives the unit adequate time to plan, conduct, or alter its existing training plan to accommodate the country and theater-specific requirements of its mission and tailor SFA missions to the needs of the FSF or host nation. The PDSS provides an opportunity to gain the information required to address planning requirements and confirms the partner FSF expectations. Ideally, SFAB units will be afforded multiple opportunities through initial planning and coordination events, host nation and country team communication and ASCC contact to accomplish PDSS goals and objectives. It is difficult to accomplish all pre-mission analysis in a single PDSS event. Depending on the maturity of the theater, multiple PDSS events may be required or authorized. Paragraphs 3-17 through 3-22 describe SFAB-specific information requirements that should be determined by the unit to ensure it can properly prepare and train upon return. 

##### **THEATER MEDICAL REQUIREMENTS** 

3-17. In addition to coordination with supporting host-nation and FSF medical assets during the PDSS, consider the in-theater medical capabilities and compare them to the SFAB’s requirements. The SFAB mission may require Soldiers to operate outside the capability of conventional force medical support. Key medical considerations for SFAB planners include— 

- Individual and collective FHP measures, including immunizations and chemoprophylaxis. 

- Suitability, capacity, and capability of local medical facilities and assets. 

- Time and distance factors associated with patient evacuation. 

- Availability and suitability of civil and military casualty and MEDEVAC assets (including airframe use authorization). 

- Suitability of Class VIII supplies and blood products. 

- Legality, foreign clearance requirements, or acceptable substitutions for prohibited or controlled substances, for example, narcotic medical supplies. 

##### **ECHELON OF ADVISING** 

3-18. The partner nation’s description of its security forces may not align with the SFAB’s assessment during the PDSS. The SFAB updates information during the PDSS and makes recommendations for changes to the chain of command. Advising at higher echelons requires a focus on staff processes and a knowledge of the partner nation and the FSF systems and processes. The SFAB aligns its personnel to the appropriate rank structures with the FSF given the mission-specific requirements (for example, a U.S. major could advise the foreign equivalent of a colonel). The SFAB identifies any cultural risks associated with the possibility of giving offense or injuring the partnership based on advisor pairings (considerations include rank, gender, age, cultural background, personality, religion, race, and ethnicity).

##### **FOREIGN SECURITY FORCES TRAINING REQUIREMENT** 

3-19. FSF operational assessments provide initial training and mission requirements. This information provides data for the SFAB’s mission analysis. The level of proficiency, equipment, and resources may vary widely from mission to mission or even unit to unit. The FSF needs shape advisor team task-organization and augmentation requirements. The SFAB conducts mission analysis to support a wide variety of SFA activities including force generation, institutional instruction, or operational assistance. Examples include the development of national-level schools or academies for initial entry Soldiers, professional military education for staff officers, or field expedient tactical training for squads. Chapter 4, section II, describes initial and continuous assessment in more detail. 

##### **EXPECTED DEGREE OF UNIT SEPARATION** 

3-20. Unit dispersion in theater significantly influences the SFAB mission. Units may consolidate at a single location or spread out across an entire AO with advisor teams significantly separated from other U.S. Forces. The expected degree to which forces separate affects the risk associated with the mission. Commanders and staffs must consider this separation when determining augmentation and support requirements. 

3-21. In the regional alignment and force package rotation model, individual advisor teams must be prepared to operate in a separate country independently of their higher headquarters. Unit separation by international borders increases the complexity of lines of communication for the task force. Prior to deployment, the SFAB task force must ensure that all subordinate units are prepared to sustain themselves independently. Considerations may include the following: 

- Uniform Code of Military Justice authority. 

- Host nation or local laws. 

- Agreements with the host nation for support (for example, ACSA). 

- Contracting enablers: 

 - Pay agent. 

 - Contracting officer’s representative. 

 - FOO. 

 - Government purchase card and cardholder. 

- Unit deployable trained advisors: 

 - Unit movement officer. 

 - Central control officer. 

 - Hazardous materials officer. 

 - Transportation Coordinator’s Automated Information for Movement System. 

- Unit prevention leader. 

- Defense Travel System reviewer. 

- COMSEC custodian. 

##### **PERSONNEL RECOVERY** 

3-22. During the PDSS, the designated SFAB personnel recovery officer coordinates with the personnel recovery coordination section at the next higher headquarters or the regional security officer of the American Embassy to assess, coordinate, and develop the unit’s personnel recovery plans and procedures. SFAB teams review and update these plans as necessary prior to each mission. SFAB teams should expect to refine personnel recovery plans at their level to provide bottom-up refinement to the theater personnel recovery plan in geographically dispersed deployments outside of a JOA. 

#### **ADVISOR TEAM TASK ORGANIZATION** 

3-23. Upon completion of the PDSS, SFAB commanders task-organize advisor teams to the identified requirements. If possible, this should include any external augmentation such as security forces. These task-organized advisor teams conduct the necessary mission-specific training, preparing themselves for the upcoming operation. SFAB commanders and their higher headquarters will determine the deployment certification guidelines for each mission. These will often vary greatly from one region to another. Once certified to deploy, commanders should refrain from breaking up the task-organized advisor team until the mission is complete.

#### **PORTS OF EMBARKATION AND DEBARKATION** 

3-24. When the planning for the deployment is routine, the SFAB will primarily plan against use of the most logistically efficient aerial ports of embarkation (APOEs) and seaports of embarkation (SPOEs) to their home station. When the destination OE is permissive, SFABs will plan against the most logistically efficient aerial ports of debarkation (APODs) and seaports of debarkation (SPODs) or as prescribed by the host nation. When planning is not routine, decisions concerning choices of APOE and SPOE will be determined by missionspecific details. When the destination OE is not permissive, APOD and SPOD decisions will also be case by case as informed by the theater campaign plan and mission-specific details. 

##### **SECTION II – DEPLOYMENT** 

3-25. For each deploying element, the SFABs must provide a C2 element to oversee the movement of forces and make coordination with higher echelons and parallel organizations. Mission variables determine the composition of the C2 element (see TC 3-05.3). 

#### **RECEPTION** 

3-26. The advance party establishes contact with the forward deployed force package, or U.S. and FSF commanders in a non-persistent AO, to conduct final pre-mission planning activities. The advance party procures secure working, storage, and living areas for the unit. It also coordinates and prepares initial training sites. The advance party coordinates for special support equipment to unload heavy supplies and transport the supplies and personnel to the training site. 

3-27. The advance party may be tasked to make initial contact with host-nation partners and liaisons. They may arrange for meetings between their element leaders and the host-nation or partner leaders. 

#### **STAGING** 

3-28. The main body of the SFAB force package deploys after completing its planning and when the commander determines the main body has met all predeployment training requirements. The unit processes through customs and immediately notifies the higher headquarters of its arrival and status. The unit obtains the proper identification, documentation, and weapons permits as required. Members of the advance party meet the main body on its arrival and brief the main body on any required changes to the operation order. 

3-29. The commander or team leader is ultimately accountable for the advisor team’s physical and personal security. The commander or team leader ensures personnel stay with the equipment to guard it. Depending on the threat situation, the commander ensures protective measures are in place to safeguard personnel and equipment. 

3-30. The SFAB force package uses in-theater preparations to finalize critical requirements. From confirming support relationships to establishing personal relationships with the supported higher headquarters, this preparation time is critical for the force package and can set the conditions throughout the deployment. Local conditions may require the SFAB force package element to establish C2, support, and sustainment relationships from outside the AO. 

#### **ONWARD MOVEMENT** 

3-31. Upon arrival in the theater of operations, the SFAB may be received by a host-nation greeting party. The greeting party may come from a higher military command, embassy or country team representative, FSF, or the local leaders. In most cases, the advance party has established protocol procedures. When met by a host-nation greeting party, the SFAB prepares to receive the party in accordance with cultural norms and customs. This could involve ceremonies, exchange of gifts, meals, and commemorations. 

3-32. The unit immediately establishes OPSEC procedures to support its mission execution and establishes its force protection plan. The leader determines the appropriate level of force protection depending on how permissive the environment is. Once protection procedures are in place, the SFAB main body proceeds to confirm relationships and mission requirements, adapting the plan and vulnerability assessments as required. SFAB advisor teams then begin movement to their respective locations.

3-33. The unit establishes procedures to promote interagency cooperation and synchronization. In support of this effort, the unit— 

- Identifies the location of key partner or U.S. Government facilities and agencies. 

- Coordinates interpreter support and linkup. 

- Confirms or establishes other coordination protocols as necessary. 

- Incorporates the newly established or changed procedures into the mission-execution plans. 

#### **INTEGRATION** 

3-34. After receiving a detailed briefing and further guidance from the advance party, unit personnel continue developing rapport with the FSF commander and counterparts. They also assess their working, storage, and living areas for security and verify the location of training sites, suitable communications node locations, medical nodes, and the FSF headquarters. The team leader— 

- Makes requests for a counterpart linkup. 

- Makes initial introductions and begins establishing rapport. 

- Briefs the unit’s mission, capabilities, restrictions, and limitations. 

- Analyzes the foreign unit’s status to finalize the SFA plan, which can include task-organization, staff functions, and other considerations. 

- Prepares and briefs the plans for training, advising, and assisting. 

- Begins the assessment process. 

- Manages necessary and vital rehearsals for CASEVAC and MEDEVAC routes and procedures, including contingency planning. 

3-35. Based on the geographical location and operational variables in that location, the unit determines the level of internal security required based on present or anticipated threats. Some considerations are improving defensive positions, establishing an internal guard system, implementing communication redundancy, and disseminating defensive plans. The unit may coordinate defensive measures with its foreign counterparts to develop a mutual defensive plan. 

3-36. Advisors discuss security measures with foreign counterparts. After analyzing the FSF security posture and facilities, the advisors encourage the FSF to adopt additional security measures as needed. Advisor teams conduct rehearsals of defensive plans with the FSF. If unable to conduct a rehearsal with its counterparts, the advisors conduct security plans rehearsals internally. 

#### **MISSION EXECUTION AND MONITORING ACTIVITIES** 

3-37. As soon as advisor teams have integrated with their foreign counterparts and have clearly established command and support relationships, they begin conducting advisor team activities and refining their initial assessments. These assessments will play an important role in guiding future advisor team planning efforts and missions. Chapter 4 describes the advisor team activities and the assessment process in more detail. 

##### **SECTION III – REDEPLOYMENT** 

3-38. The nature of SFA missions does not provide clearly defined completion points. Historically, the Army and other organizations conduct SFA missions over months and years. The SFABs must be prepared to transition, transfer, or terminate specific missions at any time. This may include replacing advisor teams from within the organization, transferring responsibility to an external U.S. or allied organization, or transitioning responsibility back to the partner nation. In all cases, take care to ensure a deliberate and thorough transition takes place to maintain gains and provide the best chance of future success for the partner nation and incoming organization. 

3-39. To capture all lessons and experience from the redeploying team’s activities, advisor teams will— 

- Prepare a final evaluation report showing their estimate of the foreign unit’s capabilities, which provides an assessment for the relieving U.S. unit and the foreign leaders. 

- Be prepared to discuss the foreign unit’s performance with its commander and submit a copy of the foreign unit’s final evaluation to the next higher FSF military commander. 

- Provide the following to the relieving U.S. unit: 

 - Training schedules. 

 - Lesson plans. 

 - Foreign operational records. 

 - The foreign unit’s final evaluation. 

- Present debriefings, after action reviews (AARs), and foreign unit final evaluations to the higher in-country U.S. commander and country team as required. 

3-40. The unit foreign disclosure officer ensures commanders, staffs, and advisors understand disclosure requirements by country, category, and classification level and that the authorities and procedures are in place to facilitate this process. Coordinate with the foreign disclosure officer to ensure that the assessment of the country is prepared for the deployment team. 

3-41. Redeployment is the return of forces and materiel to home station or a mobilization site. Before redeployment, inventory all supplies and equipment to pass on to a relieving unit or to ship back to home station. Account for all items and ensure the chain of custody for property and equipment is uninterrupted. 

**_Note._** It is highly advisable to train two personnel in every staff section and advisor team on foreign disclosure. Interaction with host nation, FSF, and even North Atlantic Treaty Organization or other coalition allies, requires foreign disclosure officer approval. 

#### **TRANSFER TO FOLLOW-ON FORCES** 

3-42. A relief in place is an operation directed by higher headquarters in which an incoming unit replaces all or part of another unit and its mission. The authority for determining mission handoff lies with incoming commanders or team leaders since they assume responsibility for the mission. SFAB units most often conduct relief in place with a follow-on force package from within their unit but must be prepared to transfer responsibilities to other U.S. Forces, joint military forces, or to not be relieved at all. 

3-43. SFAB unit commanders may elect to replace teams, elements, or subordinate units for a variety of reasons to provide continuous Army support to SFA. Host-nation operational variables, situations, or operations changes may require reshaping force packages. In addition, internal administrative concerns might prompt or support a commander’s decision to rotate teams or units. For example, an incoming unit may field new equipment that the outgoing unit lacks. Regardless of the reason, mission handoff is necessary and defined as the process of passing an ongoing mission from one unit to another with no discernible loss of continuity. 

3-44. A relief in place may affect the conditions under which the mission continues. In SFA, this may not entail an in-country relief or transfer of authority. While executing the SFA mission, advisor teams are encouraged to record all relevant information and share it with incoming units. This historical record should serve as part of an initial briefing to incoming units. This information can be disseminated through virtual meetings, video teleconferences, or (preferably) face-to-face commander and staff meetings between transitioning teams or units. This assists with achieving continuity of operations. These briefings are ideally executed in conjunction with an introduction to the FSF. When it is not possible to do a direct transition to a relieving unit, lessons learned and post-mission debriefings provide a historical record that assist in bridging the gap between activities and operations. These assessments, training plans, briefings, and other relevant topics assist the incoming unit with assuming SFA activities. Units review previous post-mission debriefings prior to conducting their PDSS.

##### **TRANSITION OF ACTIVITIES** 

3-45. The transition of all activities to the FSF occurs incrementally at multiple echelons over time. Success is defined by fulfillment of the mutually agreed upon objectives of the United States and the partner nation. In cases where SFAB activities represent contributions to building a sovereign partner nation’s existing capacity, transition may occur at the SFAB’s completion of a specified, relatively short program of instruction to the FSF. When stabilizing or rebuilding societies post-conflict, SFAB transition may be only one small part of a lengthy, large-scale stability campaign. The intent of SC is to change the security environment by enabling the host nation to provide its own internal defense and development. This leads to strengthening regional security through mutual defense in conjunction with U.S. priorities established in the mission performance plan. 

##### **TERMINATION OF ACTIVITIES** 

3-46. While SFABs maintain a persistent presence in a CCMD, once established and agreed upon mission objectives are met, they may discontinue the relationship with a particular partnered force. This determination is made through a collaborative decision between the CCMD and the SFAB commander. Terminating or transitioning a specific activity enables the SFAB to rededicate teams or combat power to other partner nations or units in the CCMD. 

##### **CUSTOMARY FAREWELLS** 

3-47. Upon departure, the host-nation representatives may bid farewell to the SFAB. Representatives may come from a higher military command, the embassy or country team, FSF members, local leaders, and other dignitaries. The SFAB prepares to receive the party and acts in accordance with cultural norms and customs. This could involve ceremonies, gift exchanges, meals, and commemorations. 

**_Note._** Submit any gift exchanges for legal review. 

#### **POST-EXECUTION EVALUATION ACTIVITIES** 

3-48. Advisor teams conduct post-deployment debriefs providing an overview and analysis of the mission and outcome, communicating the information at the appropriate levels of clearance with respect to the audience. The brief includes an overview of operational and mission variables, MOPs, MOEs, and an assessment of the tasks the SFAB performed. 

3-49. Redeployment is not the mission’s end as the unit undergoes extensive debriefing. The debriefing may occur prior to redeployment or once back at home station. The S-2 typically coordinates with higher echelon intelligence organizations to conduct the returning unit’s debriefing. All deployed personnel, to include attachments, should be available for the debriefing. The Defense Strategic Debriefing Course provides the training to conduct these debriefings. 

##### **POST-MISSION DOCUMENTATION** 

3-50. After the debriefing, advisor teams and staffs prepare three documents: an AAR, a lessons learned report, and an assessment. The purpose of these documents is to add to the SFAB and Army-wide Knowledge Management program. The documentation should describe tasks, missions, operations, and capabilities of both the SFAB units and the FSF. This information provides valuable historical information to units deploying to the same region, higher headquarters, the partner nation, and other SFA organizations. Postmission documentation provides consistency during intermittent operations. 

##### **AFTER ACTION REVIEW AND LESSONS LEARNED** 

3-51. Lessons learned validate knowledge and experience derived from observations and the historical study of military training, exercises, and combat operations. Lessons learned, when further validated, eventually lead to a change in behavior at the tactical level (such as tactics, techniques, and procedures), the operational level, the strategic level, and in one or more of the DOTMLPF-P domains. Shortly after completion of the AAR, or simultaneously with its submission, the unit submits a report of lessons learned. The report documents the commander and staff’s reflections of the operation and recommendations for the future. Units often prepare the lessons according to the elements of combat power: C2, movement and maneuver, intelligence, fires, sustainment, protection, leadership, and information. This method addresses what did and did not work during the operation and why. It also recommends changes or substitutions for tactics, techniques, and procedures in the FSF. 

##### **SUBMISSION AND ARCHIVING** 

3-52. Per AR 11-33, the SFAB will also submit AARs to the Center for Army Lessons Learned (Combined Arms Center) no later than 90 days after returning to home station after participating in a major Army, joint, or joint combined (multinational) exercise training or SC mission. Units completing combat training center rotations may submit a copy of the relevant portions of their take-home package. Submit all the post-mission - documentation to the office of the SFAB’s Army Capabilities Manager via email <u>usarmy.benning.cdid mnvr.mesg.sfab@army.mil</u> for archiving and further dissemination. 

##### **<mark>SECTION IV – ADDITIONAL CONSIDERATIONS ACROSS THE COMPETITION CONTINUUM</mark>** 

3-53. Across the competition continuum, the predeployment, deployment, and redeployment cycle should, resemble the routine practices which occur throughout standard Army unit rotational deployments as much as possible. For all deployments, the needed level of detailed planning remains high, but the complexity of the destination AOR can vary. While deploying to a zone of competition, the complexity can be low, while executing deployment into a crisis or conflict zone there is an obvious increase in complexity. 

#### **PREDEPLOYMENT IN COMPETITION** 

3-54. When executing predeployment in competition, SFABs should seek to achieve the greatest level of fidelity with established best practices, regulations, and doctrinal principles. The following steps of host-nation approval must not be neglected: 

- Requesting and receiving country clearances. 

- Initial coordination and planning events. 

- PDSS. 

- Pre-mission planning. 

- Task organization. 

- Predeployment assessment activities. 

- Mobility planning and certification. 

- Movement training and certification. 

3-55. Predeployment decisions should weigh the efficiency of deployment method highly and select the most effective APOE, SPOE, APOD, and/or SPOD for their transit from home station to the mission location. Predeployment planning during competition is the SFABs most routine iteration of the deployment cycle and functions as a developmental device to prepare SFAB personnel and organizations for deployment in more rigorous, uncertain, and austere environments. During predeployment activities, advisors should consider accessing the advisor network as part of building their understanding of the mission specific details. 

#### **DEPLOYMENT IN COMPETITION** 

3-56. In competition, SFABs deploy to their AORs most frequently and with the highest relative level of regularity. The lessons learned from previous iterations of SFAB deployment apply most cleanly and should be consulted to deploy effectively. Competition provides a relatively permissive environment for SFAB teams to establish the SOPs and tactics, techniques, and procedures which best suit the specific mission. RSOI, mission execution, and monitoring activities, provide the framework for all deployment operations. In competition, SFABs leverage a wide variety of deployment methods to reduce the inefficiencies within their deployment framework, acknowledging that these procedures might not transfer to a less permissive OE. While deployed in competition, advisors have the greatest access to the advisor network.

##### **REDEPLOYMENT IN COMPETITION** 

3-57. Redeployment in competition is the process by which SFAB teams conduct a relief in place with the incoming SFAB element or leave their FSF with all the necessary tools to continue performing at a high level. SFABs consolidate the gains created by their mission and make efforts to reduce the regression of the partner force by concretizing the benefit their mission provided. Successful SFAB advisor team redeployment in competition protects and propagates deployment gains even after the advisors have redeployed. SFAB advisor team redeployment includes the following: 

- Post-execution evaluation activities. 

- Transition to follow-on-forces. 

- Relief(s) in-place/transfer of authorities. 

- Reverse RSOI. 

- Post-deployment AARs. 

- Continued contributions to the advisor network. 

- Reset activities. 

3-58. During redeployment in competition, maintaining the relationship and the interpersonal and interorganizational rapport with the partner force is of great value and can be easily damaged by poorly conceived redeployment. Remaining available and active after redeployment is an expectation of the advisor network. 

##### **THE COMPLACENCY OF COMPETITION** 

3-59. During competition, routine activities occur at a predictable pace and day-to-day operations are often in a lower risk to force, direct effort-to-output gains environment. The stability and repetitive nature of competition invites complacency. Some efficiencies and techniques used in competition that are outside of approved SOPs may present little apparent risk in that OE. However, the use of such methods (for example, a reliance on unencrypted civilian cell phones for official meetings and reports) represent tremendous risk to mission and force while in crises or conflict. Such methods, while expeditious, should still be avoided even in competition. Avoiding complacency while in competition prevents the accumulation of bad habits and will allow for quicker adaptation when routines change and enable enduring advisor success. 

##### **PREDEPLOYMENT IN CRISIS** 

3-60. In the event of a crisis, SFABs may get very little notification or time to execute predeployment activities. SFABs may be required to significantly alter their predeployment timeline or desired predeployment details because of resource constraints or the inherent uncertainty of the AOR. Some priority recommendations from ATP 3-07.10 include the following: 

- Redeployment is a collaborative effort and selecting the most effective practical method of collaboration is key. 

- Follow OPSEC when collaborating with all external parties. 

- Consult with legal advisor, plan, and identify necessary authorities; discuss use of force, intel sharing, targeting, hostile actor handling, and medical support. 

- Identify, prioritize, and plan for risk; implement deliberate risk mitigation measures for risk to force, mission, and political risk. 

- Develop an assessment plan and use the Joint Planning Process to reinforce DOD’s Assessment, Monitoring and Evaluation policy and guidelines. 

##### **DEPLOYMENT IN CRISIS** 

3-61. RSOI remains critical to successful movement to, within, and out of an AOR. While a purposeful and deliberate RSOI is desirable, because of the wide array of complexities and breadth of challenges created by crisis, an SFAB team must be willing to accept uncertainty and risk to get into position. Leaders within SFAB can excel in crisis by learning deeply about the emerging OE, anticipating likely resource shortfalls, and adapting the existing deployment framework to the specific complexities of the AOR and the nature of the crisis.

3-62. RSOI in crisis differs from RSOI in competition by the degree of uncertainty that must be considered and accepted. Simplicity is paramount. RSOI must be tailorable to fit multiple uncertainties within both the realm of host-nation requirements and the diversity of risks to mission and force that crisis poses. 

3-63. The planned APOE, SPOE, POD, SPOD may be untenable because of the constraints of the crisis and the SFAB should consider multiple contingencies to get where they are needed. SFABs must compete with conventional Army elements within the AOR to use whatever logistics and transportation networks are available. SFABs must consider the unavailability of these resources and consider ACSAs as an alternative method to ensure RSOI is successful. 

##### **REDEPLOYMENT IN CRISIS** 

3-64. Redeployment operations in crisis may differ greatly from redeploying in competition. SFABs that initially deployed while in competition must consider the possibility of redeployment out of an AOR in crisis. Understanding the critical steps of the previous redeployment plan before the crisis is essential to execute an effective departure from the AOR. SFABs must deliberately prioritize the essential redeployment tasks and options to mitigate the risks therein. Transfers of follow-on forces or conducting a hasty relief in place may be essential to advisor team safety and mission success of the incoming U.S. personnel. For example, administrative and evaluation subtasks may be abbreviated in-country or occur predominantly after arrival at home station. In the case of escalation, SFABs may be forced to transition to a conflict-centric redeployment plan. Contingencies for both possibilities should be incorporated into the initial plan and ongoing evaluation criteria. 

##### **PREDEPLOYMENT IN CONFLICT** 

3-65. During conflict, advisors consider the risks to mission and force of a contested entry into the AOR. Predeployment considerations in conflict require the SFAB to consider the ramifications of bringing military equipment and personnel into a contested AOR and joining the partner force amid ongoing combat operations. SFABs deliberately plan for predeployment by learning not just about the FSF they will join, but also the adversary and the conflict itself. An emphasis on the legalities of SFAB personnel engaging in combat operations and the use of force is necessary to prepare for a successful entry into a conflict zone. 

3-66. Considerations also include the fact that the SFABs will have to compete for transportation and mobility resources. SFABs will likely be lower in priority than conventional BCTs and may need to exploit ACSA channels if other methods are unavailable. An in-person advanced echelon element site survey may be untenable in predeployment to conflict; as a result, leveraging U.S. intelligence assets in garrison is the SFAB’s primary means to gather required information. A secondary source for this information is the host nation itself. Communications with the partner force should be direct, specific, and followed-up in writing acknowledging the partner’s decreased bandwidth to answer information requests during conflict. 

##### **DEPLOYMENT IN CONFLICT** 

3-67. SFAB deployment in conflict must account for the almost certain unavailability or absence of U.S. Forces to conduct formal RSOI. Robust communication with partner forces will be vital to a successful deployment. RSOI in conflict must be conducted with force protection in mind and will likely occur in a disaggregated fashion to mitigate the risk of allowing the adversary to detect large arrivals of SFAB personnel. Disaggregated RSOI in conflict relies heavily on a thorough understanding of the OE and the partner force to enable rapid and deliberate integration into the conflict zone. 

##### **REDEPLOYMENT IN CONFLICT** 

3-68. Redeployment in conflict is when an SFAB returns from the AOR while the partner force is still engaging in conflict operations. The SFAB may return alongside conventional U.S. units or independently. Redeployment in conflict can occur with either follow-on advisors executing a relief in place or with a termination or reduction of the advisor mission. If an advisor team arrives to continue the advising mission, a relief in place must be conducted to consolidate the gains the departing team created. As in crisis, other redeployment tasks like administrative and evaluation tasks may be abbreviated or occur predominantly after arrival at home station.

##### **Chapter 4** 

### **Advisor Team Activities and Employment** 

_It is my assessment, and the assessment of the Secretary and the assessment of the Army staff, that we are likely to be involved in train, advise, and assist operations for many years to come._ 

General Mark Milley, 39th Chief of Staff of the Army 

Chapter 4 describes the advisor team activities (both developmental and conflict integration) and advisor team employment planning considerations. 

##### **1st SFAB in Tunisia** 

<mark>The 1st SFAB's Tunisia Advising Team arrived in country in August 2020 with the goal of achieving positive effects and setting follow-on conditions for the next SFAB unit by October 2020. It was a tall order, but one that was achievable with a good plan. The task force commander immediately determined that the key to success would be starting with an in-depth assessment of the partner's situation, with follow-on efforts focused on quick wins before the end of the fiscal year.</mark> 

<mark>What BAT 120 discovered was a mismatch between the amount of U.S. investment into Tunisia and the ability of the U.S. Embassy Office of Security Cooperation (OSC) to take advantage of it. In summary, the small OSC staff of six to eight U.S. military personnel did not have the capacity to address the rapidly expanding U.S. materiel and systems commitments coming in from both U.S. Africa Command and the U.S. State Department. This had resulted in experimental elements like the Tunisian Joint Operations Command Center possessing U.S. technical solutions but lacking both in purpose and in Tunisian military personnel to man it. The BAT further identified a command structure that was stove-piped between three separate Tunisian JTFs, none of which reported to any convening higher headquarters, which prevented a common operating picture and unity of effort between the three of them. Between these observations and other, more tactical observations regarding air-to-ground integration (AGI) processes, military intelligence education, and C-IED training opportunities for the Tunisian Groupe des Forces Speciales (GFS), BAT 120 was able to provide a course-correction recommendation to the U.S. Embassy and the Tunisian military.</mark> 

<mark>As a result of BAT 120's efforts, the defense attaché (DATT) and the OSC combined efforts to promote a strategic shift for the Tunisian military to achieve “readiness for current and future threats, synchronize investments to achieve optimal return, and cultivate efforts to achieve regional and U.S. interoperability.” The DATT and OSC announced this strategic shift to the Tunisian military leaders on 6 October 2020 at the 34th annual Joint Military Commission. With this announcement, the U.S. Embassy team and Tunisian military charted a new path forward that would build on each other's strengths and make full use of the resources available.</mark> 

<mark>Major Christopher R. Thielenhaus</mark> Infantry Magazine 

##### **SECTION I – SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE DEVELOPMENTAL ACTIVITIES** 

4-1. The SFAB’s greatest strength is flexibility of advisor employment to problem solve and develop solutions to the needs of FSF partners and support the CCDR's SFA requirements to advance U.S. interests. This section describes SFA developmental activities. The developmental activities are primarily used during competition. However, commanders may decide to conduct the developmental activities in conjunction with the conflict integration activities is when the mission variables and OE permit. This section also describes the additional employment considerations for staff and advisor team leaders when planning for SFA developmental activities. 

4-2. SFA is meant to provide a capability that a foreign defense establishment does not have the ability to provide on its own. SFA activities and missions are planned, coordinated, and executed across the executive, generating, and operational functions in permissive and uncertain environments. In some cases, the United States provides SFA as a bridging solution until the host nation organizes and develops the capability on its own. The United States can provide SFA until U.S. objectives are met, or until the foreign defense establishment addresses the capability shortfall. In other instances, the United States may provide SFA to meet specific U.S. and foreign defense establishment requirements without the intent of organizing that capability within that host nation’s foreign defense establishment. In these cases, an enduring foreign defense establishment capability is neither required, nor is it in the best interest of the United States. 

4-3. The general SFA developmental activities are organize, train, equip, build, and advise (OTEBA). Each element of these activities can be used to develop, change, or improve the capability and capacity of the partner FSF, as well as enable overall interoperability. By assessing the FSF through the lens of U.S. interests and objectives, coupled with shared interests of multinational partners, U.S. Forces can determine which area or areas within the OTEBA construct can improve the FSF to the desired capability and capacity. SFA planners should determine what is in the best interests of the multinational partners involved, or “what is good enough” to achieve shared objectives. Most critically, U.S. Forces assess the partner FSF against desired capabilities and then develop an OTEBA plan to help them build capability and capacity (see FM 3-22). 

4-4. An initial baseline assessment of partner FSF capability and capacity is essential to the design of the OTEBA approach. Assessment activities are critical in determining quantitative and qualitative progress of the partner FSF toward a desired end state. 

#### **ORGANIZE** 

4-5. Organization of partner FSF includes all activities taken to create, improve, and integrate doctrinal principles, organizational structures, and personnel management. This may include doctrine development, unit or organizational design, mission command and staff processes, and methods and policies for recruiting and manning (see FM 3-22). This includes adopting policies and plans that allow for meaningful participation of women in the FSF. 

4-6. The organize task should address the whole of the partner FSF from the executive level to the smallest tactical formation. The goal of the organize task is to improve the partner FSF organizational structure, processes, institutions, and infrastructure. To meet this end state, SFA planners and practitioners should understand the existing FSF organizational structure. Consideration should be given to how the partner FSF conduct operations before determining the best methods to address new and emerging security requirements. SFA planners and practitioners should be knowledgeable in all aspects of OTEBA, as these related tasks will likely play an important role in the organizational development of the partner security establishment. 

**_Note._** SFA can be used to develop the capability and support the development of the foreign defense establishment’s ability to generate forces through the establishment of processes, institutions, and infrastructure. SFA can also be used to develop and improve partner FSF organizational processes in force development, force integration, capabilities integration, budgeting, and personnel management. 

##### **PLAN** 

4-7. The partner nation’s social and economic conditions, cultural and historical factors, and security threats have significant implications for the organizing effort. SFA objectives should be tailored to support the SC goals between the United States and the partner nation in accordance with the TSCP. In some cases, this is relatively limited, short duration training and assistance to competent and well-established partners. In other cases, it entails long-term, comprehensive joint and interagency efforts to rebuild fractured societies, which aim to support the creation of an efficient organization with appropriate staff structure and supporting institutions. In addition to developing a standard unit structure across the partner forces, the doctrine, policies, systems, and SOPs should ideally be uniform among their units as well. As the partner force matures, U.S. leaders and trainers should expect and encourage independent organizational decisions. Ultimately, the partner nation determines the structure of its security forces including approving all organizational designs. These may include changing the numbers of forces, types of units, equipment, and internal organizational designs. 

4-8. Supporting infrastructure that provides the organizing and generating requirements of the partner forces are key to long-term success. SFAB commanders and advisor teams may work with various ministries and organizations that are responsible for the partner nation’s national security. The supported nation may have a variety of representatives participate in organizational planning, including representatives from the MOD, finance, interior, justice, public works, transportation, culture, economic development, and international relations and cooperation, for example. SFAB commanders may request augmentation from ministerial advisor teams to assess strengths, weaknesses, and joint requirements of the ministerial-level organizations. When gaps are identified, the joint and multinational ministry teams assist the partner nation in developing the appropriate systems and capabilities. These efforts could include collaboration with other joint and multinational advisor teams and may include several topic areas, including procurement, personnel, intelligence, finance, legal, and other national-level management systems. 

##### **PREPARE** 

4-9. Organizing partner forces generally involves assessing and developing several areas. The list is not allinclusive. (See FM 3-22 for more information.) The following information is only relevant when building a partner force from the ground up: 

- Recruiting and hiring. 

- Promotion screening and selection. 

- Pay and benefits. 

- Leader recruiting and selection. 

- Personnel accountability. 

- Demobilizing security force personnel. 

##### **EXECUTE** 

4-10. During the execution and monitoring of the organize task, advisor teams must exercise patience. This can be an enduring task and may take several years to reach the desired end state. Advisor teams should establish intermediate goals to track the effectiveness of the organizing plans and continually assess progress toward these milestones. SFAB elements should accumulate data pertinent to the task and measure that information through appropriate MOPs and MOEs. As the advisor teams monitor the security force actions while executing this task, they may have to adjust their guidance to the partner force as they work towards their stated goals and end state. 

##### **ASSESS** 

4-11. SFAB advisor teams conduct end-of-mission assessments at the conclusion of key organizing tasks and phases. Due to the long-term nature of this process, it may be appropriate to establish recurring quarterly reviews to evaluate progress. 

Some considerations for assessing the organizing tasks by the advisor teams are as follows: 

- Identifying the organizing end state and ensuring that the SFAB personnel and the foreign security personnel share the same vision. 

- Establishing a battle rhythm with the partner force for reviewing the organizing tasks, including the following: 

 - Updating assessments. 

 - Reviewing and adjusting milestones. 

 - Instituting new planning efforts. 

- Documenting and reporting the following to the next-higher U.S. commander: 

 - Incidents of corruption. 

 - Gross inefficiency. 

 - Violations of human rights. 

 - Actions of the partner force or government officials who habitually hinder operations through incompetence, self-interest, or suspected sympathy for the enemy or adversary groups. 

 - Sexual and gender-based violence. 

4-12. The advisor team completes an AAR and submits it to its higher headquarters. The SFAB staff reviews and submits it to the country team and higher headquarters to assist in the continued development of the partner forces’ capabilities. 

##### **Training in the Philippines** 

<mark>USAFFE [U.S. Army Forces Far East] was concerned by the slow pace of training in the mobilized regiments. “At the rate they’re moving,” said Vic Collier, [MAJ James V. Collier, Assistant G-3, USAFFE] “the regiments won’t be ready to receive divisional inductees on 15 November! I don’t know how we can speed things up—we just don’t have the trained leaders.</mark> 

<mark>The inductees need training time,” said Col Irwin [Col Constant L. Irwin, G-3, USAFFE]. “Though they all received five and a half months training at one time, few fired more than five rounds of live ammunition! Fewer still remember much of their training. Yet in eight weeks we expect them to master the use of tanks, flame-throwers, self-propelled artillery, and radio!</mark> ” 

<mark>In response to Irwin’s appeal, Sutherland [BG Richard K. Sutherland, Chief of Staff, USAFFE] said, “The Old Man [MacArthur] would like to give you more time, but the world situation won’t let him. In spite of the risk of weakening some American combat units, he is sending 500 officers to the training regiments. This should help.” On 15 November 90,000 [Filipino] reservists were ordered to report at ten partially completed mobilization centers. USAFFE crossed its fingers.</mark> 

<mark>Stephen M. Mellnick</mark> Philippine Diary, 1939-1945 

#### **EQUIP** 

4-13. Equip refers to all activities to create, improve, and integrate materiel and equipment, procurement, fielding, accountability, and maintenance through life cycle management. Security assistance is the primary means to enable the equip task and SFA activities support this by focusing on the development of partner FSF processes for new equipment fielding, employment, operational readiness, maintenance and repair, and recapitalization. 

4-14. The country team, senior defense official/DATT drives this objective through the foreign military sales program. Advisor teams can provide points of reference or facilitate the communication of specific needs. 

##### **PLAN** 

4-15. Advisor teams are responsible for identifying shortfalls in the foreign capability, communicating those shortfalls higher, assisting the FSF in requesting the appropriate equipment to correct those shortfalls, and ensuring the end user receives the proper equipment and training. The TSCP should guide equipment requirements. Equipment should be affordable, sustainable, and suitable for combating the threat. Foreign forces must be able to train on the equipment. Consider interoperability with other UAPs as well. 

##### **PREPARE** 

4-16. The requirements under the equipping task may be as simple as assisting with maintaining existing weapons or as extensive as providing everything from a Soldier’s individual military gear (including femalespecific gear) to an entire combat vehicle fleet. The FSF should be supplied with equipment that is comparable, or preferably better than, the enemy’s equipment. Their equipment should also be interoperable with the U.S. Forces and multinational partners. This may be challenging for military and law enforcement agencies in uncertain environments, who are often lightly armed and vulnerable to well-armed enemies. 

4-17. Primary considerations should include maintainability, ease of operation, and long-term sustainment costs. Few developing nations can support highly complex or expensive equipment. In many cases, having a larger number of versatile vehicles with simple maintenance requirements is often better than having a few highly capable armored vehicles or combat systems that require extensive maintenance. Contractors often perform FSF maintenance processes for new equipment. Develop the foreign sustainment and maintenance programs through training, guidance, and supervision. 

##### **EXECUTE** 

4-18. Materiel and equipment sources include U.S. foreign military sales, multinational or third-nation resale, contracts with internal suppliers, or purchases on the international market. The organizations responsible for equipping the FSF should obtain equipment that meets their needs for quality, timeliness, and cost. As part of their training, they should teach the FSF property accountability procedures to reduce corruption and to safeguard resources, as well as recommend that the FSF maintain and safeguard their equipment according to their laws and regulations. In the absence of defined foreign procedures, they should advise the foreign leaders on the implementation of basic accountability and security procedures using U.S. policies as an initial framework. 

4-19. The SFAB should monitor and assist the FSF with new equipment training. This may include assisting the FSF to establish the appropriate contracts with external organizations, monitoring the FSF’s internal new equipment training program, and, in some cases, providing trainers from the SFAB. 

##### **ASSESS** 

4-20. Advisor teams continuously assess the FSF and team effectiveness during the developmental activities. Advisor teams provide a written summary of these continuous assessments at the conclusion of key equipping tasks and phases. Some considerations for conducting an equipping task assessment are discussed in paragraphs 4-21 through 4-22. 

###### **Identify the Foreign Security Forces Equipping End State** 

4-21. This should include a comparison of the foreign equipment relative to the enemy’s equipment, identifying gaps, and presenting possible solutions to the foreign and U.S. higher headquarters. 

###### **Review of Equipping Tasks and Plans** 

4-22. A schedule for reviewing the equipping tasks should be established with foreign leaders. The equipping plan should also be adjusted in conjunction with the foreign leaders. 

###### **Documentation of Wrongdoing** 

4-23. Document and report the following to the next-higher U.S. commander: 

- Incidents of corruption. 

- Gross inefficiency. 

- Violations of human rights. 

- Actions of the FSF or government officials who habitually hinder operations through—  Incompetence. 

 - Self-interest. 

 - Suspected sympathy for the enemy or adversary groups. 

- Sexual and gender-based violence. 

###### **Complete After Action Review** 

4-24. Advisor teams complete an equipping AAR and submit it to their higher headquarters. The SFAB staff reviews and submits it to the country team or higher headquarters, assisting in the continued development of FSF’s capabilities. 

#### **TRAIN** 

4-25. Train refers to all activities taken to create, improve, and integrate training, leader development, and education across the executive-generating operating functions. These may include SFA activities to develop partner FSF execution of programs of instruction, training events, and leader development activities. 

4-26. Advisors develop partner FSF training programs and enable sustainability in training and leader development institutions. These training efforts should consider the unique character and leader requirements of the host nation. The training approach should be tailored to meet FSF objectives and priorities and should also consider the preservation of the partner’s own DOTMLPF-P system versus recreating an unfamiliar and potentially unsustainable U.S. Government-style model. 

4-27. Advisors train partner forces on the knowledge, skills, and attributes appropriate to their level of function, organization, and equipment. SFABs should develop tailored programs of instruction that are appropriate for partner FSF. They should include a comprehensive assessment of the partner FSF being trained. 

4-28. As the capability and capacity of the FSF increases, SFABs can conduct combined training to develop the capacity for foreign force and U.S. Force interoperability. The goal of combined training is to develop partners with the capacity to function as part of a combined multidomain force with multinational objectives. Combined training exercises familiarize both forces with the capabilities and shortfalls of the other force and help develop procedures to leverage capabilities and mitigate shortfalls. 

##### **PLAN** 

4-29. Advisor teams aim to assist partner forces in reinforcing the legitimacy of their government and building confidence in the local population. When providing support to the partner force, advisor teams should first identify all resources, systems, institutions, and capabilities that are available to the partner forces and themselves in theater. Advisor teams should also understand the mission and operational variables that may influence their operations and ability to provide support. Understanding these factors allows advisor teams to plan for the likely support requirements that their foreign counterparts may ask them to provide. OE conditions can rapidly change. Therefore, advisor teams should anticipate potential support requests as the operation persists. 

4-30. Providing support to SFA operations often involves an array of UAPs, making unity of effort essential for success. SFA can include U.S. conventional and special operations forces, the partner force, nongovernmental organizations, and other civilian and military joint and multinational organizations. When planning, staffs and advisor teams pay particular attention to coordinating the various external capabilities integrating them into one cohesive effort. Network engagement tasks can assist SFAB commanders, staffs, and advisor teams with integrating the various entities found within the OE into the planning process. 

##### **PREPARE** 

Advisor teams receive training on the legal, technical, and tactical considerations for providing support to partner forces. Among others, training should include the following: 

- Information regarding current partner force capabilities. 

- Theater sustainment and support assets available. 

- Rules of engagement for employing fires and attack aircraft. 

- Rules or legal guidance for providing supplies, food, and medical support. 

4-31. Advisor teams and SFAB staffs must become familiar with the partner force organizations and equipment, particularly the capabilities of weapons and equipment not found in the U.S. inventory. SFAB commanders, advisor teams, and ASCC headquarters should consider incorporating the following support-related topics into their training plans: 

- Legal responsibilities and procedures when providing medical care to the partner forces, local civilians, and other personnel. 

- Financial obligations and limitations when providing logistic and contracting support. 

- Regulations and procedures regarding the employment of joint fires in support of foreign counterparts. 

- Understanding of legal reporting requirements, specifically regarding the following: 

 - Human rights violations. 

 - Corruption. 

 - Sexual and gender-based violence. 

 - Activities that may be detrimental to mission success or violate U.S. and international laws. 

- Understanding of foreign weapon system capabilities and limitations to provide appropriate implementation guidance to the partner force while conducting operations. 

- Understanding of Leahy vetting and human rights violations, such as the Child Soldier Protection Act. 

4-32. Like any other operation, advisor teams conduct rehearsals prior to providing support to the partner force. These rehearsals will often involve the foreign counterparts and representatives from other organizations that are providing the capability in addition to the advisor teams. For example, if a BCT is providing indirect fires in support of the advisor team’s operations with the partner forces, the fires staff of that BCT should be involved in the rehearsals prior to execution. 

##### **EXECUTE** 

4-33. Significant legal review and coordination is required prior to providing support to a partner force. SFAB commanders and staffs establish clear guidance and SOPs to the advisor teams to clarify requirements, reduce confusion, and expedite the support that is often critical to the success of a particular operation. 

4-34. Support provided to the partner force can vary across the competition continuum given the specifics of the OE. Support to the partner force generally decreases as capabilities and capacities increase. While success on the battlefield is critical, especially for the Soldiers conducting the fight, SFAB commanders and advisor teams must continuously assess the tactical need of the support for the current fight versus the long-term operational and strategic development of the capability within the partner force. If advisor teams provide external support every time a partner force makes a request, the partner force is less likely to develop its own capability. 

##### **ASSESS** 

4-35. Advisor team members continuously assess their advised counterparts, the outputs of this continuous assessment are commonly recorded in formal and informal written reviews in the form of AARs, reports to higher headquarters, and the team’s internal recordkeeping. In training assessments after providing support to their foreign counterparts are an essential part of the advisor’s role. 

When conducting AARs, consider the following: 

- Was the provided support or capability organic to the partner force organizations? 

 - If yes, why was it not used? 

 - If not, is the capability being developed? 

- How often did the foreign counterparts request similar support in the past 30 days and how often was the support provided? 

- How significant was the support on the operation? 

 - What were the effects? 

 - Could those effects have been achieved with another asset or capability? 

 - Does the partner force have access to support that provides similar effects? 

- Document and report to the next-higher U.S. commander: 

 - Incidents of corruption. 

 - Gross inefficiency. 

 - Violations of human rights. 

 - Actions that hinder operations through incompetence, self-interest, or suspected sympathy for the enemy or adversary groups. 

 - Sexual and gender-based violence. 

4-36. The advisor teams complete the AARs and submit them to their higher headquarters. SFAB leaders review AARs and submit them to the country team to assist with the continued development of the partner force. 

##### **Training in the Philippines, continued** 

<mark>The locations of Bounce’s [MAJ William “Bounce” Ball, Acting Commander of the 86th Field Artillery Battalion (Philippine Scouts) in the Luzon Force] units violated every rule in the book. Normally, 155mm guns are sited three miles in the rear of the front line. But the terrain and the single road forces Bounce to place them near the front line! “That makes or exciting living,” he said philosophically. “We ring out guns with barbed wire so that crews can defend themselves. And we don’t wait for snipers and patrols to attack us; we go out at night and ambush them as they leave their line. Our big worry is “Photo Joe” the Japanese reconnaissance pilot. He smothers us with artillery if we’re the least bit careless.</mark> ” 

<mark>While crawling in and out of gun pits, I observed numerous indications of a disciplined fighting machine. Could Bounce have created it out of raw Filipino recruits in less than five months? He did. A tense atmosphere pervaded the units. Hard-faced men spoke briefly and rapidly; they had no time for pleasantries. They moved with catlike stealth and with weapons at the ready. Their eyes searched the jungle shadows constantly. They were lean, taut, super-alert killers.</mark> 

<mark>“We live under a strain,” said Bounce simply. “It’s the price we pay to survive. The enemy probes constantly; some get through in spite of our care. Then a bullet sings and someone dies. Sometimes I wonder why we try so hard. I guess it’s pride. The enemy will take over eventually, I suppose. My men suspect it. But we’ll both be double-damned before we let them win by default! You feed us and we’ll hold on till the Navy shows up.</mark> ” 

<mark>I felt humbled by Bounce’s calmness and dedication. And I envied the magic he used to inspire his men. Though I had read books on leadership and listened to many lectures on the subject, I rarely saw that quality demonstrated so effectively.</mark> 

<mark>Stephen M. Mellnick</mark> Philippine Diary, 1939-1945 

##### **Building the Korean Army** 

<mark>The Officers and men who constituted America’s commitment to a Korean army beginning in 1946 had invented the ROK’s military organization from scratch. The ROKA (and its predecessor, the Korean Constabulary) was their creation. With much effort the Americans gave it identity and nurtured it during a truly forgotten civil war that flared up in April 1948 and was not extinguished until March 1950. They trained it, supported it, and watched it nearly die in the summer of 1950 and again a year later. Over the following twelve months, under new leadership and with a new emphasis to train and mentor, KMAG reformed itself and its protégé. The results were impressivea holistic administrative strategy from new-soldier recruitment to individual and unit training that made ROK units tougher; a revamped leadership committed to professionalism and learning how to wage modern war; sixfold expansion carefully managed that resulted in increasing ROK manpower just when the Communists had peaked out; the formation of combat-ready divisions capable of entering the line and slugging it out with veteran Communist units. When the ROKA-KMAG partnership produced a state of military equilibrium capable of withstanding the hammer blows of the Chinese armies, the Communists had to acknowledge the full implications of military deadlock.</mark> 

<mark>Bryan R. Gibby</mark> 

The Will to Win: American military advisors in Korea, 1946-1953 

#### **BUILD AND REBUILD** 

4-37. Build and rebuild refers to all activities creating, improving, and integrating facilities. This may include SFA activities to develop partner FSF capability and capacity to construct or repair physical infrastructures such as bases and stations, lines of communication, ranges and training complexes, and administrative structures. 

4-38. Specific tasks will vary according to the mission’s goals, objectives, and desired end state.
