The host-nation’s priorities and their ability to sustain what is constructed should be carefully assessed and included in the plan. Since the build and rebuild task often involves physical construction and protection of facilities, effectiveness will also depend on the allocation of adequate resources by the host nation. 

##### **PLAN** 

4-39. The build and rebuild task refers to all activities related to restoring and developing FSF capability, capacity, and supporting infrastructure (see FM 3-22). This task requires an in-depth analysis of the capability, capacity, and structures required to meet the desired end state. The specific tasks vary according to the mission’s goals and objectives in addition to the partner nation’s priorities, resources, and its ability to maintain the infrastructure. This task often involves construction of facilities, transportation networks, communication systems, utilities, and a wide variety of other infrastructure projects that support the nation. While every situation is unique, success largely depends on long-range planning, prioritizing projects, and allocation of sufficient resources through the partner nation or the international political process. Advisor teams might be asked to assist or advise in complex project management tasks that support build and rebuild projects. 

##### **PREPARE** 

4-40. The FSF need facilities for storage, maintenance, training, and shelter. This includes secure facilities that allow for classified information storage, planning, and communications and other facilities such as barracks, ranges, and motor pools. Training centers and unit garrisons require a long-term security force-basing plan. The partner nation government and its senior ministries will make these determinations and provide the required resources. As part of their initial assessment, advisor teams may review their foreign counterpart’s security infrastructure. 

##### **EXECUTE** 

4-41. Construction takes significant time; therefore, it is ideal if the partner nation invests early in building or rebuilding key facilities. Advisor teams assist the FSF with developing plans to protect and maintain infrastructure. Recommend prioritizing facilities for protection and allocating resources accordingly. Key facilities include headquarters buildings, major sustainment facilities, barracks, political institutions, major utilities, police stations, and culturally significant landmarks. The partner nation may need to make large investments in time and resources to restore, create, and maintain the local and nationwide infrastructure necessary to command, control, and maintain its security forces effectively. 

##### **ASSESS** 

4-42. Advisor teams should provide assessments on relevant infrastructure in their counterpart’s AO. After the initial assessment, monitoring the building and rebuilding tasks generally consists of sending occasional progress reports higher. The advisor teams should consider the following list of considerations (in paragraphs 4-43 through 4-45) when assessing these two tasks. 

###### **Assess Infrastructure Capability Gaps** 

4-43. Alongside their foreign counterparts, advisor teams discuss and prioritize infrastructure capability gaps. These priorities are then submitted through the appropriate channels, resources requested, and resulting build and rebuild activities monitored. 

###### **Documentation of Wrongdoing** 

4-44. Advisor teams document and report to the next-higher U.S. commander incidents of— 

- Corruption. 

- Gross inefficiency. 

- Violations of human rights. 

- Incidents of sexual and gender-based violence. 

- The actions of the FSF or government officials who habitually hinder operations through the following: 

 - Incompetence. 

 - Self-interest. 

 - Suspected sympathy for the enemy or adversary groups. 

###### **Complete After Action Review** 

4-45. The advisor teams complete an AAR and submit it to their higher headquarters. The SFAB reviews and submits the AAR to the country team or higher headquarters, assisting in the continued development of FSF capabilities. 

#### **ADVISE** 

4-46. Advise refers to all SFA activities that provide subject matter expertise, guidance, advice, and counsel to partner FSF while carrying out the missions assigned to the unit or organization. Advising may occur across the executive, generating and/or operating functional structure. Advising can also occur under combat or administrative conditions, and in support of individuals or groups. SFA advisors provide expertise to the partner FSF in the development, sustainment, and employment of their security enterprise to meet their national security needs, in support of U.S. interests. Advising establishes a personal and a professional relationship where trust and confidence define how well the advisor will be able to influence the partner FSF. 

##### **PLAN** 

4-47. While advisor teams may be capable of advising two levels higher, the mission analysis determines whether that would be appropriate. An experienced, professional, and well-established FSF generally only require pairing at the advisor team’s organic level or perhaps one level higher. A less experienced foreign force allows the advisor teams to advise one, or even two, echelons higher. For example, an SFAB company commander should be able to advise a foreign brigade commander, while a brigade staff could advise a foreign corps staff. This capability drives the increased rank and experience requirements for serving in the SFAB. During mission analysis, commanders and staffs must weigh the foreign experience level, the effects the SFAB is trying to achieve, resources and time available, and other relevant factors to determine the best lay down for the advisor teams. As with all missions, commanders must assess risk. That includes considering whether the advisor teams have the appropriate experience to advise their counterparts versus being able to influence the maximum number of FSF organizations. 

4-48. Planned operations are coordinated with the FSF. An indication of an effective advising effort is the amount of personal involvement the FSF takes in its own operations. The civilian population should see that its own security forces secure it. This promotes the legitimacy of the host-nation government and builds trust and confidence in the security forces. 

4-49. SFAB advisor teams teach, coach, and advise in a cycle. As the FSF become proficient in a skill, the advisor can move on to other skills and tasks. Whether the FSF struggles and requires retraining on a specific task or quickly learns another task, the advisor team can adjust the training plan in conjunction with the foreign leaders. As the proficiency of the FSF increases, the advisors spend less time conducting training and more time coaching, advising, and monitoring the FSF training. 

4-50. Advisors must understand the organization of the local government and the influence of their advising activities. Advisor teams commonly operate in complex environments via interdependent operations. Interdependent operations require near-seamless integration of agencies representing the instruments of national power—diplomatic, informational, military, and economic. Usually, a set of agreements exists between various local agencies, security forces, and the national government. These agreements provide a coordinated effort in servicing the various needs of civilian populations. If necessary, the advisor may work with other agencies to assist in getting such agreements made. The brigade staff (including CA, strategic planner, SPO, S-8) in conjunction with the country team and the ASCC headquarters may work together to develop required agreements. If such agreements already exist and they are conducive to mission success, advisors and ASCC planners should recognize and strengthen them.

4-51. Advisors will need to remain flexible and able to problem solve outside their MOS, especially in cases of first contact with a partner force and establishing groundwork for future operations. Advisors are building relationships to advance U.S and partner force interests. 

4-52. Legitimacy is a concept that goes beyond a strict legal definition; it includes the moral and political legitimacy of a government, group, or organization. A government’s legitimacy is built upon the local and international community perceptions, which are critical in developing and maintaining local and international support. The United States may not be able to sustain a long-term relationship with a nation if it lacks a legitimate government. 

##### **PREPARE** 

4-53. Defining success in an SFA environment is difficult. Tracking and measuring progress on a day-to-day basis is not easy. Achieving a tactical objective does not, in and of itself, equate to mission success. Advisors achieve success when the FSF achieve the goals and objectives as described in their commander’s vision. The FSF should plan and execute their own training and operations with an advisor providing advice, coaching, and oversight. 

4-54. Advisors prepare for operations by reviewing internal and FSF SOPs. They study relevant U.S. doctrine, foreign doctrine, and collective tasks. Advisors must be subject matter experts on a variety of subjects. 

4-55. Advisor teams study their foreign counterparts, to include cultural norms, history of the region and people, and other relevant information that allows them to establish rapport. This includes understanding leadership processes, human nature, social and ender dynamics, and motivations. Information may take the form of books, staff rides, videos, reports, discussions, previous operational reports, professional speakers, and other research materials. Qualitatively measure rapport through advisors’ abilities to motivate their counterparts to take a desired action. 

###### **Avoiding Advisor Dilemmas** 

4-56. Advisor dilemmas are difficult situations in which advisors receive guidance from commanders that causes undue tension with either their partner forces or higher headquarters. Without resolution or amendment, these dilemmas cause advisors to fail to either meet the commander’s intent or compromise their credibility and rapport with their counterparts. The following are six types of common advisor dilemmas: 

- Conflicts of capability occur when the partnered FSF lacks the capability or capacity to accomplish the commander’s intent of the advisor team’s higher headquarters. 

- Conflicts of intent occur when the commander’s intent of the advisor team’s higher headquarters contradicts the FSF commander’s intent. 

- Conflicts of direction occur when the commander’s intent of the advisor team’s higher headquarters contradicts the explicit direction of the FSF’s higher headquarters. 

- Conflict of authority occurs when the FSF lacks the authority to accomplish the commander’s intent of the advisor team’s higher headquarters. 

- Conflict of responsibility occurs when the commander’s intent of the advisor team’s higher headquarters falls outside the FSF’s scope of responsibility. 

- Conflicts of priority occur when the commander’s intent of the advisor team’s higher headquarters is inconsistent with other SFA priorities and guidance received by the advisor team. 

4-57. Advisors and their commanders share responsibility for avoiding or resolving advisor dilemmas. Advisors and their commanders avoid advisor dilemmas through clear and open communication, understanding FSF capabilities, limitations, intentions, norms, and priorities, and fostering an environment conducive to advisor-commander dialogue. 

###### **Advising Guidance** 

4-58. Commanders and other stakeholders (for example, senior defense official/DATT, ASCC) should provide clear intent to their advisors to allow them flexibility to execute their mission with the FSF. The proximity of the advisors, intensity of the conflict, frequency of contact, and various legal considerations will provide the broad context for the SFA operations. These criteria generally do not change quickly or dramatically. However, advisor teams will often adjust their integration into the FSF’s tactical formations and the support provided, sometimes from one mission to the next. 

4-59. Advising guidance allows commanders to specify their intent to their subordinate advisors by describing how they visualize the advisor team operating alongside the FSF. Like reconnaissance guidance, advising guidance allows commanders to specify quickly and adjust their intent regarding whether the advisors are maneuvering within the foreign tactical formations and whether advisors are actively providing access to external U.S. capabilities. The following are the four general conditions under which advisors operate: 

- Accompany and enable. 

- Separate and enable. 

- Accompany and refrain. 

- Separate and refrain. 

4-60. Accompany and separate—when authorized to accompany, advisors integrate themselves into the FSF’ tactical formations during the mission. Guidance to separate directs advisors to monitor the foreign operations often from a command post or another location outside of their foreign counterpart’s tactical formation. 

4-61. Enable and refrain—enable directs the advisors to plan, coordinate, and provide actively other external U.S. capabilities in support of the FSF and their mission. This external assistance often comes in the form of— 

- Intelligence. 

- Surveillance and reconnaissance. 

- Fire support. 

- Vertical lift assets. 

- MEDEVAC. 

- Other logistical support. 

4-62. Refrain directs the advisors to withhold external U.S. capabilities to the FSF. This is often because those resources are not available, the FSF have those capabilities, or as part of a larger effort to reduce the foreign counterpart’s reliance on U.S. capabilities. 

**_Note._** Refrain does not restrict external resources that may be available to support advisor teams themselves. 

4-63. Advising guidance is not meant to be static. Review and update advising guidance frequently to account for changing operational and mission variables. Figure 4-1 on page 104 provides additional detail and context. 

**Figure 4-1. Advising guidance** 

##### **EXECUTE** 

4-64. Advisors must know the doctrine, rules, and regulations that govern their actions while working with the FSF. U.S. advisors are masters of U.S. Army doctrine while understanding the doctrine, rules, and regulations that guide their foreign counterparts. Done well, the advisor can readily detect departures from both and recommend adjustments. 

4-65. It is important to remember that advisor teams do not command foreign personnel or units. Advisors provide advice, training, and when required, access to coalition resources via the support task discussed in paragraphs 4-153 through 4-165. Advisors do not lead FSF in combat. Advisor teams are responsible for C2 within their own team. Advisors should be prepared to coach the commander of the foreign unit, which could include working side-by-side during a combat engagement. 

4-66. Advisors have no command authority over the FSF and depend on credibility and relationships to influence foreign operations. Advisors often work with counterparts of higher rank than themselves. Advisors should consider the foreign unit’s experience and capabilities and carefully choose opportunities to inject or impart knowledge. Advisor teams must be confident in the information they present. Trust and rapport can be quickly lost if advisors provide incorrect information. Generally, foreign units value advisors who are subtle in their teaching, coaching, and mentoring. Advisors who create a climate in which foreign personnel feel they are teaching themselves often prove the most effective. 

4-67. Advisor teams achieve success by accomplishing their mission through their counterparts. They must establish rapport, trust, and relationships with their foreign counterparts to build influence. This requires advisors to focus on the Army doctrinal attribute of intellect, which includes an emphasis on mental agility, sound judgment, innovation, interpersonal tact, and expertise. Successful advisors have a genuine interest in other people. They smile, adhere to cultural norms, use proper names and titles, actively listen, discuss their interests, and make the other person feel important. 

4-68. Advising the counterpart to select a particular course of action is effective only if the counterpart trusts the advisor’s professional competency. Counterparts may question the advisor’s competence if they perceive the proposed solution as unrealistic. When laying out courses of action or recommendations, the advisor takes time to explain why the recommendations are executable and effective. 

4-69. As the FSF achieve success from its efforts and proficiency increases, it will naturally take a greater role in its own matters. The advisor may sometimes feel the foreign leaders want to assume more responsibility than they can effectively manage. Advisors ought to appreciate this growing confidence. Longterm success requires the foreign leaders accept more responsibility. Advisors adjust to the growing desires of the FSF to manage themselves and adjust their advising plans accordingly. 

4-70. Trust is a fundamental building block of human relationships and at the core of leadership. Followers must trust their leaders. This is especially true in combat, when subordinates must follow orders immediately to succeed on the battlefield. However, trust cannot be mandated; it must be earned. Advisors must earn the trust of their counterparts and trust them in return. Advisors build this trust over time through consistent, principle-centered behavior and actions that demonstrate honesty, integrity, trust, dignity, and respect for all people. 

4-71. The advisor teams must support the morale and confidence of the FSF. Committing poorly trained and badly led forces in combat may result in high casualties and invites tactical defeat. While defeat in a small operation may have little strategic consequence, these cumulative effects of numerous small tactical defeats may have serious strategic consequences. If the FSF fail, the local populace may begin to lose confidence in the host-nation government’s ability to protect it. As much as possible, the foreign counterparts begin with simpler operations. As their confidence and competence grows, the foreign counterparts will naturally assume missions that are more complex. Collaborating with joint, multinational, or other foreign units can help new forces build confidence in adjusting to combat stress. 

4-72. Advisor teams carefully monitor and evaluate the operational performance of an inexperienced organization to correct weaknesses quickly. If needed, the employment plan for the FSF must allow time for additional training after an operation to address training shortfalls. Gradually introducing units into combat allows advisors and the foreign leaders to identify poor leaders, conduct retraining, or take other actions. Advisors identify competent leaders and recommend them for greater authority and responsibility. 

##### **ASSESS** 

4-73. The AAR is an essential element of building FSF capabilities. The foreign commander, with assistance from the advisor team, should conduct the AAR. The AAR begins with a review of the operation or training goals. Advisor personnel guide the foreign leaders to ask leading questions, identify important tactical lessons, explore alternative courses of actions, keep to the teaching points, and make the AAR positive. Key personnel should be present. Guide the foreign commander to discuss not only what happened, but also why it happened. They should discuss the important tactical lessons learned, alternate courses of action the unit could have taken, and other important teaching points. (See The Leader’s Guide to AAR on the Army Training Network website.) 

4-74. Advisor teams coach the foreign commander to discuss activities and key decision points (sustain and improve) for each phase of the operation including information operations, battle drills, contingencies, and implied and specified tasks. Advisors guide the foreign commander to discuss general positive points with the group. The purpose of the group AAR is not to single out a leader, staff member, Soldier, or unit to criticize. Conclude with a plan to follow up and make improvements as necessary. 

4-75. The U.S. military culture of AARs to discuss all mistakes works for the U.S. military because its culture accepts the technique. However, many cultures do not accept criticisms of one person or group relative to another. Prior to conducting an AAR, advisors should discuss these issues with the foreign leaders to avoid embarrassing group situations. The advisor should encourage a productive environment for the member or unit to discuss improvements. When an issue is unit-wide or recurring, the advisor discusses the issue with the foreign leaders to find a solution. The foreign leader implements the agreed upon solution.

4-76. Advisors should never take credit for an idea; they should empower their counterpart instead. They discuss the issue and come to a solution together, even if the advisors think they have the solution beforehand. Advisor teams mentor their foreign counterparts towards operational independence. Empowering foreign counterparts builds confidence within their organization, and advisors build trust with their counterpart. As a result, the foreign counterpart will likely seek advisors’ guidance and more readily accept their view in the future. 

4-77. Advisors conduct an internal AAR, focusing on what the team can sustain and improve. Internal AAR (advisors only) topics include the following: 

- Conduct of advisors. 

- Conduct of the FSF (done prior to the foreign AAR if possible). 

- Review rules of engagement, escalation of force, and other protective MOEs. 

- Review effectiveness of parallel planning efforts. 

- Where did the advisors develop, sustain, and improve foreign capabilities or capacities? 

- Where were advisors located during the operation and was the arrangement successful? 

- Were advisors prepared for the operation, such as equipment, knowledge of mission, integration with FSF? 

4-78. The advisor team builds, submits, and archives the AAR. The advisor team then reviews and adjusts the assessment and development plan for the FSF as necessary. Finally, advisor teams deliver the report to their higher headquarters along with their analysis of the event relative to the assessment and development plan for the FSF. They compile and report acts of corruption, gross inefficiency, foreign personnel who hinder operations through incompetence, self-interest, or sympathy (proven or suspected) for the threat to the higher U.S. headquarters. 

##### **<mark>SECTION II – SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE CONFLICT INTEGRATION ACTIVITIES</mark>** 

Advising needs two things; an advisor and a means of communication. 

<mark>Major General Donn H. Hill 3rd Commanding General</mark> Security Force Assistance Command 

4-79. This section describes the SFA conflict integration activities. The conflict integration activities are primarily used in the conflict space but can be performed in response to crisis or to demonstrate partner force capabilities in competition. ASLA are the advisor team conflict integration activities as illustrated in figure 4-2. These activities are executed with partner forces and other UAPs in OEs spanning the competition continuum and, on order, can enable combat operations with partner forces. Whether demonstrating foreign partner capacity to deter armed conflict or fighting alongside the advisor team counterparts in LSCO, SFAB advisor teams play an important role in supporting U.S. objectives. 

**Figure 4-2. Security force assistance conflict integration activities** 

4-80. Advisor teams conduct the conflict integration activities (ASLA) in lieu of the SFA developmental activities (OTEBA) when dictated by the situation and the needs of the partner force. Figure 4-3 on page 108 shows the relationship between the FSF functions, SFA developmental activities, SFA conflict integration activities, and the resources available for the mission. An advisor team’s mission statement commonly includes one of (or multiple) SFA activities, the same way the terms “movement to contact,” “ambush,” and “raid” may be used when giving a mission to a conventional maneuver platoon. 

4-81. Through ASLA, but particularly through the liaise and support activities, SFABs build interoperability, maximize the availability of partner assets for the fight, enable joint force commanders' visualization, and inform decision points. SFABs facilitate transitions. 

**Figure 4-3. Security force assistance functions and advisor team activities** 

#### **ASSESS** 

##### **Assessing the training of the Korean army** 

<mark>In the summer of 1949 Roberts (Brigadier. General. William L. Roberts, Chief of KMAG from August 1948-June 1950) began a peninsula-wide tour of Korean army units from the southeastern port city of Pusan to the Ongjin peninsula in the northwest to inspect and evaluate the “dispositions and status of training” of the ROKA. He also directed inspection teams to visit the various KMAG detachments and their associated Korean units. These were not courtesy visits. They were in-depth inspections under the direction of the KMAG G-3, with each team led by a lieutenant colonel. These teams wrote comprehensive reports detailing their observations, unit status, and recommendations. Roberts used these evaluations and his personal observations to formulate a series of policy and procedural memoranda to his senior advisors, which KMAG headquarters then consolidated into a booklet called the</mark> _<mark>Advisor’s Handbook</mark>_ <mark>, issued to the filed specifically to disseminate Roberts’s guidance regarding training and advisorship.</mark> 

<mark>Bryan R. Gibby</mark> 

The Will to Win: American military advisors in Korea, 1946-1953 

4-82. Assess refers to all activities for determining progress toward accomplishing a task, creating an effect, or achieving an objective using MOEs and MOPs to evaluate partner force capability. Once an objective is achieved, the focus should shift to sustaining it. (See FM 3-22.) Assess is both an SFA and advisor team activity. 

4-83. Assessment is an important and continuous activity for all advisor teams. Advisor teams assess partner forces by evaluating, monitoring, and reporting on their foreign counterpart’s activities. Assessments provide information that allows commanders and staffs to properly task-organize advisor teams and weigh efforts to complete the assigned SFA mission. Regardless of the SFA function or task, advisor teams conduct assessments in support of their operations. These assessments form the foundation for the other advisor team activities. They also provide insight for their higher headquarters and external organizations. 

4-84. During armed conflict, operations usually reflect combinations of conventional and irregular warfare approaches. Leaders apply doctrine for LSCO during limited contingencies that require conventional warfare approaches. Irregular warfare includes counterinsurgency and unconventional warfare, which other publications specifically address. The initial actions of LSCO will likely overlap with actions initiated during competition and crisis (see FM 3-0). 

##### **ASSESS CONSIDERATIONS THROUGH THE COMPETITION CONTINUUM** 

4-85. _Assessment_ is a continuous process that measures the overall effectiveness of employing capabilities during military operations (JP 3-0). 

###### **Crisis** 

4-86. Crisis focuses on short-term capabilities and capacities specifically needed to address the emerging crisis and either return to a state of competition or properly prepare for conflict. 

###### **Armed Conflict** 

4-87. Armed conflict focuses on expanding advisor integration and interoperability to succeed in the ongoing conflict. 

###### **Large-Scale Combat Operations** 

4-88. The assessment process does not end if the situation transitions into LSCO. In fact, SFAB teams may be even more relied upon to provide assessments to (and on) partner forces during the execution of LSCO. The primary purpose in conducting LSCO assessments is to identify the risk associated in operational planning and assess the changes in the OE. OE conditions can change rapidly and those planning and those executing SFA activities must assess these changes continuously (see FM 3-22). 

4-89. While conducting assessment in LSCO, it is important that the planners for both U.S. Forces and multinational partners develop shared end states for both individual objectives and the wider mission. In essence, U.S. Forces assess the partner FSF against desired capabilities and then develop an OTEBA and/or ASLA plan to help partner FSF build that capability and capacity (see FM 3-22). 

4-90. The assessments process as executed by the SFAB is of particular use during armed conflict and LSCO by affording the JFLCC an additional method in which to consolidate gains. 

###### **Competition** 

4-91. Competition focuses on long-term capability and capacity development to increase the lethality, legitimacy, and professionalism of the FSF partner. 

##### **INITIAL ASSESSMENT** 

4-92. The SFAB continues assessing the theater planners by elaborating on the initial findings and continuing the work of a previous partnered force. The assessments are a part of a continuous process that incorporates input from the higher command, previous partnered forces, subordinate component commands, appropriate country teams, agencies outside DOD, and post-mission reports from previous U.S. military operations and training missions. (See FM 3-22.) 

4-93. Assessments require a command-wide effort, with the commander’s support and leadership being critical for success. Advisors must be the subject matter experts or have input from the appropriate subject matter experts from across the entire staff. For SFA missions, assessments may require close collaboration with members from various U.S. country teams within embassies, other U.S. stakeholders at the CCMDs and other Army commands, and possibly the partner nation government and other multinational partners.

4-94. Assessment should be integrated into the planning and execution of SFA. While planning is top-down, assessments are conducted bottom-up (see figure 4-4). SFAB staff planners and the advisor teams conducting the SFA mission must work together ensuring that they task and properly develop appropriate MOEs, MOPs, and indicators. Executing these tasks creates effects that achieve objectives to attain end states. Without the proper MOEs, MOPs, and indicators, commanders have difficulty determining if the tasks accomplished their intended purpose. 

4-95. SFAB commanders, staffs, and advisor teams ensure assessments have both quantitative (numerical) and qualitative (narrative) metrics. Conducting assessments is both an art and science. Advisors must examine the OE through multiple perspectives and solicit quantitative and qualitative input from a wide assortment of information and people. 

**Figure 4-4. Assessment interaction** 

##### **PLANNING FOR ASSESSMENT** 

4-96. Assessments evaluate and measure the progress of SFA activities toward a strategic end state. Assessments inform and assist commanders to make appropriate decisions pertaining to plans, resources, and execution. More specifically, assessments— 

- Allow commanders to gain situational understanding of the OE. 

- Outline how conditions have changed over a defined period. 

- Are used by commanders and staffs to determine objectives and requirements for mitigating and managing risk. 

- Assist commanders in determining progress toward attaining the desired end state, achieving objectives, and performing tasks. 

##### **ASSESSMENT CONSIDERATIONS** 

4-97. Determining what is the appropriate balance of qualitative and quantitative assessments within the campaign plans and country plans is necessary to begin the assessment process. At the strategic and operational levels, assessments typically become more qualitative. The availability of hard data may drive the frequency of quantitative assessments. 

4-98. Understanding how the specific SFA assessment nests within the theater campaign or country plan assessments helps to leverage and integrate government assessment efforts and to drive the demand for additional missions to support those plans. Understanding the balance between the assessments of task achievements (inputs), progress toward intermediate military objectives (outputs), and progress toward theater strategic end states (outcomes) is vital. 

4-99. Determining the best way to leverage or shape subordinate elements and external stakeholders to inform a holistic assessment is beneficial. Some methods of achieving this are the following: 

 - Involving commanders in the assessment process to include assessment requirements in plans and orders. 

 - Explaining the importance of assessments. 

 - Involving everyone in the assessment process and findings. 

- 4-100. While conducting assessments, advisor responsibilities include the following: • Providing candid feedback, objective reports, direct observations, and detailed assessments to commanders. 

 - Clearly distinguishing between reports, observations, and assessments. 

 - Avoiding bias and pessimism. 

 - Avoiding embellishment, undue optimism, and equivocation. 

 - Protecting sensitive and confidential partner force information and sources. 

 - Considering the source of information. 

 - Maintaining a healthy skepticism of partner force information and intent. 

 - Demonstrating prudent initiative in the face of adversity or uncertainty. 

 - • Avoiding intellectual paralysis or apathy. 

- 4-101. When receiving assessments, the higher headquarters should— 

 - Respect the difference between reports, observations, and assessments. 

 - Acknowledge natural blind spots in advisor awareness. 

 - Conservatively manage expectations. 

 - Be patient in pursuit of SFA objectives. 

 - Avoid creating advisor dilemmas. 

 - Faithfully relay advisor reports, direct observations, and assessments. 

 - Reengage advisors to clarify or update reports as needed. 

 - Protect sensitive foreign information and sources. 

 - Consider the source of information. 

 - Maintain healthy skepticism of partner forces’ information and their intent. 

4-102. In addition to the considerations listed above, staffs must remember that assessments serve the commander. Commanders must ultimately provide guidance and buy-in to the methodology. Proper assessment training across all staff elements, extensive time and effort, and rigorous investment in assessment resources are crucial to ensure success. 

##### **ASSESSMENT ACTIVITIES** 

4-103. The situation and type of operations affect the characteristics of assessment. During LSCO, assessment tends to be rapid, focused on the level of destruction of enemy units, terrain gained or lost, objectives secured, and the status of the friendly force to include sustainment. In other situations, such as counterinsurgency, assessment is less tangible. Assessing the level of security in an area or the level of the population’s support for the government is challenging. Identifying what and how to assess requires significant effort from the commander and staff. 

4-104. Whether conducting LSCO or stability operations, the process of continuous assessment consists of the following activities: 

- Monitoring the current situation while collecting relevant information. 

- Evaluating progress toward attaining end state conditions. 

- Identifying problems. 

- Achieving objectives. 

- Performing tasks. 

- Recommending or directing action for improvement. 

###### **Monitoring** 

4-105. Monitoring is continuous observation of those conditions relevant to the current operation. Monitoring allows staffs to collect relevant information, specifically that information about the current situation described in the commander’s intent and concept of operations. Commanders cannot judge progress nor make effective decisions without an accurate understanding of the current situation. 

4-106. Staffs and advisor teams monitor and collect information from the COP and friendly reports. This information includes operational and intelligence summaries from subordinate, higher, and adjacent headquarters and communications and reports from liaison teams. Staffs and advisor teams also identify information sources outside military channels and monitor their reports. These other channels might include products from civilian, host-nation, and other government agencies. Apply information management and knowledge management to facilitate disseminating this information to the right people at the right time. 

4-107. Record relevant information in running estimates. Staff sections and advisor teams maintain a continuous assessment of current operations as a basis to determine if operations are proceeding according to the commander’s intent, mission, and concept of operations. In their running estimates, staff sections and advisor teams use this new information and these updated facts and assumptions as the basis for evaluation. 

###### **Evaluating** 

4-108. Advisor teams and staffs analyze relevant information collected through monitoring to evaluate an operation’s progress. Evaluating is using indicators to judge progress toward desired conditions and determining why the current degree of progress exists. Evaluation is at the heart of the assessment process where most of the analysis occurs. Evaluation helps commanders and advisor teams determine what is working and what is not working, and it helps them gain insights into how to better accomplish the mission. 

4-109. An _indicator_ is, in the context of assessment, a specific piece of information that infers the condition, state, or existence of something, and provides a reliable means to ascertain performance or effectiveness (JP 5-0). Indicators should be— 

- Relevant—bearing a direct relationship to a task, effect, object, or end state condition. 

- Observable—detecting, measuring or evaluating changes. 

- Responsive—signifying changes in the OE in time to enable effective decision making. 

- Resourced—identifying collection assets and staff resources to observe and evaluate. 

4-110. The two types of indicators commonly used in assessments are MOPs and MOEs. A _measure of performance_ is an indicator used to measure a friendly action that is tied to measuring task accomplishment (JP 5-0). MOPs help answer questions such as “Was the action taken?” or “Were the tasks completed to standard?” A MOP confirms or denies that a task has been properly performed. MOPs are commonly found and tracked at all levels in execution matrixes. MOPs help to answer the question, “Are we doing things right?” 

4-111. At the most basic level, every Soldier assigned a task maintains a formal or informal checklist to track task completion. The status of those tasks and subtasks are MOPs. Similarly, operations consist of a series of collective tasks sequenced in time, space, and purpose to accomplish missions. Staffs and advisor teams use MOPs in execution matrixes, checklists, and running estimates to track completed tasks during current operations. Staffs and advisor teams use MOP as a primary element of battle tracking with a focus on the partner force. Evaluating task accomplishment using MOP is relatively straightforward and often results in a yes or no answer. 

4-112. A _measure of effectiveness_ is an indicator used to measure a current system state, with change indicated by comparing multiple observations over time (JP 5-0). MOEs help measure changes in conditions, both positive and negative. MOEs help to answer the question, “Are we doing the right things?” MOEs are commonly found and tracked in formal assessment plans. 

4-113. Evaluations include analyzing progress. Commanders and staffs propose and consider possible causes. They address the question of whether changes in the situation can be attributed to friendly FSF actions. Commanders, staffs, and advisor teams consult subject matter experts, both internal and external to the organization, on whether they have correctly identified the underlying causes for specific changes in the situation. These experts challenge key facts and assumptions identified in the planning process to determine if the facts and assumptions are still relevant or valid. 

4-114. Evaluating also includes considering whether the desired conditions have changed, are no longer achievable, or are not achievable through the current operational approach. Staffs and advisor teams continually challenge the key assumptions made when framing the problem. When an assumption is invalidated, reframing is likely required. 

**_Note._** See ADP 5-0 and JP 5-0 for more detailed information on the assessment activities of monitor, evaluate, and recommend or direct action. 

###### **Recommending or Directing Action** 

4-115. Monitoring and evaluating are critical activities; however, assessment is incomplete without recommending or directing action. Assessment may reveal problems, but unless it results in recommended adjustments, its use to the commander or partner force is limited. Ideally, recommendations highlight ways to improve the effectiveness of operations and plans by informing all decisions. 

4-116. Based on the evaluation, the staff and advisor teams brainstorm possible improvements to the plan and make preliminary judgments about the relative merit of those changes. Staff and advisor team members identify those changes possessing sufficient merit and provide them as recommendations to the commander or partner force or adjust them within their delegated authority. Recommendations range from continuing the operation as planned, executing a branch, or making unanticipated adjustments. It is also important to integrate recommendations from the staff, advisor teams, and the partner forces to decide if and how to modify the operation to better accomplish the mission. 

4-117. Assessment helps identify threats, suggests improvements to effectiveness, and reveals opportunities. The staff and advisor teams present the results and conclusions of their assessments and recommendations to the commander as an operation develops. Just as the staff and advisor teams devote time to analysis and evaluation, so too must they make timely, complete, and actionable recommendations. 

##### **ASSESSMENT PROCESS** 

4-118. The assessment process must start with an understanding of the OE. Specifically, the theater campaign plan and the TSCP. The process must encourage unity of effort among UAPs, guide the efficient use of resources, and anticipate potential counterpart needs. The desired end state accounts for the goals and priorities of the U.S. Government, the partner forces, the host nation, and other regional factors and concerns. (See ATP 3-07.10.) Use the assessment steps and framework described in figure 4-5 on page 114. 

**Figure 4-5. Assessment framework** 

###### **Step 1: Assess Objectives and End State** 

4-119. Assessment begins once the SFAB receives the mission. Specifically, it is necessary to assess objectives, end states, and the need for the effort. The SFAB advisor teams must remain engaged with the SFAB battalion staffs who interact with brigade planners to ensure that objectives, effects, and activities are measurable as a means of evaluating initial resource allocation. Once the SFAB establishes measurable objectives and associated effects, the MOE and MOP development begins. 

###### **Step 2: Identify Variables of Interest** 

4-120. The next step in this objective-led process is identifying variables of interest, which in the case of the SFA OE are usually PMESII-PT-based metrics. Other frameworks may be useful as well, to include the warfighting functions, DOTMLPF-P, or the mission variables. 

4-121. This step entails a characterization of the OE by teams of planners, operations research analysts, intelligence analysts, and various experts. Resources available (or desired) to accomplish this understanding may include academia, media, government agencies, nongovernmental organizations, subject matter experts, social network sites, and other publicly available information. Step 2 creates the foundation for the baseline assessment. The assessment team identifies relevant assessment products (classified or open source) that civilian and military organizations produce. With the desired condition established, the assessment teams can generate which metrics to measure to indicate progress towards the planned end state. 

###### **Step 3: Data Collection and Examination** 

4-122. After the assessment team identifies the variables of interest for either the MOP or the MOE, the next step is collecting and examining the data. The assessment team generates a baseline and hypotheses related to the OE through careful examination of data. Each variable requires information to populate the data. The team incorporates data collection plans into the operations planning process. This way, data requirements are known prior to the commencement of activity. The plan includes the timing and frequency of data collection, organizations, or personnel responsible for collection, and required special tools, reporting formats, hardware, and software. 

###### **Step 4: Develop a Baseline, Hypothesis, and Test** 

4-123. Step 4 is an assessment of the environmental design and a proposal theory of effort to test that design. The team develops a desired future state to create a baseline model of the OE. This desired future state is a baseline reference point for future comparisons. Advisors should assess and compare activities, desired effects, and objectives to this baseline. A baseline assessment includes ensuring that stated objectives are clear, understandable, and measurable. A baseline assessment enables the team to assess progression from the baseline to the end state using the stated objectives. A baseline assessment is an opportunity for assessors to apply design theory to the plan by stressing the need for a forward-thinking approach to the objectives. Step 4 includes mapping out the design theory (to the best of the assessors’ abilities) to drive MOP measurement and develop hypotheses as to which MOE these activities influence. Mapping the nonlinear design of the environment and potential effects of activities allows for anticipation of the possibility of the team performing activities well, but not having the desired outcome. 

###### **Step 5: Analysis and Assessment** 

4-124. The analysis and assessment step emphasizes the all-important evaluation of the MOE and MOP relationship analysis. Armed with sufficient data, observations, and hypotheses, assessment teams can begin analysis. The team identifies and highlights gaps in the assessment plan to identify and assign actions needed to adjust collection and analysis plans to improve the assessment process. Understanding current and desired conditions requires acknowledging the underlying assumptions. During the analysis step of the assessment process, staffs continually challenge assumptions they identify during planning. If they subsequently disprove assumptions, then reframing the problem may be appropriate. This step requires tactical patience. Conducting SFA is a complicated undertaking, with expected analysis and assessment taking some time. If the success threshold for the MOE criteria is not met, yet the corresponding (theorized) MOP criteria were met, the assumption as to the impact of those efforts may be false. 

###### **Step 6: Roll Up** 

4-125. When rolling up assessment data, account for the fact that the MOPs and MOEs at each assessment level may vary. As lessons and trends emerge, incorporate the analysis of these MOPs and MOEs into higherlevel assessment processes (if applicable). Tactical and operational assessment metrics are likely different from theater-level assessment metrics. However, the effects of various missions feed into a theater-level assessment that must account for all operations within the plan. 

###### **Step 7: Report and Recommend** 

4-126. Following an AAR, the team develops an assessment summary that includes recommendations for future operations, training, follow-up assessments, and factors, which require further analysis. Submit and archive summaries at the conclusion of assessments. When applicable, personnel conducting the assessment consult with all staff members to validate recommendations prior to reporting to the commander. 

##### **ASSESSMENT TYPES** 

4-127. The SFAB performs four types of assessments: operational, organizational, environmental, and institutional (see figure 4-6). Assessments occur at all levels of the partner force and are continuous, collaborative, and simultaneous in nature. As explained in paragraphs 4-128 through 4-148, the four types of assessments seem separate and distinct but may occur simultaneously while a unit is assessing the partner force progress and its mission daily.

**Figure 4-6. Foreign security forces assessments** 

###### **Organization** 

4-128. The organizational assessment compares the current organizational structure and operational state of the partner force to the designed or intended capabilities and capacity to execute directed missions. To determine what the organization is designed to do, the organizational assessment answers these questions: 

- What are the partner forces’ roles? 

- What are the partner forces’ missions? 

- What are the partner forces’ developmental goals and objectives? 

- How reliable are the partner forces’ leaders? 

4-129. The SFAB starts the organizational assessment during the predeployment phase. Commanders and staffs must focus on objectives and end states of the partner force’s higher governing body first rather than the organization. Starting with the organization first is often a planning pitfall. Commanders often make the mistake of organizing their units with the partner forces based on similarities of size and capabilities. This is common when the partner forces are conducting operations other than military, such as policing or humanitarian aid, for example. 

4-130. As the SFAB conducts MDMP and continues its organizational assessment, task-organization becomes clearer to the commander. The commander, advised by staff, determines who is best able to gather the data and complete the remaining steps of the assessment process and advising mission. 

4-131. Figure 4-7 provides an example of an organizational assessment rubric. As with other examples in this chapter, this rubric is not all-inclusive and serves as a starting point for advisor teams as they develop their assessment tools. 

**Figure 4-7. Organization assessment rubric, example** 

###### **Operational** 

4-132. The operational assessment reveals strengths and gaps in the partner forces’ ability to perform their missions, roles, or functions. It shows how well the partner forces can conduct their missions. A clear understanding of the partner forces’ operational mission serves as the starting point for the operational assessment. 

4-133. Effective partner forces display certain characteristics and capabilities described in paragraphs 4-134 through 4-144. 

###### **Proficient Partner Forces** 

4-134. Proficient partner forces— 

- Are tactically and technically proficient, can execute their national security mission, and integrate with allies. 

- Can conduct basic offensive and defensive collective tasks in support of military operations. 

- Can work with adjacent organizations to suppress lawlessness and insurgency. 

- Are competent in maintaining civil order, enforcing laws, controlling borders, and detaining criminal suspects. 

- Are trained with a modern police ethos to include security procedures, investigation basics, evidence collection, and court and legal procedures. 

###### **Self-Sustained** 

4-135. Partner forces that can manage their own equipment throughout its lifecycle (procurement to disposal) and conduct administrative support. 

###### **Professional and Competent Leaders** 

4-136. Partner force leaders at all levels possess sound professional standards and appropriate military values and are selected for promotion based on competence and merit. 

###### **Professional Traits** 

4-137. Professionalism traits include the following: 

- Partner forces that are honest, impartial, and committed to protecting and serving the entire population, operating under the rule of law, and respecting human rights. 

- Partner forces are loyal to the central government and serve national interests, recognizing their role as servants of the people and not their masters. 

- Partner forces are integrated into society: 

 - A population is less likely to see a security force that represents the nation’s major ethnic groups as the instrument of just one faction. 

 - They observe cultural sensitivities toward the incorporation of various groups and ethnicities. 

4-138. The capabilities and characteristics described in paragraph 4-137 support the overall campaign design. Evaluations of the partner force’s capabilities and characteristics reflect its ability to influence the OE to reinforce the overall design. The success or failure of a partner force may ultimately rest more on its overall influence in an operations area rather than its ability to execute specific tasks. 

4-139. Figure 4-8 provides an example of an operational assessment worksheet that an advisor team may use when conducting an operational assessment of a partner force. Use this worksheet when designing an evaluation for a partner force during the conduct of an operational mission. Advisors use the worksheet as a tool to record key data, observations, and other notes while observing a partner-force operation. After the operation, advisors use this information to build a more detailed assessment summary per their SOP. 

4-140. SFABs must establish standard assessment rubrics, worksheets, summaries, and reports in their SOPs that will aid the advisor teams and facilitate a common understanding across the various echelons of advisors with the partner force. Tailor these products to specific countries or regions and their related SFA goals. 

**Figure 4-8. Operational assessment worksheet, operational mission, example** 

4-141. DA Pam 11-31 uses the C ratings in figure 4-9 on page 120 when describing the strategic capability and capacity of a foreign military. They provide a useful framework for building effective country plans and managing resource tradeoffs in assessing foreign partners’ capabilities and capacities to perform military missions and their interoperability with the U.S. Army. At a strategic level, the capability and capacity of a foreign partner can be delineated using four levels—C-0, C-1, C-2, and C-3 (see table 4-1 on page 121). 

**Figure 4-9. Environmental assessment rubric, tactical level, exampleTable 4-1. Foreign partner capability and capacity levels**

||**_Foreign Partner Capability and Capacity Levels_**|
|---|---|
|**_Level_**|**_Description_**|
|C-0|Partner force has no demonstrated interoperability with the U.S. Army; C2 interface with the U.S.<br>Armyis onlyat the national level or the foreign force has no regular engagement with the U.S. Army.|
|C-1|Partner force shares information or situational awareness through liaison teams with the U.S. Army,<br>requires alignment of capabilities and procedures to establish operational norms, and has some<br>routine engagement with the U.S. Army.|
|C-2|Partner force has digital C2 capabilities, actively participates in information operations with the U.S.<br>Army, and partner force has digital C2 capabilities, actively participates in information operations with<br>the U.S. Armyand routinelyexercises or operates with the U.S. Army.|
|C-3|Partner force’s interoperability is network-enabled through shared situational awareness, mobile C2,<br>collaborativeplanning,networked fires,combat identification,intelligence,and reconnaissance.|

4-142. In addition to the capability and capacity ratings, DA Pam 11-31 also describes MNF interoperability ratings. _Interoperability_ is the ability to act together coherently, effectively, and efficiently to achieve tactical, operational, and strategic objectives (JP 3-0). There are four levels of interoperability—I-0, I-1, I-2, and I-3 (see table 4-2). 

**Table 4-2. Levels of interoperability** 

|**_Four Lev_**<br>|**_els of Interoperability_**|
|---|---|
|**_Level_**||

|I-1|Partner force shares information or situational<br>awareness through liaison teams with the U.S. Army,<br>requires alignment of capabilities and procedures to<br>establish operational norms, and has some routine<br>engagement with the U.S. Army.|

4-143. The criteria used in the ratings shown above and found in DA Pam 11-31 may not be relevant to an advisor team’s mission and their assessments. However, they provide an example of what these criteria may look like. Assessment criteria and ratings must be established through the advisor team’s chain of command and in conjunction with the partner force to provide the most useful and relevant information for future planning and operations. The operational assessment example in figure 4-9 defines C ratings that may be more relevant at the tactical level. 

4-144. When evaluating a partner force during training events, a similar worksheet can be used to the one shown in figure 4-8 on page 119. However, instead of using the operations cycle of plan, prepare, execute, and assess, an advisor team may use the eight-step training model as the framework to evaluate the foreign unit. 

###### **Environmental** 

4-145. This assessment considers current conditions in the environment and their effects on partner force organization. (See ATP 3-07.10.) The environmental assessment validates the suitability of a partner force’s directed missions. This may also reveal the requirement to develop additional capabilities or capacities in the partner forces beyond those normally associated with a similar type of security force. A tactical-level environmental assessment generally uses the mission variables of METT-TC (I) as a framework. A higher level or operational-level environmental assessment uses the operational variables of PMESII-PT as a framework to answer the following questions: What are the PMESII-PT impacts on the partner force? What are the threats? Are there any geographical considerations? 

4-146. Most of the information contained within the environmental assessment is gathered during IPOE. SFAB staffs and advisor teams constantly update this information and exchange information with the partner forces to the extent possible without violating security procedures. Figure 4-9 on page 120 gives an example of what a tactical-level environmental assessment rubric, using METT-TC (I), may look like. 

###### **Institutional** 

4-147. The institutional assessment evaluates the partner force’s capabilities by examining DOTMLPF-P, or a similar construct, to close existing capability gaps. The institutional assessment answers the following questions: Why does the organization have capability and capacity gaps? The best framework to assess partner forces’ institutions is the DOTMLPF-P framework. 

4-148. Figure 4-10, on pages 123 and 124, provides an example of an institutional assessment rubric, which uses the DOTMLPF-P framework and can assist advisors in preparing their assessments. The statements in the rubric provide an initial frame of reference to help evaluate the partner force institutions. The rubric contains both objective and subjective analysis and requires the advisor’s interpretation and sound judgment. This rubric is not all-inclusive and is only an example (in this case, an institutional assessment of a foreign military education course). 

**Figure 4-10. Institutional assessment rubric, exampleFigure 4-10. Institutional assessment rubric, example (continued)**

###### **Continuous Assessment** 

- 4-149. Advisor teams evaluate partner forces on a continuous basis. Metrics for evaluating success change as the operational conditions shift. An advisor team’s ability to learn and adapt leads to a more accurate assessment of the partner forces. 

- 4-150. Assessments are a continuous process of examining inputs, activities, outputs, outcomes, and the impact on objectives. As the assessments are being done, planners and assessors ask the following questions: 

 - Has the objective been achieved? 

 - If the objective has not been achieved, are we measuring the objective correctly? 

 - If the objective has been achieved, what are the impediments to achieving the objective? 

- 4-151. It is important to delineate assessment responsibilities across several stakeholders to account for differing levels of organizational authority and expertise and to maximize the level of objectivity into the assessment process. It is possible for an individual and staff section to have several different assessment roles and responsibilities. Some of the roles and associated responsibilities are the following: 

 - Data collectors who attend the SFA events and collect relevant information. 

 - Advisors who perform formal assessments of programs and activities. 

 - Designated personnel who review the assessments and certify those assessments as legitimate. 

- Staff and advisor team members who aggregate assessments to determine where assessment gaps and redundancies exist. 

- Advisor teams that develop recommendations to the commander and staff, based on the assessment findings of the SFA plans and missions for possible solutions or capability requirements. 

4-152. Advisor teams conduct assessments in support of each SFA activity. SFA activities should not begin, progress, change, or cease without conducting assessments. The SFAB staffs and advisor teams, in partnership with the partner forces, should spend considerable time updating assessments to shape the operation, consolidate gains, and prevent wasted time and resources on activities that are not reaching the SC objectives. 

##### **LSCO Support in Burma** 

<mark>In December, 1943, it could be truthfully said that never in history had Chinese troops launched a successfully strategic offensive against a modern enemy. In the second Burma campaign, Stilwell proposed to rewrite history, staking his reputation on the unproven thesis that, given proper equipment and training, Chinese soldiers were, man for man, as good as any in the world. In his belief in Chinese valor, Stilwell stood alone. Not only did the British and overwhelming portion of the U.S. Army staff in Asia believe it was impossible for the Chinese to attack and destroy battle-proved Japanese divisions of roughly equal strength in northern Burma, but the Chinese general staff in Chungking held the same low opinion of its soldiery.</mark> 

<mark>What Stilwell proposed to do was this: to take raw troops, divorce them from the possibility of retreat, abandon fixed supply lines as completely as did Sherman in Georgia, make them dependent on air drops alone, drive them two hundred miles through jungle, swamp, and mountain to conquer a skillful, entrenched, and desperate enemy.</mark> 

<mark>The bare outlines of the campaign were simple enough. The great objective was the open lowland of north-central Burma, where the meter-gauge railway wound its way through the linked towns of Mogaung and Myitkyina. If these two towns could be seized, a road might be built through the jungle from India to reach them, and then pushed on toward the Chinese border of Yünnan, to end the great blockade.</mark> 

<mark>Joseph W. Stilwell</mark> The Stilwell Papers 

#### **SUPPORT** 

4-153. Advisor teams support partner forces with capabilities and capacity to facilitate mission success. As an advisor team activity, support specifically refers to lethal, nonlethal and tactical support provided to the partner force at the tactical or operational level. 

4-154. During mission planning, advisor teams may identify capability gaps within the partner force that hinder the ability of the foreign counterparts to achieve mission success. Support may include providing access to capabilities such as MEDEVAC, fire support (specifically Joint Air-Ground Integration Center capabilities), or specialized engineer resources. The amount of support should be based upon the conditions, resources, and time available. Advisors must responsibly coordinate and provide support to the partner force to help achieve success but not to create a reliance on U.S. Forces. Building the green COP and feeding it into the JTF/combined joint task force blue COP will allow for sufficient communication and support for joint operations and coordination between mission capabilities. 

##### **SUPPORTING CONSIDERATIONS THROUGH THE COMPETITION CONTINUUM** 

4-155. _Support_ is the action of a force that aids, protects, complements, or sustains another force in accordance with a directive requiring such action (JP 1, Vol. 2). Advisor considerations while executing the support activity include competition, crisis, and armed conflict. 

4-156. Advisor teams should receive training on the legal, technical, and tactical considerations for providing specific support requests to FSF. This training should include information regarding the current foreign security force capabilities, sustainment, and support assets available, rules of engagement for employing fires and rules or legal guidance for providing supplies, food, medical support, among other considerations. Advisor teams and SFAB staff must become familiar with the foreign security force organizations and equipment, particularly the capabilities of weapons and equipment not found in the U.S. inventory. 

4-157. Support provided to a foreign security force can vary across the competition continuum, but generally is substantial while in conflict and lessens as the situation transitions to competition or as the FSF’s capabilities and capacities increase. While success on the battlefield is critical, especially for the Soldiers conducting the fight, SFAB commanders and advisor teams must continuously assess the tactical need of the support for the current fight versus the long-term operational and strategic development of the capability within the foreign security force. If advisor teams provide external support every time a foreign security force makes a request, the foreign security force is less likely to develop the capability on its own. 

###### **Competition** 

4-158. Competition support focuses on the long-term development of multiple FSF capacities and capabilities. 

4-159. Advisor teams aim to assist FSF in reinforcing the legitimacy of their government to the local populace as well as building confidence within the FSF itself. When providing support to the FSF, advisor teams must first identify the resources, systems, institutions, and capabilities that are available to the FSF and themselves in theater. Additionally, advisor teams should understand the mission and operational variables that may influence their operations and ability to provide support. Understanding these factors allows advisor teams to plan for the likely support requirements that their foreign counterparts may ask them to provide. OE conditions can rapidly change; therefore, advisor teams should anticipate potential support requests as operations persist. 

###### **Crisis** 

4-160. Crisis support changes to focus on short-term reactions and developmental needs to address the specific crisis at hand. This could include C2 systems, logistics support, or whatever the specific crisis might dictate. 

###### **Armed Conflict** 

4-161. Support in armed conflict focuses on only the most immediate requirements to defeat or reduce the current threat. This should focus on specific warfighting capability gaps that are needed to survive and thrive in the execution of LSCO. During execution, the most highly sought after support capabilities that SFAB advisors can access for the partner force are communications systems, joint fires, and the detailed allied and partner (blue and green) COP. 

##### **SUPPORTING IN LARGE-SCALE COMBAT OPERATIONS** 

4-162. Support in LSCO can vary widely from discrete foreign military sales cases to U.S. and FSF formations fighting side by side on the battlefield. In all cases, the SFABs have an important role to play in interpreting and communicating the wants and needs of both the U.S. and the partner forces. Embedded SFA advisors, with their unique knowledge of the region and partner FSF capabilities, support and enable an agile response by the joint force in the rapid transition to combat operations and during execution of those operations. Whether it is a capabilities shortfall a partner force needs (for example, short-range air defense, antitank guided missiles, or C2 systems) or an immediate effect (for example, joint fires, resupply, or MEDEVAC), a joint force commander will likely seek out the advice of SFAB personnel to validate FSF’s requests for support. 

4-163. When engaged in large-scale training exercises or combat operations, the SFAB advisor team’s knowledge of U.S. Army maneuver doctrine can substantially mitigate the friction of U.S. formations working alongside a foreign security force. For example, the complexity of an operation like a passage of lines can be significantly reduced from the integration of advisors at echelon and at the contact and passage points between U.S. conventional formations and the FSF.

4-164. SFA advisor teams support the partner FSF with capabilities and capacity to enable mission success. Effective support can provide sustainment and protection to partner FSF allowing for an effective transition to armed conflict. 

4-165. In the transition to armed conflict, embedded security force advisors, with their unique knowledge of the region and partner FSF capabilities, support and enable an agile response by the joint force in the rapid transition to combat operations (see JP 3-0 for more information). SFA forces collocated or embedded with the partner FSF support and enable synchronization of activities, fill key capability gaps, and help to ensure unity of purpose and mutual support in offensive operations (see FM 3-0 for more information). 

##### **Liaison between the 8th Army and the ROKA** 

<mark>While the ROKA officers reorganized their remaining regiments as best as they could by re-integrating stragglers as they caught up with their parent units, KMAG headquarters assumed a new role to act as a liaison between Walker’s (Lt Gen. Walton “Bulldog” Walker, Commander of Eighth Army, [September 1948-December 1950]) Eighth Army and the Koreans. For the moment, KMAG—thanks to its field advisors— was in a position to provide the situation awareness Walker desperately needed. KMAG also became responsible to ensure that Walker’s directives were passed to the Korean Army and followed. This was a significant change in focus for advisors, as they now were held responsible for the performance of their units in discharging Eighth Army orders. Among these were directives to standardize personnel, logistics, and operations reporting in accordance with accepted American practices. It took a few months, but by September, KMAG could report that the Korean army at least was operating along recognizable principles in conducting its military operations.</mark> 

<mark>Bryan R. Gibby</mark> The Will to Win: American military advisors in Korea, 1946-1953 

#### **LIAISE** 

4-166. _Liaison_ is that contact or intercommunication maintained between elements of military forces or other agencies to ensure mutual understanding and unity of purpose and action (FM 6-0). Advisor teams should be able to liaise with a U.S partner, especially during shifts between competition, crisis, and conflict. Liaison teams work to increase understanding of mission and tactics with other forces, convey information, enhance mutual trust, and improve teamwork. (See JP 3-08 for more information.) 

4-167. When advisor teams are working with foreign counterparts and there is a U.S. unit in the same or adjacent AO, the advisor teams have a responsibility to liaise and coordinate with the U.S. unit. In addition to the informal liaison via the SFAB advisors, formal liaison and coordination should occur between the partner force and the other U.S. units. This is true regardless of the context. For example, the U.S. unit and the partner force may be partners in a MNF answering to the same commander. In another case, the U.S. unit may have a unilateral mission under a different chain of command. In either of these examples, the partner force and the U.S. unit may have exchanged liaison officers at their echelon or between their higher headquarters. Neither of these situations removes the responsibility of the advisor teams to conduct liaison and coordination. From the perspective of the other U.S. unit, the SFAB advisor teams have expert knowledge of the partner force that they do not have. This can be critical to avoid misunderstandings and misperceptions. Through liaisons, advisor teams conduct, coordinate, and share information gained from assessments, information analyses, and commanders’ guidance. 

4-168. The ability to monitor and coordinate are the two most essential specific tasks for proper liaison functions. SFA advisors must be able to monitor the partner FSF unit to which they are assigned, as well as their own parent organization. Monitoring should track combat power status, enable situational understanding, and synchronize lethal and nonlethal effects. As a representative of the commander, SFA advisors must understand their own commander’s intent, guidance, mission, and concept of operations. They must also be technically and tactically competent to coordinate on behalf of their own commander, and with the partner FSF for tactical matters to achieve and maintain unity of effort.

4-169. As the need for formal liaisons can change frequently, every operation that SFABs engage in will have differing liaise task requirements. As such, SFABs should train all advisors to conduct the liaise activity captured in paragraphs 4-170 through 4-182. 

##### **LIAISING CONSIDERATIONS THROUGH THE COMPETITION CONTINUUM** 

4-170. Advisors execute liaise through the competition continuum by both monitoring and coordinating activities between the two forces. An effective liaison aggressively seeks to develop an understanding of ongoing operations and does not passively wait for information to arrive. 

###### **Competition** 

4-171. Liaising efforts are widely spread across numerous FSF entities, both MOD and executive functions, and are focused on relationship building, identification and sharing of best practices, and long-term capability and capacity goals. 

###### **Crisis** 

4-172. Liaising efforts tailor to the specific area of crisis and focus on identifying and communicating the most current information on the crisis to build the widest COP for both the United States and the FSF. 

###### **Armed Conflict** 

4-173. Liaising efforts tailor very closely to monitoring and reporting on the ongoing conflict. Special attention should be given to ensuring the same COP is understood at both the parent and liaised organizations. 

4-174. The complexity of the liaising activity increases dramatically as the situation moves from competition and crisis to conflict. As such, liaising in LSCO might look drastically different than liaising in competition. 

##### **LIAISING IN LARGE-SCALE COMBAT OPERATIONS** 

4-175. SFAB advisors may be asked to perform the liaison activity with a partner force during LSCO. In this scenario, the SFAB advisor teams may provide direct support to a partner force and serve as an embedded link between the foreign force and other U.S. organizations. For example, if a foreign division is serving within a combined JTF during LSCO, SFAB advisor teams may be placed within the division, brigade, and even battalion headquarters of the foreign military division. This increases the capability of the foreign force to integrate into the larger operation, tie into U.S. enablers, and provide greater situational understanding to both the foreign and U.S. Forces. When operating in this role, SFAB advisor teams will likely have contact with liaison detachments which may include DLDs and theater liaison detachments (TLDs). 

##### **LIAISON DETACHMENTS** 

4-176. Liaison detachments, including DLDs and TLDs, provide SFA to the partner FSF by bridging C2 system interoperability gaps over a short time horizon. Liaison detachments assist subordinate, parallel, joint, or multinational headquarters in an operational area by providing digital information management and communications interface capability between U.S. systems and otherwise non-interoperable systems (see FM 3-22 for more information). The DLD or TLD allow the FSF to communicate on a multinational network, filling an interoperability gap. TLDs and DLDs provide SFA by supplementing a foreign security force's military and civilian senior leaders' ability to expand access and create options for FSF C2. While DLDs and TLDs fill short-term gaps, SFABs assist with longer-term development of partner capabilities and capacities to, over time, close interoperability gaps (see ATP 3-94.1 for more information on TLDs and DLDs). 

4-177. During LSCO, a theater army provides liaison detachments to foreign militaries or government agencies to rapidly deconflict a C2 interoperability gap. For example, the tactical equivalent of a DLD occurs when temporarily attaching a radiotelephone operator with a filled radio to an adjacent leader when they have lost (or never had) the ability to communicate during the mission at hand. At the upper tactical and operational levels, liaison detachments possess the necessary network and communications architecture to assist with necessary coordination and communication for synchronizing operations between a multinational land force and the U.S. CCDR or coalition component commander. Moreover, TLDs and DLDs ensure communications interoperability across the joint force. When adjacent units lack adequate communications capabilities, TLDs or DLDs may ensure consistent and reliable COP sharing. In this case, the liaison facilitates sharing the green, blue, and purple COP between the partnered unit and U.S. Forces.

**_Note._** When used in this capacity during LSCO, the SFAB typical guidance of advising at echelon or two levels higher may not apply. 

##### **FUNCTIONS OF A SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE BRIGADE LIAISON OFFICER** 

4-178. Liaisons, whether individually or in teams, perform several critical functions that are consistent across the range of military operations. The skill set, rank, and experience requirement for an advisor acting as a liaison is a deliberate decision made by the commander. Liaisons should never be the person the organization can spare; it needs to be a critical trusted advisor. Liaisons should be prepared to perform four functions. 

###### **Monitoring Operations**
