4-179. Liaison officers must monitor the operations of both the unit of which they are a liaison officer and the advisor team or SFAB that sent them and understand how each affects the other. As a minimum, the liaison officer must know the current situation and planned operations, understand pertinent staff issues, and are sensitive to parent commanders and the organic SFAB commander’s desires. 

###### **Coordinating Operations** 

4-180. Liaison officers facilitate synchronization of current operations and plans between the sending organization and the unit at which they are acting as a liaison officer. The liaison officer does this through coordination with other liaison officers, members of the adjacent staff, and the parent command. Liaison officers should meet routinely with staff officers and commanders in the headquarters and readily know how to contact them. The understanding and procedures liaison officers build while monitoring the situation help facilitate the synchronization of the SFAB’s operations and plans with those of the unit at which they are conducting liaison by coordinating the communication process. 

###### **Advising Commands** 

4-181. The liaison officer is the receiving headquarters’ expert on the SFAB’s capabilities and limitations. Liaison officers must be able to advise the commander and the staff on the optimal use of the command they represent. 

###### **Assisting Levels** 

4-182. The liaison officer must assist on two levels. First, the liaison officer must act as the conduit between the SFAB and the receiving headquarters. Second, by integrating into the receiving headquarters and attending various boards, meetings, and planning sessions, liaison officers can answer their questions and ensure they make informed decisions with the SFAB, their advisor teams, and with the partner force requirements in mind. (See JP 3-33 for more information.) 

##### **Advising during Combat Operations in Korea** 

<mark>During the retreat toward Pusan, the KMAG advisors had been forced on many occasions to drop their advisory roles and become operational. Faced with a desperate situation, they insisted that their suggestions be followed, and in the field they virtually commanded ROK Army formations. The methods were sometimes harsh but frequently were the only means of slowing the rout and making the ROK forces take up defensive positions along the way south. Had the KMAG advisors not employed such measures in the time of crisis, the U.S. aid from Japan and the United States</mark> might well have arrived too late to have saved South Korea. 

#### **ADVISE** 

4-183. Advising is an extension of diplomacy and professionalism maintaining long-lasting relationships between mission partners. This has the capacity to exist and leverage through competition, crisis, and armed conflict with the possibility of future operations and continued support. Therefore, advise exists both as a developmental activity, and a conflict integration activity. 

4-184. As an advisor team conflict integration activity, advise refers to the process of providing guidance, coaching, counseling, and mentorship to a counterpart to make their operations or activities more successful. Advisor teams conduct this role with their counterparts throughout all stages of training, operations, and execution of SFA activities. This section describes how SFAB advisor teams fulfill their advising function alongside partner forces while addressing each of the remaining SFA activities. 

4-185. Partner force development through SFA requires effort over time. Measure success by the increase in the partner forces’ capability, capacity, competency, commitment, and confidence in areas in which U.S. Forces are assisting them. The SFA activities include plan, resource, generate, employ, transition, and sustain. Through SFA, the partner forces’ capability and capacity to execute these activities should improve over time. 

##### **ADVISING CONSIDERATIONS THROUGH THE COMPETITION CONTINUUM** 

4-186. The scope of advising begins with the broadest scope in competition and narrows into more focused missions as it progresses into crisis and conflict. For example, in competition when the price of suboptimal FSF adaptation is low, advisors conduct holistic unit advising potentially encompassing many tenants of operations within their team specialties valuing interpersonal gains more highly than proficiency; however, in crises and conflict, the risk of neglecting specific advising such as base security or tactical mission task proficiencies is much higher and advisors transition to a more assertive method of advising. This dynamic is depicted in figure 4-11. 

**Figure 4-11. Advisor assertiveness framework** 

###### **Competition** 

4-187. Freedom of movement is very permissive; force protection considerations are minimal and driven by guidance from the embassy. Command relationships remain linear and direct. Advising efforts may be focused on training management and specific collective training exercises. 

###### **Crisis** 

4-188. SFA advising actions shift to de-escalating and returning to the competition phase. Freedom of movement is more restrictive, security posture is heightened, and command relationships may shift to a more JTF or multinational structure. Advising efforts may be focused on deployment operations, initial operational planning, and ensuring shared understanding of the status of the crisis. 

###### **Armed Conflict** 

4-189. Freedom of movement is severely restricted and must be specifically directed from higher headquarters. Security posture is at its highest level; command relationships and reporting are cumbersome. Advising efforts may be focused on battle tracking and planning future tactical operations. 

##### **ADVISING IN LARGE-SCALE COMBAT OPERATIONS** 

4-190. In certain situations where it is both desired and supportable, advisor teams may provide advising capabilities to partner forces while actively engaged in LSCO. The nature of the advice provided could range from institutional questions outside of the active LSCO fight, employment considerations of assets or capabilities in the fight, or questions as to coalition plans and how to execute certain tactical maneuvers. 

4-191. When coordinating with partner forces, the advising team should take translation requirements into account to be sure the proper orders and intent are relayed and understood. Even when there is not a language barrier, the “English to English” translation of U.S. Army or joint doctrine to FSF doctrine might necessitate an advisor to ensure the nuanced commander’s intent is understood by the partner. 

##### **SECTION III – ADVISOR TEAM EMPLOYMENT** 

4-192. The ability to task-organize and employ advisors in a variety of ways and across a wide range of environments is the SFAB’s greatest contribution to the CCMD. This section describes additional employment considerations for commanders, staffs, and advisor team leaders when planning for operations. 

#### **OPERATIONAL CONSIDERATIONS** 

4-193. Commanders and staffs must deliberately consider and plan against the operational considerations listed in paragraphs 4-195 through 4-206. These factors will influence the right and left limits within which advisor teams can operate. 

4-194. SFABs maximize their personnel to capitalize on engagements of opportunity to advance U.S. interests. SFAB teams act as a force multiplier and problem-solving entity enabling independent operations for the partner force. Commanders can seek to decentralize teams in country to maximize advising efforts across the AOR. Commanders must take into consideration the separate existing lines of effort and ensure that team operations do not interfere with other ongoing operations. 

##### **DECENTRALIZED OPERATIONS** 

4-195. The SFAB can expect missions that require multiple advisor teams across multiple countries. Additionally, the SFAB has the capacity to deploy an entire brigade to a single country or subordinate battalions to a specific region. The requests generated from the embassy, ASCC or the CCMD determine the requirements. Advisors are encouraged to leverage disciplined initiative and the advisor network in mitigating the communication challenges associated with decentralized operations. Decentralized forces require additional sustainment support. 

##### **PROXIMITY, FREQUENCY, AND DURATION** 

4-196. The proximity of advisors to the partner force is an important planning factor. The SFAB’s mission, authorities, rules of engagement, status-of-forces agreements, risk assessments, and other factors determine how closely the advisor teams work with their counterparts. These factors determine the initial advising proximity and frequency and will be adjusted as advisor teams complete their initial assessment and as they continue monitoring activities over time. Frequent contact may help develop trust between the two forces; however, advisors must view their relationships with their partners as a separate line of effort. A relationship should be intentional in nature; it does not just happen. The relationship can span from advisors living with their counterparts, periodic visits in preparation for specific events or operations, or even advising through video teleconferences. 

4-197. Advisor activities, advisor team mission, and advisor location are categorized into three levels which describe the degree of interaction with their mission partner over time. 

###### **Level 1 – Persistent** 

4-198. Advisor teams develop their counterparts on a continuous, persistent (usually daily) basis either from an embedded footprint or in proximity to facilitate required frequency of engagements. Engagements range from daily to weekly depending on partner requirements. Duration is long-term covering multiple force package rotations. 

###### **Level 2 – Episodic** 

4-199. During level 2, episodic advising, advisor teams work with their counterparts on a less frequent basis to enable their continued development. The duration of this interaction varies, usually 2-3 months, depending on partner requirements. Proximity and frequency remain similar to level 1 advising. 

###### **Level 3 – Short Duration** 

4-200. Advisors provide advice from a centralized location (such as at a regional training center or school), during a battlefield circulation, or a staff assistance visit. 

##### **CONFLICT INTENSITY** 

4-201. Conflict intensity plays an important role in determining the placement and access of SFAB units alongside the partner forces. Acknowledging the current position along the competition continuum (competition, crisis, and conflict) is a joint decision between the U.S. Department of State, DOD, and the partner force. 

4-202. In a permissive environment, the SFAB can disperse advising teams across a larger geographic area. In a less-permissive environment, the SFAB may experience limited operational access associated with higher risk and increased threat. The rules of engagement, legal authorities, and the status-of-forces agreement with the host nation guides the advisor team’s higher headquarters operations under various conflict intensities. 

##### **RULES OF ENGAGEMENT** 

4-203. The advisor team’s higher headquarters will develop and disseminate rules of engagement. When necessary, SFAB commanders and staffs request modifications to the rules of engagement through the appropriate channels. SFAB leaders must ensure advisor teams understand how the rules of engagement affect the type of support they can provide to their counterparts. Advisors must communicate these limitations to the partner forces to manage expectations and maintain credibility. SFAB members must also understand the application of self-defense and collective self-defense as it applies to their counterparts. Finally, the SFAB members must understand the variations and caveats to the law of armed conflict and rules of engagement that the partner forces operate under. 

4-204. Higher echelons of the partner force typically develop their rules of engagement. SFAB members may train and advise their counterparts to follow the tenants of the law of armed conflict and rules of engagement. This is particularly during partner training or operations. Violations must be reported through higher headquarters in accordance with established SOPs. 

##### **NATIONAL CAVEATS** 

4-205. National caveats are a nondoctrinal concept that describes the limitations or restrictions imposed by a nation on the use of its military forces, assets, or capabilities as part of a MNF or other UAPs. They are established to ensure that national interests, policies, and legal obligations are mutually respected during multinational operations. National caveats present unique challenges and can result in a partner force being unwilling or unable to execute certain tasks even if similar U.S. Army units would face no restriction. Conversely, U.S. law may restrict active participation in activities that the FSF are expected to execute based off the laws of their own country. It is not the responsibility of the advisor to enforce U.S. laws or U.S. Army norms upon FSF; however, all possible violations of the law of armed conflict must be immediately communicated to the higher headquarters. It is important for advisors to identify and understand when national caveats might be present and to communicate those details. 

##### **LEGAL CONSIDERATIONS** 

4-206. SFAB commanders, staffs, teams, and advisors must adhere to or consider several legal agreements. These may include authorities under U.S. law, status-of-forces agreements with host nations, ACSAs, law of armed conflict, the Uniform Code of Military Justice, host-nation laws, and other forms of legal authority under which the SFAB operates. National-level legal agreements should be started early in the planning process prior to advisor teams arriving on location or else advisor team activities may be delayed. Advisors also act in an ethical manner always as representatives of the U.S. Government. When questions of legal authority, obligation, or ethical conflicts arise, advisors contact their brigade judge advocate for further guidance. 

#### **ORGANIZATIONAL REPORTING STRUCTURE** 

4-207. The SFAB employs advisor teams in a variety of configurations based on assigned tasks and the operational and mission variables. Each of the following configurations provides benefits and drawbacks: 

- Horizontal. 

- Vertical. 

- Mixed. 

4-208. These reporting structures describe how the SFAB commanders and staffs manage advisor teams conducting SFA. This includes coordinating themes and messages, coordinating information among the echelon of advisors, gathering and archiving assessments, resolving conflicts between advisor teams, and supporting elements or the partner forces, and other functions directly related to the success of the SFA mission. They do not describe the various command and support relationships that may be in place. 

4-209. Organizational reporting structures apply to the SFAB advisor teams regardless of how many teams are employed. Well-established organizational reporting structures depict how each advisor team communicates with other SFAB units and organizations in-theater. This should include guidance on what reports, requests, and other information SFAB elements share between each organization. For example, an advisor team operating in a non-JOA environment may receive its missions through the country team, submit administrative reports through its organic higher headquarters, and request contract support through the supporting ASCC. All these considerations should be established and clearly articulated to each advisor team and its supporting organizations. 

4-210. Take care when establishing reporting structures to promote maximum decision-making authority at the advisor team level. An overly complex process or onerous requirements may risk the credibility of the advisor and slow the decision-making process. 

##### **HORIZONTAL** 

4-211. The horizontal reporting structure is the most challenging arrangement to exercise C2 over during the SFA mission, however, it is the most useful reporting structure while employing large numbers of advisor teams. Horizontal reporting is characterized by the dispersal of company, battalion, and brigade leaders throughout one or multiple AO. This limits personnel at the command post to receive and disseminate reports, manage current operations, plan for future operations, and to battle track SFA operations. 

4-212. When using the horizontal reporting structure, subordinate elements report SFA-related information directly to their battalion main command post. The battalion main command posts serve as the first echelon to receive SFA-related reports directly from advisor teams. For example, if a single Infantry battalion has seven advisor teams dispersed across an AO, the battalion main command post becomes C2 node where all seven advisor teams send their SFA mission-related reports. 

4-213. The horizontal reporting structure allows the SFAB to apply maximum influence through their approximately 36 advisor teams when operating in one or multiple AOs. However, since many of the advisor teams consist of key SFAB leaders, the command post assumes a greater responsibility for receiving reports than the other reporting structures. The command post must also remain capable of conducting planning for current and future SFA operations, controlling information collection activities, and assisting with emergencies as required. 

##### **VERTICAL** 

4-214. Vertical reporting structures provide the most traditional flow of information and reporting. When using this structure, the SFAB operates command posts in every company and higher organization. Use this structure when the SFAB is less concerned with employing the maximum number of advisor teams and is more concerned with maintaining C2 of its SFA mission. Vertical reporting structures are particularly effective when synchronized at echelon with the partner force. 

4-215. When using this reporting structure, no more than one brigade or battalion-level advisor team is away from the command post at any given time. This allows senior leaders to be present for issuing guidance for current or future operations as required. At the company level, the advisor teams cannot conduct advising operations and maintain a command post without additional augmentation. However, units may organize individual members of the company advisor teams with subordinate advisor teams to provide limited duration technical support. 

##### **MIXED** 

4-216. The mixed structure is a combination of both the vertical and horizontal structures and is used within the same SFAB, at either the battalion or brigade level. For example, one Infantry battalion could establish a horizontal structure with all seven of its advisor teams reporting to the battalion command post. Another battalion could establish a vertical reporting structure that limits the number of advisor teams to approximately four within its AO. 

4-217. In another scenario, the Infantry battalion designates one company to establish a command post. The other company does not operate a command post and instead organize into MATs. In this case, the Infantry battalion may have two advisor teams reporting directly to the battalion command post while the remaining two report through their company command posts to battalion. There are situations that may result in advisor teams, within subordinate battalions, reporting directly to the brigade command post due to location, priority of effort, available resources, assets, and other considerations. These situations fall under the mixed reporting structure. Figure 4-12 on page 136 shows an example of a mixed brigade reporting structure along with vertical, horizontal, and mixed battalion reporting structures. 

**Figure 4-12. Organization reporting structure** 

#### **ADVISOR TEAM ORGANIZATION** 

4-218. When working alongside partner forces, the SFAB may arrange advisors in a variety of ways. It is important to note whether the team is focused on improving capacity, capability, or interoperability. Advisors generally find themselves working with tactical partner force commanders and staffs, providing guidance and assistance during planning and operations. However, there are cases when other relationships may be appropriate, to include augmentation and sometimes partnership arrangements. This section describes these relationships. 

4-219. After identifying initial SFA requirements, commanders and staffs assign advising teams against those requirements. Augment advisor teams with the necessary personnel to complete the assigned mission. Typically, this includes personnel with technical knowledge such as signal, logistics, maintenance, and other functions. Advising teams coordinate with their assigned counterparts to begin SFA operations. As the mission persists, the advisor force package will likely change as the advising team refines the partner force requirements. 

4-220. Advising teams maintain continuity with their counterparts during planning and mission preparation. As elements of the partner force become more capable, the advisor relationship can shift between different tasks of OTEBA. The advisor may assist in coordinating resources and assets, as well as oversee some processes, such as close air support, joint fires, MEDEVAC, EOD, employment of joint intelligence, surveillance, and reconnaissance assets, and engagements with other UAPs. These outside enablers may provide capabilities absent in the partner force. 

##### **SPECIAL AREAS OF CONCERN** 

4-221. SFAB employment should remain flexible in nature to adapt to the environment and cater to the needs of the partner force. Advisors problem-solve by finding and generating solutions for their partners regardless of MOS. SFAB units may not align precisely with the task organization of their partner force, for example, perhaps their counterparts do not have an engineer battalion or field artillery battalion. These BATs can be task-organized and used to address other areas of concern (or that same area of concern but with an alternate partner). Paragraphs 4-222 through 4-228 are examples of utilizing advisor teams in specific areas of concern. 

###### **Advise Higher** 

4-222. Advisor teams may be tasked to augment a higher SFAB element or to advise a higher echelon within their specific functional expertise. Advising higher than two-levels up occurs most often when the assigned mission partner’s organization does not contain a like-element to advise. For example, when advising FSF that does not have a dedicated engineer organization, or wholly lacks engineer capabilities, the EBAT may augment the brigade advisor team and focus on advising brigade staff. In a second example, an EAT, which is typically aligned with a partner engineer battalion or brigade, may advise at the division staff level. In this case, the EAT augments a brigade advisor team and aligns with staff elements responsible for the protection warfighting function 

###### **Advise a Nonstandard Partner** 

4-223. If the partner forces do not have a singular individual responsible for a function, the advisor can find a nonstandard partner who is capable of handling that function. For instance, if a partner force lacks a formal SPO section, the LBAT could find whoever is integrating logistics, regardless of their formal position within the partner force, and advise them. 

###### **Weight the Advisor Allocation** 

4-224. If one region or sector is permissive and the counter partner force is competent, the commander can decide to task-organize a smaller advisor force for that area while creating a more robust capability in another sector. 

##### **PARTNERING WITH COMMANDERS** 

4-225. By accompanying the partner commander during the receipt of mission from their higher headquarters, the team leader can provide necessary guidance to other advisor teams needing to plan and prepare for potential missions. Team leaders may provide counsel and guidance to the partner commander regarding mission planning. The advisor monitors how the subordinate partner forces comprehend their commander’s intent and all specified or implied tasks. Team leaders continuously compare the information that they receive through their advisor network versus what their partner counterparts receive through their chain of command. This continuous comparison is a method of analysis that allows team leaders and their counterparts to operate with a shared understanding and resolve potential issues before they become serious issues. 

##### **PARTNERING WITH STAFF** 

4-226. Staff advisors assist the partner force with developing operational plans, managing current operations, and integrating warfighting functions. SFABs should organize staff advisors across the warfighting functions and augment with specialty advisors as necessary based on assessments of counterpart partner forces and their requirements. Advisors may also be assigned to partner force integrated cells, such as current operations and future operations planning. Regardless of the formal structure, advisors should be nested with how the partner force runs their operations process. Prior to assigning advisors against staff advising requirements, ensure those personnel have the appropriate experience and training to provide value to the partner forces and their mission. This can be challenging, even when advising only one echelon higher. (See FM 6-0 for more information on MDMP and individual duties and responsibilities.) Figure 4-13 on page 138 shows an example of how advisor team leaders may array their advisor team to fulfill typical staff roles for internal planning or operating alongside a partner force staff. 

**Figure 4-13. Advisor team staff roles, example** 

4-227. While aligning a specific advisor position against a particular warfighting function or staff section can be effective, advisors should ensure that they maintain awareness of the whole operation and understand the interaction of the warfighting functions and staff sections. The partner force may not have a counterpart for that advisor, and that is when that advisor is expected to exhibit flexibility and identify other engagements of opportunity to advise partner force. 

##### **PARTNERING WITH COMPANIES** 

4-228. Advisors sent to elements below battalion level help their partner force counterparts analyze the mission and commander’s intent from higher headquarters. They assist the partner force in executing the partner force equivalent of the troop leading procedures, the eight-step training model, and training management. Advisors also assist their counterparts in analyzing the mission variables. From these variables, advisors help their foreign counterparts develop a course of action that meets the higher headquarters’ concept of operations and commander’s intent. The advisors report assessments to their higher headquarters. Through their understanding of the broader plan, they identify discrepancies between the lower and higher echelons and resolve issues through coaching their counterparts. Finally, advisors provide guidance and assistance during the conduct of operations. 

#### **ADVISOR TEAM PLANNING MODELS** 

4-229. Through the planning process, advisor teams have an obligation to help the partner force achieve their objectives in support of U.S. national interests (for example, as described by the integrated country strategies, and theater campaign plans). The advisor team must understand the U.S. rules of engagement, higher headquarters and national caveats, the assigned mission from the CCMD and the relevant partner force authorities. Within this context, advisor teams must develop their foreign counterparts and ensure their longterm success using local, feasible, and sustainable solutions. 

4-230. Different sources can generate advisor team missions. Advising teams receive some missions through OPCON or TACON command relationships. At other times, adjacent units, the partner force, or the advising team themselves generate missions. Regardless of the origin, the planning process must account for the higher headquarters with tasking authority, the partner force, and the advisor team capabilities. All planning steps require a continuous assessment and communication with the partner force and higher headquarters. 

4-231. When an advisor team receives an order from their higher headquarters, a variation of the following steps occur: 

- Higher headquarters issues an order. 

- The advisor team receives the mission and begins mission analysis. 

- The advisor team develops an initial course of action and nests the plan with the higher headquarters’ mission intent, national objectives, and partner force objectives. 

- The advisor team executes a course of action analysis and comparison with the partner force, communicating the results back to orders-issuing-authority, including any partner force constraints. 

- Higher headquarters issues a fragmentary order or new operation order upon the advisor team receiving course of action approval. 

- The advisor team and the partner force execute orders production. 

- The assessment continues. 

#### **PARTNER FORCE PLAN ASSISTANCE** 

4-232. When a partner force plans a mission that is outside the scope of the advisor team’s assigned mission, the advisor team requests permission from their higher headquarters to participate. While the partner force does not control the advisor team, the advisors want to support their partners whenever possible. When planning a mission that is initially generated from the partner force, the advisor team generally follows these steps: 

- The partner force issues a plan (advisor team receipt of potential mission). 

- The advisor team continues to conduct advisor team activities with the partner force and assists with their planning process. 

- In parallel, the advisor team requests permission from higher headquarters to participate in the mission and requests resources (mission analysis). 

- The advisor team supports the partner force course of action development, analysis, comparison, approval, and orders production. 

- Advisor teams reach back to their higher headquarters for any adjustments to their orders and guidance. 

- At a minimum, advisor teams battle track the partner force, maintain a green COP, communicate to their higher headquarters, and continue to request resources in support of the partner force mission. 

- Upon mission completion, advisor teams update their partner force assessments and then recommends the following: 

 - Follow-on missions. 

 - Training plan adjustments. 

 - Refining support requirements. 

4-233. The process for partner force planned operations can differ and change between competition, crisis, and conflict. The relationship and rigor of previous advising efforts in (intentional or unintentional) preparation for partner-planned-operations will be a major determinant of the viability, efficiency, and permissions of participation. 

##### **ADVISOR TEAM PLAN** 

4-234. When advisor teams generate a plan at their level, that plan is constrained by both the support of the partner force and the higher headquarters. Receiving concurrence for these plans is more difficult since they require both the UAPs and the advisor team’s higher headquarters approval. They can be the most useful, however, since these plans are in direct response to identified capability gaps within the partner force. When generating a plan at their level, advisor teams follow a variation of several steps described in paragraphs 4-235 through 4-242. 

##### **CAPABILITY GAP** 

4-235. While conducting advisor team activities, the advisor team identifies a capability gap within the partner force. 

##### **COURSE OF ACTION FOR FUTURE OPERATION** 

4-236. The advisor team leader proposes the course of action to the partner force to generate buy in for the future operation to be conducted. (It must mesh with what the partner force wants; the partner force cannot be simply compelled by the demands of the advisor team.) 

##### **INITIAL PLAN** 

4-237. The advisor team develops an initial plan to help the partner force correct the capability gap. For example, the advisor team’s receipt of potential mission. 

##### **PARTNER FORCE ASSISTANCE FROM ADVISOR TEAMS** 

4-238. Advisor teams then guide the partner force to use their doctrine and resources to fill that gap. If the gap is due to a lack of doctrine or resources, the advisor team requests support from the appropriate headquarters (mission analysis). 

##### **VALIDATE REQUIREMENTS FOR U.S. SUPPORT** 

4-239. Before giving U.S. doctrine or additional resources to the partner force, the advisor team conducts a full foreign disclosure review and assessment to validate the requirements (course of action development, analysis, and comparison). 

##### **COMPLETE PLAN SUBMISSION** 

4-240. Upon completion of the foreign disclosure review, the advisor team develops and communicates a suitable, feasible, distinguishable, and complete plan between the higher headquarters and partner force to fill the capability gap (course of action approval). 

##### **ORDERS PRODUCTION AND EXECUTION** 

4-241. Advisor teams then work with their higher headquarters and the partner force during orders production and execution. 

##### **RISK MANAGEMENT** 

4-242. Risk management has a pronounced role when planning advisor team missions. Because of the nature of these missions, deliberate thought and planning must take place to ensure advisor teams and their higher headquarters have implemented the appropriate contingency plans and risk mitigation steps appropriate to the competition, crisis, and conflict. 

#### **PARALLEL PLANNING** 

4-243. When planning with a partner force, it is crucial to understand their objectives to solicit buy in. Regardless of the U.S. picture (integrated country strategy, TSCP, national security plan), the planned mission will not likely go forward if the partner force is not bought in. 

4-244. The specifics of the SFAB’s mission determine the character of its parallel planning relationship with the partner force. When planning with the partner force, the SFAB team leader and subordinates will provide advice and guidance to their counterparts. Advisors must understand the vision and goals of their higher headquarters and partner force to do this effectively. While advisors operate in an environment with unique variables, understanding the broad context of their SFA mission allows them to provide advice and guidance to the partner force that is consistent with U.S. goals and objectives. 

4-245. When planning with a partner force, advisors must understand their counterpart’s priorities and end state, higher headquarters’ goals and objectives, and other operational variables. Advisor teams must then understand, visualize, and describe their desired goals, objectives, and an end state in relation to their foreign counterparts. Providing this information to subordinate advisors gives them the necessary context for planning with their counterparts in support of a common vision and allows them to execute disciplined initiative. 

4-246. When advising a partner force, advisor teams conduct parallel planning at all appropriate echelons as dictated by the mission. The SFABs must develop their own internal advising plans while being prepared to coach their counterparts through operations and staff processes. 

4-247. Staff sections conduct continuous mission analysis. They analyze the SFA goals against their task organization, authorities, limitations, and potential problems. Staffs use warfighting functions as a guide to organize this analysis. In some cases, the nature of SFA may require a different model to analyze operations. 

Staffs may determine they cannot accomplish the mission through cross-leveling resources or task organization and that it requires augmentation. The advisor team submits a request for forces to its higher headquarters as necessary. Chapter 5 describes augmentation considerations in more detail. 

4-248. The advisor team should always conduct an abbreviated mission analysis prior to mission planning with the partner forces at a minimum. Mission analysis by the SFAB organizations allows the following: 

- Giving time to do prior coordination to avoid potential conflicts between the partner forces and other UAPs. 

- Providing updated SFA themes and guidance to the advisor team. 

- Providing the advisor team time to develop effective teaching and mentoring tools to foster counterpart understanding of the operation. 

- Allowing advisors to stay with their counterpart throughout the partner force planning process, which requires preplanning and coordination between the advisor team and the foreign counterpart unit. 

4-249. SFAB priorities may differ from the partner force. Within the boundaries established by the SFAB’s higher headquarters, operational plans should be responsive to the priorities of the partner forces and coordinated with other UAPs through reporting and liaisons. Advisors coach their counterparts to also account for other organizations and their priorities in the AO. Some aspects of planning include the following: 

- Selecting the application of combat power to minimize population casualties. 

- Coordinating regional and provisional plans. 

- Disseminating this information to subordinate units. 

- Providing time for subordinates to plan and prepare for operations. 

- Developing situational estimates. 

- Coordinating plans with other advisors in the AO, including those outside of the SFAB. 

- Synchronizing both FSF and U.S. COPs in addition to both shareable enemy operating pictures to develop a shared understanding with the partner force. 

4-250. The outcome of advisors planning with the partner force counterparts should be a plan or an order that— 

- Fosters mission command through a clear commander’s intent. 

- Assigns tasks and purposes to subordinates. 

- Contains the coordinating measures necessary to synchronize the operation. 

- Allocates or reallocates resources. 

- Directs preparation activities and establishes times or conditions for execution. 

4-251. The principles of mission command enable advisor teams to provide broad guidance to their subordinate elements and facilitate disciplined initiative. Building an environment of trust, communication, and initiative among subordinate commanders, staffs, and advisors facilitates mission command and leads to successful deployment operations, as discussed in chapter 5. 

This page intentionally left blank. 

##### **Chapter 5** 

### **Augmentation** 

_It is their war, and you are to help them, not win it for them._ 

T.E. Lawrence 

Chapter 5 describes augmentation considerations for permissive and hostile environments. This chapter also describes the various capabilities that may be available through augmentation by warfighting function. 

##### **SECTION I – AUGMENTATION CONSIDERATIONS** 

5-1. Commanders at all levels assess requirements for augmentation based on operational and mission variables and coordinate for the necessary forces. These could include joint, component, civilian agencies, or contractor support. Commanders and staffs evaluate criteria and requirements due to changing conditions involving the civilian population, enemy forces, and infrastructure. The main determination for the type and amount of augmentation required will be in the initial mission analysis determining whether the environment is permissive or hostile. When requesting augmentation, SFAB and advisor team commanders must plan for deliberate integration and synchronization of augmented personnel and equipment. The augmented personnel should be included in the SFAB predeployment or home station training whenever possible. 

#### **PERMISSIVE ENVIRONMENT (COMPETITION – CRISIS)** 

5-2. Support and augmentation requirements for a permissive environment can be very different from those required in a hostile environment. A _permissive environment_ is uncontested conditions in which joint forces have freedom of movement (JP 3-0). The SFAB is purpose-built to self-deploy and conduct partner engagement without augmentation in a permissive environment with a few notable exceptions. First, the SFAB does not possess organic non-tactical lift or mobility. All movements of advisors will have to be coordinated with an outside agency. Second, the SFAB possesses limited logistics and medical capabilities; some logistics and medical support will need to come either from the host nation or off the local economy. Conduct a thorough mission analysis upon receipt of mission to identify all external support requirements. Permissive environment support and augmentation requirements may include the following: 

- Interpreter support. 

- Housing. 

- Field feeding. 

- Mobility. 

- Medical support. 

- Security augmentation. 

#### **HOSTILE ENVIRONMENT (CRISIS – CONFLICT)** 

5-3. Defining the needed support requirements when entering or transitioning into a hostile environment will be the most crucial outcome of mission analysis. A _hostile environment_ is an operational environment where the freedom of movement is contested (JP 3-0). Forces available will largely determine augmentation and support availability and whether the SFAB is entering a mature or new JOA. SFABs possess the capability to self-deploy to a hostile environment, but they have limited ability to self-secure, move, or sustain themselves without augmentation or FSF assistance. Hostile environment support and augmentation may include the following: 

- Security augmentation to include protection assets such as— 

 - CBRN. 

 - Engineer. 

 - Air defense. 

 - UASs. 

 - Counter-unmanned aircraft system (C-UAS). 

- Interpreter support. 

- Sustainment. 

- Communications. 

- Intelligence. 

- Mobility. 

- Medical support. 

- Fires. 

- Access to quick reaction force. 

#### **COORDINATION** 

5-4. Augmentation coordination involves higher headquarters, contributing organization staffs, SFAB staff, subordinate advisor teams, and UAPs. Coordination needs to be as thorough and detailed as possible. All parties should understand the concept of the operation, including detailed coordination instructions. If necessary, coordination includes an interpreter to facilitate explanation and understanding. 

5-5. Coordination with higher headquarters involves requests for augmentation, identification of contributing organizations, liaisons with identified organizations, and notification upon arrival. Coordination with subordinate advisor teams involves determining how the augmented support is task-organized within subordinate units and clearly stating the intent and objectives for the augmentee. 

5-6. Units determine force requirements when requesting augmentation from UAPs. Units conduct an analysis to determine additional requirements for working with U.S. Forces. The requirements can include the following: 

- Personnel considerations, including— 

 - Personnel status. 

 - Health records. 

 - Accountability rosters. 

 - Battle roster numbers. 

 - Survival, evasion, resistance, and escape training requirements. 

 - Threat briefings. 

 - Interpreter support. 

- Medical requirements, including— 

 - Immunizations. 

 - Medical records. 

 - Specific theater medical training and health requirements. 

- Training requirements, including— 

 - Technical certifications. 

 - Weapons qualifications. 

 - Vehicle training. 

 - Training under simulated CBRN conditions. 

- Logistics considerations, including— 

 - Transportation. 

 - Fuel. 

 - Ammunition. 

 - Recovery. 

 - Food. 

 - Any unique requirements including the means to recover, fuel, arm, and repair vehicles and equipment that are not organic to the requesting unit. 

#### **MULTINATIONAL AND FOREIGN SECURITY AUGMENTATION** 

5-7. Each multinational military or foreign force has unique capabilities, methods, and force structure. Conducting allied, foreign, and U.S. Forces comprehensive capabilities briefs aids in understanding these variables across the organizations. Previous experience working with one nation does not translate to working with another. 

5-8. Detailed communications are key when planning reconnaissance and security operations with a multinational or foreign military partner and begin immediately upon receipt of the mission. Confirmation briefs during planning aid in reducing language, cultural, and military capability barriers. Exchange of liaison personnel, with interpreters as necessary, occurs as early as possible in the planning process. SFAB units and staffs can consider ways to begin planning for operations with a multinational or partner force including but not limited to the following discussion points: 

- C2: 

 - Compatibility of radios and digital systems. 

 - Multinational radios encryption capabilities. 

 - COMSEC requirements (North Atlantic Treaty Organization key). 

 - Multinational radio capabilities and limitations affecting the United States’ ability to conduct retransmission. 

 - MNFs’ retransmission capabilities. 

 - Digital versus analog effective communications planning. 

 - Digital collaboration capabilities. 

 - Secret Internet Protocol Router Network capabilities. 

 - Placement and use of liaison officers and planners. 

 - Type and frequency of rehearsals. 

 - Rules of engagement. 

 - National caveats that may restrict the force. 

 - Task organization. 

 - Command relationship authorization. 

- Maneuver: 

 - Exchange of tactics, doctrine, and SOPs. 

 - Maneuver assets, capabilities, and limitations. 

 - Explain operational terms and graphics (and rank structure as applicable). 

 - Work to reduce the language barrier to the greatest extent possible. 

 - Task-organize changes and impacts of command relationships (if authorized). 

 - Incorporate route clearance, mobility, and countermobility. 

- Intelligence: 

 - Classification and authority to release information. 

 - Intelligence sharing in both directions. 

 - U.S. and multinational information collection capabilities. 

 - U.S. and multinational information collection priorities such as CCIR. 

 - Interrogation procedures and restrictions, which vary widely from nation to nation. 

- Fires: 

 - Assets, capabilities, and limitations. 

 - Integration of all warfighting functions to maximize effectiveness. 

 - Cross-boundary fires (approval authority). 

 - Call for fire and counterbattery procedures and authorities. 

 - Use of precision-guided munitions. 

 - Clear understanding of fire support coordination measures and airspace coordinating measures. 

 - Established fires and quick-fire radio frequencies. 

 - Include in rehearsals. 

- Protection: 

 - Multinational CBRN defense capabilities to assess, protect against, and mitigate CBRN threats and hazards. 

 - Multinational air defense artillery and tactical ground-to-air radar systems detection and integration, to include C-UAS capabilities. 

 - Multinational EOD and survivability capabilities. 

 - Detainee operations and procedures. 

 - Priority assets that require engineer support to increase survivability, such as command posts and communications platforms. 

- Sustainment: 

 - ACSAs, which determine national financial obligations. 

- Types of command support relationships and nesting with ACSAs. 

- Resupply of nationally distinct items (specifically Classes III, V, IX, and CBRN defense equipment). 

- Transportation and distribution capabilities or requirements. 

- Medical capabilities. 

- Fuel requirements and compatibility for foreign equipment. 

- Power generation requirements (220/110-volt international adapter and transformer options). 

##### **SECTION II – AUGMENTATION ANALYSIS** 

##### **Advisor functions in Korea** 

<mark>Though technically the advisors’ least important role, participation in combat operations was the one assignment that carried the most risk and had the potential for the greatest gain. How much an advisor had to do personally was a good gauge of a Korean officer’s or unit’s competence. General Roberts (Brigadier General. William L. Roberts, Chief of KMAG from August 1948-June 1950) made it very clear that the advisor’s role was to prepare their counterparts to do for themselves what they could no longer rely upon the Americans to do—defend their country. Advisors had no official combat role to perform, and they had no command authority over Korean troops; nevertheless, many found themselves participating in actual operations against Communist guerrillas, bandits, and even North Korean security and army forces. In June 1949, just a month after the most severe fighting along the 38th parallel, and in the midst of a concerted antiguerrilla campaign, Roberts reminded his advisors that though their chief task was training, they needed to be prepared to assume a wartime function. “This army,” he told them, “Even to do its [national] defense mission, will, in</mark> my opinion, need your stabilizing advice.” 

5-9. SFAB commanders and staffs use the six warfighting functions of C2, movement and maneuver, intelligence, fires, sustainment, and protection as a framework for conducting analysis and determining what additional augmentation may be required. The warfighting functions are a proven framework to assist the unit with determining augmentation requirements, but staffs can use any framework with which they are comfortable., The purpose is to identify requirements and gaps regardless of the framework chosen. The augmentation examples provided in paragraphs 5-10 through 5-68 are thorough, but not all-inclusive. 

#### **COMMAND AND CONTROL** 

5-10. The SFAB has a C2 structure that integrates warfighting functions into its organization. The structure does this through direct integration (placing team members directly with their counterparts) or decentralized methods (placing various team members throughout the partner force at key locations). Direct integration is most useful when the intensity of conflict is high and information requirements drive quick reactions, such as counterfire missions. Use decentralized methods when the SFAB is in regional support or as part of a coalition supporting various nonintegrated forces. Successful mission accomplishment in decentralized methods of augmentation relies on task execution at lower echelons and emphasizes leader initiative and involvement. 

##### **CIVIL-MILITARY OPERATIONS** 

5-11. CMOs are the activities performed by military forces to establish, maintain, influence, or exploit relationships between military forces and indigenous populations and institutions. CMO support U.S. objectives for host-nation and regional stability (see JP 3-57). In an SFAB context, CMO augmentation may reduce civil-military friction, allow access to additional support channels, or enhance unity of action between civilian and military entities. The advisor team's mission may be a portion of a joint force commander's plan regarding the civil component; however, the U.S. Department of State and host-nation civil authorities may preclude advisors from directly influencing the civilian population. In this case, the SFAB commander may request civil-military operation capabilities from the U.S. Department of State, joint force commander, or other authorities to engage the civil component of the OE. SFAB commanders may leverage FSF existing structure to coordinate with the interagency and indigenous institutions within their AOR.

##### **INFORMATION** 

5-12. Information plays a significant role in the FSF executing its mission. From information management to activities in cyberspace, to public diplomacy and international messaging, how the FSF view and employ information likely permeates everything they do. Therefore, the SFAB must prepare to support the FSF’s information functions. 

5-13. The SFAB has a sparse information capability that does not include specialists outside of communications technicians. During mission analysis, when the SFABs determine their own capability gaps and request external support, they request appropriate personnel or units. These may include cyberspace, public affairs, information operations (Functional Area 30) personnel, and psychological operations or other units and capabilities. The desired capability and capacity of the FSF and the analysis of the SFAB’s organic capabilities drives the need for external capabilities. 

##### **NETWORK AND COMMUNICATIONS** 

5-14. Signal operations support the SFAB by providing communications and the digital network enabling C2. The three network operations tasks of enterprise management, network assurance, and content management, provide the SFAB with the ability plan, conduct, and sustain operations effectively. These capabilities provide the SFAB with access to cyberspace, higher and lower organizations, DOD information network, the FSF, and other UAPs as necessary. Since the SFAB often operates in austere environments, augmentation may be required to ensure reliable communications. 

5-15. Network and communication augmentation support is scalable and flexible. The SFAB S-6 anticipates, plans, and coordinates for additional signal capabilities as necessary. (See FM 6-02.) The competencies of the Signal Corps are the following: 

- Electromagnetic spectrum (EMS) access. 

- Network transport. 

- Network and systems management and integration. 

- Network and data security. 

- Data architecture and engineering. 

#### **MOVEMENT AND MANEUVER** 

5-16. The SFAB may require additional maneuver forces to enhance the advisor team’s physical security or to support the FSF mission. In an established JOA, the SFAB requests additional maneuver support through the higher headquarters. In some cases, its higher headquarters may direct the SFAB to coordinate directly with UAPs or the FSF for additional maneuver forces. When deployed to a theater that does not have an established JOA, the SFAB coordinates with the host-nation FSF or government via the embassy country team for additional maneuver forces. 

##### **AVIATION** 

5-17. Army Aviation conducts air-ground operations as the aerial maneuver force of the combined arms team or as an independent maneuver force to support ground forces conducting unified action tasks. Army Aviation executes its seven core competencies through the following tactical, enabling, and sustaining tasks (see FM 3-04): 

- Provide accurate and timely information. 

- Provide reaction time and maneuver space. 

- Destroy, defeat, disrupt, divert, or delay enemy forces. 

- Provide air assault ground maneuver forces. 

- Provide air movement of personnel, equipment, and supplies. 

- Evacuate wounded or recover isolated personnel. 

- Enable C2 over extended ranges and complex terrain. 

5-18. When supporting the SFAB mission, attack, lift, and UAS capabilities are the most probable aviation augmentation requirements. When available, Army Aviation augmentation provides the SFAB with the ability to conduct air movement of personnel, leaders, critical supplies, equipment, and systems as well as attack aviation support to combat operations. Due to the flexible nature of aircraft, short-duration and shortnotice augmentation can be requested through supporting aviation units. For example, a UAS may be able to provide observation on a suspected enemy indirect fire site while flying back from another mission. 

##### **UNMANNED AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS** 

5-19. The UAS is a feature of the OE throughout the world and the competition continuum. A UAS can enhance movement and maneuver by providing situational awareness. SFABs and FSF also employ UASs to enhance targeting, gather intelligence, conduct rehearsals, assess protection planning, deliver supplies and myriad other applications across the warfighting functions. SFABs are assigned small UASs at the MBAT, MCAT, and MAT level but should seek additional support from the partner force and their higher headquarters to either augment their internal capabilities or fill capability gaps. This augmentation may include equipment familiarization training, AOR legalities advising, emissions detection and reduction information and techniques, and additional training on employment tactics, techniques, and procedures. 

##### **SECURITY FORCES** 

5-20. The SFAB requests additional security elements when conducting operations in many environments. Due to the SFAB’s limited organic capacity for maneuver against an enemy force, the SFAB requests additional security to provide the appropriate level of combat power necessary to conduct operations alongside the FSF. These additional security elements may come from other U.S. Forces, contractors, or allied nations. If required, advisor teams should be prepared to provide a security force. In a non-JOA environment, the SFAB may receive additional protection through local police forces. 

##### **SPECIAL OPERATIONS FORCES** 

5-21. The SFAB may request special operations forces’ support for a wide variety of operations or tasks. Special operations forces conduct a wide variety of missions and activities including unconventional warfare, foreign internal defense, SFA, counterinsurgency, direct action, special reconnaissance, counterterrorism, military information SPO, CA operations, countering WMD, foreign HA, and hostage rescue and recovery. (See JP 3-05.) 

5-22. Army special operations forces conduct most missions and activities with and through indigenous, foreign, irregular, and regular partners. Army special operations forces engage with many of the same partners and populations SFAB Soldiers encounter. Army special operations forces often conduct similar activities to those of the SFAB but with different partners, at a different scale of operations, and for specific, specially defined purposes. Army special operations forces frequently have pre-established relationships and influence in foreign partner nations. Army special operations forces and the SFAB should be natural and complementary partners. 

5-23. Numerous criteria determine when conventional forces, special operations forces, or combinations are appropriate to conduct SFA. Military-force levels, force characteristics, and other factors present planners with optimal, acceptable, and persistent force package options when resourcing SFA missions. Deployment of a regionally aligned force for an SFA mission depends on the validated requirement of the CCDR. Often, special operations forces are already conducting missions in areas where a nation is requesting SFA. In many cases, the host nation may be expanding the parameters of its SFA request and the SFAB may find itself relieving special operations forces of its mission to allow special operations forces to transition to other tasks. The SFAB and the special operations forces conduct operations independently but collaborate and coordinate when it is determined they have shared interests. 

5-24. U.S. operations generally involve both conventional and special operations when conducting SFA. Army doctrine defines command and support relationships of both forces operating in the same area. Recent operational experience has shown that, in improving the effectiveness of the FSF, the supported commander conducting SFA may be either a conventional force or special operations forces commander. Subordinate commanders and staffs of both conventional forces and special operations forces may act in the supporting role to the U.S. ambassador. Typically, the SC organization or the DATT at the American Embassy manages this relationship. 

#### **INTELLIGENCE** 

5-25. The SFAB commander may require additional intelligence capabilities to support the mission. The SFAB intelligence officer has limited resources, specifically in personnel and time, to analyze and process intelligence. As such, intelligence advisors are especially affected by the advisor dichotomy. During mission analysis, the SFAB staff analyzes organic intelligence capabilities, restrictions of intelligence activities within the AO, available augmentation capabilities, and sustainment requirements of additional intelligence resources. The seven intelligence disciplines are human intelligence, CI, signals intelligence, measurement and signature intelligence, open-source intelligence, geospatial intelligence, and technical intelligence. In addition to these seven disciplines, there are other complementary intelligence capabilities as well, refer to ATPs in the 2-22 series, for example, ATP 2-22.6, ATP 2-22.7, and ATP 2-22.31. 

##### **HUMAN INTELLIGENCE** 

5-26. _Human intelligence_ is the collection by a trained human intelligence collector of foreign information from people and multimedia to identify elements, intentions, composition, strength, dispositions, tactics, equipment, and capabilities (ADP 2-0). HUMINT uses human sources as a tool to gather information about the plans, intentions, capabilities, and operations methods of threat military forces. While the SFAB does not have a full team, it does have a human intelligence technician and collector. The Army human intelligence collectors (MOS 35Ms) are assigned to the MI company and may perform limited human intelligence functions. If required, SFAB commanders may request human intelligence collection teams to support and assist with SFA. Prior to requesting or employing human intelligence collection teams, however, staffs must examine status-of-forces agreements, rules of engagement, and political conditions that may enable the employment of human intelligence collection teams and human intelligence operations to support foreign forces or environments. Human intelligence tasks include the following: 

- Conducting source operations. 

- Debriefing U.S. and allied forces and civilian personnel including dislocated civilians, third-country nationals, and local inhabitants. 

- Interrogating detainees. 

##### **COUNTERINTELLIGENCE** 

5-27. _Counterintelligence_ is information gathered and activities conducted to identify, deceive, exploit, disrupt, or protect against espionage, other intelligence activities, sabotage, or assassinations conducted for or on behalf of foreign powers, organizations or persons or their agents, or international terrorist organizations or activities (JP 2-0). The SFAB is assigned CI personnel within the MI company that may perform CI functions and, if needed, may be augmented with external capabilities. CI focuses on the detection, identification, analysis, neutralization, or exploitation of foreign intelligence entities, foreign terrorist organizations, and insider threats to protect Army and designated DOD forces, information, and technologies worldwide. Army CI applies its resources to four primary mission areas—counterespionage, CI support to force protection, information collection to identify and counter threat intelligence collection targeting Army equities, and CI support to technology and critical infrastructure protection. MI company CI agents gain additional authorities to execute CI collections, investigations, and operations in support of the SFAB through the ASCC or Army Counterintelligence Command CI technical channels, as required. CI support is available in a JOA but may require additional coordination in a non-JOA environment. Legal guidance can enable or prevent certain CI activities within the AO. 

The activities that CI agents conduct within covering agent program activities include: 

- Foreign intelligence entity focused research and analysis. 

- Blunt-force operational profiles development. 

- Foreign intelligence entity threat education. 

- CI portion of vulnerability assessments. 

- Intelligence, security, law enforcement, CI, and supported organizations liaison. 

- Foreign intelligence entity threat countermeasure development. 

- Insider threat detection support. 

- CI lead development. 

- CI focused debriefing. 

5-28. _Signals intelligence_ is a category of intelligence comprising all communications intelligence, electronic intelligence, and foreign instrumentation signals intelligence, however transmitted, individually or in combination (JP 2-0). Signals intelligence is one of several military functions operating within the EMS; it provides EW support when used in military operations for threat warning, combat information, and situational awareness. Signals intelligence provides technical intelligence information, complements intelligence derived from other sources, and is often used for cueing other sensors to potential targets of interest. For example, signals intelligence may identify information used to cue the SFAB or the FSF to confirm potential enemy activity. The discipline is subdivided into three subcategories: communications intelligence, electronic intelligence, and foreign instrumentation signals intelligence (see FM 2-0). 

##### **MEASUREMENT AND SIGNATURE INTELLIGENCE** 

5-29. _Measurement and signature intelligence_ is information produced by quantitative and qualitative analysis of physical attributes of targets and events to detect, characterize, locate, and identify targets and events; and derived from specialized, technically derived measurements and signatures of physical phenomenon intrinsic to an object or event (JP 2-0). 

5-30. Depending on the OE assessment, the SFAB commander and staff may consider measurement and signature intelligence information to support the SFAB and FSF missions. Measurement and signature intelligence information and capabilities are available through the theater or regional intelligence channels by requesting the required information. 

##### **OPEN-SOURCE INTELLIGENCE** 

5-31. _Open-source intelligence_ is publicly available information collected, exploited, and disseminated to address a specific requirement (JP 2-0). Open-source intelligence is derived from publicly available information collected by trained and certified open-source intelligence collectors/practitioners, who use tools, processes, and analytics to generate operationally relevant intelligence from the vast amounts and variety of publicly available information. SFAB elements will coordinate with ASCC intelligence staff to identify opensource intelligence support requirements. (See ATP 2-22.9-1 and ATP 2-22.9-2 for more information on open-source intelligence.) 

##### **GEOSPATIAL INTELLIGENCE** 

5-32. _Geospatial intelligence_ is the exploitation and analysis of imagery and geospatial information to describe, assess, and visually depict physical features and geographically referenced activities on or about the Earth. Geospatial intelligence consists of imagery, imagery intelligence, and geospatial information (JP 2-0). 

5-33. The SFAB could require geospatial intelligence capabilities to support assigned missions. An example of using geospatial intelligence personnel to support the FSF may be providing it with detailed graphics to conduct operations. As with measurement and signature intelligence and the other more technical capabilities, the information these assets provide is generally available for request through theater or regional intelligence channels. 

##### **TECHNICAL INTELLIGENCE** 

5-34. _Technical intelligence_ is intelligence derived from the collection, processing, analysis, and exploitation of data and information pertaining to foreign equipment and materiel for the purposes of preventing technological surprise, assessing foreign scientific and technical capabilities, and developing countermeasures designed to neutralize an enemy’s technological advantages (JP 2-0). Technical intelligence is an intelligence reach capability that ensures the Army understands the threat’s full technological capabilities as well as its limitations and vulnerabilities. This enables friendly forces to adopt the appropriate countermeasures, operations, and tactics, techniques, and procedures. The SFAB S-2 establishes accounts to technical intelligence databases and data portals to access the reports, countermeasures, and other specific tactics, techniques, and procedures for the SFAB to utilize during its operations.

##### **COMPLEMENTARY INTELLIGENCE CAPABILITIES** 

5-35. JP 2-0 recognizes the following intelligence activities and applications: identity intelligence, biometrics-enabled intelligence, forensics-enabled intelligence, and document and media exploitation. The Army also recognizes various specialized capabilities that can contribute to all-source or single-source intelligence through intelligence coordination with other warfighting functions and branches. These specialized and technical capabilities, many of which are not typically conducted by military intelligence units, can provide valuable insight into threat activities and intentions and can fill intelligence gaps. In some instances, they can provide critical information that supports security, consolidating gains, and rear area operations. These capabilities include but are not limited to the following: 

- Biometrics. 

- Cyberspace. 

- Document and media exploitation. 

- EW. 

- Forensics. 

- Identity activities. 

- Space. 

#### **FIRES** 

5-36. The _fires warfighting function_ is the related tasks and systems that create and converge effects in all domains against the adversary or enemy to enable operations across the range of military operations (ADP 3-0). Generally, the SFAB relies on nonorganic fires capabilities. While many diverse fires systems and processes exist, understanding the intricacies of each system will be fruitless without understanding the JOA and organizational relationships between sensor, shooter, and target. Advisors prioritize understanding the relationships and authorities to enable interoperability across UAPs within the AOR. When operating in a JOA, the SFAB may request Army fires systems for specific lethal and nonlethal effects to support offensive, defensive, and stability tasks. The fires warfighting function includes the following tasks: 

- Delivering fires. 

- Integrating all forms of Army, joint, and multinational fires. 

- Conducting targeting. 

5-37. Due to the organization of the SFAB and the absence of organic indirect fires systems, it is critical to establish the fires support communication channels with supporting theater elements to ensure that fire support is available when necessary. Additionally, due to the nature of their mission working with FSF, the clearance of fires, rules of engagement, fire support coordination measures, and airspace coordination measures ought to be well defined and communicated between all the organizations involved in the operations. (See JP 3-09, FM 3-09, FM 3-96, and ATP 3-09.42.) 

##### **JOINT AND MULTINATIONAL FIRES** 

5-38. Joint or multinational fires are fires delivered during the employment of forces from two or more components or nations in a coordinated action to produce the desired effects to support a common objective (see JP 3-16). Developing policy, guidance, and plans to employ operational and strategic fires are primarily joint activities. Joint fires include surface-to-surface, surface-to-air, and air-to-surface fires. 

5-39. When integrating joint and multinational fires, coordination and synchronization responsibilities are heightened and requests for indirect fire support are elevated to a higher headquarters element. It may be necessary to establish a liaison with the supporting unit, especially with multinational units. 

5-40. Fire support coordination in multinational operations requires special arrangements with MNFs and local authorities. Some mission partners may not use fire support coordination measures, or such measure may not be transposable with other partner’s systems. In a lack of transposable coordination measure, building and maintaining the blue and green COP is crucial to effective and expedient fires at echelon. Special arrangements include implementing communications and language standards, exchanging liaison personnel, and adopting processes and procedures to enable and enhance interoperability. 

##### **FIELD ARTILLERY** 

5-41. _Field artillery_ is the equipment, supplies, ammunition, and personnel involved in the use of cannon, rocket, or surface-to-surface missile launchers (JP 3-09). Field artillery contributes to operations by massing fires on single or multiple targets with precision, near precision, and area fire capabilities. Field artillery can rapidly shift fires throughout the AO to support the scheme of maneuver and to counter unforeseen enemy reaction to achieve the maneuver commander’s desired effects. 

5-42. The SFAB has a field artillery advisor battalion that is not equipped with cannon or rocket batteries. In a JOA, SFAB fires personnel must coordinate with a component field artillery headquarters (field artillery brigade and division artillery) or a field artillery unit that has been assigned a support relationship with the SFAB. 

##### **CLEARANCE OF FIRE PROCEDURES** 

5-43. Advisors can increase the timeliness of fires clearance through fire support coordination measures and execution of battle drills. They can do this by rehearsing these battle drills frequently alongside their partners. Through timely reporting, the FSF and SFAB advisor teams maintain situational awareness to clear their AO of both blue forces and green forces. The SFAB uses its command systems to relay that information to coalition forces to coordinate and clear the fires. 

##### **TARGETING** 

5-44. Targeting is a critical component of the fires warfighting function. The SFAB must assign a fires support coordinator to lead the brigades’ targeting process, as required, to integrate lethal and nonlethal effects with SFAB and FSF operations. The SFAB and advisor teams should be prepared to synchronize multiple targeting cycles with multiple partner forces when conducting multinational operations, which may require additional targeting expertise within the advisor teams. The SFAB staff structure has limited targeting personnel, much of this capability resides on the advisor teams. The SFAB staffs rely heavily on subordinate advisor teams to provide targeting information and recommendations to the staff. This can result in field artillery teams integrating with SFAB staffs to facilitate targeting, instead of partnering with a FSF unit. Higher headquarters can request additional support as necessary. (See JP 3-60 and FM 3-60.) 

##### **AIR DEFENSE ARTILLERY** 

5-45. The SFAB has no organic or assigned air defense personnel or equipment. In a JOA environment, the SFAB falls under an area air defense plan. When air defense artillery personnel are assigned or attached to the SFAB as augmentation, air defense artillery commanders and staffs brief the supported commander and staff on the capabilities and limitations that the air defense artillery personnel and equipment can provide. 

5-46. When necessary, based on the enemy threat assessment, the SFAB considers integrating air defense artillery throughout all phases of major operations. The air defense artillery performs the following essential tasks: 

- Integrating air defense artillery assets according to the area air defense plan. 

- Allocating available assets throughout the operational area. 

- Deploying early warning systems. 

- Coordinating airspace control activities with theater, corps, and subordinate air defense artillery cells. 

- Recommending air defense artillery tactics and techniques to best counter air and missile threats. 

#### **PROTECTION** 

5-47. The _protection warfighting function_ is the related tasks, systems, and methods that prevent or mitigate detection, threat effects, and hazards to preserve combat power and enable freedom of action (FM 3-0). Preserving the force includes protecting personnel (combatant and noncombatant) and physical assets of the United States and UAPs, including partner nations. Protection is a critical consideration for the SFAB given its potential employment in areas where established protection infrastructure may be limited. The SFAB may receive greater support from theater protection assets within a JOA. Outside of established JOAs, comprehensive, integrated, layered, redundant, and enduring protection planning and coordination are required to ensure sufficient protection. Every OE has unique protection planning considerations. 

5-48. Protection is an essential consideration for SFAB commanders and staffs and could require significant augmentation based on assessed threats and mission requirements. In permissive environments, the SFAB relies on protection capabilities and security provided by the FSF. In uncertain or hostile environments, the SFAB may require augmentation with complementary protection capabilities. An _uncertain environment_ is a condition in which the joint force’s freedom of movement may be contested (JP 3-0). Chapter 6 describes protection advising in much greater detail but listed in paragraphs 5-49 through 5-59 are key protection augmentation elements that may be available to SFABs. 

##### **CHEMICAL, BIOLOGICAL, RADIOLOGICAL, AND NUCLEAR** 

5-49. CBRN units and personnel provide critical support through the core functions of assess, protect, and mitigate. Information obtained through these core functions is linked through the integrating activity of hazard awareness. The proliferation of WMD signifies a growing worldwide threat and the SFAB must prepare to operate in this environment. The SFAB has individual protection and decontamination equipment but limited detection equipment and no unit decontamination equipment. The SFAB must assess the threat environment and request CBRN augmentation based on risk assessment. The CBRN capabilities will typically be sourced by FSF or the ASCC. 

##### **ENGINEERS** 

5-50. Engineers provide technical expertise across their disciplines of combat, general, and geospatial engineering. The SFAB contains an EBAT, however, the assigned engineer advisors lack most capabilities outside of advising. If an engineer advisor were to be assigned a mission which would require significant support activities to the partner, then specialized equipment would have to be augmented to that team (for example, a bulldozer, soil analysis equipment, surveying equipment). Engineer advising battalion individual members have technical expertise in reducing explosive hazards within their EOCA capability (limited conventional) to enable and maintain SFAB advisor team mobility. As the situation dictates, especially when the SFAB encounters significant construction requirements, commanders and staffs must consider augmentation of the SFAB with additional engineer capabilities or contracted construction support. The EBATs provide the SFAB with the experience necessary to provide recommendations on engineer support required. 

5-51. When available, various types of engineer organizations can provide capabilities and augmentation to an advisor team to include the following: 

- General engineering: 

 - Restores damaged areas, constructing, and maintaining lines of communications, establishing base camps, repairing or restoring infrastructure. 

 - Provides engineering assessments (United States Army Corps of Engineers, Forward Engineer Support Team). 

 - Provides master planning for facility and design support, developing and maintaining facilities.
