 - Provides power generation and distribution and contracted construction oversight. 

- Combat engineering: 

 - Mobility support. 

 - Countermobility support. 

 - Survivability support. 

 - Additional enabling capabilities. 

- Geospatial engineering: 

- Terrain analysis and terrain visualization. 

- Tailored map products. 

- Geospatial foundation data. 

- Theater geospatial data. 

- ASCC geospatial planning cells. 

##### **AIR DEFENSE ARTILLERY** 

5-52. Air defense artillery participates in protection activities by using active and passive intervention techniques to defend both mission and force from aerial threats. The air defender views operations through a trained eye with a focus on preventing detection from manned and UASs, satellite imaging capabilities, and enemy air defense capability relative combat power analysis. SFABs are not assigned air defense artillery personnel, however, and must request necessary augmentation if additional systems or personnel are required to accomplish the air defense tasks. Some areas for consideration are radar detection systems, aerial sensor system signature detection, relative air defense combat power systems analysis, enemy and friendly UAS capabilities, and C-UAS capabilities of the stakeholders within the OE. Air defenders do not just focus on conventional military aircraft systems but also consider commercial and consumer-grade UAS capabilities that are available within the SFAB’s AOR. 

##### **EXPLOSIVE ORDNANCE DISPOSAL** 

5-53. SFAB explosive hazard advisors may provide immediate action to mitigate an explosive hazard to prevent the loss of life when no other EOD capability can reasonably respond. However, EOD advisors lack the capability and capacity of an EOD team. Therefore, if advisor teams do or may encounter explosive hazards, they must request EOD augmentation. 

5-54. EOD staff and unit augmentation may provide the following capabilities: 

- Supporting disposal. 

- Developing EOD plans. 

- Coordinating EOD operations. 

- Supervising EOD operations. 

##### **MILITARY POLICE** 

5-55. Military police provide police, detention, security, and mobility support capabilities that may augment SFAB protection capabilities to enhance security and preserve combat power. Given the core competencies of policing, investigations, and corrections, military police should be requested to augment the SFAB with police advisors when requirements exist to advise and assist partner nation police or corrections organizations. In recent conflicts in Iraq and Afghanistan, military police provided scalable and flexible police development training teams critical to accomplishing police advise-and-assist missions. (See FM 3-39, FM 3-63, and ATP 3-39.10.) 

5-56. Military police provide a unique mixture of capabilities across the military police disciplines that may augment SFAB protection or advisor capabilities including the following: 

- Police operations: 

 - Law enforcement. 

 - Forensics and biometrics support. 

 - Criminal investigations. 

 - Civil disturbance control. 

 - Evidence response teams. 

 - Host-nation police training support. 

 - Customs support. 

 - Support to border control and boundary security. 

- Detention operations: 

 - Confinement of U.S. military prisoners. 

 - Detainee operations. 

 - Host-nation corrections training and support. 

- Security and mobility support: 

 - Area security. 

- Base and base-camp defense. 

- Response force operations. 

- Critical asset security. 

- High-risk personnel security. 

- Route and convoy security. 

- Antiterrorism and physical security support. 

- Military working dogs. 

#### **SUSTAINMENT** 

5-57. The _sustainment warfighting function_ is the related tasks and systems that provide support and services to ensure freedom of action, extend operational reach, and prolong endurance (ADP 3-0). Sustainment considerations for the SFAB are like protection in that SFABs have access to established theater infrastructure in established JOAs but require significant augmentation when employed outside of a JOA. 

#### **OTHER CONSIDERATIONS** 

5-58. The SFAB may also require augmentation that does not necessarily align with the warfighting functions. Higher headquarters may direct that augmentation, or it could take the form of UAPs or ministry liaison teams. 

##### **UNIFIED ACTION PARTNERS** 

5-59. Augmentation from UAPs depends on the specific requirements of the SFAB’s mission and the participation of other nations’ forces. In uncertain environments, the presence of nonmilitary UAPs may be limited. Many civilian, law enforcement, and other agencies require a permissive or semi-permissive environment before agreeing to participate in SFA operations in theater. 

5-60. Potential UAP augmentation may include— 

- Joint and multinational military partners. 

- U.S. federal agencies like the U.S. Department of State, the U.S. Department of Justice, and the U.S. Agency for International Development. 

- International governmental organizations like the World Bank, International Monetary Fund, World Health Organization, and the International Committee of the Red Cross. 

- Nongovernmental organizations like Doctors Without Borders, Human Rights Watch, and the Mines Advisory Group. 

##### **LANGUAGE SUPPORT** 

5-61. SFAB operations are highly dependent on foreign language support. Exploiting enemy documents, communicating with the foreign partner, host-nation organizations, and local populations necessitates using interpreters and linguists. 

5-62. Advisor teams must analyze linguist needs to determine the minimum level of foreign language proficiency needed including additional requirements for security clearances, specific ethnicities, genders, or other considerations. The FSF might already have some English proficiency which may reduce the requirement for linguists. If they do not, interpreters (categories I or II) may be requested. This includes determining the translation accuracy requirements. The national-level documents and agreements (for example, statement of requirement, ACSA) that are done to enable an advisor team to be in country will require linguist support from a category II or III interpreter. (See ATP 3-07.10.) 

##### **MINISTRY LIAISON TEAMS** 

5-63. The ministerial liaison team serves as a single unit collocated with the host-nation MOD, interior, or other institutions to provide continuous advising support to host-nation personnel and leaders. Higher headquarters determines whether to augment the SFAB with a ministerial liaison team. 

5-64. The ministerial liaison team may include subject matter experts on OCS, budgeting, strategic plans and policy, foreign area officer, provost marshal, strategic intelligence, logistics, force modernization and design, human resources, medical, and legal advisors. SFAB teams work with the ministerial teams to assist in building core institutional capacity, competencies, and capabilities to support defense-related processes.

##### **ADDITIONAL AUGMENTATION** 

5-65. Augmentation requirements may change as the advisor teams and partner force transition between competition, crisis, and conflict. Commanders require augmentation when they determine they can no longer accomplish the mission with assigned force structure or equipment. Augmentation allows commanders to accomplish tasks and enables success. In addition to pre-mission planning, commanders must constantly assess operations to determine what augmentation capabilities are required and integrate those forces into their operations as seamlessly as possible. As with any SFAB function, augmentation requires careful consideration with the foreign partners. Two areas requiring continuous augmentation analysis are the protection and sustainment functions which are discussed in chapters 6 and 7. 

### **Chapter 6 Protection** 

_Now, integrated deterrence means using every military and non-military tool in our toolbox, in lockstep with our allies and partners. Integrated deterrence is about using existing capabilities, and building new ones, and deploying them all in new and networked ways… all tailored to a region’s security landscape, and in growing partnership with our friends._ 

Secretary Lloyd J. Austin III, 28th Secretary of Defense 

Chapter 6 discusses the protection warfighting function considerations through the competition continuum and how advisor teams shape protection advising for FSF partners. 

##### **SECTION I – PROTECTION ACROSS THE COMPETITION CONTINUUM** 

6-1. Protection during competition, crisis, and conflict all regard preserving the available assets and synchronizing them in concert to mitigate the risks to mission and force. Accounting for the severity and probability of various outcomes, commanders, staffs, and protection warfighting function personnel assess and devise protection measures for present and likely future risks. Protection across the competition continuum may change in the ratios of efforts directed to prioritized assets or the protection method application, however protection remains conceptually coherent throughout the continuum. 

#### **PROTECTION DURING COMPETITION** 

6-2. Army operations during competition below armed conflict bring together the activities intended to promote regional stability and to set conditions for a favorable outcome of a military confrontation. Army Forces can protect the force by understanding and effectively preventing and mitigating adversary threats and hazards. Commanders and staffs mitigate and prevent threats and hazard effects by directing, coordinating, and synchronizing protection capabilities in support of Army operations during competition below armed conflict that— 

- Harden key facilities, including fighting positions, bomb shelters, C2 nodes, motor pools, and other critical infrastructure that may be vulnerable during crisis and armed conflict. 

- Incorporate mitigation measures to reduce operational risk to missions (risk management). 

- Implement measures to enable a healthy and fit force: 

 - Preventing injury and illness. 

 - Protecting the force from health hazards (FHP). 

 - Improving protection capabilities during competition below armed conflict preserves combat power during armed conflict. 

- Establish defensive measures to reduce the vulnerability of individuals and property to terrorist acts, to include limited response and containment by local military and civilian law enforcement. 

- Add personal security and defensive measures to protect Service members, high-risk personnel, civilian employees, family members, DOD facilities, information, and equipment (antiterrorism). 

- Integrate physical measures designed to safeguard the following:  Personnel. 

 - Prevent unauthorized access to equipment, installations, material, and documents. 

 - Safeguard against espionage, sabotage, damage, and theft (physical security). 

- Identify, control, and protect critical information and indicators that would allow enemies and adversaries to identify and exploit friendly vulnerabilities (OPSEC). 

- Conduct security operations to protect friendly forces, lines of communications, and activities within a specific area (area security). 

- Protect information networks (cyberspace and electromagnetic protection). 

- Maintain a safe and secure environment by countering criminal, terrorist, and hybrid threats, reducing crime, establishing order, preserving readiness, and enforcing the rule of law (police operations). 

- Safeguard and preserve Army resources (to include Soldiers, DA Civilians, and Army property) against accidental loss. 

- Provide for public safety incidents during Army operations and activities. 

- Protect civilian networks or groups identified by Army Forces as critical in facilitating the provision of essential services and overall governance (populace and resources control). 

- Establish security measures and deception measures to protect indigenous natural and man-made materiel resources of a nation-state, deny threats access to resources, and detect and reduce the effectiveness of criminal activity. 

- Protection of U.S. assets to deny or defeat enemy and criminal activities against them (populace and resources control). 

- Prepare for and execute the recovery and reintegration of isolated personnel (personnel recovery). 

- Conduct activities to protect against air and missile threats, including threats from small, UASs using active or passive measures (air and missile defense support). 

- Correct misinformation and counter disinformation (public affairs). 

- Assess threat CBRN capabilities and FSF vulnerabilities. 

- Advise on implementation of CBRN defense measures to reduce risk. 

6-3. During competition below armed conflict, protection considerations also include unit home station activities, such as maintaining operational readiness, training, and contingency planning. Combined exercises and training, military exchange programs, and foreign military member attendance at Army schools are examples of home station shaping activities. At home stations, commanders employ force protection measures. These measures, along with the primary protection tasks and the synchronization and integration of the Army Protection Program, maintain safe and secure environments. They further enable commanders to generate and preserve combat power during training and deployment tasks that are associated with Army sustainable readiness requirements that support multidomain operations. 

#### **PROTECTION DURING CRISIS** 

6-4. Army operations during a crisis are those activities designed to deter an adversary’s undesirable actions. Commanders are generally focused on actions to protect friendly forces, assets, and partners and to indicate U.S. intent to execute subsequent phases of a planned operation. Army protection capabilities SPO to prevent armed conflict during mobilization, during the transit of Army Forces and cargo, along movement routes, at initial staging areas, and at subsequent assembly areas where uncertain threat conditions require a delicate balance between protection and building combat power. Protection cells coordinate closely with staff personnel to identify information and assets that need protection and to apply appropriate protection and security measures consistent with the collective threat analysis. 

6-5. Army operations to consolidate gains during a crisis correspond with, stabilize, and enable the civil authority phases of a joint operation. Commanders continuously consider the coordination, synchronization, and integration of protection capabilities necessary to consolidate gains and achieve the desired end state. Consolidate gains activities include populace and resources control, SC, law and order reestablishment, HA, and critical infrastructure protection and restoration. (See FM 3-0 for a discussion on consolidating gains.) 

#### **PROTECTION DURING ARMED CONFLICT** 

6-6. As the conflict emerges or intensifies, protection considerations take on increased importance to preserve advisor and FSF combat power. Those considerations include the following: 

- Reinforcing the relationship with the partner force following escalation. 

- Adapting the protection mindset to the inherently more dangerous and deadly nature of the conflict. 

- Identifying what protection capabilities and capacities were developed in crisis and competition that can be used in conflict (or what should be disregarded). 

- Identifying the changes in legal authorities that did not apply below armed conflict. 

- Expanding protection considerations to include awareness of nongovernmental organizations operating within the AOR/OE to preempt misallocating resources or targeting a neutral element. 

- Recategorizing threats based off the change of situation. 

6-7. Advisors maintain an amenable relationship when working with the partner force whenever possible, deescalating internal conflicts and proactively mitigating interpersonal friction without complicating the advisor-advised relationship. These relationship maintenance measures may incentivize partner forces to positively intervene when an SFAB protection gap exists. Commanders and advisors plan for and implement protection capabilities, to include de-escalation techniques. Advisor teams avoid physical and lethal engagements with their foreign counterparts while maintaining their right to self-defense. Maintaining honest, yet respectful, relationships with the foreign counterparts is one of the best ways for advisor teams to maintain their own physical security. (See ATP 3-37.15 for more information on foreign security force threats.) 

#### **PROTECTION IN LARGE-SCALE COMBAT OPERATIONS** 

6-8. During LSCO, Army leaders must anticipate that joint support will be limited and must use a combination of measures to protect the force. Commanders and staffs deliberately plan and integrate protection capabilities to preserve combat power, mitigate identified vulnerabilities, and exploit opportunity. 

6-9. Protection can be derived inherently from combat operations (such as security operations), or it can be deliberately applied as commanders direct, coordinate, and synchronize tasks and systems that comprise the protection warfighting function to apply maximum combat power. Leaders also implement protection measures to complicate the targeting of their units during armed conflict by moving irregularly, using camouflage, and maintaining dispersion. They coordinate, synchronize, and integrate enabling operations and enabling tasks with the primary protection tasks to prevent or mitigate detection, threat effects, and hazards. 

6-10. Information activities throughout the range of military operations may deter enemy actions, prevent escalation of tensions, and prevent the civilian populace from participating in a resistance when a military intervention occurs. Effective information activities are essential to protection efforts from competition below armed conflict to conflict to ensure the protection of the mission, of the force, and the populace. 

6-11. Commanders continuously direct, coordinate, and synchronize protection capabilities necessary to consolidate gains and achieve the desired end state. Protection capabilities are focused on security tasks to stabilize the area and protect the force, routes, sustainment assets, and critical infrastructure. Activities to consolidate gains also include minimum-essential stability operations tasks (populace and resources control, detainee operations, law and order reestablishment, HA, and critical infrastructure protection and restoration). 

##### **SECTION II – PROTECTION IN SUPPORT OF SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE** 

6-12. Protection focuses on preserving combat power and enabling freedom of action. Commanders and staffs manage protection efforts throughout the operations process to protect the force and enable freedom of action by identifying and counteracting potential threats and hazards. In general, protection planning consists of a continuous assessment of the following: what threats or hazards jeopardize assets, how critically assets are affected, and how vulnerable the assets are to the identified adverse effects. Protection safeguards the force, personnel (combatants and noncombatants), systems, information, and physical assets of the United States, allies, and partners. The SFAB must consider all aspects of protection to include internal, external, and foreign security force protection capabilities. Advisor teams will focus on the following categories of protection considerations in all stages of the conflict continuum: 

- Protecting the SFAB and friendly forces. 

- Advising FSF partners on how to think about risk and vulnerability as it pertains to their forces and critical support nodes (for example, terminals, and critical commodities like Class III, Class V, Class VII, for example). 

- Leveraging the advisor network for coordinating and augmenting FSF protection capabilities. 

6-13. SFAB leaders must consider protection throughout every Army operation. Military engagement and SC require commanders and their staffs to consider protection measures commensurate with potential threats and hazards, even in a permissive environment. The friendly force protects itself through its own actions. Friendly force protection includes measures or tasks executed by the SFAB to preserve combat power. These tasks include establishing internal security measures, implementing a field sanitation program, establishing internal SOPs for conducting operations with the foreign security force, executing preventative medical checks and inspections, conducting risk assessments prior to operations or training events, cyber awareness, and ensuring all personnel are aware of the identified risks.

#### **CRITICALITY ASSESSMENT** 

6-14. The criticality assessment provides the SFAB with a list of key assets and infrastructure based on the necessity for mission success. Criticality is continuously assessed based upon the impact of temporary or permanent asset loss on both the enduring and episodic mission. Criticality is assessed according to a four step range from most catastrophic to least, according to the degree of mission impact: Catastrophic, Critical, Marginal, and Negligible (see ADP 3-37 for more information on criticality). The advisor team conducting a criticality assessment also analyzes the replacement time for the assets. This factor is particularly applicable for SFABs within the competition continuum because teams often operate within nonpermissive environments that preclude speedy replacement or repair parts delivery. As transitions in mission or locale occur, protection cell or working group members recognize the ramifications of these changes and update the criticality assessment. 

#### **VULNERABILITY ASSESSMENT** 

6-15. A vulnerability assessment is an evaluation tool to determine the magnitude of a threat or hazard’s effect on installations, personnel, units, exercises, ports, ships, residences, facilities, information, or other assets. It identifies the areas of improvement required to withstand, mitigate, or deter acts of violence or terrorism or attacks against threats (see ADP 3-37). The SFAB coordinates with the FSF staff to address specific public and private sectors and how they are vulnerable against threats. Continuous assessments are used to identify the characteristics and procedures that make assets, areas, infrastructure, and events vulnerable. The general sequence of a vulnerability assessment is the following: 

- Step 1—list assets and capabilities and the threats against them. 

- Step 2—determine the common criteria for assessing vulnerabilities. 

- Step 3—evaluate the vulnerability of assets and capabilities. 

6-16. DOD has created several decision support tools, including criticality, accessibility, recuperability, vulnerability, effect, and recognizability (CARVER), to perform critical assessments in support of the vulnerability assessment process to include the following: 

- Assessing mission, symbolism, history, accessibility, recognizability, population, and proximity is a targeting analysis tool that is geared toward discovering personnel vulnerabilities. 

- Assessing CARVER is a targeting analysis tool that identifies a potential target from a terrorist perspective, highlighting what the enemy might perceive as a soft or valuable target. 

6-17. Evaluation criteria allow teams and commanders to develop lists and assessments that will enable robust evaluation in asset vulnerability. Vulnerability evaluation criteria may include the degree to which an asset may be disrupted, the quantity of the asset available (if replacement is required due to loss), dispersion (geographic proximity), and key physical characteristics (see ADP 3-37). 

#### **PROTECTION LISTS** 

6-18. The SFAB works with their FSF partner to develop protection prioritization lists. The protection prioritization lists help commanders identify or assess assets that require protection prioritization within their assigned areas (see ADP 3-37). These lists are a fighting product that commanders, teams, and staffs employ to wisely align critical assets and their defenses. The FSF commander prioritizes intent and impacts on mission planning to determine critical assets (see figure 6-1 on page 162). 

6-19. A _critical asset_ is a specific entity that is of such extraordinary importance that its incapacitation or destruction would have a very serious, debilitating effect on the ability of a nation to continue to function effectively (JP 3-26). Critical assets can be people, property, equipment, activities, operations, information, facilities, or materials. For example, important communications facilities and utilities, analyzed through criticality assessments, provide information to prioritize resources while reducing the potential application of resources on lower-priority assets. Stationary weapons systems might be identified as critical to the execution of military operations and receive additional protection. The lack of a replacement may cause a critical asset to become a top priority for protection. (See ADP 3-37 for more information on the protection prioritization list.)

6-20. Advisor teams have the responsibility to advise the FSF on the best practices for protecting the FSF's assets, the team, and the mission. The Army's approach to these practices is represented in the process of producing and maintaining the protected asset list (PAL) according to the assessed risks and mitigation measures. Critical assets are materiel, personnel, or infrastructure (both military and non-military) that are critical for mission success, prioritized in order of criticality. Another best practice is identifying critical assets by assessing the criticality of the asset, vulnerability to relevant threats, and probability a threat or hazard may occur. The protected asset list is a listing of critical assets and is prioritized according to the commander’s guidance; the PAL indicates protected assets and the specific mitigation or protection measure. 

6-21. The PAL is the primary tool used to assign the protection assets available for the protection tasks. The conflicts of the late 2010s and early 2020s show that adequate protection requires commanders to make decisions regarding prioritization and asset dedication; there will never be sufficient resources to defend everything. The process of developing the PAL provides a framework for advisors to coach their FSF partners in deliberate protection measures. For example, the commander may assess a high risk of tactical UAS threats and deliberately assign a C-UAS asset to protect the battalion tactical operations center. 

**Figure 6-1. Priority protection list, example** 

#### **COORDINATING AND ADVISING PROTECTION CAPABILITIES** 

6-22. The prioritization of protection assets is situationally dependent and resource informed. The integration of protection capabilities provides strength and structure to the overall protection effort. Integration occurs vertically and horizontally and includes UAPs throughout the operations process. Protection task integration throughout the operations process helps establish control measures against potential threats and hazards. SFAB teams use the advisor network at echelon to advise and assist FSF in the allocation, prioritization, and layering of protection capabilities to mitigate or assume risk for all identified and prioritized vulnerabilities. 

The collaboration, integration, and synchronization between the advisors at echelon assist in identifying threats and hazards. Advisors need to articulate and advocate for why not all assets listed on the protection prioritization list receive continuous protection. Some critical assets only receive protection assets based on available resources. Determining and directing protection priorities may involve the most important decisions commanders make and their staff support. 

6-23. The SFAB will have to coordinate with their supported FSF, multinational division or corps, or JFLCC for the augmentation of non-FSF organic protection units or capability. Army SC activities develop the ability of partner countries to operate with U.S. and allied military forces across the warfighting functions (see JP 3-0). 

##### **SECTION III – PROTECTION ADVISING** 

6-24. Protection advising is a facet of all SFAB operations and along the entire competition continuum. While protection provides an overall approach to preserving combat power, there are additional protection considerations that the SFAB must address to achieve comprehensive and layered protection. The integration and synchronization of security and security operations with the protection tasks enable commanders to protect forces, critical assets, and information. SFABs leverage the limited organic protection capabilities within an advising team to advise FSF partners in the application of protection principles and tactics, techniques, and procedures. Table 6-1 on pages 164 through 166 provides a list of Army publications that advisor teams may reference prior to training FSF partners on protection tasks.
