#### **RISK MANAGEMENT** 

6-25. Commanders and advisor team leaders must manage and mitigate risk in every operation. Due to the nature of SFAB operations, the advisor team may operate under an increased level of operational risk relative to other conventional units. Advisor teams may be employed in areas without established infrastructure, geographically dispersed, and alongside FSF. Team leaders and assistant team leaders must identify potential risks, prioritize those risks, and then implement mitigation measures to reduce the likelihood and danger of those risks. If properly managed, accepting a higher level of risk may lead to tactical opportunities. They can reduce the risk through careful planning and foresight. _Risk_ is the probability and severity driven chance of loss, caused by threat or other hazards (ATP 5-19). Commanders assess associated risks by the degree to which the incident affects combat power or mission capability. Risk can never be fully eliminated however, so commanders must be prepared to accept some level of risk in their operations. 

6-26. SFAB commanders and staff use the protection warfighting function to assist mitigating risk when employing advising teams. The SFAB will leverage internal capabilities to assist advising teams in respect to protection. These include but are not limited to; EOD, cyberspace operations, EW, information operations, military police, CBRN, and engineering. These listed organic forces are limited in capability and cannot perform all tasks traditionally associated with their MOS or branch. The advisor network will provide a mechanism for assessing operational risk during employment and provide a method for commanders to allocate resources during transitions.

6-27. Employment with FSF should inherently reduce potential incidents that adversely affect both forces’ readiness to execute missions. Since SFAB advisor elements are generally much smaller than the FSF they work with, the FSF can provide significant capability and capacity to the SFAB element to enable SFAB-internal protection and thereby mitigate inherent risks to the SFAB team (for example, using foreign heavy equipment operators and their equipment to construct protective earthworks for the SFAB team’s tactical or sustainment areas). Leveraging FSF assets, the SFAB preserves organic combat power and mitigates the residual risk. 

#### **SURVIVABILITY** 

6-28. SFAB leaders assess survivability as the ability of a friendly force to withstand enemy effects while remaining mission capable. It represents the degree to which a formation is hard to destroy. Survivability consists of two aspects—avoiding and withstanding. Avoiding seeks to prevent an enemy attack, surveillance, or accurate targeting. Withstanding is minimizing or preventing degradation after having been attacked. Survivability operations conducted by the SFAB seek to avoid and withstand threats and hazards from enemy forces and insider threats. Friendly survivability operations include constructing fighting positions, hardening facilities, and employing camouflage and concealment. In an advisor-specific context, avoiding includes de-escalation and conflict negotiation. 

6-29. FSF survivability operations allow them to avoid or withstand threats and hazards. Due to their close working relationship, advisor teams indirectly benefit from the increased FSF survivability measures. Advisor teams should discuss the importance of improving survivability with their foreign counterparts and assist them in conducting assessments, developing improvement plans, and prioritizing resources and projects. When appropriate, advisor teams can provide and coordinate additional U.S. and other agency resources in support of the FSF efforts. 

6-30. Without augmentation, advisor teams do not have the organic structure to create more than rudimentary protective works for themselves. Therefore, additional resources provided by the FSF are essential to the survivability of advisor teams. These operations are typically resource-intensive and require heavy equipment and significant labor. (See ATP 3-37.34 for more information of survivability.) 

#### **CHEMICAL, BIOLOGICAL, RADIOLOGICAL, OR NUCLEAR** 

6-31. CBRN protection measures mitigate CBRN threats and hazards from having an adverse effect on military operations and nonmilitary personnel, equipment, and facilities. CBRN weapons can cause mass casualties and large-scale destruction. Many nation-states and nonstate actors possess, seek to possess, develop, and proliferate such weapons. 

6-32. Development of the FSF's CBRN protection capability allows the FSF to preserve combat power. The SFAB EOD advisors assist FSF CBRN training. The CBRN training may include self/team decontamination, identify CBRN threats and ordnance, emergency decontamination, point detection and monitoring, and leak, seal, and package of CBRN explosive ordnance. EOD advisors are not trained or equipped for mass decontamination or area detection. The FSF also supports advisors by preventing enemy acquisition of these weapons as well as assessing, protecting, and mitigating CBRN threats and hazards. 

6-33. The SFAB EOD and CBRN advisors along with UAPs, leverage the advisor network to prepare for CBRN operations. Specifically, they assist the FSF in planning decontamination for both FSF and SFAB advisor teams who have been affected by CBRN hazards. They coordinate with FSF, U.S. Forces and UAPs to ensure decontamination and protection equipment are available. This includes level 2 mission-oriented protective posture gear, water and decontaminants are pre-positioned for forces that have the highest probability of encountering CBRN effects. (See FM 3-11 for more information on CBRN operations.) 

#### **FORCE HEALTH PROTECTION** 

6-34. While FHP is an aspect of the protection warfighting function, there is substantial overlap with the sustainment warfighting function. See chapter 7 for discussion of FHP. 

#### **ELECTROMAGNETIC SPECTRUM** 

6-35. The EMS is a physical medium in which all branches of the government and partner nations conduct activities. EMS signature is the sum of detectible emissions from equipment such as radios, information technology systems, GPS, and wireless electronic materiel and personal effects (for example, cell phones). EMS signature detection is a predominant means of location finding and targeting for military forces. Advisor teams evaluate the EMS signature that their equipment emits and adopt appropriate measures to minimize or eliminate unnecessary emissions. Advisor teams also use available resources to continually evaluate the cumulative EMS signature of the team, FSF, and other adjacent units. When the environment is nonpermissive, advisor teams are responsible to protect themselves and FSF by using best practice signature management tactics, techniques, and procedures. 

6-36. In addition to protection by means of signature management, advisors consider protecting the EMS itself from illicit or hazardous interference. Protecting frequencies used by friendly and partner forces is as important as protecting any other critical asset. Failure to protect friendly frequencies and deny the enemy EMS interference, can result in the exploitation, geolocation, and denial of numerous friendly systems. Advisor teams know what jammers/spoofers are prevalent in their AO and can identify interference indicators. National, criminal, or violent extremist entities use jammers and spoofers throughout the competition continuum. Many EMS interference systems are low cost, easy to obtain, and prolific. Advisor teams must make a robust communication contingency plan, understand the joint spectrum interference resolution process, and report interference to S-6 for deconfliction in the event of friendly or enemy jamming. Advisor teams should also coordinate with their cyberspace electromagnetic activities cell to locate any active threats and assist in the development of their electronic protection plans. 

6-37. Nation States and criminal organizations may possess sophisticated EMS equipment that represent a prominent security risk to the SFAB enterprise. Adversaries use data acquired by these systems to develop a pattern of life to exploit SFAB and FSF. An example of a sophisticated system is a rogue base station, also known as international mobile subscriber identity catchers. These exploit cell phones and may not only geolocate and target team and FSF members, but also provide a means to employ cyberweapons and deny the use of friendly capabilities. Hobbyists and criminal organizations may pose a similar risk through use of less sophisticated commercial tools. Advisor teams must be educated on the risks involved while using their government and personal devices. Teams who suspect EMS interference coordinate with their cyberspace electromagnetic activities cell or signals intelligence counterparts to acquire systems and training to identify threats and locate the enemy transmitters. 

#### **PHYSICAL SECURITY** 

6-38. _Physical security_ is that part of security concerned with physical measures designed to safeguard personnel; to prevent unauthorized access to equipment, installations, material, and documents; and to safeguard them against espionage, sabotage, damage, and theft (JP 3-0). The Army employs physical security measures in-depth to protect personnel, information, and critical resources in all locations and situations against various threats by developing and implementing effective security policies and procedures. This approach is based upon continuing evaluation and employment of protective measures including physical barriers, cleared zones, lighting, access and key control, intrusion detection devices, defensive positions, and nonlethal capabilities. (See ATP 3-39.32.) 

6-39. SFAB commanders, staffs, and team leaders consider physical security throughout the planning and operations cycle to ensure that critical areas are secured against likely threats. Within the SFAB, the provost marshal may provide critical expertise to develop physical security plans, direct physical security measure implementation, and coordinate for assets to facilitate physical security. The U.S. Army Military Police School's Physical Security course enhances the ability of military police to perform physical security inspections and assessments. Critical areas the SFAB may consider for extensive physical security measures may include— 

- Bases or base camps. 

- Access or entry control points. 

- Key command posts, communication nodes, or sustainment facilities. 

- Weapons, arms, ammunition, and explosive storage areas. 

- Troop housing areas or other high concentration areas, such as dining facilities. 

6-40. The SFAB can influence the FSF's ability to secure its critical assets against unauthorized access, damage, or criminal activity by supporting the foreign partner with physical security inspections and assessments and providing support to implement physical security measures. FSF that can protect their critical assets can apply greater combat power to the mission. For instance, poor physical security of the foreign bases, weapons, command nodes, and housing areas can severely compromise the SFAB's mission success by threatening the FSF' readiness through enemy attrition of personnel, morale, and equipment. 

6-41. Physical security measures implemented by both the SFAB and the FSF complement each other and produce an enhanced security posture. Due to the interactive basis of the relationship, the security of one force is directly related to the security of the other. Advisors rely on their foreign partners when they collocate within the foreign bases, operate from their partner's facilities, or depend on foreign infrastructure to enhance their own protection. 

#### **PERSONNEL RECOVERY** 

6-42. _Army personnel recovery_ is the military efforts taken to prepare for and execute the recovery and reintegration of isolated personnel (FM 3-50). U.S. Army forces work together with DOD and other UAPs to recover individuals and groups who become isolated. Isolation refers to persons separated from their unit or in a situation where they must survive, evade, resist, or escape. U.S. Army forces also support the recovery of other persons designated by the President or SecDef. The personnel recovery mission includes preparing Soldiers, other Service members, DA Civilians, and contractors in danger of isolation while participating in any activity or mission sponsored by the United States. The personnel recovery task is particularly important to the SFAB as additional maneuver forces may or may not be present in the operational area where the SFAB is located (within or outside of a designated JOA). 

6-43. The SFAB commander assesses the operational variables and typically considers personnel recovery from three perspectives: United States and foreign partner together in tactical isolation, foreign or host nationled recovery of U.S. and FSF combined element, or U.S.-led recovery of U.S. Forces only, because foreign forces are incapable or untrustworthy. The SFAB commander provides personnel recovery planning guidance and isolated Soldier guidance prior to executing all missions. The commander considers the following four general approaches (sometimes referred to as methods) to conduct personnel recovery: 

- Unassisted. 

- Immediate. 

- Deliberate. 

- External support. 

6-44. Effective guidance addresses the challenges of isolation within a unit's AO. Effective guidance is concrete enough for Soldiers to implement. Isolated Soldier guidance applies to the entire command since the uncertainty and complexity of military operations expose everyone to risk of isolation, regardless of rank. Isolated Soldier guidance must include the set of circumstances (isolation criteria) under which Soldiers or small elements execute their isolated Soldier guidance and their mission changes from their primary mission to one of evasion (see FM 3-50 for more information). 

6-45. The FSF or host nation may also employ national police in response to personnel recovery requirements. When foreign or host-nation forces are in the lead, personnel recovery assistance provided by the foreign force to the SFAB facilitates rapid initiation of personnel recovery. The mission is incredibly time-sensitive and high priority. As part of their training and rehearsals with the FSF, SFAB commanders, staffs, and advisor team leaders should incorporate these types of operations into the training plan. The FSF are generally more familiar with the OE and population, therefore, a well-trained foreign partner has a higher chance to recover the missing personnel. 

#### **AREA SECURITY** 

6-46. _Area security_ is a type of security operation conducted to protect friendly forces, lines of communications, and activities within a specific area (ADP 3-90). The SFAB has no organic capabilities to perform area security. SFAB advisors enhance the area security operations of the partner FSF. Inside a JOA, area security is usually the responsibility of the commander assigned to an AO. The SFAB relies on mutual support from U.S. and foreign maneuver forces for its protection. Outside a JOA, the SFAB’s protection beyond its own inherent local security capabilities relies heavily on the success of partnered area security. Area security often focuses on the following activities:

- Base or base camp defense. 

- Critical asset security. 

- Node protection. 

- High-risk personnel security. 

- Response force operations. 

- Lines of COMSEC. 

- Checkpoints and combat outposts. 

- Convoy security. 

- Port area and pier security. 

- Area damage control. 

6-47. Partnered area security is complementary to local security and extends the reach of the SFAB to protect itself beyond the extent of its local security. Advisor teams often operate in austere environments; therefore, their protection often relies on their ability to defeat local threats. In conjunction with their foreign partners, advisor teams defeat threats in the surrounding areas before potential threats can generate combat power and present an overwhelming threat. Advisors are significantly limited (as well as constrained in non-JOA environments) in their ability to perform area security beyond the extent of their local security. Advisor teams achieve protection by collaborating with the FSF to perform partnered area security operations. Partnered area security leverages the strengths of each and thereby achieves synergy to defeat threats in the area beyond the reach of the advisor team capabilities or authorities. 

6-48. As part of partnered area security, advisors provide the FSF with capabilities that facilitate their area security operations. For instance, while the FSF may provide the bulk of the combat power through ground maneuver forces, the advisors provide linkage to U.S. joint or multinational enterprises (such as joint fires, intelligence, or logistics). The ability of the advisors to coordinate support (for example, aviation, EOD, special operations forces) to the FSF enhances its ability to accomplish security in areas and populations. The objective of U.S. military operations is to protect partner nation political, economic, and social institutions against immediate threats, and to stabilize the OE so the FSF can gain and maintain area security. Preserving the legitimacy of the partner nation by developing the FSF to solve their own internal security problems is of strategic importance and essential to attaining long-term security outcomes. 

#### **LOCAL SECURITY** 

6-49. _Local security_ is the low-level security activities conducted near a unit to prevent surprise by the enemy (ADP 3-90). Local security is an inherent responsibility of every individual, organization, and commander. Local security involves efforts taken to defeat local threats to provide immediate protection to the friendly force. The SFAB contains local security capabilities to retain the ability to defend itself no matter the OE. Security tasks—outside of local team security—are beyond the capability of the advisor team and must be performed by another security force, which may be the FSF. The FSF contribute to the SFAB local security by providing personnel, equipment, and facilities to the SFAB. 

6-50. Local security includes observation posts, local security patrols, perimeter security, overwatch positions, and other measures to provide close-in security for a force. The SFAB must always place local security in the forefront of its considerations, as the ability to continuously secure itself is critical to its ability to perform its assigned tasks and fulfill its mission. The SFAB routinely works with the FSF in small teams and minimal additional security personnel. When necessary, the advisor team employs its own members to provide overwatch during foreign engagements. The designated member(s) are disengaged from the meeting or training, focusing on the environment and participants. The overwatch personnel must remain vigilant to the behavior and actions of everyone present. The designated member(s) must also be cognizant of customs and courtesies of the foreign counterparts as not to mistake normal behavior and action with aggressive action or conversation tone. 

6-51. On those occasions when SFA operations are politically sensitive, successful insider attacks may have a greater strategic effect than a conventional force-on-force attack. Therefore, these attacks are a goal of many enemy organizations and must be considered while planning and conducting SFAB operations. In such cases of increased risk of attack, security measures must be modified accordingly. All SFAB members have a responsibility to contribute to local security. Unless augmented with a dedicated security force, SFAB team members collectively share the responsibility to contribute to continuous local security through constant observation, awareness, and rotational security duties, as necessary.

6-52. The FSF should have the ability to secure themselves and provide for their own local security. Advisors teach their foreign partners the five fundamentals of security: 

- Provide early and accurate warning. 

- Provide reaction time and maneuver space. 

- Orient on the force or facility to be secured. 

- Perform continuous reconnaissance. 

- Maintain enemy contact. 

6-53. FSF operating consistently with the fundamentals of security detailed in FM 3-90 develop basic local security competencies. Once proficient at these fundamentals, they begin to perform additional forms of security operations, which allow them to prevent surprise and preserve reaction time for commanders and advisors. 

#### **ANTITERRORISM** 

6-54. Antiterrorism is related to physical security and consists of proactive defensive measures used to deter, detect, delay, deny, and defend individuals and property against terrorist acts. These measures include limited response and containment by security forces. The SFAB antiterrorism resources include risk management, antiterrorism planning, awareness training, command information programs, validation of defensive plans, CI, security considerations, and other resources. 

6-55. Insider attacks can jeopardize mission success and damage the relationships between the advisor and FSF. As such, advisor teams should be cognizant of the FSF’s ability to train, monitor, and assess their antiterrorism measures and update the dynamic risk assessment, as appropriate. 

#### **EXPLOSIVE ORDNANCE DISPOSAL** 

6-56. The officer and NCO in the SFAB EOD cell serve as the explosive hazard advisors. SFAB EOD provides explosive ordnance technical, tactical, and operational recommendations to the advisor team leader. Using the advisor network and UAPs, SFAB EOD can assist in improving the EOD readiness of FSF partners. 

6-57. SFAB EOD provides explosive ordnance technical and tactical advice to foreign EOD counterparts which increases FSF ability to detect, identify, render safe, and dispose of, explosive ordnance. SFAB EOD provide limited EOD operational support to FSF. 

6-58. The SFAB EOD officer will procure EOD equipment, including training aids, and will advise FSF on developing an EOD training program. This program often includes an EOD team leader validation program and instruction on accountability and maintenance of EOD equipment. 

6-59. EOD personnel have access to law enforcement sensitive data which is beneficial to intelligence personnel. EOD personnel can provide analyses which should be disseminated through intelligence channels on common explosive tactics, techniques, and procedures, ways to detect explosive threats, and methods to reduce risk to mission and force (see ATP 4-32). 

6-60. The increased global production of insensitive munitions and next generation antiaccess area denial systems will impede FSF and U.S. operations. SFAB EOD will be a key enabler to help protect the force and ensure critical infrastructure, lines of communication, and C2 nodes remain operational. Partner EOD capacity will be critical to offset the density of next generation dispensable insensitive munitions which can be expected at key nodes such as APODs and SPODs. 

##### **JOINT EXPLOSIVE ORDNANCE DISPOSAL TECHNICAL SUPPORT CENTER** 

6-61. As the executive agent for EOD, the U.S. Navy’s Joint EOD Technical Support Center is the approval authority for what EOD information can be shared with and what material can be sold to FSF. SFAB EOD collaborates with the Joint EOD Technical Support Center to identify information that is releasable to FSF. The Joint EOD Technical Support Center is the approval authority for foreign military sales of common EOD render-safe tools, sets, kits and outfits. Working with this office, SFAB EOD can nominate foreign military sales of EOD material through the ASCCs to FSF partners to increase FSF survivability thus reducing allies and partners support requirements during LSCO.

##### **EXPLOSIVE ORDNANCE CLEARANCE AGENTS** 

##### **WARNINGEOD forces are specifically trained in render-safe procedures and the disposal of explosive ordnance. While other forces can destroy limited explosive ordnance by detonation, they are not properly equipped, trained, or authorized to perform render-safe procedures or other disposal procedures.** 

6-62. EOCAs are combat engineers trained to perform limited disposal operations on limited explosive ordnance, for which they are trained and authorized, on the battlefield. The United States Army Training and Doctrine Command Proponent Office—EOD publishes the EOCA ordnance list, and the joint EOD theater commander publishes the EOCA ordnance supplement list as part of their ordnance order or battle. EOCAs may assist EOD in the disposal of explosive ordnance per the EOCA identification guide. 

###### **Explosive Ordnance Clearance Agents Capabilities** 

6-63. EOCA personnel may perform a reconnaissance of suspected explosive ordnance to be able to positively identify the ordnance using the EOCA identification guide and supplemental EOCA ordnance list. Leaders should use the EOD nine-line report to report the items. EOCA personnel can only teach FSF ordnance identification of non-U.S. ordnance in accordance with Naval School Explosive Ordnance Disposal (NAVSCOLEOD) foreign disclosure procedures. 

6-64. EOCA personnel can support survivability efforts to mitigate the blast and fragmentation danger area of an identified explosive ordnance using protective works. Commanders should increase survivability efforts to protect personnel and equipment from the blast effects and fragmentation when personnel must remain in the hazard area. 

###### **Explosive Ordnance Clearance Agents Limitations** 

6-65. EOCA personnel cannot move or destroy multiple explosive ordnance (such as a cache). EOCA personnel cannot conduct a reconnaissance of an improvised explosive device (IED) or handle IEDs. EOCA personnel can only work with captured enemy ammunition under the direct supervision of EOD personnel. EOCA personnel cannot be used for explosive ordnance response calls. EOCA personnel cannot teach ordnance demolition procedures without an EOD team leader certified by a NAVSCOLEOD foreign disclosure officer. To provide C-IED identification awareness training to non-EOD FSF, the advisor must complete a course like the Army’s Dismounted C-IED Tactics Master Trainer course or the C-IED Training Integrator course. 

#### **POLICE OPERATIONS** 

6-66. Police operations advising focuses on peacetime policing actions and institutional development. Military police operations involve the application of control measures and enforcement mechanisms within an AO to maintain law and order, safety, and other matters affecting the general welfare of the population. The Army conducts military police operations to maintain order within its communities and formations. When directed, the Army also applies military policing activities to civilian populations to restore order when the rule of law has broken down or is nonexistent. Police operations include law enforcement, forensic analysis and biometric support, criminal investigations, customs support, support to civil security and civil control (including host-nation police training and support), police engagement, crowd control, traffic management and enforcement, and evidence response team support (see ATP 3-39.10). Police operations advising may be necessary when the SFAB's mission includes increasing the capability and capacity of FSF law enforcement organizations. While other UAPs (such as the U.S. Department of State's Bureau of International Narcotics and Law Enforcement Affairs) are often responsible for assisting FSF law enforcement organizations, through interagency support, DOD may be required to lead the effort when civilian agencies are unable to perform these missions based on the security environment.

6-67. Given the technical skills required in training police organizations (escalation of force, evidence collection, preservation and storage, forensic analysis, and criminal investigations), the SFAB commanders and staffs should request additional military police support when working with FSF law enforcement agencies. Assessments may lead to training the FSF law enforcement organizations to prevent, investigate, and disrupt transnational organized criminal activities that threaten order and stability in the host nation or geographic region A host nation's ability to maintain civil order and police its population according to the rule of law is critical to SFAB protection. Nations undermine their stability and legitimacy when their police organizations fail to achieve order, enforce laws, and minimize corruption. Failure to achieve public security can significantly degrade the SFAB's protection and threaten its ability to focus on its primary mission. (See ATP 3-39.10 for more information on police operations.) 

#### **UTILIZING NONLETHAL OPTIONS** 

6-68. There is a wide range of scenarios in which nonlethal capabilities may be employed. They may be used to disperse dangerous mobs or deny access to critical facilities such as weapon storage sites, embassies, enemy prisoner of war facilities, nuclear power plants, troop lodgments, and so forth. 

6-69. Nonlethal capabilities are employed with the intent to compel or deter adversaries by acting on human capabilities or materiel while minimizing fatalities and damage to equipment or facilities. This concept describes required capabilities that enable the FSF to employ nonlethal capabilities which provide alternatives to, or raise the threshold for, application of lethal force; augmenting lethal capabilities; and protecting friendly forces across the combined operation. Nonlethal capabilities will provide FSF with the necessary means to control populations, enforce mandates, and protect themselves in consonance with the specified rules of engagement for the operation. The successful accomplishment of any operation in which nonlethal weapons are employed requires extensive preparation, of which individual and unit training are vital. Training should be designed to give individuals an understanding of the entire subject area and enable them to function efficiently as members of a unit or independently. This training must be intensive and realistic. Training with nonlethal weapons capabilities is critical to ensure employment of these capabilities with confidence and proficiency. 

#### **DETENTION OPERATIONS** 

6-70. The primary advisor to oversee detention operations is the provost marshal, however, the technical details and procedures required to perform detention operations to standard may require augmentation by military police correction and detention specialists or designated law enforcement professionals. The SFAB should maintain oversight and awareness of its counterpart's detention operations to include the following: 

- Ensuring humane treatment of detainees. 

- Monitoring and enforcing pretrial correction standards consistent with the host nation rule-of-law. 

- Improving and maintaining gender appropriate health, hygiene, and social services. 

- Establishing and maintaining the appropriate oversight of the corrections system. 

- Promoting the rehabilitation and reintegration of detainees back into the population. 

6-71. While the SFAB has limited or no requirements to perform detention operations itself, it is imperative that its counterpart FSF perform their detention operations according to their nation's rule-of-law, while abiding by international laws, norms, and standards. When the SFAB's counterparts conduct detention operations, the SFAB commanders and staffs coordinate through the provost marshal to provide technical oversight to prevent violations of human rights and the laws of armed conflict. The provost marshal should coordinate with the staff judge advocate advisor and host nation law enforcement professionals to provide accurate oversight and have a shared understanding of the applicable laws and regulations. Failure to treat detainees humanely may lead to negative operational and strategic outcomes. 

6-72. FSF detention operations contribute to the SFAB's protection by removing potential threat populations from the operational area. The effectiveness of the host nation's criminal justice systems (police, corrections, and judicial) to adjudicate civil disputes, prosecute violations of criminal laws, and incarcerate convicted criminal offenders is critical to the overall stability and order of the nation and region. In places where legitimate criminal justice systems exist, the SFAB benefits from the protection provided by maintaining stable social and political conditions. When the criminal justice system is undermined by insurgent or criminal activity, corruption of government personnel and institutions, or the presence of competing governance structure (criminal, terrorist, or insurgent), the SFAB's protection is significantly reduced by the surrounding instability, disorder, or criminalized power structures. The ability of the host-nation FSF to achieve legitimate civil order is essential to preventing conflict and consolidating gains to achieve long-term U.S. strategic objectives.

#### **POPULACE AND RESOURCES CONTROL** 

6-73. Populace control provides security for the indigenous populace, mobilizes human resources, denies enemy access to the population, and detects and reduces the effectiveness of enemy agents. It involves establishing public order and safety, securing borders, protecting population centers and people, holding individuals accountable for criminal activities, controlling the activities of individuals or groups that pose a security risk, reestablishing essential civil services, and setting conditions in the operational area that support stability through unity of effort. Populace control may become necessary because of military operations or man-made or natural disasters. International law requires the military force to focus on essential tasks that establish a safe, secure environment and address the immediate humanitarian needs of the local population. 

6-74. During times of conflict, traffic on main supply routes may encounter dislocated civilians that could hinder military traffic. The FSF assist and divert dislocated civilians from main supply routes and other areas to dislocated civilian camps. They may also deny the movement of civilians whose location, direction of movement, or actions may be a threat to themselves or to the evolving combat operation. The host-nation government is responsible for identifying routes for the safe movement of dislocated civilians out of an AO. The military police assist CA and the host nation in redirecting dislocated civilians to alternate routes. Military police coordination to FSF enhance their ability to reduce confusion and congestion along key ground lines of communication during a conflict. The FSF’s ability to perform straggler and dislocated-civilian-control operations, prevent possible infiltration of the enemy, return stragglers to military control as part of the combined operation, and provides security from the rear area to the forward line of own troops. (See ATP 3-39.30 for more information on dislocated civilian operations.) 

6-75. The Defense Security Cooperation Agency's HA, Disaster Relief and Emergency Management Advisor team provides advising for coordination of program management over DOD HA activities. DOD conducts HA to provide relief and aid for conditions such as human suffering, disease, and hunger. Emergency management activities aim to save lives, avert crises, and strengthen SC relationships between partner nations and the United States. Emergency management advising enables the five HA activities that equip, train, and build the capacity of a FSF government to provide essential humanitarian services. 

#### **OPERATIONS SECURITY** 

6-76. OPSEC is a form of security, as defined by JP 3-10. OPSEC is a significant challenge during SFA activities because of the diverse nature of parties interacting with the advisor team and advised element. The need to protect critical information about friendly intentions, capabilities, and activities from hostile elements facilitates the SFAB's overall security. The nature of SFA implies that many host-nation officials and the population have information regarding some U.S. activities before they occur, which increases operational risk. However, critical information and key activities can be protected by identifying the operational risk associated with certain types of information, designating the appropriate people with which information can be shared, monitoring the success of the OPSEC program, and implementing changes as necessary. 

6-77. Advisors must consider the possibility of informants within the FSF when designing the OPSEC program. Adversaries may have members or sympathizers within the FSF acting as informants. 

6-78. Higher headquarters should provide extensive OPSEC in support of their advisor teams and their foreign partners. COMSEC is an implied subcomponent of OPSEC. COMSEC is essential throughout the planning and execution of SFA activities. Advisors must be trained to protect sensitive communications equipment and cryptographic materials. COMSEC between the SFAB and the foreign partner can be challenging, as they may not have compatible systems. In cases where the FSF lack capability, advisors establish alternative communications methods in conjunction with the FSF. Regardless of how advisors communicate with their foreign partners, they must protect sensitive information. 

##### **SECTION IV – ADDITIONAL THREATS AND HAZARDS** 

6-79. While protection provides an overall approach to preserving combat power, there are additional protection considerations that the SFAB must address to achieve comprehensive and layered protection. The integration and synchronization of countering explosive hazards, fratricide avoidance, and local security with the protection tasks enable commanders to protect forces, critical assets, and information. Developing the FSF allows the advisor teams to leverage the FSF capabilities to provide additional force protection. The advisor teams are particularly concerned with SFAB’s primary protection tasks follows— 

- Survivability. 

- CBRN. 

- EOD. 

- C-UAS. 

- Emergency medical service. 

#### **COUNTERING EXPLOSIVE HAZARDS** 

6-80. Countering explosive hazards reduces casualties and damage and enables the commander’s freedom of action. IEDs can provide the enemy with a tactical advantage in ways that other weapons cannot. Engineer and EOD units enhance protection through the execution of countering explosive hazards. Engineers enhance protection through route clearance patrols, and breaching and clearing obstacles enables freedom of maneuver. EOD enhances protection through the identification, render safe, exploitation, and disposal of explosive hazards, weapons systems, and related materiel. Countering explosive hazards by render-safe procedures allows the development of tactics, techniques, and procedures to counter the threat. This includes technical and forensic exploitation to improve functions that include intelligence, targeting, force protection, materiel and non-materiel solutions. 

#### **COUNTER-IMPROVISED EXPLOSIVE DEVICES** 

6-81. IEDs can compensate if an adversary lacks heavy weapons by providing greater target lethality and survivability to the adversary than small arms. IEDs provide a countermobility capability against mounted and dismounted units and a means to attack hardened targets, such as armored vehicles and fortifications. Like landmines, IEDs often canalize friendly movement into ambushes. IEDs provide standoff that reduces bombers' vulnerabilities by keeping them out of range of U.S. weapons and sensors. The IED's indiscriminate nature and anonymity make it a psychological weapon, heightening the combat stress of friendly forces. 

6-82. SFAB commanders and staffs include C-IED in their planning and training at home station to reduce unit vulnerabilities and take proactive measures to identify and target the IED networks when deployed to reduce the IED threat. The SFAB assists FSF to integrate their activities with UAP’s C-IED efforts. 

6-83. At a minimum, C-IED activities should focus on the three lines of efforts of the C-IED framework: network engagement, train the force, and defeat the device. The level of effort may vary by phase throughout the course of the operation. The SFAB should develop and execute appropriate C-IED plans to ensure comprehensive support to an operation (see ATP 4-32.2). 

#### **FRATRICIDE AVOIDANCE** 

6-84. SFABs conduct combat identification before and during target engagement. The destructive power and range of modern weapons, coupled with the high intensity and rapid tempo of LSCO, increase the potential for fratricide. The primary preventive measures to limit friendly fire incidents are combat identification training, command emphasis, disciplined operations, coordination measures, close coordination among component commands, rehearsals, reliable and interoperable coordination systems, battle tracking, and enhanced situational awareness. Risk management is also fully integrated with planning and executing operations. Commanders identify and assess situations that increase the risk of friendly fire incidents. 

#### **FOREIGN SECURITY FORCES THREAT** 

6-85. An FSF threat is the potential for violence posed by FSF working with, or granted access to, U.S. service members, civilians, or contractors. An FSF threat attack is a violent act perpetrated against a U.S. 

service member, civilian, or contractor by an FSF member or members who have access to U.S. service members, civilians, or contractors. When operating in an expeditionary, multi-partner environment, the potential for a violent attack by FSF increases significantly since they operate alongside U.S. service members and civilians. Advisors must be able to de-escalate tensions when working with FSF. 

6-86. Commanders and advisors plan for and implement protection capabilities, to include de-escalation techniques. Advisor teams avoid physical and lethal engagements with their foreign counterparts while maintaining their right to self-defense. Maintaining honest, yet respectful, relationships with the foreign counterparts is one of the best ways for advisor teams to maintain their own physical security. (See ATP 3-37.15 for more information on foreign security force threats.) 

#### **COUNTER-UNMANNED AIRCRAFT SYSTEMS** 

6-87. UASs proliferate modern society and range from large government-owned devices to simple and easily obtainable commercial models. These systems provide an adversary the means to detect, observe, and target friendly forces. The enemy will attempt to identify friendly strengths, weaknesses, and key C2 elements. The enemy attempts to identify and target key communication nodes or critical capabilities that will disrupt the U.S. Forces’ ability to maintain security and limit the U.S. Forces’ freedom of maneuver. Therefore, advisor teams must consider the impact these devices may have on their operations. Mitigation measures may include using camouflage and concealment, requesting specialized equipment and training to C-UAS, increase dispersion, interdict threat launch points with aggressive patrolling, or simply maintaining awareness of the potential UAS threat while conducting operations. 

6-88. There is no single solution to countering the low-slow-small, UAS threat. Training the force to recognize and neutralize low-slow-small UAS threats prior to an attack is the preferred method but is not always feasible. If an air attack is imminent, the commander should use the most efficient method available to protect the unit and minimize effects. Combined arms forces must maintain flexibility in their options to quickly recognize and counter the effects from an enemy low-slow-small UAS. Ultimately, achieving the best C-UAS defense is through proper planning, training, coordination, and execution of unit training strategies. The maneuver force must plan, train, and execute operations as an integrated combined arms team employing all forms of passive air defense techniques when active air defense is limited or not available. Friendly forces should treat a downed UAS as a potential IED, subject to the same protection and exploitation concerns. 

##### **WARNINGWhen approaching a downed UAS, take necessary precautions for unknown possible threats to include explosive, absorption, thermal, heavy metal, and inhalation hazards.** 

##### **Chapter 7** 

### **Sustainment** 

_There is nothing more common than to find considerations of supply affecting the strategic lines of a campaign and a war._ 

###### Carl von Clausewitz 

Chapter 7 describes the SFAB’s sustainment advising battalion, the levels of LATs, sustainment SFA activities and the collective responsibility to coordinate SFAB sustainment requirements to facilitate shared sustainment understanding across the competition continuum. It also details sustainment operations within the advisor teams. 

##### **<mark>SECTION I – SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE BRIGADE SUSTAINMENT ADVISING BATTALION MISSION AND SCOPE</mark>** 

7-1. The primary function of the sustainment advising battalion’s subordinate elements (the LBAT, LCAT, and LAT) is to develop the sustainment capability and capacity of FSF. The secondary function is coordinating sustainment support between the SFAB advisor teams and the theater support structure. The LBAT, LCAT, and LAT utilize the advisor network to ensure the accuracy of logistics status reporting and logistics package requirements. 

7-2. When an SFAB operates inside a CCDR’s designated JOA, the brigade nests with established sustainment structures. Inside a JOA, the expeditionary sustainment command oversees the distribution management, transportation operations, materiel management, personnel services, and OCS. The SFAB S4, along with the SPO section, will initially coordinate sustainment support through their higher headquarters or from a maneuver brigade support battalion or division sustainment support battalion (DSSB) through its area support mission and responsibilities. Operating inside a JOA allows the LBAT, LCAT, and LAT to utilize established sustainment systems and distribution networks. The SFAB receives its sustainment support from one or more of the following entities: the host nation, OCS, the ASCC, an embassy country team, area support from a contracting support brigade, a brigade support battalion, and DSSB. 

7-3. When an SFAB operates outside a CCDR’s designated JOA or when there is no JOA established, the brigade may not have traditional Army sustainment systems and distribution networks to rely on for resupply, distribution, maintenance, transportation, and medical SPO. With no other conventional forces in the JOA, the SFAB S4 and the LBAT SPO section must coordinate sustainment support through their higher headquarters to access contracting support, host-nation support, or support from the Defense Logistics Agency, American embassy, or the ASCC for their location. These entities support administrative, sustainment, and life support functions. 

##### **<mark>SECTION II – SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE BRIGADE SUSTAINMENT ADVISING BATTALION ORGANIZATION</mark>** 

7-4. The sustainment advising battalion can force generate an LBAT and two LCATs each with two LATs (see figure 7-1 on page 178). Within the sustainment advising battalion, the headquarters support company contains a SPO section, MSS, maintenance section, and distribution section. The SPO may advise as augmentation to an advising team when additional sustainment advising capabilities are required. The MSS can provide a medical advising capability to the LAT for the FSF from Role 1 through Role 3. LATs can advise FSF on sustainment operations from brigade through theater level. The LCAT and LATs may also provide internal sustainment support to the SFAB when aligned with a force package. 

**Figure 7-1. Sustainment advising battalion task organization** 

#### **BRIGADE SUSTAINMENT STAFF** 

7-5. The SFAB sustainment staff plans, directs, controls, and coordinates sustainment in support of operations. Key members of the SFAB’s sustainment staff include the S-1, S-4, property book officer, brigade surgeon, and the S-8. These staff sections perform common staff responsibilities described in chapter 1. The staff develops internal brigade policies and plans in its respective technical areas and provides guidance, priorities, and allocations to subordinate commands. 

7-6. Due to the limited organic sustainment capability within the SFAB, the brigade S-4 and SPO section have unique roles relative to other conventional BCTs. The brigade S-4 conducts mission analysis, consolidates sustainment requirements, maintains and monitors the green LOGCOP, and communicates those requirements through their higher headquarters or other sustainment entities. Once the required support relationships are established, the SPO section or force package S-4 assists in synchronizing the support provided by these external sustainment organizations. When employed as part of a force package, the S-4 must be prepared to synchronize support requirements in-lieu of the SPO. Force package commanders and staffs acknowledge the advisor dichotomy during mission analysis realizing that if the support and coordination requirements place a high level of demand on these staffs, their advising capacity decreases. 

#### **SUPPORT OPERATIONS** 

7-7. _Support operations_ is the staff function of planning, coordinating, and synchronizing sustainment in support of units conducting decisive action in an area of operations (ATP 4-92). The SPO section provides centralized and integrated planning for all SPO within the brigade. SPO functions generally include transportation, maintenance, ammunition (see ATP 4-35.1), supply and field services, and distribution operations. the SPO section may develop FSF in sustainment operations. 

7-8. The SPO is the principal staff section responsible for coordinating and synchronizing distribution, transportation, replenishment, and maintenance support from across the sustainment enterprise for all units assigned or attached to the SFAB. The SPO collects green LOGCOP information from the advisor teams and collates them into a cohesive AOR logistics picture. The brigade S-4 identifies requirements through daily logistic status reports, running estimates, and mission analysis. 

7-9. SPO is responsible for the short-range to mid-range (future operations) planning and focus on coordinating the execution of the sustainment plan. The SPO section also develops the concept of support in coordination with the brigade S-4. 

#### **HEADQUARTERS SUPPORT COMPANY** 

7-10. The headquarters support company’s primary mission to develop FSF in sustainment operations. Their secondary mission is to plan, coordinate, and synchronize logistics and Army Health System support for the SFAB. The sustainment advising battalion’s headquarters support company provides SFA-related C2, administrative, and logistics support for all organic units to the SFAB. 

#### **MEDICAL SUPPORT SECTION** 

7-11. The primary mission of the MSS is internal medical support. Second, is advising, supporting, liaising, and assessing FSF medical elements. The MSS is required to have a thorough knowledge of other services and partner forces’ medical capabilities, limitations, organizations, and procedures. The MSS serves as the medical subject matter experts for the SFAB advisor teams. 

##### **<mark>SECTION III – EXTERNAL SUPPORT COORDINATION AND CONSIDERATIONS FOR ADVISOR TEAMS</mark>** 

7-12. The LBAT’s task is not to provide sustainment support to the SFAB, although they are a part of the advisor network and can counsel teams on sustainment operations. Rather, the SFAB is dependent upon theater sustainment forces, host nation, or OCS to meet team support requirements. Logistics support relationships are critical to the SFAB’s success and must be defined, managed, and updated as necessary. SFAB units often receive support from other theater logistics units tasked with area support responsibility. 

#### **SUPPORT RELATIONSHIPS** 

7-13. As described in chapter 1, command and support relationships establish clear responsibilities and authorities between higher headquarters, subordinate, supported, and supporting units. Clearly defined support relationships are critical for advisor team success. Recognizing that the conventional logistics framework typically applies to that organization’s own units, when external support is needed, the advisor team should work with the BAT-level S-4 to include support in the supporting unit’s governing operation plan, operation order, or JOA. When it is not in a designated JOA, the SFAB must rely on support relationships with adjacent conventional units or other entities, such as host-nation support, the Defense Logistics Agency, a contracting support brigade, or an embassy’s COM. 

7-14. Support relationships determine the flow of logistics reports and requests. The senior logistician and SPO section must clearly define these processes throughout all echelons of the SFAB in conjunction with the supporting sustainment organizations. Teams may coordinate external requests: team-to-supportorganization; team-to-S-4; or team-to-SPO; other communication chains are less common but allowed. 

#### **AREA SUPPORT** 

7-15. Area support is a task assigned to a sustainment unit directing it to support units within or passing through a specified location. SFAB elements request access to the LOGCOP or participate in AOR stakeholders’ logistics synchronization event to understand and coordinate with area supporting units. As a caveat, area support applies to a set geographic boundary which may cross multiple unit boundaries or stop short of these boundaries. These boundaries should be considered in the sustainment rehearsals both in predeployment planning and in-theater operations planning. 

7-16. Area support may be habitual or conducted on a limited basis depending on mission variables and orders. The senior SFAB logistics section primary, generally the S-4, projects and communicates support requirements to the supporting or DSSB so they can provide the required support. When an SFAB requires resupply through area support from a BCT or a DSSB, the SPO section coordinates with the supporting unit’s SPO section, S-4, or other sustainment planner to integrate their requirements into the concept of support and to synchronize SPO (see ATP 4-90). 

#### **DISTRIBUTION, REPLENISHMENT, AND RESUPPLY OPERATIONS** 

7-17. Advisor team leaders gather the external logistics requirements that their advisor teams generate and relay the requests to the SFAB S-4 and SPO. The SPO officer coordinates and synchronizes distribution operations with external assets as dictated by the theater sustainment structure. These assets are provided by a DSSB, sustainment brigade, expeditionary sustainment command, contract logistics support, or other means. 

7-18. Resupply operations require continuous and close coordination between the advisor team and the supporting units when operating in a JOA. The SFAB may establish accounts and relationships with additional sustainment units in the JOA such as an expeditionary sustainment command, sustainment brigade, or a DSSB to receive additional transportation, ammunition resupply, supply and field services, and distribution support. Advisor teams assess the required days of supply for each class of supply in the predeployment planning cycle and continuously develop the logistics status picture to mitigate risks of delayed or degraded logistic support availability. 

#### **TRANSPORTATION** 

7-19. Advisor teams are assigned internal transportation equipment to facilitate training while in the continental United States and to enable operational reach during the advising mission in a deployed environment. The SFAB’s organizational equipment provides the necessary vehicles, repair tools, and sustainment vehicles to provide limited sustainment for their personnel and equipment. However, the force structure is intended to fall under an established support relationship from a higher headquarters. The assigned equipment is likely unsuitable for an expeditionary or austere environment, especially in a crisis or conflict zone. The OE may also inhibit deployment of part or all of these transportation assets, limit sustainment capabilities available to the team, or inordinately delay the arrival of Class IX repair parts or the Class III petroleum, oil, and lubricant products necessary to execute operations with the assigned vehicles. SFABs perform cost-benefit analysis to weigh the time and financial burden of operating the assigned equipment vice acquiring equipment or transportation services in theater. FSF and embassy personnel can advise the advisor teams on logistical support for transportation systems available within the AOR. Additional transportation contract support may be coordinated through ACSAs, foreign military sales, or FOO/pay agent activities to provide the required transportation capabilities. 

#### **MAINTENANCE OPERATIONS** 

7-20. Maintenance personnel and commanders, on advisor teams, must stress the importance of crew and operator preventative maintenance checks and services to keep equipment in an operational status and make every practicable attempt to repair nonmission-capable equipment. The maintenance advisor uses the advisor network to coordinate with the battalion S-4, who coordinates with a designated maintenance activity or the SPO for additional support. If authorized by the commander, maintainers can also utilize battle damage assessment and repair, or request/suggest operation of the equipment below fully mission capable status for a limited time while Class IX parts are awaiting arrival. In this case, a thorough risk assessment must be provided to the signing commander. 

7-21. In a location where a JOA and traditional military echelons of support have not been established, the SFAB may need to contract additional maintenance personnel, purchase, and adapt local Class IX repair parts, request unique part fabrication from host-nation machinists. They may also have to request replacement equipment, end items, and repair parts through the ASCC to the contracting support brigade or other Army contracting organizations. These techniques may not be applicable to all situations when operating outside of a JOA. 

#### **RECOVERY OPERATIONS** 

7-22. _Recovery_ is actions taken to extricate damaged or disabled equipment for return to friendly control or repair at another location (JP 3-34). SFAB commanders must emphasize the use of self- and like-vehicle recovery methods to the greatest extent possible. As FSF and indigenous civilian recovery assets may be inadequate to handle military vehicle recovery, advisor teams request recovery assets from their S-4 for external support. Vetting of indigenous recovery support is prudent in predeployment planning; if such vetting is impractical, escorting the recovered equipment from the incident site to its destination is recommended. 

#### **OPERATIONAL CONTRACT SUPPORT** 

7-23. OCS is the process of planning for and obtaining supplies, services, and construction from commercial sources in support of CCDR-directed operations (see ATP 4-10). The austere nature of the SFAB creates a tremendous need for this capability. Part of planning an SFAB’s mission, when host nation, embassy, or adjacent unit GS is unavailable, is determining which type of contracted support best meets the brigade’s sustainment needs. In any contracting support option, the SFAB is required to provide contracting officers’ representatives and receiving officials for contracted logistic services and commodities. 

#### **ACQUISITIONS AND CROSS-SERVICING AGREEMENTS** 

7-24. ACSAs provide U.S., allied, friendly nations, and international organizations access to material and services on a short-term basis. When leveraged by the SFAB, ACSAs provide the mechanism for logistics support during operations and exercises. The senior logistics advisor will be prepared to access ACSAs for life support when applicable. If a pertinent ACSA is not in place, the senior logistics advisor is encouraged to request one through the U.S. Embassy. Changing or superseding an ACSA requires U.S. Embassy approval. Training is provided through the Defense Acquisition University. (See FM 3-22 and AR 11-31 for more details on executing ACSAs.) 

#### **CONTRACTING OFFICER’S REPRESENTATIVE, FIELD ORDERING OFFICER, AND PAY AGENT** 

7-25. The contracting officer representative is an employee, military or civilian, of the U.S. Government, a foreign government, or a North Atlantic Treaty Organization coalition partner appointed in writing by a warranted contracting officer and serving in the position as an extra duty. Contracting officer representative responsibilities include monitoring contract performance and performing other duties specified in their appointment letter (see ATP 4-92). 

7-26. The FOO’s role is to procure authorized, urgently needed supplies and services from local sources during operations because normal supply channels are either not available or cannot provide them in a timely manner. FOOs derive their purchasing authority from a warranted contracting officer. FOOs have authorization to purchase supplies or nonpersonal services immediately available up to the established micropurchase threshold. 

7-27. A pay agent is an extension of the finance office’s disbursing officer at the unit level. The deputy disbursing officer or disbursing agent trains the pay agent to account for government funds and make payments in relatively small amounts to local vendors. The pay agent is paired with a FOO and cannot be appointed as a FOO or certifying officer, may not order or request goods or services (such as contract for), nor hold other accountable positions such as a property book officer as this would be a conflict of interest (see ATP 1-06.1). 

##### **SECTION IV – HEALTH SERVICES SUPPORT** 

7-28. Army and joint force medical assets provide healthcare to U.S. Soldiers across the entire competition continuum. Commanders, with the input of their medical support staff, are responsible for the operational management of the health service support and FHP missions for training, predeployment, deployment, post-deployment, and where applicable, advising and training the FSF. 

7-29. Although some features of the SFAB’s medical capabilities are common to maneuver BCTs, there are many differences. SFAB medical planners and team medics must conduct a detailed medical support assessment prior to operations in a new AO. At the least, this includes a review of lessons learned from prior SFA missions and the associated elements to ensure successful medical support for the brigade and its mission (see FM 4-02 and ATP 4-02.2). 

#### **HEALTH SERVICE SUPPORT** 

7-30. Health service support encompasses all medical support and services performed, provided, and arranged by the Army or the joint force. These services include organic and area medical support, hospitalization, treatment aspects of dental care, behavioral and neuropsychiatric treatment, clinical laboratory services, and treatment of CBRN patients (see FM 4-02 and ATP 4-02.2). 

#### **MEDICAL SUPPORT SECTION** 

7-31. The MSS can provide limited Role 1 Army Health System support to SFAB advisors and medical advising to foreign partner Role 1 to Role 3 capabilities. The MSS can provide limited routine medical care and trauma management, behavioral health, physical therapy, medical logistics, and medical maintenance for advisors. The section can also support SFAB brigade elements that do not have organic medical support. When providing medical support to the brigade, the dispersed nature of SFAB operations may make it difficult to physically support each advisor team. However, they may employ alternative methods of care, such as telemedicine, to bridge the gap. The MSS may provide medical care within its scope of capability to partner FSF in emergency situations to save life, limb, or eyesight. 

7-32. The MSS officer in charge (OIC) is responsible for operational activities the section conducts and serves as the brigade medical operations officer. Additionally, the MSS OIC determines the general set-up and security of the MSS’s AO in consultation with the force package commander and appropriate medical leaders. They direct, coordinate, and supervise team operations based on the brigade’s medical support plan. The MSS OIC provides assessments of the medical support and training requirements of the FSF to higher headquarters and requests appropriate materiel and personnel support from higher, adjacent, and subordinate elements to support the mission. The MSS OIC assists the other medical sections in planning, coordinating, and monitoring medical SPO. 

7-33. The MSS NCOIC assists the OIC and supervises the operations of the section and advises their assigned foreign counterpart. The MSS NCOIC is vital to effective supervision of the medical section activities including maintenance of section equipment, calibration of diagnostic tools, requests for general categories of supplies, and Class VIII supplies. They also prepare reports of medical section activities and functions with the MSS OIC. 

#### **FORCE HEALTH PROTECTION** 

7-34. FHP encompasses measures to promote, improve, or conserve the mental or physical well-being of Soldiers, such as operational public health, veterinary services, combat operational stress control, dental services, and laboratory services. The amount of health service support and FHP available depends on the AO, OE, advisor force package JOA status, and whether the subordinate advisor teams are operating within or outside a designated JOA. Operations outside a designated JOA may not have a well-established medical support network and depend on the ASCC and the host nation for life support functions, health service support, FHP, air MEDEVAC, and other health service support requirements. 

7-35. FHP encompasses all the preventive activities that promote the physiological and psychological well-being of Soldiers. FHP measures create and maintain a healthy and fit force, prevent injury and illness, and protect the force from health hazards. 

7-36. Preventing friendly health issues preserves the effectiveness of the SFAB by keeping its members available. Methods to prevent disease begin with personal hygiene and field sanitation (in accordance with TC 4-02.3), food and water sanitation, waste management, and pest control. Every region has unique health hazards that must be identified and analyzed by the SFAB medical personnel: preventing and controlling communicable diseases, illnesses (including vector, food, and waterborne diseases), injuries, and diseases, protects the health and readiness of Soldiers. Assessing these hazards summarizes acute or immediate risk mitigation actions, such as identifying which, if any, vaccines are required prior to deployment. 

7-37. The SFAB surgeon cell is involved in the early planning stages of each operation to perform health-threat-assessments and to identify effective personnel protective measures to counter these threats. Advisor teams and the MSS own a mutual responsibility to ensure adequate operational public health resources are used in deployed environments to prevent disease among early-entry forces and to prepare the way for follow-on forces. 

7-38. Preventive medicine personnel and units provide essential information, services, and recommendations for countermeasures to the SFAB medical officers as well as to commanders, team leaders, and individual Soldiers to sustain the health of the force. The MSS will brief deploying advisor teams on existing diseases friendly forces may bring with them to their AO, which could be transmitted to the local population or their foreign partners. The MSS will provide advisor teams health surveillance and medical intelligence, information which addresses occupational and environmental health threats from industrial hazards, air and water pollution, endemic disease, CBRN, and directed-energy device weapons (high-powered microwaves, particle beams, lasers). Preventative medicine is a team effort, both the MSS and advisor team working together, with team members ensuring candid and honest reporting to the MSS. 

7-39. When possible, and in accordance with ATP 4-02.8, during the PDSS, preventive medicine personnel will conduct an occupational and environmental health site assessment, documenting environmental conditions, identifying potential occupational and environmental health threats, guiding the occupational and environmental health data collection activities, and informing risk assessments. The occupational and environmental health site assessment will serve as a comprehensive baseline assessment, followed by periodic reassessments. Food and water risk assessments are also conducted outside the continental United States at food facilities where SFAB personnel eat, such as host-nation military dining facilities, caterers, and hotels. In accordance with JP 4-02, food and water risk assessments are performed by veterinary or public health personnel to identify and mitigate risk from intentional and unintentional food contamination. The assessments are conducted to associate a risk level to food facilities with mitigating practices for dining safety, not to inspect a facility instructing them how to operate. Medical officers should also conduct a hospital assessment and/or identify medical capabilities within the AOR. 

7-40. It is critical that all advisor teams working with a foreign partner, advocate for the continuous improvement of FHP processes. This will support the FSF’s overall health and well-being. Advisors may have a resistance to diseases that the foreign personnel have not encountered, the opposite is also a possibility. SFAB medical planners account for all these considerations during the planning process. This is briefed to advisors during the predeployment medical threat brief as a part of their Soldier readiness processing. Deployed advisors’ medical representatives will reach back to the MSS at home station with additional concerns, including requesting additional health surveillance including air, soil, water, and vectors. 

7-41. Regardless of conditions, advisor teams attempt to maintain its internal standards of health protection according to the Army and U.S. Government policy. While modern technology may provide significant defense against disease and hazards, the inability to access technology solutions may require SFAB personnel to adopt rudimentary practices developed by local inhabitants to combat health hazards. In these cases, the SFAB medical personnel identify and adopt these local practices to share among unit Soldiers. Medical surveillance will continue through the deployment to characterize and counter threats to the Soldiers’ health, well-being, and performance and provide commanders appropriate information to take appropriate risk management actions. Each team will also have at least one field sanitation-trained personnel to assist with medical surveillance. 

#### **MEDICAL AND CASUALTY EVACUATION** 

7-42. _Medical evacuation_ is the timely and effective movement of the wounded, injured, or ill to and between medical treatment facilities on dedicated and properly marked medical platforms with en route care provided by medical personnel (ATP 4-02.2). The SFAB has very limited organic MEDEVAC resources and is highly dependent upon the JOA medical units for augmentation.

7-43. Due to the lack of dedicated MEDEVAC platforms, advisor teams are encouraged to utilize nonmedical vehicles or aircraft for CASEVAC. Advisor teams can mitigate risk to casualties by conducting creative and adaptable CASEVAC rehearsals and augmenting CASEVAC platforms with a medic, combat lifesaver, and medical equipment. 

7-44. When deployed outside a JOA, coordination with the appropriate nodes—American Embassy, ASCC, host nation, and other capability providers—are critical prior to and during deployment. To ensure that the brigade receives timely evacuation of casualties, a continuous assessment of available capabilities is required. Area evacuation assets are preplanned, coordinated, and synchronized outside of a JOA, such as through various U.S. entities, host-nation assets, multinational, or contract evacuation assets. Early coordination and communication are vital to the success of MEDEVAC support to advisor team operations. Evacuation assets must be assessed, verified, and re-verified consistently during an advisor team’s deployment. Advisor teams and medical treatment providers need to familiarize themselves with the process for nonstandard MEDEVAC augmentation from both governmental and private sources 

##### **Appendix A** 

### **Advisor Guidance and Mission Statement** 

This annex provides the format and elucidates the thought process that higher commanders must consider in employing SFAB advisors most effectively. 

A-1. Commanders issue standardized guidance to allow relevant subordinate elements to begin their mission before orders production and dissemination is complete or to initiate lengthy processes early to synchronize effort. Some examples of standardized guidance are special instructions to staffs, reserve planning priorities, and reconnaissance guidance. Advisors, being a tailor-built force, also require like guidance to maximize their effectiveness within the force structure and orders production process. Using the below standardized advisor guidance and mission statement, commanders provide timely and meaningful direction to SFAB elements within MDMP, allowing advisors to carry out the advising mission and achieve the commander’s end state in a timely manner. 

A-2. A _mission_ is the essential task or tasks, together with the purpose, that clearly indicates the action to be taken and the reason for the action (JP 3-0). A _mission statement_ is a short sentence or paragraph that describes the organization’s essential task(s), purpose, and action containing the elements of who, what, when, where, and why (JP 5-0). Advisor guidance (whether from a theater sustainment command office to an advisor force package, a higher-level advisor team to a lower-level advisor team, an MNFLCC or CFLCC, to an advisor team embedded with an FSF partner formation) is uniquely oriented toward the advisor mission and capability and retains a similar structure to the tactical mission statement; commanders use this format to maintain familiarity with advisors of all ranks, relevant staff members, and subordinate commanders. 

- A-3. The advisor mission statement contains the following: 

 - An advisor activity—developmental (OTEBA) or conflict integration (ASLA)—used in the style of a tactical mission task. 

 - A type of advising guidance (like the focus and tempo decision provided in reconnaissance guidance (see figure 4-2 on page 107 for types of advising guidance). 

 - A decision on organizational reporting structure (see paragraphs 4-207 through 4-217 for more information). 

 - Plug-and-play format: [Who: Advisor Team] performs [What: advisor activity], [How: advising guidance (see figure 4-2 on page 107 for types of advising guidance)], organizational reporting structure] in [Where: location] at [When: time requirement] in order to [Why: purpose]. 

- A-4. Advisor mission statement examples: 

 - MAT 1234 Trains and Advises, using separate and enable & vertical reporting structure, in South America NLT 30FEB20XX in order to build partner capability and capacity. 

 - BAT 400 Assesses, Supports, Liaises, and Advises, using accompany and enable & mixed reporting structure, the 1st Atropian Mechanized Division in Atropia, NLT 30FEB20XX in order to enable 1st Atropian Mechanized Division ability to defend in Atropia. 

 - BAT 520 Organizes, Trains, Equips, Builds/Rebuilds, Advises, separate and refrain & horizontal reporting structure in Gabal NLT 30FEB20XX in order to build partner capability and capacity; be prepared to execute ASLA using accompany and enable & horizontal 1st Gabal Infantry Division NLT 31FEB20XX in order to defend Gabal territorial integrity. 

This page intentionally left blank.
