##### **Appendix C** 

### **Advisor Assessment Tools** 

What follows is a standardized example of a wholistic assessment framework. The tools represented in this appendix are _a way_ for SFAB teams and leaders to communicate both objectively and subjectively the status of a partner force’s capability and capacity data. The utility of the frameworks is primarily derived from the conversations and correspondence that advisor teams and stakeholders have in creating, maintaining, and communicating the framework from observer/assessor to stakeholders rather than the completed assessment rubric itself. The assessment methods and tactics, techniques, and procedures advisor teams use may include these or others, as prescribed by CCMD, ASCC, or other higher headquarters assessment requirements. 

#### **INTEROPERABILITY ASSESSMENT RUBRIC** 

C-1. The Interoperability Assessment Rubric is modified from the American, British, Canadian, Australian, and New Zealand (ABCANZ) interoperability metrics and assesses how well the FSF partner unit implements interoperability standards and the relative difficulty of incorporating the partner force into a NATO or other multinational formation. 

C-2. The ABCANZ interoperability program is an effects-based, outcome-focused organization founded on deliberate analysis of interoperability gaps and the development of human, procedural and technical interoperability solutions required by armies to close or mitigate interoperability gaps. 

C-3. The Army, as part of ABCANZ, recognizes four levels of interoperability for use with the partner to assess interoperability gaps against the three domains of interoperability (human, procedural, and technical). These levels are: 

- Level 0 (Not Interoperable). 

- Level 1 (Deconflicted). 

- Level 2 (Compatible). 

- Level 3 (Integrated). 

C-4. Level 0 _(Not Interoperable):_ Partners have no demonstrated interoperability. C2 interface with the U.S. Army is only at the next higher echelon. Partner formations must operate independently from U.S. Army formations and operations. 

C-5. _Level 1 (Deconflicted):_ U.S. Army and partners can coexist but do not interact. Requires alignment of capabilities and procedures to establish operational norms, enabling partners and the U.S. Army to complement each other’s operations. 

C-6. _Level 2 (Compatible):_ U.S. Army and partners can interact with each other in the same geographic area in pursuit of a common goal. U.S. Army and partners have similar or complementary processes and procedures and can operate effectively with each other. 

C-7. _Level 3 (Integrated):_ U.S. Army and partners can integrate upon arrival in theater. Interoperability is network-enabled to provide the full range of military operations capability. Partners can routinely establish networks and operate effectively with or as part of U.S. Army formations. 

**_Note._** See AR 34-1 for more information on the U.S. Army’s levels of interoperability. 

C-8. Tables C-1 through C-3, on pages 190 through 195, further show the relationship of SFABs to partner interoperability.

#### **ORGANIZATIONAL ASSESSMENT RUBRIC** 

C-9. The Organizational Assessment Rubric (see tables C-4 through C-13 on pages 196 through 205) is modified from the U.S. Army’s Unit Status Reporting (reporting system). It measures the capability of a partner unit as assessed on the warfighting functions in the OE. 

###### **Table C-4. Assessment rubric** 

|**_Warfighting_**<br>**_Functions_**|**_C-0:_**<br>**_Incapable_**|**_C-1:_**<br>**_Marginally Capable_**|**_C-2:_**<br>**_Capable_**|**_C-3:_**<br>**_Fully Capable_**|
|---|---|---|---|---|
|Mission Command<br>Purpose/Direction|Poorly resourced or<br>untrained staff.<br>No or little<br>understanding of<br>art of command/<br>science of control.|Resources or staff<br>lacking.<br>Commander<br>provides some<br>purpose or direction.|Most resources<br>available, staff is<br>trained but lacks<br>experience.<br>Commander<br>provides purpose<br>and direction.|Fully resourced,<br>fully staffed, all<br>aspects of MC<br>warfighting<br>functions met.<br>Commander<br>provides clear<br>purpose and<br>direction.|
|Movement and<br>Maneuver<br>Achieve tactical<br>advantage|Unit lacks<br>resources to<br>employ subordinate<br>elements relative to<br>enemy.|Unit resourced, but<br>incapable of<br>employing forces.<br>Unit capable of<br>employing forces but<br>is not resourced.|Unit achieves most<br>tactical advantages<br>regardless of<br>resource shortfalls.|Commander,<br>assisted by the<br>staff, employs<br>combat power to<br>achieve<br>advantage.|
|Intelligence<br>Understanding of<br>OE|Intelligence<br>collection poorly<br>executed, intel<br>products do not<br>facilitate<br>understanding,<br>collection assets do<br>not exist.|Intel collection<br>limited by lack of<br>collection assets or<br>lack of training.<br>Intel products<br>provide minimal<br>understanding.|Intelligence<br>collection results in<br>products that<br>facilitate basic<br>understanding of<br>operational<br>environment.|Intelligence<br>collection,<br>products and<br>understanding<br>facilitate<br>understanding and<br>enable<br>commander to<br>make timely<br>decisions.|

#### **WILL TO FIGHT ASSESSMENT RUBRIC** 

C-10. The Will to Fight Assessment Rubric (see tables C-5 through C-13 on pages 197 through 205) is a metric which attempts to quantify the assessed morale of partner formations and measure a unit’s predicted capacity to endure in the OE. The W-rating is assessed in four tiers: W-1, unwilling and unable; W-2, marginally unwilling and unable; W-3, willing and able; W-4, very willing and able. 

**Table C-5. Will to fight** 

||**_Will to Fight Assessment Rubric_**|
|---|---|
|**_Variables_**|**_Overarching Questions_**|
|Military Education System|Is the officer education system and noncommissioned officer education<br>system equitably available, or are slots distributed in an unfair manner? Is<br>there a leader development program outside of the institutional domain?<br>What is the process to evaluate Soldier performance? Does a “train the<br>trainer” function exist?|
|Recruiting and Retention|How does the military gain new members—compulsory service, draft,<br>volunteer, and so forth? How does the organization incentivize recruitment<br>and retention? Are recruiting and retention centrally controlled by a<br>dedicated cadre or distributed to lower echelons to manage? Are recruiting<br>and retention governed by civilian policy? Does the unit conduct public<br>visibility/public relations events in their communities?|
|Organizational Trust and<br>Cohesion|How is the relationship between adjacent units and how well do they<br>cooperate? Do the members of the organization believe their leadership<br>works on their behalf? What is the relationship between the military and<br>government? Does the unit have accountability programs—SHARP, EO,<br>DEOCS, for example? Do the members of the organization both provide and<br>integrateperformance feedback?|
|Sustainment & Medical|Does the sustainment enterprise allow for timely logistics and repair<br>operations? Are there adequate sustainment personnel on hand to ensure<br>fleets are maintained to standard? Are sustainment operations included in<br>operations plans and trained during maneuver training? What is the quality<br>and reliabilityof food,clothing,and housing?|
|Training Realism|Does the unit conduct METL and mission-focused training with progressively<br>more challenging conditions (night, CBRN, contested, and so forth.)? Does<br>the military have a professional OC/T capability and institutionalized doctrine<br>process? How often is doctrine assessed and updated based on emerging<br>threats? Is there a dedicated CTC or formal system for EXEVALs?|
|Soldier Quality of Life|How does military compensation (all pay plus benefits) compare to the<br>comparable civilian population? What is the comparative quality of life<br>between service members and general population? Does promotion and pay<br>keep pace over time with comparable civilian population? Are Soldiers<br>adequately equipped for their operational environment? Is there a system to<br>record and resolve concerns?|
|History|Does the partner unit have recent operational experience, and is it a source<br>of pride? Does the partner unit have a deeper history and is it a source of<br>pride for the service members and the organization? How has the partner<br>unit performed in past conflicts? Does the partner force have a history of<br>warfare or violence (either by or against) their adversary?|

Terms for which ATP 3-96.1 is the proponent are marked with an asterisk (*). The proponent publication for other terms is listed in parentheses after the definition.

##### **SECTION II – TERMS** 

###### ***advisor network** 

The system for the integrating and consolidating partner force capability and capacity improvements over time at multiple echelons. 

###### **agility** 

The ability to move forces and adjust their dispositions and activities more rapidly than the enemy. (FM 3-0) 

###### **area security** 

A type of security operation conducted to protect friendly forces, lines of communications, and activities within a specific area. (ADP 3-90) 

###### **Army personnel recovery** 

The military efforts taken to prepare for and execute the recovery and reintegration of isolated personnel. (FM 3-50) 

###### **assessment** 

A continuous process that measures the overall effectiveness of employing capabilities during military operations. (JP 3-0) 

###### **battle rhythm** 

(Army) A deliberate daily cycle of command, staff, and unit activities intended to synchronize current and future operations. (FM 6-0) 

###### **combat power** 

(DOD) The total means of destructive and disruptive force that a military unit/formation can apply against an enemy at a given time. (JP 3-0) 

###### **common operational picture** 

(Army) A display of relevant information within a commander’s area of interest tailored to the user’s requirements and based on common data and information shared by more than one command. (ADP 6-0) 

###### **convergence** 

An outcome created by the concerted employment of capabilities from multiple domains and echelons against combinations of decisive points in any domain to create effects against a system, formation, decision maker, or in a specific geographic area. (FM 3-0) 

###### **counterintelligence** 

Information gathered and activities conducted to identify, deceive, exploit, disrupt, or protect against espionage, other intelligence activities, sabotage, or assassinations conducted for or on behalf of foreign powers, organizations or persons or their agents, or international terrorist organizations or activities. (JP 2-0) 

###### **country team** 

The senior, in-country, United States coordinating and supervising body, headed by the chief of the United States diplomatic mission, and composed of the senior member of each represented United States department or agency, as desired by the chief of the United States diplomatic mission. (JP 3-07.4) 

###### **crisis** 

An emerging incident or situation involving a possible threat to the United States, its citizens, military forces, or vital interests that develops rapidly and creates a condition of such diplomatic, economic, or military importance that commitment of military forces and resources is contemplated to achieve national and/or strategic objectives. (JP 3-0) 

###### **critical asset** 

A specific entity that is of such extraordinary importance that its incapacitation or destruction would have a very serious, debilitating effect on the ability of a nation to continue to function effectively. (JP 3-26) 

###### **depth** 

The extension of operations in time, space, or purpose to achieve definitive results. (ADP 3-0) 

###### **direct support** 

(DOD) A mission requiring a force to support another specific force and authorizing it to answer directly to the supported force’s request for assistance. (JP 3-09.3) 

###### **endurance** 

The ability to persevere over time throughout the depth of an operational environment. (FM 3-0) 

###### **field artillery** 

(DOD) Equipment, supplies, ammunition, and personnel involved in the use of cannon, rocket, or surface-to-surface missile launchers. (JP 3-09) 

###### **fires warfighting function** 

The related tasks and systems that create and converge effects in all domains against the adversary or enemy to enable operations across the range of military operations. (ADP 3-0) 

###### **general support** 

Support given to the supported force as a whole and not to any particular subdivision thereof. (JP 3-09.3) 

###### **general support-reinforcing** 

(Army) A support relationship assigned to a unit to support the force as a whole and to reinforce another similar type unit. (FM 3-0) 

###### **geospatial intelligence** 

The exploitation and analysis of imagery and geospatial information to describe, assess, and visually depict physical features and geographically referenced activities on or about the Earth. Geospatial intelligence consists of imagery, imagery intelligence, and geospatial information. (JP 2-0) 

###### **hostile environment** 

An operational environment where the freedom of movement is contested. (JP 3-0) 

###### **human intelligence** 

(Army) The collection by a trained human intelligence collector of foreign information from people and multimedia to identify elements, intentions, composition, strength, dispositions, tactics, equipment, and capabilities. (ADP 2-0) 

###### **indicator** 

In the context of assessment, a specific piece of information that infers the condition, state, or existence of something, and provides a reliable means to ascertain performance or effectiveness. (JP 5-0) 

###### **information collection** 

An activity that synchronizes and integrates the planning and employment of sensors and assets as well as the processing, exploitation, and dissemination systems in direct support of current and future operations. (FM 3-55) 

###### **intelligence preparation of the operational environment** 

The systematic process of analyzing the mission variables of enemy, terrain, weather, and civil considerations in an area of interest to determine their effect on operations. (FM 2-0) 

###### **interoperability** 

The ability to act together coherently, effectively, and efficiently to achieve tactical, operational, and strategic objectives. (JP 3-0) 

###### **knowledge management** 

(Army) The process of enabling knowledge flow to enhance shared understanding, learning, and decision making. (ADP 6-0) 

###### **liaison** 

That contact or intercommunication maintained between elements of military forces or other agencies to ensure mutual understanding and unity of purpose and action. (FM 6-0) 

###### **local security** 

The low-level security activities conducted near a unit to prevent surprise by the enemy. (ADP 3-90) 

###### **measurement and signature intelligence** 

Information produced by quantitative and qualitative analysis of physical attributes of targets and events to detect, characterize, locate, and identify targets and events; and derived from specialized, technically derived measurements and signatures of physical phenomenon intrinsic to an object or event. (JP 2-0) 

###### **measure of effectiveness** 

An indicator used to measure a current system state, with change indicated by comparing multiple observations over time. (JP 5-0) 

###### **measure of performance** 

An indicator used to measure a friendly action that is tied to measuring task accomplishment. (JP 5-0) 

###### **medical evacuation** 

The timely and effective movement of the wounded, injured, or ill to and between medical treatment facilities on dedicated and properly marked medical platforms with en route care provided by medical personnel. (ATP 4-02.2) 

###### **mission** 

The essential task or tasks, together with the purpose, that clearly indicates the action to be taken and the reason for the action. (JP 3-0) 

###### **mission statement** 

A short sentence or paragraph that describes the organization’s essential task(s), purpose, and action containing the elements of who, what, when, where, and why. (JP 5-0) 

###### **mission variables** 

Categories of specific information needed to conduct operations. (ADP 1-01) 

###### **open-source intelligence** 

Publicly available information collected, exploited, and disseminated to address a specific requirement. (JP 2-0) 

###### **operational control** 

The authority to perform those functions of command over subordinate forces involving organizing and employing commands and forces, assigning tasks, designating objectives, and giving authoritative direction necessary to accomplish the mission. (JP 1, Vol 2) 

###### **permissive environment** 

Uncontested conditions in which joint forces have freedom of movement. (JP 3-0) 

###### **physical security** 

That part of security concerned with physical measures designed to safeguard personnel; to prevent unauthorized access to equipment, installations, material, and documents; and to safeguard them against espionage, sabotage, damage, and theft. (JP 3-0) 

###### **protection warfighting function** 

The related tasks, systems, and methods that prevent or mitigate detection, threat effects, and hazards to preserve combat power and enable freedom of action. (FM 3-0) 

###### **recovery** 

Actions taken to extricate damaged or disabled equipment for return to friendly control or repair at another location. (JP 3-34) 

###### **reinforcing** 

A support relationship requiring a force to support another supporting unit. (FM 3-0) 

###### **risk** 

Probability and severity driven chance of loss, caused by threat or other hazards. (ATP 5-19) 

###### **risk management** 

The process to identify, assess, and mitigate risks and make decisions that balance risk cost with mission benefits. (JP 3-0) 

###### **running estimate** 

The continuous assessment of the current situation used to determine if the current operation is proceeding according to the commander’s intent and if planned future operations are supportable. (ADP 5-0) 

###### **security force assistance** 

(DOD) The Department of Defense activities that support the development of the capability and capacity of foreign security forces and their supporting institutions. (JP 3-20) 

###### **signals intelligence** 

A category of intelligence comprising all communications intelligence, electronic intelligence, and foreign instrumentation signals intelligence, however transmitted, individually or in combination. (JP 2-0) 

###### **situational understanding** 

The product of applying analysis and judgment to relevant information to determine the relationships among the operational and mission variables. (ADP 6-0) 

###### **staff section** 

A grouping of staff members by area of expertise. (FM 6-0) 

###### **support** 

The action of a force that aids, protects, complements, or sustains another force in accordance with a directive requiring such action. (JP 1, Vol 2) 

###### **support operations** 

The staff function of planning, coordinating, and synchronizing sustainment in support of units conducting decisive action in an area of operations. (ATP 4-92) 

###### **sustainment warfighting function** 

The related tasks and systems that provide support and services to ensure freedom of action, extend operational reach, and prolong endurance. (ADP 3-0) 

###### **targeting** 

The process of selecting and prioritizing targets and matching the appropriate response to them, considering operational requirements and capabilities. (JP 3-0) 

###### **technical intelligence** 

Intelligence derived from the collection, processing, analysis, and exploitation of data and information pertaining to foreign equipment and materiel for the purposes of preventing technological surprise, assessing foreign scientific and technical capabilities, and developing countermeasures designed to neutralize an enemy’s technological advantages. (JP 2-0) 

###### **uncertain environment** 

A condition in which the joint force’s freedom of movement may be contested. (JP 3-0) 

###### **warfighting function** 

A group of tasks and systems united by a common purpose that commanders use to accomplish missions and training objectives. (ADP 3-0)
